JRN-01 | Started small, weak and untrained at 14-15 | He began training at 14-15 as the smallest kid in his class after losing arm wrestling to a girl; he could not do a single push-up and could only train about 10 minutes (knee push-ups, sit-ups, light curls).
JRN-02 | Built training tolerance gradually | His first sessions were about 20 minutes of push-ups, sit-ups and air squats, and he extended them step by step; doing more at the start would have burnt him out.
JRN-03 | Phase 1: bedroom calisthenics, ages ~14-17 | With no gym open to minors, he trained bodyweight at home, curled chairs and rocks and sprinted in the mountains while growing almost a foot.
JRN-04 | Phase 2: an adjustable dumbbell set, ages 17-20 | He trained daily in his bedroom with one adjustable dumbbell set, starting with 5 lb curls, and did unsupported dumbbell rows every day for 4 years because he had no bench.
JRN-05 | Push-ups built his chest, not the bench | Hundreds of daily push-ups with no rest days made the biggest single difference to his physique; dropping them for bench was a mistake, and his chest is now only maintained.
JRN-06 | Looked untrained for years | Despite years of training he did not look like a lifter for a long time; with average genetics it took about 6 years to look trained and 10+ to look jacked, and his lesson is patience.
JRN-07 | Team sports gave him his conditioning | He played football then basketball as a teen, mostly running and hill sprints, but liked the weight room more than the games, which showed him lifting was his path.
JRN-08 | First gym and barbell at 20 in the US | He moved to the US for a scholarship master's and entered his first gym at 20 having never touched a barbell.
JRN-09 | Glad fitness YouTube did not exist back then | He made his best gains knowing nothing about angles, protein timing or optimal volume, and thinks that knowledge might have made him timid; he is also glad he did not start a channel in 2016, when he would have spread misinformation.
JRN-10 | No supplements for his first 7 years | He used no supplements for years; milk was his only protein source as a teen because whey was not available.
JRN-11 | Bodyweight path from 110 to 220 lb | He went from about 110 lb at 15 to a fat peak of 235-240 lb around age 23, crash-cut to ~205-210, recomped at 210 for about three years, then slowly bulked to 220 lb, which he now maintains.
JRN-12 | His gains rate halved about every five years | Each five-year block brought roughly half the bodyweight gain of the one before.
JRN-13 | Best gains came from a tiny surplus held for years | As a skinny teen eating what his parents served, plus a little extra each day, he gained steadily for years without tracking, supplements or ever getting chubby; he calls the clean bulk that poverty forced on him "a blessing".
JRN-14 | The dirty bulk to 240 lb was wasted years | His dirty bulk gave the most fat and least muscle of any period, left lasting love handles and a wrecked appetite, and his small-framed 6 ft body could not carry the weight.
JRN-15 | Dysmorphia hid his obesity at 240 lb | At 240 lb he was borderline obese while body dysmorphia told him the bulk was working; broken ribs while squatting, rib pain and swollen ankles after short walks finally made him cut.
JRN-16 | The panic crash cut and rebound | He cut about 30 lb in a panic, found it traumatic, then panic-bulked to 225 lb from dysmorphia before settling; repeated 30-lb gain/cut cycles piled up injuries and stubborn fat.
JRN-17 | Three-year recomp at 210 lb | Holding ~210 lb for about three years, he leaned out while legs grew and strength rose (variations and accessories kept progressing when main lifts stalled); the main benefits were healing, no pain and better mental health, and he calls it his "proudest progress".
JRN-18 | Slow bulk to 220 lb after the recomp | After the recomp he bulked very slowly to 220 lb and maintains it; reaching 220 with subpar genetics took close attention, not bro splits.
JRN-19 | Body composition beats scale weight | An old photo 10 lb lighter showed him much fatter; at 6 ft he thinks you need about 200 lb to look big.
JRN-20 | More diet mistakes than training mistakes | He says he made more mistakes in diet than in training, some causing damage he cannot repair.
JRN-21 | No program at first meant stalling | For his first one to two years he trained randomly (machines, bench) with no program; he still gained but never adapted neurologically and stalled, and a plan would have been far more productive.
JRN-22 | A simple linear dumbbell plan gave his best gains | About three years on a simple linear dumbbell program in his bedroom took him from skinny to visibly trained; he trained full body for three years as a beginner.
JRN-23 | Copying YouTube-fitness plans stalled him | Following others' plans after discovering fitness YouTube stopped his growth and left him in pain, e.g. months of machine-only 20-rep pump work with no results.
JRN-24 | The powerbuilding era and his biggest plateau | A compound-only program grew some parts and not others, and his own powerbuilding program (BBBS, written 2017) brought "the biggest plateau of his life": barely any gains, constant injuries and damaged mental health, with recovery taking until 2020.
JRN-25 | From strength chaser to hypertrophy-only | Told that getting strong would make him big, he obsessed over maxing the big three and got injuries and fat instead; that is why he now optimizes purely for hypertrophy and says he "doesn't care about strength".
JRN-26 | Two years in "intermediate hell" | Novices can do anything but intermediates must program precisely; his old program left him a "lifetime intermediate" and repeatedly injured.
JRN-27 | He writes his own programs | He says he writes his own programs because a self-written plan may be less optimal but more suitable, and you learn from its mistakes; his gains tracked his programming skill.
JRN-28 | The "final program" idea, later dropped | His "final program" took about four years to build and test; he later rejected the idea of a final program because the body keeps evolving.
JRN-29 | Experience-based, not study-based | He learned from YouTube lifters (Alan Thrall for deadlift and press, Candito for squat, Omar Isuf for bench) and his own experiments; he has never read a programming book or changed his training because of a study.
JRN-30 | Load jumps too big, range of motion too short | When he first progressed he added 10 lb per side each time and took "forever" to see the jumps were too big after stalling; his recurring sin is stacking weight and shortening range of motion until he resets lighter.
JRN-31 | Never ran 5×5 | He used evolving rep ranges from the start but admits 5×5 would have moved him through the novice phase faster; dumbbells-only is fine for novices, but barbell patterns should be learnt early.
JRN-32 | Years of daily training with no rest days | In his early years he trained every day with no rest days or deloads, plus morning runs, without problems; he says his injuries from that period came from programming, not frequency.
JRN-33 | Extreme training days without harm | He has trained for many hours a day plus mountain running without rhabdomyolysis; in his view overtraining is at worst junk volume.
JRN-34 | 2020-2023 standard: 6 days, 2-3 hours | For years he trained six days a week for two to three hours, close to failure, with no deloads, and still progressed year-round; he says most people do not need that long.
JRN-35 | Why his sessions were long | Volume mattered for him: high-but-not-maximal strength work with little tonnage, then many supersetted variations and accessories; replacing compounds with isolation would take 4-5 h/day, and in 2021 he said he was not strong enough to trade volume for intensity below 5 days.
JRN-36 | 2023: many exercises, few low-rep sets | In 2023 he still trained six days with many exercises per session, but with only a few low-rep sets each, far less volume than his GVS program.
JRN-37 | Shift to less volume, less frequency, more intensity | Over 3-4 years his programming moved to less volume, fewer days and more intensity while he kept progressing; he now thinks his first-year YouTube programs were too high in volume and frequency.
JRN-38 | 2024 routine: evening sessions after work | In 2024 he trained five days a week in the evening after a full workday.
JRN-39 | Training fitted around long work weeks | He fits training around long work weeks, family and channel work, and would cut training time if he could get the same gains.
JRN-40 | Strength kept, size lost, on near-zero volume | In grad school he only worked up to 1RM bench and overhead press, kept a 3-plate bench for months, but lost a lot of size.
JRN-41 | Every part he grew, he trained hard | His most-grown parts are the ones he trained "like a madman"; none "just grew".
JRN-42 | Biceps and the cheat-curl years | Biceps were always his best part because he put everything into them, mainly years of heavy cheat curls; he reached a 100 lb cheat curl in 2022 and then quit them.
JRN-43 | Arms grew from 10 to 18.4 inches | He built his arms "through isolation, not compounds" despite small wrists and what he calls poor arm genetics; 19 in may take another 2-3 years.
JRN-44 | Triceps neglected for six years | He barely trained triceps for his first ~6 years and now does catch-up work; in 2024 biceps and abs were his strongest-looking parts and triceps and shoulders his weakest. He wishes someone had told him one year in.
JRN-45 | Shoulders: de-prioritized, then isolation and pressing | He skipped vertical pressing for two years so his arms would outgrow his delts, then in 2022 added overhead press and lateral and rear-delt work; shoulders and traps improved through isolation and more serious pressing.
JRN-46 | Legs: skipped for five years, then squats | He skipped legs for about his first five years (which gave him a big upper body), then mostly only squatted; his legs visibly grew after two years of frequent squatting, and after an injury he regained 3 plates with bigger legs.
JRN-47 | Hamstrings fixed, thighs reached 28 inches | Five years of squat-only leg work left him quad-dominant; RDLs and hyperextensions added an inch to his thighs during the recomp, and he reached his thigh goal, with leg extensions and adductors next.
JRN-48 | Same squat every time made him regress | When he only back squatted, or squatted the same way 3×/week, he regressed and got hurt; his legs grew from back squats plus lots of safety-bar and front squats, with leg press, extensions and hack squats filling volume.
JRN-49 | Calves: his biggest regret | He never trained calves directly for about nine years (sports built them to 16 in), and not training them sooner is his "biggest bodybuilding regret".
JRN-50 | Back: thick but narrow until pull-ups returned | Deadlift-only years gave him a thick but zero-width "surfboard" back, and he hit his upper back only as a helper for other lifts; dropping pull-ups for two years cost back development he regrets, and his lats grew strictly from pull-ups and pullovers once he trained them properly.
JRN-51 | Abs built with thousands of crunches | Thousands of anchored crunches a day as a teen built his abs, but five years of hitting only the upper abs left an imbalance; in 2023 he did a moderate weekly dose of sit-ups and leg raises.
JRN-52 | Forearms grew from isolation, not grippers | Over a decade of grippers gave him a ball of muscle on skinny forearms; reverse curls, wrist curls and arm-wrestling methods grew them, quickly, and they are now his favourite muscle.
JRN-53 | Neck: 16 to 18 inches | His neck was 16 in without direct training in 2020; he set an 18-in goal and reached it by 2024.
JRN-54 | Recent lift choices: dips instead of bench | In 2023-2024 he quit benching in favour of dips and push-ups, went months without deadlifting, and progressed weighted dips steadily.
JRN-55 | Strength milestones felt like nothing | He felt "absolutely nothing" on hitting the 3-plate bench he was promised would change everything; he says PR culture sells meaningless milestones, and he will never give strength advice or chase a 225-lb overhead press.
JRN-56 | Broken ribs squatting at 240 lb | He broke both sides of his ribcage (twice) and tore intercostals squatting at about 240 lb; he still has chronic intercostal pain, managed with a rib routine between warm-ups and top sets.
JRN-57 | Bench injuries at heavy touch-and-go | In four years of benching he had one pec strain and repeated right-shoulder injuries (rotator cuff, AC), always at heavy touch-and-go sets; at 23 he saw the problem was chronic and changed approach.
JRN-58 | Years of tendinitis, fully healed | Street basketball gave him about two years of debilitating patellar tendinitis, and he had tendinitis in both knees and elbows for years; he healed fully and returned to heavy squats, and three pain-free years "probably saved" his relationship with lifting.
JRN-59 | "No pain, no gain" gave his worst years | Under a "no pain, no gain" mindset he squatted and deadlifted with broken bones and overate; those were his worst years, and his best were when he loved lifting.
JRN-60 | No back injury from deadlifting | He had no back injury in four years of conventional deadlifting, crediting avoiding "fishing rod" form and home bed bridges, and pulled heavy every two weeks for two years pain-free.
JRN-61 | Scoliosis and the mixed-grip regret | He has had scoliosis since his teens (one shoulder lower) and pulled mixed grip for about two years without competing, which he regrets; it did not grow his forearms.
JRN-62 | Normal testosterone, stable since puberty | He had all the signs of low testosterone at 17 (never tested) but fixed his environment; yearly blood tests show T slightly above normal and stable since puberty, with only a minimal rise that cannot explain his mass.
JRN-63 | Family history of cancer | His great-grandfather, grandfather and father died of prostate (and colon) cancer; he considers himself next in line, uses diet as his main prevention, and would be happy to reach 60.
JRN-64 | Corticosteroids at 10-11 | Corticosteroids for facial paralysis (Bell's palsy) made him gain weight fast, and he started middle school short, fat and acned.
JRN-65 | Rarely sick | He was not sick from age 15 until an illness in 2023, when he chose training and recovery over making videos.
JRN-66 | Cold showers and a freezing garage gym | He takes daily cold showers and trains in an unheated garage through long winters with no pump and still grows; he cannot train in humidity.
JRN-67 | Endurance kept, running ability lost | His sports conditioning persisted and four years of giant sets without dedicated cardio preserved his endurance, yet at his current mass he is poor at running and jumping and gasses out quickly outside lifting.
JRN-68 | Training is his mental-health anchor | He gets antsy or achy after 2-3 days off and says the "dark thoughts return" after 3-4; rest days become weighted-carry days.
JRN-69 | Discipline over motivation | He is truly motivated for only a small share of workouts; training is scheduled like work and he goes regardless, e.g. a freezing garage after a 10-hour shift. Motivation he built at 13-14 grew into discipline.
JRN-70 | The "gymcel" phase | From about 18 to 23 he trained without enjoyment, was anxious after missed days, trained through broken ribs until he dreaded the gym and looked down on "normies"; freeing himself from the obsession brought better gains.
JRN-71 | From rigid to balanced | He now says he is happiest, has his best gains in years, and can take a week off or move sessions for family; he credits his wife for making him less rigid.
JRN-72 | Years of bigorexia | Body dysmorphia hid his obesity at 240 lb; he made big progress against it using the practices from his own videos but still sometimes feels tiny.
JRN-73 | Lifting replaced video games and lasted | He has an addictive personality and drops passions once fed up, but lifting has lasted and he is certain he will bodybuild for life; it displaced video games after a two-month internet outage at 15, and he later quit games because they made him depressed.
JRN-74 | Bullied until getting big ended it | He was bullied and beaten until about 14; fighting back stopped it but made him aggressive, and getting big ended fights entirely and gave him peace.
JRN-75 | Anger as fuel backfired | Channelling anger and bad memories into heavy lifts made him aggressive outside the gym, so he stopped; he says using trauma as fuel produces more trauma.
JRN-76 | The mental gains outweigh the physique | Lifting made him better in every aspect of life; he values what it did for his mind and willpower far above the muscle and would give up all his muscle to keep it.
JRN-77 | Patience: the journey over the finish line | His early years were enjoyable because the journey mattered; chasing a finish line turned lifting into self-punishment, and he would rather reach his goal five years later with sound joints.
JRN-78 | Average genetics, started early, never quit | He was told as a kid there was no point trying and later that he had good genetics "all along"; he rates his genetics 5/10 and says he is bigger than viewers only because he started sooner and never quit.
JRN-79 | Without training he would be 150-160 lb | He believes that without lifting he would weigh about 150-160 lb at 6 ft; friends who did not change their environment stayed the same.
JRN-80 | Intermediate with some advanced parts | In 2020 he had lifted about ten years but counted only two to three as serious bodybuilding, and called himself intermediate with some advanced parts; advanced means a decade of hard training.
JRN-81 | Still expects his best gains ahead | He expects the best gains of his life in his 30s and the coming five years, and still finds weaknesses after 15 years.
JRN-82 | A year-long plateau would be his failure | If he spends a year in a plateau, he says he will call himself a failure rather than blame a natural limit.
JRN-83 | He nearly took drugs in 2016-17 | He thought he had hit his natural limit, blamed his testosterone and seriously considered drugs though he was still progressing; he spent hours on tren and deca content and admits he was "one bad day away".
JRN-84 | What stopped him | Deportation risk as an immigrant, his own research, and meeting users whose lives looked grim stopped him and made him an active opponent; he would not take even a risk-free cycle, and he says young men write weekly saying they quit or never started because of his videos.
JRN-85 | Naivety protected him as a beginner | As a beginner he did not know what steroids were; his mother thought whey was a steroid.
JRN-86 | Straight edge | He takes no drugs, has never smoked or been drunk and barely drinks; his issue is dependence. Lifting from 15 kept him away from bad crowds, and one heavy weed experience scared him off.
JRN-87 | Whole foods for life | After fast food at 15 he committed to whole foods only; a relative's death made him change his diet, and he eats raw garlic and ginger daily.
JRN-88 | A cleaner environment, a bigger body | His youthful gaming, junk-food, low-sleep lifestyle was "not conducive to gains"; the purer the environment he gives his body, the bigger he gets.
JRN-89 | Introvert with monk-like priorities | He is an extreme introvert who needs solitude and repetition; he prioritizes lifting and work, then family, and sacrifices social gatherings.
JRN-90 | Why the channel exists | He started the channel during the 2020 lockdown, after about ten years of lifting, to promote natural bodybuilding and give the advice he wished he had had; he calls it a community, not a route to fame, money or numbers.
JRN-91 | From refusing money to Ko-fi funding | He refused monetization and sponsors for years; once he became sole breadwinner, Ko-fi pledges turned the channel into a paid second job, still without ads or sponsors.
JRN-92 | From refusing to coach to a coaching school | For years he refused to coach, saying he was too impatient and harsh; in 2025 he coaches in a paid School of Strategy and calls it "the best choice of his life".
JRN-93 | Harsh with beginners | He berates beginners because a kick in the butt worked for him, but admits it pushes people away.
JRN-94 | Channel milestones | The channel was terminated without warning in 2022 and restored within 24 hours with other creators' help, fought long copyright battles over anime clips, and grew to 100K subscribers by 2023 with an almost entirely male audience.
JRN-95 | Competing: wanted, never done | He has repeatedly talked about entering a natural show but has not competed.
MND-01 | Discipline over motivation | Motivation spikes and fades; discipline gets you to the gym. The top level is training without deliberation because it's simply on the schedule and you love it.
MND-02 | Motivational content replaces action | Hype and motivational videos are a dopamine spike that substitutes for training and makes you passive (the "David Goggins effect"); a video should be the spark that sends you to train, not a replacement.
MND-03 | Motivation must come from inside | If you need a crew, a commercial gym or cheering friends to train, your motivation is fragile; lockdown quitters and newcomers who vanished once he stopped babying them show it.
MND-04 | Discipline and toughness are learned, not genetic | Discipline, fighting spirit and pain tolerance are habits you can build at any age; calling them genetic is a cop-out. Parents who teach discipline give a head start, never an excuse.
MND-05 | Willpower is trainable and finite | Each push-through strengthens willpower and each give-in teaches you your will is weak; habits save willpower for when they fail.
MND-06 | Schedule training; never ask whether to go | Put training in the schedule like lunch or work and decide once that you train for life; asking yourself if you want to go invites "no".
MND-07 | Training is non-negotiable | Bad sleep, a bad meal, rain, cold, homework or work hours don't cancel a session; if the program says train, you train, and if you can't, fix the program or yourself.
MND-08 | Go on the days you don't want to | The reluctant days are the ones to go; the reluctance fades and he has never regretted going.
MND-09 | Reschedule missed sessions, don't drop them | Push a missed session back and rearrange the week; planned rest days are fine but impromptu skips breed laziness. Repeatedly missing scheduled days signals bad programming or training that isn't fun.
MND-10 | Habit-building tactics | Chain training to an existing habit, give small immediate rewards (a manga page per rest period), make key exercises convenient (superset them), write schedules on paper, make the first task easy, and build up from tiny steps if you can't hold a routine.
MND-11 | Discipline is rented, not owned | Rent is due daily: once a good habit is built, never let it slip, because temptation never disappears and restarting is worse than never stopping.
MND-12 | Greatness comes from tiny daily repetitions | A physique is "a temple built one rock a day": diligent repetition becomes habit, then discipline, not hyped bursts.
MND-13 | Discipline taken too far is a straitjacket | Training should be neither winging it nor the Ten Commandments; you wrote the program, so keep flexibility and don't be its slave.
MND-14 | Adherence and enjoyment come before optimisation | A lift or program you love beats a "better" one you hate; a disliked lift gets skipped or done without intensity. Adherence sits at the top of the muscle-building pyramid.
MND-15 | Training must be fun; "bodybuilding must suck" must die | Treating training as duty or punishment led him to injury and resentment; martyrs and grinders who hate their training don't last. He claims the "must suck" idea is pushed by PED users.
MND-16 | The ideal program excites you | Before tuning other variables, check that each training day is realistic, doable and exciting; you should never dread it.
MND-17 | Protect your love of lifting | Never turn bodybuilding into a job; his top priority is never falling out of love with it, and a lighter, hobby-like attitude keeps the passion for decades. If you lose the love, try a new sport and do only movements you enjoy.
MND-18 | Plan training on paper, not by instinct | "Instinctive training" without a plan is lazy dice-rolling; the "educated barbarian" arrives knowing exactly what to do.
MND-19 | Consistency beats intensity and optimisation | The park trainee doing daily chin-ups and push-ups outgains the perfect-program lifter who trains sporadically; years of steady work beat short bursts of madness.
MND-20 | Whoever sticks with it longest wins | Elite physiques come from a small consistency edge, not losing what others skip; do slightly less and let consistency make up the difference, which avoids burnout.
MND-21 | Starting extreme guarantees burnout | Extreme starts (huge deficits, double sessions, copying Goggins on day one) fail and are often a subconscious setup to say "it was too hard"; start where you can touch the bottom.
MND-22 | No deadlines on physique goals | Deadlines create stress, rushing and shortcuts, and imply you plan to stop; aim for a goal "whenever it comes".
MND-23 | Building muscle isn't hard, it's long | Confusing "long" with "hard" makes people quit; it isn't a race, and each session adds one block to the pyramid.
MND-24 | Lifting is lifelong; age is no excuse | What you gain with work you keep with work; "age cope" (downhill after 25, only maintain after 30) dodges responsibility and becomes self-fulfilling. See NAT-34.
MND-25 | Most people quit, so sticking with it sets you apart | Most trainees quit early or at the intermediate stage, so simply staying puts you ahead of almost everyone.
MND-26 | Start now | Don't wait for January 1, a "winter arc" or the perfect plan; "tomorrow" means never and waiting for perfection is laziness in disguise.
MND-27 | Stop program-hopping | If a program still makes you progress, don't change it; almost every methodology works if done long enough, and jumping between fads (including each new program he publishes) keeps naturals in a hope-despair cycle.
MND-28 | But don't be stubborn with a method that fails | Stay confident but open: stubbornness cost him years, while a mind that switches constantly is also a problem. A diet or lift is a tool, not an attachment.
MND-29 | Aim for an unattainable ideal | Your ultimate ideal should be unrealistic; "realistic" means low standards, and aiming high leaves you with more even when you fall short. A manga physique is a fine ideal.
MND-30 | A star and a ladder | Keep a lifelong goal body plus smaller, realistic yearly objectives.
MND-31 | Have a self-chosen goal and a "why" | A clear goal you chose (aesthetics, strength, calisthenics or sport) dictates your program; without one, influencers hand you theirs or "progress" itself becomes the goal. People who train for someone else's goals quit or stay insecure.
MND-32 | Keep an evolving goal | A finite or reachable goal (beating a specific person) makes you stop or disappoints once reached; let the goal move.
MND-33 | Keep your goal body in sight | Print or draw your goal body and put it where you see it daily, but only after removing self-limiting beliefs, or the picture reminds you of failure.
MND-34 | Rehearse lifts mentally | Visualise heavy lifts and the evening's sets during the day, and picture the target muscle working; he calls the last "biologically meaningless" but says it boosts belief and effort.
MND-35 | Visualisation works only with action | Visualisation or "manifestation" isn't prayer; it only helps alongside training.
MND-36 | Belief is self-fulfilling | Limiting beliefs mean low goals, low effort and poor results (Pygmalion/Galatea effects); believing your body won't grow kills the drive for years. Surround yourself with bigger natural lifters. See GEN-04.
MND-37 | Hold yourself to higher standards than others | Be harder on yourself than on the people around you.
MND-38 | Use high natural standards | Being stronger than the untrained average is not "strong"; fitness should raise standards. Skepticism about historical naturals lowers them; take PED users out of setting the standard.
MND-39 | Don't compare yourself to others | Beat yesterday's self; comparison leads to depression, ego or lower standards unless you directly compete. A strong lifter with small shoulders may wrongly think he's at his limit.
MND-40 | Role models for inspiration, not imitation | Take traits, not identities: copying is necessary as a beginner, but fanboyism surrenders individuality and creates an artificial ceiling. Naturals fanboying PED users is the worst case.
MND-41 | That includes him | Disagree with him, judge his advice by applying it, and stop watching (his channel too) if it confuses or depresses you; he gives concepts, not protocols.
MND-42 | Copy elite naturals' mindset, not their methods | Elite naturals got big by different methods; their shared mindset matters more than any method.
MND-43 | Choose realistic natural role models | The bigger the gap to your role model, the worse the dysmorphia; prefer past naturals (LaLanne, 1940s lifters) and people who struggled to grow, not enhanced influencers or genetic outliers. See GEN-30.
MND-44 | A fictional ideal is fine as a power fantasy | An anime or manga physique works as a goal if you know you won't fully match it; it becomes dysmorphia fuel once you think it's naturally achievable.
MND-45 | Learn to program for yourself | Programming is the skill that takes you furthest; a subpar program you wrote teaches more than a professional's, and his programs are puzzle pieces meant to teach it. "Take the reins" eventually.
MND-46 | Infer and adapt; avoid dogma | "From one thing, know ten thousand things": adapt principles rather than follow anything as gospel. He follows strongman, powerlifting, arm wrestling and sumo to avoid an echo chamber.
MND-47 | Practice before theory | Theory without application is worthless; the lifter without theory is more useful than the theorist without lifting ("pen and sword"), and the most important study is the one you run on yourself.
MND-48 | Don't consume more fitness content than you train | More time on YouTube fitness than in the gym is unacceptable, and most of what you watch should be information, not entertainment.
MND-49 | Be a student of the game | The best natural physiques belong to nerds obsessed with refining their training; years trained don't equal experience.
MND-50 | Novices: habit before theory | For novices, discipline and love for the iron matter more than theory; detailed theory is for advanced lifters chasing small edges.
MND-51 | Most workouts won't be optimal | Demanding perfection causes paralysis by analysis; get the stimulus first.
MND-52 | Lift calm, not hyped | Start sessions calm, neutral and determined and save intensity for the moment you must push; hype isn't replicable, tires your adrenaline response and creates dependence.
MND-53 | Anger as fuel is unsustainable | Anger, trauma or resentment can get you started but runs dry and keeps those emotions alive; breaking that cycle improved his life.
MND-54 | Feeling isn't strength | Mood shouldn't dictate performance: feeling bad or awkward warm-ups don't mean your strength is gone; if spirit is high, push through.
MND-55 | The ability to push is the first requirement | Intense people gain even without a program, while people who can't push themselves stay small on any program; the best technique fails without strong intent.
MND-56 | Phone away, no socialising | Chatting and scrolling lengthen sessions, rush sets and break rest times; music on shuffle, phone untouched, and a partner only spots and loads plates.
MND-57 | Save music for hard sets | Use podcasts or lessons for warm-ups and volume work so music keeps its adrenaline boost for working sets; build a varied shuffled playlist, since all-hype music causes habituation.
MND-58 | Shout only when necessary | A committed mid-set shout re-invigorates you, a timid half-shout psychs you out, and shouting costs mental stamina.
MND-59 | Lifting is meditation | Once technique and program are ingrained, the body is busy and the mind rests; lifting can be strenuous without being stressful.
MND-60 | Finish the session | Don't drop the lifts that stress you or skip the end of the session (isolation, calves); that is where mental fortitude is built. Treat every exercise as equally important.
MND-61 | Leave the rep you know you'd get | Repeated gym failures build a "loser's mindset" and mental plateaus, while knowing you had another rep builds confidence; with conservative steps a miss costs a rep, never a deload. See ch. 3 for failure as an intensity tool.
MND-62 | Strength fuels motivation but isn't the goal | Bodybuilding is about what the weight does to the muscle; detach from main-lift numbers (a squat stalling 3 months is fine if other knee-flexion lifts progress). See ch. 7.
MND-63 | Strength is instant gratification | Weight on the bar every week is gratifying, so size-seekers drift into strength training; he thinks this explains 5x5 and Starting Strength's popularity.
MND-64 | Be honest whether you want strength or looks | Self-test: does a PR or a new vein in the mirror excite you more? If it's looks, call yourself a bodybuilder and drop the "functional" backstory; "power copers" hide poor muscle gains behind strength.
MND-65 | Round numbers create psychological plateaus | He stalled at a 3-plate bench until 325 lb was loaded by mistake and he pressed it easily.
MND-66 | He stopped measuring during slow recomp | Slow recomposition numbers demoralised him even while his physique improved, so he stopped measuring.
MND-67 | A healthy ego carries you forward | "Ego death" in bodybuilding is the death of progression; the problem is a big but fragile ego that rejects criticism.
MND-68 | "Ego lifter" is mostly an insult | The label is usually an ad hominem used to shame heavy lifting or straps; squatting ATG because the internet bullied you is the real ego lifting. Still, don't grind reps you know you shouldn't.
MND-69 | Admit when you're wrong | It's only wrong to be wrong when you refuse to accept it; his defensiveness about drifting cheat-row form cost him days before he fixed it and grew again. Justifying your form ahead of time means you know it's wrong.
MND-70 | Hard work beats talent that doesn't work | Talent is double-edged because gifted people quit at the first roadblock; grinders with "garbage genetics" who stayed went further. A talented person who works as hard stays ahead, so work harder. See GEN-08.
MND-71 | Limits are mostly mental | Never call something impossible; ask under what conditions it is possible. When he stopped telling his body what it could do, it proved far more capable, and he hasn't found his limit yet.
MND-72 | The brain is the limiting factor | For naturals the real limiter is knowledge and programming, not the body; plateaus are usually intellectual. Keep the mind ahead of the body or you quit around the intermediate stage.
MND-73 | Forever-intermediates are stuck by mindset | Being stuck at intermediate is psychological and self-inflicted, never genetic; the biggest downfall is entitlement to gains. What separates intermediate from advanced is time and the decision to keep going.
MND-74 | Take full responsibility | "It is not always your fault, but it is always your responsibility": you control your reaction and preparedness, not events. "Injuries just happen" absolves people.
MND-75 | Locus of control can be learned | An external locus of control is antithetical to self-improvement but can be switched to internal; it is why "just eat less" doesn't land with someone who feels powerless.
MND-76 | Reject the blackpill | Demoralisation is an excuse for passivity and a self-fulfilling prophecy; a depressed person can still gain, a demoralised one never will. He claims many blackpill creators profit from it while living fulfilled lives.
MND-77 | Ignore the crabs in the bucket | Ridicule and "genetics talk" hold people down; take advice only from people making something of their lives, never from those who quit and judge from the roadside.
MND-78 | Action cures overthinking | Talking about a problem soothes you into inaction; he can explain bodybuilding endlessly but can't give you the drive to go do it.
MND-79 | Seek voluntary hardship | Struggle builds the toughness to keep training for years; cold exposure won't make you jacked, but it keeps you training. "If you can train there you can train anywhere."
MND-80 | Pain for its own sake is stupid | Puke-bucket workouts, bragging about limping, training through tendonitis and "seek pain" culture are pointless; growth needs sensible work for years, and ignoring declining health is cowardice.
MND-81 | Accepted hardship stops being hardship | Accept the work willingly and early; that's why training feels doable and even enjoyable.
MND-82 | Defeat teaches more than victory | A victory confirms your plan; a loss gives you homework. Failing and getting back up prepares you for crises.
MND-83 | Assess a lapse without self-flagellation | After a lapse, reflect and try again; moral self-punishment and pity parties feed the cycle.
MND-84 | Look back and see your past stupidity | If you don't look back after a year or two and think you were an idiot, you're stagnating.
MND-85 | You go up or you go down | There is no stagnation for humans; "maintaining forever" is a bad mindset.
MND-86 | Use criticism as a ladder | Elite naturals improve when criticised instead of getting upset; compliments are "sweet poison", though you should accept them graciously.
MND-87 | Every lifter meets muscle dysmorphia | Bigorexia ("I am too small") hits every lifter, him included; he considers it more dangerous than other dysmorphias because fulfilment seems doable.
MND-88 | Dysmorphia has no cure; collaborate with it | The ideal moves as you improve, so feeding it is like paying a blackmailer; filter the drive, neither obey nor silence it. Professional help or medication is fine if needed.
MND-89 | Dysmorphia spirals | It drives overly aggressive methods that stall progress, which deepens it; it can lead to scheming instead of training, overtraining, injury, quitting or PEDs, which worsen it.
MND-90 | Dysmorphia is socially contagious | It grows from comparison, media, parents and peers; comparing an unpumped mirror reflection with curated photos is harmful, and feeling comfortable only when very lean is dysmorphia.
MND-91 | Steady maingaining soothes dysmorphic anxiety | Steady, trackable gains calm dysmorphia; big fat-heavy bulks followed by cuts mess with your head. See ch. 11.
MND-92 | Love your body and still want more | Self-hatred as fuel works at first, then destroys you; accepting your present state lets you move forward, and pride comes from building the body.
MND-93 | Never being fully satisfied keeps you growing | Fully content people stop progressing; contentment isn't the end, since later progress can come from well-being instead of fighting demons.
MND-94 | The barbell is a therapist | Lifting is the single best thing for mental health; it reduced his anxiety and kept him together in a crisis.
MND-95 | Anxious or depressed lifters need structure beyond the gym | Don't rely only on training to soothe anxiety or rest days become high-anxiety days; walk outside on rest days, follow an exact A-B-C-D plan without tweaking, and reach stable mood before chasing bodybuilding.
MND-96 | Lifting is central, not your whole life | "Gymcelling" (isolating in the gym, believing a better body fixes everything) is a net negative; if you feel good only while training, the solution lies elsewhere, and the gym should build confidence you then use socially.
MND-97 | Dedication is healthy, obsession isn't | But mocking passionate lifters as "gymcels" or "try-hards" is projection by people who never committed.
MND-98 | Not everyone must bodybuild | Training is non-negotiable, but if you hate the gym do martial arts, swimming or tennis; being average or a happy dad bod is fine.
MND-99 | Lifting suits addictive personalities | It is the ideal outlet because the body eventually stops you; move a gaming addiction to lifting.
MND-100 | Leave the intensity in the gym | The gym should drain negative energy so you are calm at home; don't carry competition intensity into daily life.
MND-101 | Pick one "king" pursuit | Pursuing several disciplines at once (boxing and bodybuilding; calisthenics, powerlifting and bodybuilding) makes you mediocre at each; let the rest serve the king.
MND-102 | Bodybuilding is transformation of the spirit | He sees it as more spiritual than physical, with the body as a by-product; purely materialistic motives burn out, and the spiritual view keeps you going when results slow.
MND-103 | A strong body builds a strong mind | Changing your body proves you can change anything; lifting gives direct feedback from your own work, and the body is "ridiculously easy to change".
MND-104 | Lifting for women is a start, not a reason | It's a legitimate start, but if it stays your main reason you plateau, quit or stop once you have a girlfriend; lift for yourself.
MND-105 | "I want to look good" is enough | All training goals are "vain" self-improvement, so admit aesthetics if that's what satisfies you.
MND-106 | Focus on the journey, not the result | Result-obsessed lifters quit; results are a by-product, and sometimes you do everything right and nothing happens for a while.
MND-107 | Mockery of your lifting comes from envy | Swole shaming ("vain", "narcissist") comes from envy or insecurity and shows your improvement bothers them; keep going and widen the gap. A partner discouraging your lifting may be jealous.
MND-108 | Take pride in being above average | Natural lifters should drop fake humility; real humility is objectively assessing where you stand.
MND-109 | Envy is destructive | Learn how someone got what you want instead of assuming they cheated; naturals accusing each other of being enhanced is jealousy. Admiration is the useful version.
NAT-01 | PED gains don't stay | Stop the drugs and most of the muscle goes, because the hormonal environment it grew in is gone, even with PCT and hard training; remove the drug and the physique collapses, remove the training and it largely stays.
NAT-02 | Natural muscle is kept for life | Naturals lose muscle slowly and regain it quickly; keeping it only takes eating what you need and training, with no substance dependency. He calls this "the number one reason" to stay natural.
NAT-03 | There is no safe dose | No responsible or recreational use exists; use and abuse are the same. Prescription or replacement-dose testosterone causes the same side effects as underground testosterone at the same dose.
NAT-04 | Bloodwork doesn't make PEDs safe | Blood tests don't catch heart damage or cancer early, and individual response can't be known in advance; he compares it to scanning while still smoking.
NAT-05 | The physical harms creep in | Organ failure, heart disease, high blood pressure and hematocrit, cancers, gyno and tendon ruptures; benefits arrive fast and harm builds unnoticed ("boiling frog"), and some damage is permanent.
NAT-06 | PEDs cost quality and length of life | In his view pros now die around 40, and ex-users alive at 70 are rare survivors with wealth and top care; "Arnold-era guys are still alive" is survivorship bias.
NAT-07 | Natural bodybuilding is the path to health | Being fit and healthy into your 50s-70s beats being massive for 2-3 years in your 20s; enhanced bodybuilding keeps only the aesthetic by-product.
NAT-08 | Tendon ruptures are PED injuries | Pec, biceps, lat and quad tears come from muscle outgrowing connective tissue on drugs; a natural won't snap a tendon unless he ego-lifts or uses very short ranges. Drugs also mask injury signals. See ch. 9.
NAT-09 | Other costs of PEDs | Money, unknown product quality, dependence on an illegal supplier, risk of jail, and lifelong TRT logistics (insurance, travel to countries that ban it).
NAT-10 | There is no such thing as one cycle | Small doses escalate: cycle, PCT, cycle again, then blast-and-cruise with "TRT" as the cruise. "It is far easier never to start than to quit."
NAT-11 | PEDs are addictive hard drugs | Coming off drops you below baseline (depression, mood swings, low libido), which pushes users back on; he rates the mental dependency "as severe as heroin".
NAT-12 | Exogenous hormones shut down your own production | Natural production may not restart, oral-only cycles suppress too, and repeated cycling forces permanent blast-and-cruise.
NAT-13 | PEDs don't cure insecurity or dysmorphia | They amplify it; the next cycle never brings the size that makes you happy, and most users start "to fill a void".
NAT-14 | PEDs amplify who you are | Hormones shape personality; drugs amplify existing traits and insecurities, and users show only their good days online.
NAT-15 | Drugs cover up bad training | Users never learn to train, so their only answer to a plateau is more drugs; programming knowledge can be passed on, drug gains cannot.
NAT-16 | PEDs are "liquid tonnage" | Each injection is like weeks of training ("injected time"), and PEDs cause hypertrophy with no exercise at all, depending on dose.
NAT-17 | Muscle responds to hormone duration, not spikes | Injected testosterone stays flat and high while natural levels fluctuate, so even at an equal dose the injector stays anabolic all the time; naturals build through long, stable exposure (an "anabolic bank").
NAT-18 | Drugs scale with dose | Gains keep rising with dose while food doesn't scale; differences between naturals are small and the real gap is natural vs enhanced.
NAT-19 | PEDs work enormously | "Steroids just let you train harder" is a fake-natty lie; nobody would risk felonies for black-market vials if they did little, and a proper cycle makes almost anyone look "insane".
NAT-20 | PED response is unpredictable | PEDs create new potential rather than amplifying natural potential, and response and side effects can't be predicted; analysis based on hypothetical steroid results is invalid.
NAT-21 | Steroid mass is partly wet | Mass-gaining steroids add fat and water; huge short-term muscle gains are impossible even on drugs.
NAT-22 | Enhanced lifters recover from what crushes naturals | Offseason bloating, keeping muscle while cutting and recovering from huge workloads are drug effects; if a cycle lets you keep a training plan, that plan was too harsh to work naturally.
NAT-23 | Enhanced advice doesn't transfer to naturals | You can't tell whether an enhanced lifter grew from the stimulus or the drugs; copying IFBB pros is "the worst mistake a natural can make", and most mainstream advice comes from users, directly or second-hand.
NAT-24 | Naturals must train heavy and compound-focused | Machines, cables, light weights and very high reps work only on drugs; every top natural he knows trains heavy, and naturals "must be told they need to get stronger".
NAT-25 | For naturals, programming is everything | Volume, intensity and frequency can't be ignored; his "holy trinity of fake-natty nonsense" is volume without an intensity window, avoiding compounds and not understanding frequency. A big guy saying these don't matter is probably enhanced.
NAT-26 | Enhanced volume is mostly junk | A natural should do as little as possible to get as much as possible and never replace hard sets with pump work; copying enhanced volume exceeds natural work capacity and leads to burnout.
NAT-27 | A pump without fibre damage won't grow a natural | Pump-chasing came from enhanced pros; Bronze and Silver era lifters saw the pump as a pleasant by-product.
NAT-28 | Bro splits came from enhanced athletes | Lazy programming works on drugs; naturals on bro splits stay small, and big bro-splitters aren't natural. "Instinctive training" is also promoted mostly by users.
NAT-29 | Naturals need frequency | Muscle protein synthesis lasts a few days after training while users stay anabolic around the clock, so one brutal session a week wastes most of the week; "frequency doesn't matter" is spread mostly by steroid users.
NAT-30 | Naturals must isolate lagging parts | PEDs give diffuse growth to untrained parts; naturals must train forearms, traps and abs directly.
NAT-31 | Bulk/cut culture comes from pros | "Bulk in winter, cut in summer" and gaining muscle at maintenance come from enhanced pros; a natural should look big all year and avoid yo-yo diets. "No food makes you shredded; that's steroids." See ch. 11.
NAT-32 | Naturals and pros have different priorities | Pros: drugs > diet > recovery > training. Naturals: training > recovery > nutrition > supplements. A good program is the best testosterone booster.
NAT-33 | Natural gain rate is slow | A natural gains in a decade what a user gains in under a year.
NAT-34 | Natural men peak in their 30s-40s | Testosterone doesn't "crash at 29/30/40"; the idea of peaking in your 20s comes from YouTube lifters on PEDs. Most muscle is built between your 20s and 50s, and age as an excuse in your 40s is a cope.
NAT-35 | Train through your 20s for your prime | Muscle built young protects you in old age; low T and bad bodies in older men come from years of inactivity, not age itself.
NAT-36 | Naturals can get big | Being small means you haven't trained enough, long enough or correctly, not that you're natural; the goal is to look enhanced while natural.
NAT-37 | Reachable lean natural sizes | He gives height-bodyweight pairs a lean natural can reach after years of proper training; a young natural who gets there will draw steroid accusations. His own numbers are in ch. 1.
NAT-38 | The natural-enhanced gap is about 40-60 lb | Pros carry roughly 40-60 lb more lean mass than a natural of the same height, and users would lose ~50 lb in three months off.
NAT-39 | Natural arm ceilings | 19-20-inch cold arms are possible naturally; 21 in is doubtful, since old measurements were inflated and fat makes 20 easy. Big arms are an "old man's game".
NAT-40 | Very lean and very big is the clearest non-natural marker | Naturals can't hold their hormones when very lean, so natural competitors should look big at moderate leanness rather than skeletal; natural shows demanding extreme dryness copy pro standards.
NAT-41 | Historical naturals show what's possible | Steve Reeves, 1950s physiques, 1910s-20s Indian bodybuilders and Silver Era physiques are natural benchmarks, and today's naturals are bigger thanks to better methods. He holds that massive drug use is certain only from the 1960s-70s.
NAT-42 | Naturals have big upper-back and leg potential | There's no special natural limit on upper-back size; upper back and legs are the most underdeveloped areas in naturals, and natural quads and hamstrings have enormous potential.
NAT-43 | Low T in young men is mostly lifestyle | Video games, junk food, alcohol, porn, no sun and no exercise give the body no reason to produce testosterone, and men damage their hormones then blame genetics. Fix that first.
NAT-44 | Training raises testosterone over time | Muscle, strength and hormones grow in a positive loop: physique first, better hormones follow. His own blood tests suggest his T kept rising slowly.
NAT-45 | Normal-range hormones are almost irrelevant for naturals | Big naturals with 10+ years of training have average to above-average T, nothing supraphysiological; low-normal T doesn't stop progress, and the T obsession was imported from PED users.
NAT-46 | TRT is not natural | Any exogenous hormone, even 50 mg on top of normal production, revokes natural status for life; TRT is replacement, not optimisation, and shuts down your own production.
NAT-47 | Real TRT is rare | Real TRT replaces hormones you can't produce because of a diagnosed condition, at low-to-mid natural levels; mostly for men in their 70s-80s or rare young men whose hormones never kicked in. That disqualifies ~99% of YouTube TRT users.
NAT-48 | TRT is a gateway and a loophole | Clinics prescribe to men with normal levels and turn them into lifelong customers, with doses creeping up; he likens it to the painkiller epidemic. TRT at 18-25 "rejects life" and is a band-aid over a toxic lifestyle.
NAT-49 | Worried young men shouldn't get tested | Results tend to confirm the fear and lead to quitting, boosters or a TRT clinic; an untrained, sedentary teen tests low by default. Fix lifestyle and train first.
NAT-50 | Don't plan on TRT later in life | Your body is anabolic now, so grow now; men in their 50s-60s do fine without TRT, and he doesn't plan it himself.
NAT-51 | "Natural" means never having taken PEDs | Take them once and you can never be natural again, so users can't cycle off and sell to naturals.
NAT-52 | Time frame is the best indicator | Genetics speed progress, but nobody gets 10 years of results in 2; years of nothing followed by sudden huge gains means poor training or drugs.
NAT-53 | Size swings are a tell | Sudden jumps in size and strength, disappearing and returning smaller, hiding in hoodies or camera angles, and a neck that shrinks dramatically suggest cycles.
NAT-54 | Androgen-sensitive muscles are weak evidence | Traps, delts, upper chest, upper back and neck blow up on drugs, but naturals can build them; isolated blown-up muscles and a "developed everywhere" look are stronger signs, and clear lagging areas point to natural. Naturals grow along a kinetic chain.
NAT-55 | Face and skin signs | Moon face, a face that squares out during size jumps, adult jaw growth (a GH sign), sudden severe back acne and a constant red flush are signs; hair loss and acne also occur naturally, and natural facial masculinisation is slow.
NAT-56 | Enhanced muscle looks different | Enhanced muscle looks "impossibly hard" with no fat layer; wet bulks look inflated; "pregnant bellies" and thick waists come from GH and insulin, not compound leg training.
NAT-57 | Behavioural tells | Users can't explain how they built the muscle ("they just grow"), downplay what PEDs do, or pair a big lean physique with unimpressive training; sudden changes in mood and voice are suspicious.
NAT-58 | FFMI is worthless for judging natural status | The 25 cutoff comes from a small 1995 study that didn't control for training, and every underestimated body-fat percent counts as muscle. Size-strength gaps, vascularity and "natural" in a title are also invalid signs.
NAT-59 | Drug tests can't settle natural status | Only random testing works, YouTubers test on their own schedule, natural leagues are "mostly natural" at best, and nobody can prove a negative, his own status included.
NAT-60 | Natty-or-not content mostly hurts viewers | It tells beginners that modest physiques need drugs, lowers standards, pushes them toward PEDs and gives fakes free publicity; focus on your own training, though you should still know whether your advice-giver is enhanced.
NAT-61 | Judgements depend on the viewer's standards | Ordinary people, French audiences or intermediates call ordinary good physiques unnatural; "Schrödinger's bodybuilder" is first small because he never tried, then big because of genetics.
NAT-62 | Most users start as young novices | Most people who start PEDs are teens or early intermediates with poor programming, rarely 10-15-year veterans; teens take SARMs "as casually as candy".
NAT-63 | Why people start | Faster results, less effort, believing they hit their natural limit, fear of weakness or of wasting youth ("too late after 25"), and a phase of romanticising PEDs every user goes through.
NAT-64 | The temptation is real | Nearly every lifter feels it, him included; understand it instead of calling users stupid. If you keep researching steroids "out of curiosity", you're preparing to take them, and PED use spreads through envy.
NAT-65 | SARMs aren't safer | They are only less researched; pills avoid needles, so teens start there and move on to orals and injections. Avoid them like steroids.
NAT-66 | Steroids harm women even more | Women risk permanent virilisation even on supposed Anavar; women can't look like men naturally, and natural men can't get "too muscular" for women.
NAT-67 | PEDs to escape bad genetics fix nothing | Using drugs to escape your genetics is "odium fati": it creates lifelong dependence; he calls it the "only semi-valid" PED argument, but whiny.
NAT-68 | The only safe message is "never take PEDs" | "Stay natural 5 or 10 years, then cycle" sets a goalpost teens shrink to 2 years; harm-reduction content to a mostly natural, young audience recruits users, who remember only the benefits.
NAT-69 | Stigma protects young lifters, but aim for empathy | Shaming PED use is a useful cultural safeguard and refusing to stigmatise is "cowardice", yet he aims for empathy over insults and prefers environments where nobody needs drugs over bans.
NAT-70 | Argue on danger, not cheating | Argue against PEDs on health and facts, not on cheating, morality or pleasure.
NAT-71 | Natural is a baseline; be a "noble natty" | Pride comes from building a big, strong body natural, not from being natural; a skinny untrained natural advertises for the PED camp. The movement mustn't gatekeep: a repentant ex-user who trains well can qualify.
NAT-72 | Separate yourself from enhanced culture | Unfollow enhanced influencers and follow believable, high-level naturals who love training, studying rather than copying them; natural bodybuilding needs its own standards. He stopped watching the Olympia because it worsened his dysmorphia.
NAT-73 | Blackpill and delusion both come from enhanced standards | "Nobody is natural" and "pro looks on rice and broccoli" are two sides of the same distortion; a natural who uses "I'm natural" as an excuse for being small is as harmful as a fake natty.
NAT-74 | The problem is lying, not use | Honest enhanced lifters are acceptable, some even good role models; fake natties' real crime is killing trust that naturals can grow.
NAT-75 | Users aren't bad people | Adults may harm their own health and he wouldn't outlaw it, but users aren't free from judgement or entitled to taxpayer-funded care; taking PEDs is bad, the people are misguided.
NAT-76 | Enhanced culture set natural bodybuilding back | In his view the supplement industry and enhanced pros grew together and "killed" natural bodybuilding, costing decades of method progress; the genetics obsession is a modern drug-user effect.
NAT-77 | His channel's mission | Discourage young people from PEDs and show how to get big naturally; convincing one person makes it worthwhile. Users he knew are part of why the channel exists.
NAT-78 | His own reasons to stay natural | Being a role model, a doctor-flagged genetic predisposition that exogenous hormones could trigger (including prostate cancer risk), and his time already going into programming, diet and recovery. He says he has never used, is straight edge, and knows PEDs from users' testimony.
NAT-79 | People he claims are enhanced | He claims Mike O'Hearn, Nether (prohormones), Blaha, Liver King, Katcherian, Zyzz, The Rock, Mike Israetel, Dorian Yates, Mentzer and 70s-80s icons including Arnold ("the first fake natties") used PEDs, and once suspected Joel of GH from face growth; he also argues HIT "proves nothing" because, in his view, Mentzer and Yates used.
NAT-80 | People he believes are natural | He believes Eric Bugenhagen, Jeff Nippard, Steve Reeves, GVS, Omar Isuf and (2023-2025) Joel are natural, and that Henry Cavill's physique is naturally achievable; he says PED users with average genetics wrongly assume elite naturals cheat.
GEN-01 | Genetics exist but are never an excuse | How you play your cards matters more than the cards; whatever your starting point, doing the work decides the outcome, and blaming genetics avoids responsibility. Genetics only make it easier; mindset decides whether a natural gets big.
GEN-02 | "Bad genetics" usually means bad training | Most people who blame genetics have average genetics and are training badly (wrong intensity, too little volume, no direct work, inconsistency) or not hard; compound-only lifters blame genes for muscles they never isolate.
GEN-03 | No true non-responders or hardgainers | Stagnation always has a reason other than "you"; skinny to big is always possible naturally if the parameters are met, and genetics decide how long you stay skinny, not whether you escape it. He says he has helped many so-called non-responders grow.
GEN-04 | Belief in bad genetics is self-fulfilling | You give up early, get poor results and take them as proof; two clones told "good" vs "bad" genetics would differ after 10 years. See MND-36.
GEN-05 | Blaming genetics is cowardice and ingratitude | It moves responsibility outside yourself and insults people born without working limbs; resenting nature leads to self-hatred and stagnation.
GEN-06 | "Schrödinger's genetics" | Before working you can't know your potential; those who stick with it get called gifted or high-T, those who quit get called low-T.
GEN-07 | Between delusion and despair | The realistic middle ground between delusion and despair about your genetics is where most gains are made.
GEN-08 | Poor genetics can be a gift; gifted people often waste it | Poor genetics force you to become a grinder, while easy early success makes the gifted conceited: they don't review methods, get fatter and quit. Put on a real regimen they become monsters. See MND-70.
GEN-09 | Being average is fine | Average muscles still grow, just more slowly; algorithms showing only genetic freaks make average lifters think their genetics are bad. There is no karma: lazy gifted people may outperform you, and resenting it only hurts you.
GEN-10 | Can everyone build a good physique? | He says no person on Earth lacks the ability to build a good physique.
GEN-11 | There is no locatable natural limit | Progress continues, slower, as long as you keep raising weight, volume and intensity; about 99% of people who say they hit the limit only reached the point where the work stopped seeming worth it, or never had a progressive overload scheme. He calls it "a cultural limit, not a biological one".
GEN-12 | The "limit" is about time and effort | The defensible version is chronological, not numerical: a point where progress becomes negligible and more needs more volume, frequency and gym time, which people decide isn't worth it.
GEN-13 | Natural potential is not infinite | Natural potential isn't equal to enhanced potential; this tempers his "no limit" rhetoric.
GEN-14 | Claimed-limit timelines are nonsense | After 5-10 years you're nowhere near potential; anyone saying gains stop after a few years is wrong, in his view usually to justify drug use.
GEN-15 | Plateaus are not limits | A 5x5 stall is the scheme's limit, a stalled arm "means it's time for an arm day", and avoiding heavy weights causes plateaus; many beginners take their first plateau as the limit and jump on drugs.
GEN-16 | Low "natural standards" kill drive | Size tables and rules of thumb for natural maximums are wrong, and he says he disproves them; 18-inch arms or 3D shoulders are not "impossible naturally".
GEN-17 | Novice gains are only half the story | Novice gains get you about half your potential; the other half comes slowly and most give up. The last pounds of muscle are what make a physique exceptional.
GEN-18 | Everyone passes through every level | Gifted people move through novice and intermediate faster but can't skip any level.
GEN-19 | Elite naturals mainly have time | A big natural is accumulated tonnage, often millions of pounds over a lifetime; no single week or month matters much. When a natural is big you see tonnage, when an enhanced lifter is big you see drugs.
GEN-20 | A great natural physique takes about 10 years | It takes ~10 uninterrupted years of well-written, high-frequency training, and almost nobody does that; anyone who truly does ends up jacked, and if not, something was wrong. Habits applied today show in 5-10 years.
GEN-21 | Don't judge your genetics early | Judge only after years of serious training; judging from your teen body is "idiotic". He says 99.9% of fitness YouTube hasn't trained long enough to talk about genetics.
GEN-22 | Ten serious years beat almost everyone | Anyone training seriously for 10 years ends up better than about 99% of people, so "everyone has good genetics"; almost no natural actually does it.
GEN-23 | Most people can reach his level | With a narrow frame and average genetics he reached ~210 lb at 6 ft with a full six-pack; most people could match or pass that naturally if they never stop.
GEN-24 | Realistic gain numbers | He gives concrete benchmarks for normal and gifted progress by height.
GEN-25 | Small lean lifters claiming a limit aren't doing enough | Lean lifters claiming a limit at modest bodyweight aren't eating or training enough; a natural man should never be outsized by a woman.
GEN-26 | Gym averages differ by culture, not biology | The difference between French and US gym averages is cultural.
GEN-27 | Freaks exist but are rare | A freak looks great without training or explodes on random training; they're irrelevant unless you compete with them.
GEN-28 | His genetics scale | He ranks lifters 1-10: "a 5 can only achieve what a 5 can", so rejoice at a 10's potential, go back to your own grind, and as a 5 work twice as hard.
GEN-29 | Test of giftedness: how long simple progression lasts | The best operational test is how long you progress on load-only linear progression with the same sets, reps and exercises; longer novice gains suggest better potential.
GEN-30 | Don't copy gifted lifters | Gifted lifters succeed despite subpar methods, can't tell what works because everything works, and credit hard work; take advice from a 5-7 who had to figure it out. The same applies to creators who succeed against the grain.
GEN-31 | Strength giftedness isn't size giftedness | Great leverages or a strong nervous system let some outlift you on day one without much growth; such lifters must train reps and tonnage and not give up on size.
GEN-32 | Signs of giftedness in beginners | Rapid noob gains, pronounced peaks and valleys between muscles even when small, looking big at a low bodyweight (good insertions, small joints), and teens with six-packs and veins without training. Skinny "tube-armed" beginners can still turn out gifted.
GEN-33 | Athletic background is mistaken for genetics | Years of sport (a wrestler training daily since childhood, teammates secretly lifting since 13) look like genetics; former athletes progress fast on returning to lifting. He wonders whether myonuclei explain it.
GEN-34 | Structural stress resistance matters (2020) | In 2020 he called your structure's ability to resist stress (spine shape, joint robustness) the most important genetic factor, so injury advice from someone with a "metal" back doesn't transfer.
GEN-35 | The best body-fat range for growing is genetic | Some grow well at 11-12% while he'd suffer at 10%.
GEN-36 | Frame sets potential width | Skeleton, especially clavicle length, sets potential mass and shoulder width and can't be trained; a wide V-taper is a gift, but any training improves your V-taper and delt mass builds width relative to frame.
GEN-37 | Muscle outweighs frame | A wide frame with no muscle looks bad and a narrow frame with muscle looks good; with a narrow frame and wide waist like his, cover it with as much muscle as possible. "Otter mode" only suits wide clavicles and a small waist.
GEN-38 | Clavicle angle is genetic | Up-pointing clavicles give capped shoulders and less trap room; down-pointing give more trap room but sloped shoulders; short clavicles make traps pop. Training changes the look, not the angle.
GEN-39 | Waist and hip width are bone structure | Cutting can't fix a structurally wide waist or hips.
GEN-40 | Tall lifters need more time and mass | Tall lifters have more absolute potential but more frame to fill, so visual change is slower and they need far more mass to look muscular; they don't gain strength slower, and patient tall lifters "become monsters".
GEN-41 | Short lifters look jacked faster | Short lifters win on proportions in bodybuilding, though they have lower absolute size and strength potential and reach what they call their limit sooner.
GEN-42 | Tall lifters mustn't use short men's standards | Most YouTubers are 5'7-5'10; tall lifters using their standards fall far short of their potential. Tall lifters can't skip body parts, need more work, must build strength (not machines only) and must hammer long legs.
GEN-43 | Limb proportions shape the look and the lifts | Short arms look bigger (a bodybuilding gift); long arms favour pulls, triceps and forearms but make biceps harder, and long-armed lifters get more bench ROM for shoulders while short-armed lifters like him need vertical pressing. Long legs with a short torso make quads hard to grow (hips shoot up in the squat), which is anatomy, not bad form.
GEN-44 | Insertions change shape, not whether a muscle grows | Long bellies and low insertions look small untrained but have more size potential, a "blessing in disguise"; short bellies pop more in 3D and peak. Balance is what matters.
GEN-45 | You can't know your insertions until the muscle is trained | "Bad" lats or calves are often just undeveloped; his "high lat insertions" were a cope and turned out fine after proper training.
GEN-46 | Some shape traits are fixed | Biceps peak and sweep, pec shape (heavy flyes can't widen the chest), the bare triceps tendon near the elbow, ab segment number and placement, and vein position are fixed; the size of abs, calves and biceps is trainable.
GEN-47 | Small joints don't limit size | Small joints and thin bones make muscle look bigger; thick bones push muscle out but don't build more, though bigger frames carry mass more easily. Wrists don't thicken from training.
GEN-48 | Thicker joints give more strength potential | So wraps help lifters with small wrists or knees.
GEN-49 | Grip depends on hand size | Grip depends more on hand size and finger length than forearm strength; small hands limit grip volume and therefore forearm growth, a vicious circle.
GEN-50 | Calves are not 100% genetic | Calf failure comes from inconsistency and poor exercise choice; big calves usually come from being heavy young or from genetics. Bad insertions limit absolute size but not looking beefy.
GEN-51 | Forearms depend on insertions | Forearms aren't purely genetic, but insertions (a high belly) drive their look and potential; people who grew forearms from pulls alone probably have gifted insertions.
GEN-52 | Small arms are a training problem | Only rare outliers grow arms on compounds alone, and then only biceps or triceps, never both.
GEN-53 | Dominance mostly reflects training history | Years of bench and OHP make you torso-dominant, and curls plus diamond push-ups make you limb-dominant; some people are genuinely back-gifted.
GEN-54 | A lagging part needs a different stimulus | No body part refuses to grow, and you can't have bad genetics for a part you never train; perfectly equal development is unrealistic.
GEN-55 | A gifted lower body saves time | Legs need the most tonnage, so leg genetics are a big time saver.
GEN-56 | Genetics matter only when advanced | Genetics start to matter when sculpting weak points as an advanced lifter; novices whine most but should care least.
GEN-57 | Time in an anabolic environment drives size | Cumulative exposure counts, and gain is bounded by your years with a prime hormonal profile, so don't rush bodyweight.
GEN-58 | Teen genetics haven't expressed yet | He claims total testosterone peaks later than free testosterone, so a teen's genetics haven't fully shown.
GEN-59 | Training ranks first among growth factors | His order: training, health, sleep, nutrition, stress, age, response to training, fibre distribution, insertions, frame, body chemistry; against genetics-first lists like nattyornot's.
GEN-60 | Noob gains are latent at any age | Age 35 is no doom; noob gains are available at any age, most potent right after puberty.
GEN-61 | Somatotypes are not real categories | "Ectomorph" just means skinny; the body responds to environment (he went from "ecto" when skinny to "meso" when fat). The real traits are morphology, metabolism, leverages and limb lengths, and outside shape doesn't tell you volume or intensity response.
GEN-62 | Women's potential differs by region | Women have fewer upper-body androgen receptors, so heavy shoulder and upper-trap work is mostly wasted and that volume goes better on legs; they have good potential for biceps, mid-back and rear delts and can't train in curves they weren't born with.
GEN-63 | Ethnic generalisations are overstated | In his view not all Black men are gifted, jacked untrained men are outliers everywhere, and selection rather than labour explains them; Asian men's "bad genetics" is a self-limiting belief. He sees South Asians as often having wide clavicles and big legs.
GEN-64 | Genetics pessimism is spread by enhanced culture | It is the most common self-limiting belief in fitness YouTube; in his view enhanced influencers spread "genetics is everything" (steroids as "injectable genetics") to protect their credibility, and exposé channels and low-standard coaches black-pill viewers.
GEN-65 | "100% genetics" is cope | Saying bodybuilding is all genetics is cope from people who ran a strength program for years without growing.
GEN-66 | He rates himself average or below | He started as a 140 lb stick at 18 and 6 ft, was told at 20 he'd hit his limit, and kept growing for years. See ch. 1.
GEN-67 | His frame | Very short clavicles and narrow shoulders, a wide waist and narrow hips, tiny wrists, average knees, above-average ankles; his back is thick but not wide and he is trap-dominant.
GEN-68 | His gifts and weak points | Gifts: neck, traps, inner forearms, high pec insertions, short quad attachments and a "metal" lower back. Weak points: lat width, pec gap, calves (long insertions), outer forearms and grip (big palm, short fingers). One arm is biceps-dominant, the other triceps-dominant.
VOL-01 | Volume, intensity and frequency form one system | Volume, intensity and frequency are one trinity; none works alone, and pushing one while ignoring the others breaks the system.
VOL-02 | Growth comes from tonnage in a relevant window | Hypertrophy is driven by tonnage (reps x weight) accumulated over time, but volume counts only when the load sits in a relevant intensity window.
VOL-03 | Count working sets only | Only relevant tonnage from hard sets counts; warm-ups and very light work add tonnage but of low quality.
VOL-04 | Stop a muscle when loads become irrelevant | End a muscle's work when it can no longer return to a load relevant to the opening strength work; judge this against absolute (fresh) intensity, not the rising relative intensity of a tired muscle.
VOL-05 | Muscular endurance caps the number of sets | The real limit on sets is muscular endurance: being able to return to baseline and repeat. It sets your maximum recoverable volume and differs by body part.
VOL-06 | Tonnage creeps up slowly, driven by set and rep progress | Tonnage should stay stable and rise slowly without big dips or spikes; let progress in sets and reps drive tonnage, and use tonnage math mainly to diagnose stalls.
INT-01 | Intensity is a number, not a sensation | Intensity is load relative to your max (% of 1RM, RPE), or mechanical tension on the target muscle; screaming, burning, pain, nausea or breathlessness are not intensity.
INT-02 | Pain and nausea are not growth indicators | There is no logical link between pain felt and hypertrophy; "no pain no gain" is in his words a "male power fantasy".
INT-03 | Effort is the main hypertrophy variable for naturals | Intensity (effort) is the most important hypertrophy variable; extra volume is a later lever for continued growth, and "hard sets are king".
INT-04 | Intensity alone is not a program | Intensity is not the only variable: a "high intensity" program is really a low-volume one, and bodybuilding needs both; only-heavy and only-volume are emotional positions, not programming.
INT-05 | Naturals must lift heavy relative to their strength | Pump and volume alone don't work for naturals; heavy work puts you in relevant windows, gives high-quality tonnage in little time and builds the strength that drives volume.
INT-06 | Heavy work saves time | Compounds at relevant loads rack up tonnage fast; very light work would need 4-10 h sessions to match it.
INT-07 | Most gym-goers never get close to failure | Quality volume means sets very close to failure; stopping when all reps look identical is just warming up, and he disagrees that ~65% effort is enough.
INT-08 | Effort is a skill learned per exercise | You learn to push and to grind separately for each exercise; keeping form clean under near-maximal loads only comes from training close to failure regularly.
INT-09 | Absolute vs relative intensity | Absolute intensity is what you can move fresh and warmed up; relative intensity climbs rep by rep, set by set and through the session as fatigue builds.
INT-10 | Put the highest intensity at the start of the session | Because relative intensity keeps climbing, programming only by absolute percentages misprograms; optimal intensity exists only at the session's start, which is where strength work belongs.
INT-11 | A set's intensity is the average of its reps | A heavy low-rep set can be more intense overall than a long set that only gets hard at the end, so low-rep sets near 90% keep a place.
INT-12 | Intensity is not tied to a rep range | Low reps can be low intensity and high reps high intensity; "low reps for strength, high reps for size" is false, and any range means nothing without effort.
INT-13 | "Heavy" is relative to the lifter and context | Whether a load is heavy depends on the lifter and where it falls in the session; don't let other people's standards push you into ever-lower reps.
INT-14 | Raising intensity without adding load | Pre-fatigue, supersets, variations and density raise intensity without more weight, but fatigue-inflated intensity should stay marginal, never the foundation of the program.
INT-15 | Work mostly between ~60% and ~95% of 1RM | The productive window is roughly 60-95% of 1RM; below ~60% is junk volume, and true maxes above ~95% aren't worth the injury risk or the lost tonnage.
INT-16 | Average intensity about 85% | ~85% is the average across training, not a per-set target; individual days can swing as long as the bulk averages ~85%, and that average sets fatigue, frequency, sets and reps.
INT-17 | Rep range: mostly 6-12, all work 3-15 | Bodybuilding work sits mostly at 6-12 reps within an overall 3-15 span; above 15-20 is junk, and singles to triples should not make up the bulk.
INT-18 | High-rep sets rarely reach real failure | Above ~15-20 reps each extra rep adds little intensity, and cardio, lactic burn or technique stop you before the muscle does. In 2025 he adds that true failure is reachable at 12-15, and higher ranges are more forgiving for grinding.
INT-19 | Low reps build size too | Low reps (3-5, 4-8) work for size and help naturals by raising the absolute intensity used for higher-rep work; he rejects the claim that anything under 5 (or 7-8) reps is useless.
