INT-20 | Never test true 1RMs for hypertrophy | True 1RMs have an atrocious fatigue-to-stimulus ratio, give little tonnage and raise injury risk; working up to a max and stopping gives you only the warm-up.
INT-21 | Low-rep work is a tonnage trap if overdone | A true 3RM gives little tonnage and can't be repeated for four sets, while many easy triples aren't truly intense; with low reps, fewer sets are better.
INT-22 | Fixed rep "zones" are myths | "6-8 strength / 8-12 size / 12+ endurance", "10-15 is best, never below 8" and "compounds low, isolations high" are wrong as fixed rules; choose 6-10, 8-12 or 10-15 by preference and by what lets you progress.
INT-23 | Still, match the rep range to the lift | Each lift and muscle has its own best window found by experiment; compounds usually go lower and isolations higher, and heavy/medium/light ranges should stay close together.
INT-24 | A rep range is a bracket around near-failure | Pick the weight so the target rep lands near failure; without high intensity you plateau in any range.
INT-25 | The target is muscular failure | Count only failure where the target muscle is the limit. Cardio, lactic burn, pump, occlusion or a structural breakdown (artificial or mechanical failure) feel hard but build little and cap tonnage.
INT-26 | Failure = no further clean rep | Failure is the point where you cannot do another rep with clean form; the set ends as soon as you need body English or a torso shift, and partials after failure only move the standard.
INT-27 | Never actually miss a rep | "Going to failure" means hitting the last rep you can complete; a missed rep is a programming error that raises injury risk, hurts morale and ruins the rest of the session.
INT-28 | Selective failure: isolations yes, squats and hinges no | Take isolation, upper-body presses, rows, pulldowns and other low-risk lifts to failure on every set; keep 1-2 reps in reserve on squats, deadlifts, good mornings and other risky lower-body compounds.
INT-29 | Isolation lifts deserve full intensity | Isolations have the best stimulus-to-fatigue ratio and low injury risk, so push them as hard as a deadlift; small muscles respond to intensity like big ones.
INT-30 | Failure is a calibration tool, not the goal | Proximity to failure is what matters; failure is the baseline for judging effort, and failing on everything misallocates recovery ("always training to failure is stupid").
INT-31 | Decide where to fail by recoverability | Failure is fine where you can train the muscle again soon (curls, laterals), not where it blocks a pattern for a week; recovery cost depends on the load at which you fail.
INT-32 | Hard sets must be repeatable within 2-3 days | Keep enough in the tank to train the muscle again in 2-3 days; a set you can only recover from every 15 days is not a good hard set. Repeatability is "the bread and butter of natural bodybuilding".
INT-33 | Reps in reserve on squats and deadlifts | On heavy squats, deadlifts and their variations leave the "danger rep" in the tank for form and injury prevention.
INT-34 | RIR picks your battles | RIR is not the enemy of failure: before grinding, ask whether the rep is worth the risk that day; the last two grinding reps aren't magic, and heavy compounds benefit most from RIR.
INT-35 | Use RIR on low-rep work, starting small | RIR belongs in shorter rep ranges; 0-1 RIR on 10-20-rep sets is pointless. Lifters new to RIR should apply it to one hinge or knee-flexion lift first, not across the whole program.
INT-36 | Learn failure before using RPE or RIR | RPE and RIR only mean something once you've trained to failure many times, so beginners must learn failure first.
INT-37 | Percentages vs RPE vs feel | Percentages, RPE and RIR are interchangeable A-tier tools and he recommends % of an estimated 1RM, yet in his own training he uses none of them: a rep range from experience, push to failure, add weight when the top gets easy.
INT-38 | Strength-athlete sandbagging doesn't suit bodybuilders | Planned sub-maximal strategies from strength sports (capping at 70%, RIR sandbagging, speed pulls) don't fit bodybuilding; progressing 4-6 reps with reserve "is just sandbagging".
INT-39 | Safer lower-body lifts go to failure | On lower-body days, take RDLs, hyperextensions, leg extensions and lunges to failure instead of squats and deadlifts; never good mornings to failure without safeties.
INT-40 | Hard sets are the most important sets | A hard set uses a challenging load, ends at muscular failure and is repeatable enough to accumulate tonnage; make as much of your tonnage as possible come from hard sets, including accessories, not only strength work.
INT-41 | A growth set moves the muscle off its baseline | A set that produces growth leaves the muscle unable to repeat the same effort within the session; it needs days, not 3-4 minutes, to recover. Too light and the muscle returns to baseline too easily.
INT-42 | Repeatability tests show whether a set was hard | If you can nearly match a set shortly after, it wasn't hard enough.
INT-43 | Load checks for too-light work | If you still hit the same back-off numbers after the main work, or the bar flies on squats and deadlifts, the weight is too light; if you could do 7 sets instead of 5 with the same reps, add load.
INT-44 | Sandbagging: when it is fine and when it isn't | Sandbagging is giving less than full effort without a reason. Chosen sandbagging (warm-ups, planned RIR, a muscle trained again in 2 days) is fine; sandbagging forced by the scheme wastes quality tonnage.
INT-45 | Fixed multi-set schemes force sandbagging | 5x5, 5x10, 10x10, 4x10 and fixed 8s or 12s force you to hold back early sets; with real effort reps decline across sets.
INT-46 | Use evolving (declining) rep ranges | Write ranges so reps trickle down toward failure each set; small brackets mean more intensity and more sandbagging risk, wide ones the reverse.
INT-47 | Too much work forces survival pacing | Piling exercises or huge volume into a session makes you pace and sandbag the top work, turning lifting into cardio with weights; if you pace sets to survive later exercises, the program is badly built.
INT-48 | Opening strength work is an appetizer | High intensity lives in 1-3 sets of opening strength work that should prime you, not wreck you; then come variations and accessories at relevant intensity.
INT-49 | Bodybuilding intensity is sustained, not one explosion | Bodybuilding effort is "a steady fire" of repeated hard sets rather than one all-out effort.
INT-50 | Back-off sets stay tied to the top weight | Back-off weight is tied to the strength-work weight and still pushed near failure in a sensible rep range; once you'd have to strip the bar, stop doing hard sets on that lift.
INT-51 | Don't open a session with junk | Never do light high-rep work before the main sets; lifters who know the lifts shouldn't start a program at low intensity, and pyramiding up to 8 reps is just a warm-up.
INT-52 | Grinding: damaging on compounds, useful on good isolations | Grindy compound reps drain you more than anything and the grind-through mentality causes injuries; reps should be challenging, never damaging. On isolations with a good strength curve, slow grinding reps keep you near failure longer and are desirable.
INT-53 | Injury risk is near the max, not at 90% | With solid technique and recovery-matched volume, injury risk rises less with intensity than people think; the danger zone is near a true 1RM.
INT-54 | The pump is a by-product, not a goal | Chasing the pump means light weights, high reps and no attention to failure or load; tension and fibre damage drive growth, and a program can give no pump and still grow you.
INT-55 | Techniques are the cherry on top | Drop sets, clusters, tempo, short rests, rest-pause and myo-reps are added to a quality set, never replacing it or hurting the hard sets.
INT-56 | Rest-pause is his preferred technique | At failure, rest briefly while focusing on the muscle, then add reps; post-pause reps must be the hardest of the set. It works best at higher reps, so it pushes rep ranges up.
INT-57 | Drop sets and forced reps: occasional at most | Drop sets on every set are pump-chasing junk; use them only to reach target reps once rest-pause fails. Forced reps, forced negatives and beyond-failure work are an extra set in disguise with a poor stimulus-to-fatigue ratio.
INT-58 | Lengthened partials: may work, not his tool | Lengthened partials may work and many intermediates report gains, but he finds them clunky and hard to standardise and gets more from rest-pause.
INT-59 | AMRAPs out of loaded bodybuilding work | AMRAPs don't belong on top sets, strength work or loaded accessories, where the weight should dictate a rep range; keep them for finishers and unweighted calisthenics.
INT-60 | Burnouts and widowmakers are occasional tools | Burnouts and widowmaker squats are occasional high-effort tools, not regular programming.
INT-61 | Slow-tempo work kills intensity | Long-tempo sets are boring, kill focus and intensity, and make fatigue hard to track.
INT-62 | HIT and Mentzer: right on intensity, too far on volume | He sees himself as doing high intensity across many sets; one set to failure every 6-8 days is too little for naturals. Mentzer was right to focus on intensity but went too far.
INT-63 | Train with focus, save the hype | No phone, chatting or dates in the gym; think about the next set between sets. Save loud music for top sets so you don't desensitize.
INT-64 | Athletes and fighters: stimulate, don't annihilate | For athletes and fighters, lifting stays sub-maximal and never grinds; all-out effort belongs in competition.
VOL-07 | What junk volume is | Junk volume is work outside a relevant intensity window: unchallenging loads, pump chasing, and sets ended by cardio or pump rather than muscular failure.
VOL-08 | Junk is relative and mainly hurts when it replaces hard sets | Junk volume depends on your muscular endurance and is mainly a problem when it replaces hard sets or takes time from other muscles; calling every set after a top set junk is Mentzer logic.
VOL-09 | Typical junk schemes, including 10x10 | 10x10/GVT, 5x10 and 5x20 schemes, 25 chest sets, 13+ biceps sets in a session, 10x20 cable work and many sets of light laterals are typical junk. 10x10 is a band-aid: if you can repeat 10 reps for 10 sets the load is uninteresting.
VOL-10 | Most programs are too greedy | He trims most reviewed programs and says most bodybuilders could cut a fifth to a third of their training for the same results.
VOL-11 | Do the least volume that keeps you progressing | Do as little as needed for as much growth as possible: the minimal sets that keep you progressing and injury-free, leaving room to add later. Volume "only serves the purpose of progression".
VOL-12 | Both extremes fail: one set and 10x10 | HIT's single set is too little for naturals and GVT/10x10 too much; naturals grow in the middle. Multiple hard sets compound reps and give room to modulate effort.
VOL-13 | Minimalism is a tool, not an ideology | Minimal programs fit strength, athletes, beginners and busy people, but tend to build unbalanced physiques for bodybuilders; at an advanced level the maximalist's last ~20% decides things.
VOL-14 | Too little vs too much: err slightly high | Constantly doing too little gives plateaus and too much gives injury or regression; when guessing, err slightly high, but add conservatively.
VOL-15 | People respond differently to volume and intensity | Some respond more to volume and others to intensity; a muscle that won't grow may need more volume, and someone who can't recover needs less volume and more intensity.
VOL-16 | Train between medium and maximum recoverable volume | Maximum recoverable volume (MRV) is the most work you can recover and grow from; aim between medium and maximum. MRV is individual, found by experiment and only diagnosable after the fact, and it rises with training age.
VOL-17 | Signs of too much volume | Lingering soreness or failing to repeat performance session to session signals too much volume; at high weekly sets with slow strength gains, try less volume and more intensity.
VOL-18 | Manual laborers need less gym volume | Office workers can aim at their MRV; manual laborers should stay lower with high intensity and low frequency, and athletes must count the volume from their sport.
VOL-19 | A weekly set count means little on its own | Arbitrary weekly numbers ignore frequency, rep range and distribution; the optimal count is whatever produces growth, and ±1-2 sets don't matter. Different body parts naturally take different counts.
VOL-20 | If you need a number: 8-14 hard sets a week | As a rough answer, 8-14 weekly sets per muscle; at 3x/week and high intensity, 9-15 hard sets is enough to keep growing.
VOL-21 | Weekly sets by training level | Novices need few weekly sets and build up; intermediates build toward a cap of about 12.
VOL-22 | Almost no one needs 16-20 weekly sets | 16-20 weekly sets per muscle is more than almost anyone needs and is impractical to schedule.
VOL-23 | Two to four sets per exercise, usually three | Most exercises get 2-4 sets ("rule of three"); fewer for advanced lifters on taxing lifts, with a 4th set only as icing.
VOL-24 | Heavy low-rep work: one to three sets | Heavy strength work gets 1-3 sets, never 5 or 10; one heavy set leaves room for more accessory volume, three means easier accessories.
VOL-25 | Two to four sets per muscle per session | 2-4 sets per muscle per session is the most productive range and is used in all his programs; 6+ sets in one session forces lower frequency.
VOL-26 | At most two exercises per muscle per day | Use at most two exercises per body part per day; a third variation is excess fatigue.
VOL-27 | More sets per session means less intensity per set | Packing a muscle's sets into one session costs both frequency and per-set intensity; low per-session sets suit high frequency.
VOL-28 | Session set totals by split (2024) | Total sets per session shrink as training days increase.
VOL-29 | Add volume only on a stall, two sets at a time | Add volume only when load, reps and tonnage stall, in small steps; past a point progress should come from intensity rather than more sets.
VOL-30 | Never jump volume | Volume spikes bring no results, sickness or overuse injury, and people then wrongly blame volume.
VOL-31 | Introducing a new exercise | Start a new exercise at low volume and build up, trimming other work to make room; or use several sets with very light weight.
VOL-32 | Bringing in a new small muscle | Start direct work for a newly trained small muscle at 2-3 weekly sets and build.
VOL-33 | Add conservatively, cut liberally | Programming is subtraction as well as addition: be conservative when adding and liberal when removing. "If you can shrink a day, always shrink it."
VOL-34 | Cut sets, not reps; cut strength sets first | To reduce fatigue, cut sets rather than reps, and cut strength-work sets (to 1-3) before lowering back-off intensity.
VOL-35 | Move volume from dominant muscles to weak points | Budget weekly volume: take some from a dominant muscle and spend it on weak points or recovery; dominant parts won't shrink.
VOL-36 | Maintenance takes about half the volume | Keep intensity and roughly halve the volume to maintain a muscle that has reached your goal; taper it down gradually.
VOL-37 | Don't cut volume pre-emptively for age or a cut | Lowering volume and intensity in advance because of age causes the muscle loss people then blame on age. During a cut, stay at the edge of the intensity you can handle rather than dropping to ~60%.
VOL-38 | Work capacity is built gradually | Work capacity is what lets you do the volume that drives growth; it is built and lost gradually, and more muscle raises it.
VOL-39 | "Intermediate syndrome": use a bridge program | Jumping from a low-volume novice program to a high-volume one fails; use a bridge program, because an advanced program "must be earned".
VOL-40 | Advanced lifters pack more tonnage into the same time | Advanced lifters need more tonnage but get no more gym time, so they raise density with supersets; eventually the only way to add volume is longer sessions.
VOL-41 | Strength work is ~20%, the rest makes you grow | Strength work opens the session and gives the highest-quality tonnage but is a minority; the variations and accessories that follow provide most of the growth stimulus.
VOL-42 | Most tonnage at moderate reps; offset heavy and volume work | The bulk of tonnage sits at 4-12 reps, and "volume work" means above 6 reps. When the strength set is heaviest, the following volume work should be lightest, offsetting tonnage swings with variations.
VOL-43 | Count indirect volume | Compounds spread tonnage across several muscles, so count it: bench is triceps and front-delt volume, and a 300 lb squat isn't 300 lb of quad tonnage. Shared structures such as the lower back cap how much you can send through them.
VOL-44 | Isolation vs compound share | He has given both an isolation-heavy and a compound-heavy ratio.
VOL-45 | Upper back is a volume black hole | The upper back takes huge volume and recovers fast, so it gets the most sets, as long as the lower back isn't taxed; most people undertrain it.
VOL-46 | Legs respond to minimal, hard volume | Legs grow from intensity and progression, not "a million sets": one quad movement plus one hinge or knee-flexion movement at about 3 hard sets each.
VOL-47 | Chest grows on modest volume, but presses need enough | Chest grows on modest weekly volume and about three exercises is the session limit; still, very low pressing volume (3x5 bench) is too little.
VOL-48 | Vertical presses and shoulders tolerate more | Vertical pressing tolerates up to double the horizontal press sets; shoulders get 1-2 isolations plus 1-2 vertical presses per day, and more after a press plus one isolation is junk.
VOL-49 | Arms are main lifts, not accessories | Treat arms as main lifts with 2-4 hard sets per muscle per session; bro-style 20 sets/wk per arm muscle is junk. If ~12 weekly arm sets don't grow arms, check progression first, then change exercises or even lower volume.
VOL-50 | Small muscles: less per session, recover fast | Small muscles take fewer sets per session but recover quickly; feed them through varied angles, frequency and supersets.
VOL-51 | Back-off sets: two or three after the work sets | After 1-3 strength sets, usually 2-3 back-off sets of the same pattern with a modest drop. After a 3x3, a heavy 3x8-12 back-off on the same lift is too much, so use a variation.
VOL-52 | Evolving sets: keep the span narrow | When the number of sets evolves, keep the span to 2-4 sets; it is an advanced tool because swinging sets changes volume far more than swinging reps.
VOL-53 | Match total reps when changing schemes | When swapping set/rep schemes keep total reps similar, or know why you are changing volume.
VOL-54 | Finishers and pyramids drift into junk | Pyramids and drop-set finishers drift toward junk; weight progression builds quality tonnage. A finisher only makes sense after real work has used up the muscle.
VOL-55 | Nucleus overload: a light complement, later rated minor | Nucleus overload (NO) is frequent, high-volume, very light work kept easy enough to repeat, used to complement strength work for the same muscle, never to replace it. It is the one exception to intensity windows.
VOL-56 | Train until the day's stimulus is done, then leave | There is no fixed time cap: train as long as the day's stimulus takes, don't drag sessions into junk, and cram the necessary work into the least time.
VOL-57 | Normal sessions run 60-90 minutes | About 60-90 min is a normal session and 90 min is "pushing it"; if you're done after 60 min of a planned 90, cut the day. Saying training past 1.5 h is pointless is a myth.
VOL-58 | His own session lengths | His own sessions went from multi-hour days toward fewer sets per session.
FRQ-01 | Frequency is the pillar naturals ignore most | Frequency is one of the three pillars with volume and intensity and the most ignored, so for naturals it matters most; when sets happen comes before how many.
FRQ-02 | Spread weekly sets over several sessions | The same weekly sets give better results spread out: lumping them forces lighter loads and adds cardio limits and junk, while spreading allows heavier loads, more tonnage and better recovery. Frequency exists to fit volume in.
FRQ-03 | Once a week is suboptimal unless you are coasting | Training a muscle once a week is always suboptimal for a natural unless you are deliberately coasting on it; it leaves the muscle idle most of the week and brings soreness and poor work capacity.
FRQ-04 | Frequency is a recovery schedule | There is no universally optimal frequency, only one that matches the work done to its recovery time; retrain a muscle once it is ready, because leaving it dormant leads to stagnation.
FRQ-05 | Choose frequency first, then template and sets per day | Pick frequency first; templates mostly dictate frequency and grouping, and per-session numbers must scale to the number of days.
FRQ-06 | Higher frequency, less per session | The more often you train a muscle, the less volume (and the shorter the session) each time; low frequency needs high volume and intensity per session.
FRQ-07 | Take only part of the recovery "pot" each session | Recovery is a fixed-size tub: fill it with quality tonnage as often as possible, taking only part of it per session; training once a week doesn't make the tub bigger.
FRQ-08 | Every pattern weekly, compounds 2-3x a week | Technique and strength depend on frequency: every movement pattern should return at least once a week, and compounds should be practised 2-3x a week. Once a lift is automatic, heavy-work frequency can drop.
FRQ-09 | Soreness means frequency is too low | Soreness signals too little frequency, not growth; with proper frequency it disappears.
FRQ-10 | Never sacrifice intensity for frequency | Frequency should not cost per-set intensity; trading frequency for intensity is fine, and stronger lifters need longer recovery windows.
FRQ-11 | Frequency has limits as a progression tool | Frequency is capped by the days in a week, so it can't drive progress through the intermediate and advanced stages; raising frequency on a stalled isolation keeps the muscle fatigued. Hitting a muscle more than 2-3x/week is earned.
FRQ-12 | Small muscles more often, big muscles harder with more rest | Small muscles burn out fast in a session but recover fast, so train them more often with less per session; the opposite belief is "backwards".
FRQ-13 | Frequency also exists inside the session | Intra-session frequency (how sets follow each other, rest and session length) matters too; alternating posterior-chain and quad work lets muscles recover between sets and allows more tonnage.
FRQ-14 | At least ~48 hours between hard sessions for a muscle | ~48 h is the default and minimum between hard sessions for the same muscle; don't hit a muscle hard two days in a row.
FRQ-15 | Light back-to-back hits are allowed | Full recovery matters for peak performance, not for hypertrophy, so lighter hits on consecutive days are fine.
FRQ-16 | Match the recovery interval to the template | Each template implies a recovery interval for each muscle.
FRQ-17 | Late view: longer gaps for strong lifters | By late 2024 he spaces sessions further apart.
FRQ-18 | The last hard session sets the next one | Check whether the muscle is back to baseline before retraining it; if a moderate session leaves days of unused rest, move the next one earlier, and if you can't match volume next time, you needed more rest.
FRQ-19 | Legs about every 48 hours, later twice a week | Legs grow from volume spread out, not a weekly crushing day: a heavy knee flexion or hip hinge about every 2 days, with two leg days a week as the minimum beyond novices.
FRQ-20 | Upper body about three times a week | Upper-body muscles are numerous, less taxing and recover fast, so train them about every 2 days; chest does better 2-3x a week with fewer, more precise sets than on one chest day.
FRQ-21 | Horizontal press at least twice, bench under three times | Bench once a week doesn't progress the bench, but raw bench 3+ times a week brings shoulder overuse; press horizontally at least twice and use push-ups, flies or close-grip for extra frequency.
FRQ-22 | Vertical pressing about twice a week | Vertical pressing ~2x a week is needed for big shoulders; 3x OHP is overcorrection and once a week at 5x5 is too little. Do both a vertical and a horizontal press on each press day.
FRQ-23 | Squat twice a week long term | Once a week is not enough, 2x a week is the long-term sweet spot and 3x is hard to sustain; daily squatting works but builds strength more than size.
FRQ-24 | Deadlift rarely, the hinge pattern more often | The conventional deadlift needs the least frequency of the big lifts (low frequency, high intensity, moderate volume), while the hinge pattern can return every 2-3 days with variations.
FRQ-25 | Upper back: most frequency-tolerant, spread over days | The upper back tolerates training nearly every day with varied angles and intensities; train back at least 3x a week, add back movements to the split "as Lego blocks", and never put all back volume on one day.
FRQ-26 | Pull-ups can be done very often | Pull-ups are low-impact and the upper back recovers fast, so they can be done daily, even to failure; grease-the-groove works for them as an exception.
FRQ-27 | Bring in a neglected muscle by frequency first | For a neglected or small muscle, raise frequency (1x → 2x → 3x a week) before adding sets per session.
FRQ-28 | Arms: biceps on every upper day | Arm intensity is fixed at failure and per-session volume is limited, so arm growth comes from frequency; put biceps work on every upper day, and isolations need more frequency than the compounds they accompany.
FRQ-29 | Bodyweight work often, spread through other days | Bodyweight and calisthenics movements, abs and nucleus-overload work are cheap to recover from, so do them often and spread them through other days instead of giving them a day of their own.
FRQ-30 | Four days a week is the sustainable sweet spot | 4 training days a week is his favourite and sustainable for life; 3 is fine, and intermediates need at least 3.
FRQ-31 | Advanced means more sessions | Advancing means more sessions per year, not just more sets.
FRQ-32 | Add days by spreading existing work | Start with 3-4 days and expand; add a day by spreading existing tonnage, not by piling on more, and never jump from 3 to 6 days at the same per-day volume.
FRQ-33 | Long days → add a day; thin days → compress | If sessions run too long, add a day; if days are thin or redundant, compress them.
FRQ-34 | Who six days a week suits | Six days suits fast-recovering novices, advanced lifters who need frequency to accumulate volume, and rare volume responders with poor leverages.
FRQ-35 | Busy lifters: two or three full-body days | Busy or sleep-deprived lifters don't need high frequency: 2-3 full-body days, or upper/lower twice with isolation in short gaps. A physique built on 5 days a week can be maintained on 2.
FRQ-36 | Daily training is possible but not advised | Training every day works if volume and intensity are reduced and the split never builds enough fatigue to need a day off, but he doesn't advise it for others; for depressed lifters, daily training for structure can come before gains.
FRQ-37 | The bro split is the worst template for naturals | One muscle per day once a week gives too little frequency and too much per-session volume, which fills sessions with junk; the fix is to hit each muscle twice a week.
FRQ-38 | The gentleman split is his favourite | His preferred template is an upper/lower skeleton whose days are built around synergistic muscle groups and a main compound, with flexible boundaries.
FRQ-39 | Upper/lower is the most convenient skeleton | Upper/lower saves warm-up time, groups synergistic muscles and manages fatigue well, so it is the base for most templates; its weakness is poor upper-back frequency, and minimal upper/lower days waste it.
FRQ-40 | Run PPL as pull-push-legs | Never run push-pull-legs: pull day's hinges and rows fatigue the legs and lower back right before squats. Use pull-push-legs (or legs-push-pull) or put a rest day between pull and legs.
FRQ-41 | PPL is a good split | PPL is a very good split with an unwarranted bad reputation; run it about 6x a week (each muscle 2x) or 3x a week, and for 4-5 days just cut a day.
FRQ-42 | Full body works best three times a week | Full body is best at 3x a week with ~48 h between sessions; it is the hardest template to program because compounds get crammed and small muscles neglected, so skew it to priorities and rotate variations.
FRQ-43 | First self-made program: full body or PPL | For a first self-made program use full body or PPL; body-part-style and gentleman splits need more experience and work capacity.
FRQ-44 | Templates for intermediate and advanced lifters | Intermediates run upper/lower twice plus an arm day; advanced lifters run upper/lower three times over 6 days, rotating variations and taking rest days as needed.
FRQ-45 | The template's name matters less than its control | The label of a split matters less than how it controls volume, intensity and frequency; the same principle is good or bad depending on how it is applied.
FRQ-46 | Torso/limbs splits risk imbalance | Torso/limbs splits risk imbalance, because torso work already trains the limbs and people overemphasise the torso.
FRQ-47 | Double sessions are valid but rarely needed | AM/PM double sessions work but are hard to program; make the morning the hard session (legs) and the evening the fun one, and for most people one combined session is fine.
FRQ-48 | Pair synergistic muscles; no "chest day" | Group synergistic muscles, such as chest with shoulders and triceps since presses train all three; never give triceps or chest a day of its own.
FRQ-49 | Each day is justified by a compound | Decide what a day is before designing it: build it around at least one main compound and name it by its main pattern ("bench day", "squat day").
FRQ-50 | Upper back, calves, abs and neck fill any day | The upper back is synergistic with nothing, so it fills gaps in any day; calves, abs, neck and upper back are the easiest superset fillers.
FRQ-51 | Back belongs with legs more than with biceps | Back and biceps is a low-synergy pairing; back days should carry posterior-chain and leg work ("a leg day in disguise"), e.g. an RDL or paused squat supersetted with chin-ups.
FRQ-52 | Don't mix big upper and big lower compounds | Squat + bench or bench + deadlift in one session means re-warming up, switching the pump and losing synergy; lower days can take traps, neck, abs and forearms but never big upper muscles.
FRQ-53 | Small upper work fits on lower days | Forearms, grip, neck, traps, rear delts, biceps and long-head triceps fit on lower days, ideally in squat rest periods; avoid shoulders and lateral/medial triceps there because they need warming up.
FRQ-54 | Split boundaries are flexible | You are not a slave to the template: calves on upper days, chin-ups on push day, upper back on lower day are fine; rigid splits are "mostly idiotic", and you don't hit every muscle on a "full body" day just because of the label.
FRQ-55 | Touch every upper group on upper days | Every upper day should touch each upper muscle group at least lightly; small extra doses on non-dedicated days ("stacking pebbles") add frequency, blood flow and some tonnage.
FRQ-56 | Alternate horizontal and vertical emphasis | On two or more upper days, give each a horizontal or vertical focus and alternate which pattern opens mirror days, so each pattern gets priority every week.
FRQ-57 | Separate heavy squats and heavy deadlifts | Never do heavy squats and heavy deadlifts on the same day or on consecutive days; if they share a day, make one heavy and the other a lighter variation in a higher rep range, squat first.
FRQ-58 | One heavy lower pattern opens each lower day | Each lower day opens with one heavy knee flexion or hip hinge; the next heavy-ish movement is the other pattern, and which one leads alternates between days.
FRQ-59 | Alternate taxing and easy days | Don't stack demanding days: alternate upper and lower and follow a long or taxing day with a lighter one; too many easy days in a row wastes time.
FRQ-60 | Heaviest lower day right before the rest day | Start the week with an upper day and put the heaviest lower or highest-tonnage day right before the rest day or weekend, so rest follows the sessions that drain the most.
FRQ-61 | Spread rest days out | Don't train 4-5 days in a row and then take a block off; spread sessions and rest days.
FRQ-62 | Write the split as a rotation, not a week | The week is a human invention: write the split as a rotation of days and let rest days float with recovery.
FRQ-63 | Place squat days with care | Squat after a rest day or an arms/accessory day, not the day after heavy bench, trap or upper-back work (pre-fatigued shoulder girdle) or heavy lower-back work; you can deadlift after almost any day except squats or deadlifts.
FRQ-64 | Manage mental fatigue across the week | Heavy lower compounds are the most mentally draining, so separate them and keep the window of mentally taxing work short.
FRQ-65 | Schedule around physical work | Blue-collar workers should keep weekdays minimal and intense, put most volume on days off and leave recovery before heavy work days; white-collar workers can train 4-6x a week.
FRQ-66 | An arm day is the least taxing day | An arm day is a low-CNS day: a few upper compounds (close-grip bench, dips, rows) then isolation, never a pump-only day or lower-body lifts; it can double as active recovery.
FRQ-67 | An arm day is earned and optional | Add an arm day only if big arms are a goal and only once legs are trained at least twice a week with plenty of upper-back work; it adds to arm work on other days rather than replacing it.
FRQ-68 | Where the arm day goes | Put the arm day on a weekend or low-energy day, or between two CNS-heavy lower days, not next to upper days whose arm work it would duplicate.
FRQ-69 | Pyramid of priority within a session | Open with the most demanding, most important lift (heavy low-rep strength work while fresh), then lighter compounds, then isolation; if time runs short, cut from the bottom, never the top.
FRQ-70 | At most two strength lifts per workout | Use at most two strength lifts per session, and don't put two non-supersetted strength lifts for different parts of one body part back to back.
FRQ-71 | Every session holds both intensity and volume | Don't split training into heavy/light days or strength/volume weeks, which give volume without intensity and intensity without volume; open every day with strength work and vary the emphasis on a spectrum.
FRQ-72 | Put skipped work early, as supersets | Work people tend to skip (neck, abs, calves) goes early in the session, supersetted into the main lifts' rest periods, not at the end; it doesn't hurt main-lift performance.
FRQ-73 | Upper days long, lower days short | Lower lifts hit more muscle and move more tonnage per set, so lower days carry fewer exercises; upper days have many small muscles that need isolation and should look about twice as long on paper.
FRQ-74 | Intense days shorter, voluminous days longer | The more intense the day, the shorter it should be; don't pile volume after a heavy deadlift.
FRQ-75 | Mirror days give rotation | A/B mirror days (same emphasis, different lifts, rep ranges or order) reduce boredom and overuse injuries; train the muscle, not the movement. Novices don't need them.
FRQ-76 | Keep rotations simple, strength work regular | Keep rotations simple (A/B) and within the same movement pattern, and keep strength work regular rather than rotated; if alternatives don't share a pattern, the missing work must appear elsewhere.
FRQ-77 | A good program is flexible | If moving or dropping one exercise breaks a program, it is a bad program; followers can cut exercises or volume freely, and the more advanced you are, the more flexibility you need.
FRQ-78 | Stick with a good program for years | Run a good program as an endless repeating cycle rather than jumping every 6 months; frequent changes produce artificial PRs, and for a bodybuilder all training is hypertrophy training, so "blocks" make little sense.
FRQ-79 | Fighters and athletes: lifting is secondary | For fighters, lifting is minimal and scheduled away from the sport; an upper-dominant split suits them because lower lifts tax the CNS and tendons more.
FRQ-80 | Novices: three full-body days | Novices do well on 3 full-body days with weekends off; frequency hardly matters for beginners, and they need only one heavy squat and one heavy hinge a week.
FRQ-81 | Women are not programmed like men | A woman not seeking upper-body size gains nothing from training it 3x a week; his women's novice program is 3 full-body days emphasising legs and glutes, and even women wanting a bigger upper body shouldn't get an upper-dominant split.
RST-01 | Rest about 2-3 minutes, never more than 5 | Rest as little as possible while staying performant; more than 5 min is overkill. He doesn't time rests and goes roughly by feel.
RST-02 | Rest so the muscle returns to baseline | The aim of rest is for the muscle to be back to baseline for the next set; too-short rests wreck performance and turn training into cardio.
RST-03 | Same-muscle exercises still need full rest | Between two exercises for the same muscle, rest 2-3 min; performance matters for biceps as much as for quads.
RST-04 | Low reps get straight sets and long rests | Low-rep sets run as straight sets with long rests; higher-rep sets can use rest-pause and shorter rests.
RST-05 | Rest-based tests | Redoing a set after 3 min should give fewer reps; but a big crash means poor work capacity, and a 1RM you can repeat after 10 min wasn't a true max.
RST-06 | Don't superset the opening strength work | Heavy opening lifts (squat, deadlift, bench, hinges, heavy knee flexion, weighted chins) run as straight sets with full rest to keep focus and energy; at most pair them with small non-bracing work (neck, abs, curls, rear delts).
RST-07 | Never superset two movements for the same muscle | Pairing two lifts for the same muscle is pre-fatigue or a finisher, a different tool, and a last resort otherwise.
RST-08 | Leg exercises don't chain into each other | Lower-body supersets are harder because leg lifts tax the cardiovascular system; pair leg lifts with static or non-leg moves, and moving straight from one leg exercise to another is "a cardinal sin of hypertrophy". Keep ab work away from heavy bracing lifts.
RST-09 | Superset all accessory and isolation work | Sitting idle between sets of curls, shrugs, laterals, pushdowns or leg extensions wastes time and tonnage; the target muscle needs rest, the rest of the body doesn't. He uses supersets in all programs, even for novices.
RST-10 | Pair unrelated or antagonist muscles | Supersets of non-competing muscles cost little or no performance, only cardiovascular capacity; the loss grows in a long full-body day.
RST-11 | Default pairings: press with pull | Pair a horizontal press with a horizontal pull and a vertical press with a vertical pull; such a superset is part of a day, not its emphasis.
RST-12 | Near-free volume to fill rests | Rear delts, leg curls, wrist curls, calves, traps, biceps and abs add stimulus with almost no systemic fatigue, so they slot into rest periods; use machine and warm-up downtime too.
RST-13 | No rest inside a superset, rest after the round | A superset is exercises back to back with no planned rest, then rest before repeating the round; walking between stations is fine.
RST-14 | Giant sets: 4-5 exercises, most intense first | A giant set is 4+ exercises; 4-5 is the sweet spot and longer ones eat time instead of saving it. Order them from most to least intense, and the last exercise is the one to cut.
RST-15 | Nonstop giant sets for conditioned lifters | Advanced, conditioned lifters can cycle giant sets nonstop, since with 5 exercises the first has recovered by the time you loop back; isolation-only giant sets work because isolation isn't systemically fatiguing.
RST-16 | Build up to giant sets gradually | Progress from easy supersets to hard giant sets, or you'll be cooked and wrongly blame the system; beginners who can't superset yet use short gaps instead of dropping supersets.
RST-17 | Superset only what is logistically close | In commercial gyms superset equipment that sits close together and accept the gamble when it doesn't; never force supersets that don't fit, and if you can't superset, split the exercises.
RST-18 | End lower days with an isolation giant set | Finish with a giant set of isolations for different muscles, back to back.
RST-19 | Most gym time is rest; supersets reclaim it | Most gym time is spent resting, so supersets cut session length without removing warm-ups, lifts or hard sets.
RST-20 | Session length as a diagnostic | If the same work fits in 1 h with supersets as in 2 h, the 2 h was lazy rest; know how long your program takes, because cramming 90 min into 60 raises intensity non-optimally.
RST-21 | Don't idle between sets | Naps, baths or phone time between sets are laziness; fill downtime with another exercise, and keep podcasts for strength-work rests.
PRG-01 | Progressive overload is non-negotiable | Without progressive overload there is no muscle gain; something has to go up for the muscles to go up, and a coach who never mentions it is a "hack".
PRG-02 | The target is tonnage rising over time | The goal is tonnage (volume × intensity × frequency) rising over time within relevant intensity windows; "tonnage is king". Strength helps by raising tonnage per session, but maxing out won't make you big.
PRG-03 | A natural must eventually get stronger | To keep growing a natural must eventually get stronger; a main lift stuck for years means the physique is stuck too.
PRG-04 | Strength alone is not size | Strength counts only inside a program that raises tonnage; low-rep-only strength progress gives little muscle long term, and "more weight = faster growth" is the #1 mistake.
PRG-05 | The number doesn't matter, the trend does | The absolute weight means nothing; only that it keeps rising. There is no magic hypertrophy rep range: singles to 20s build muscle if taken to fatigue.
PRG-06 | Overload comes before growth | Overload and growth feed each other (bigger muscle lifts more, more tonnage, bigger muscle), but overload comes first.
PRG-07 | Performance is the best gauge of growth | Performance and strength progression are the best gauge of hypertrophy; soreness and pump are poor indicators, and a pump with no progress means drop the method.
PRG-08 | Body measurements: slow and disputed | Track progress via strength first; body measurements are slow and misleading for programming, yet he also calls them the only reliable way to know a muscle grew and measures every part he specializes.
PRG-09 | Rising lifts at the same bodyweight = muscle | If lifts, volume or tonnage rise at the same bodyweight or without much fat gain, you are adding muscle.
PRG-10 | Dirty-bulk strength can mislead | Aggressive or dirty bulks create artificial strength gains that stop tracking muscle.
PRG-11 | Overload has many modes | Overload can come from load, reps, sets, frequency, rest, technique/variation or range of motion; weight on the bar is just "the tip of the iceberg", and more sets and reps at the same weight are valid overload.
PRG-12 | Bar weight must stay in the mix | Weight on the bar is the simplest, most reliable and easiest lever to log and must stay in the mix, but load-only progression is "designed to fail" because the ability to add weight outpaces recovery (the "Milo paradox").
PRG-13 | Reps-only or sets-only progression stalls | Adding only reps or only sets at a fixed weight also stalls: the weight becomes too easy, reps drift into low-quality or cardio-limited volume, and sets become junk volume. You need both load and volume.
PRG-14 | Order of the levers | Step loading adds stimulus as reps first (evolving range), then sets, then frequency, and weight last, always in small steps; elsewhere he ranks importance as weight, then reps, then sets, then frequency, with drop sets, TUT and pre-exhaustion only "the cherry on top".
PRG-15 | Volume and frequency are reserve levers | Volume and frequency are pulled when weight progress stops being realistic; intensity rises automatically as load progresses. When daily volume and intensity both stall, frequency is the lever left.
PRG-16 | Start with minimal volume | Start with minimal volume and keep volume as a gear in reserve; starting high leaves nothing to progress with. "Progressive overload is a loaded rifle": use only the bullets you need.
PRG-17 | Tempo can't replace load | Slow tempo/TUT is not a substitute for load; tempo is hard to track and drifts with fatigue, so standardise it rather than progress it.
PRG-18 | Frequency is overload too | Frequency is overload ("load and time"), but more frequency only helps if it produces progression.
PRG-19 | Load anything that can be loaded | Fixed-load implements (bodyweight, maxed dumbbells) leave only reps, sets, rest or tempo, none equal to adding weight; use dip belts, vests and dumbbells, and slow the tempo only when you've maxed the rack.
PRG-20 | Evolving rep ranges are the core method | Give each lift a fixed rep range, start near the bottom, add reps weekly, add weight at the top and stay in the same range. He calls it the best way to progress.
PRG-21 | Add weight when the first set hits the ceiling | Only the first (top) set needs to reach the top of the range, with the others close; waiting for every set to hit the top turns the range back into a static scheme.
PRG-22 | Readiness depends on the range | On a heavy low range less is needed to add weight; you needn't max every set.
PRG-23 | Reps should fall across sets | At the same weight, reps should drop from set to set; identical reps on three or more sets mean sandbagging or too light a load.
PRG-24 | Straight sets, no pyramids | Use the same weight on all working sets; pyramids and reverse pyramids ("D tier") pile up junk volume.
PRG-25 | Every set stays inside the range | If a set falls below the floor after a jump, drop the next set and win it back next session; if the first set misses, return to the previous weight. Falling below means the jump was too big or the range was badly set ("too greedy").
PRG-26 | Evolving sets: add a set, then weight | When the first set tops the range, add a set; at the top set count, add weight and drop back a set, then rebuild.
PRG-27 | Set ranges one set wide | Keep set ranges one set wide; wide set ranges stall progress, especially on compounds. 3-4 sets beats a fixed 4 because you can try a heavier weight without "failing".
PRG-28 | Introduce evolving ranges gradually | Evolve reps before sets; beginners keep sets fixed. Convert a program like "a drop of ink": isolation first, then upper compounds, then lower-body lifts (or, 2022, start on strength work and extend to accessories), never everything at once.
PRG-29 | Rep PRs build into weight PRs | With evolving ranges you nearly always get a rep PR ("forget 10 lb jumps"), and rep PRs accumulate into weight PRs.
PRG-30 | Each lift gets its own range, kept stable | Decide each lift's range before placing it in a day; one global range for everything is simplistic. Never change a lift's range just because you failed to add weight, and if you do modify it, stick with the change.
PRG-31 | Stay in a range at least a month | Stay in a given rep range at least a month; varying ranges across the program and within each day (light/heavy mix) aids progression and freshness.
PRG-32 | Evolving ranges: slower start, fewer plateaus | Evolving ranges progress more slowly at first than static schemes but avoid plateaus and give a steady flow, trading strength speed for volume at a useful intensity without load pressure.
PRG-33 | Evolving ranges need tracking and brackets | Evolving ranges require tracking and judgement; their freedom can let tonnage dip, so ranges stay inside strict brackets ("infinite possibilities but a narrow optimum"). Misused, they still produce terrible programs.
PRG-34 | Open ranges vs fixed rep targets | Two types: open ranges where you take every rep (8-12), and fixed rep targets for strength work where you stop at the target even with reps left, keeping strength a step ahead.
PRG-35 | Range width signals intent | A narrow range means quick jumps are expected, a wide one slower progress; a range is a microcosm of a block, not a block.
PRG-36 | Evolving ranges for novices: disputed | He has said both that novices should not use evolving ranges (hard to track, need beta-testing, dangerous for lazy novices) and that evolving schemes are "very intelligent" and good for novices.
PRG-37 | Ranges that are too narrow fail | Narrow ranges leave no room for set evolution and drift toward sandbagged 5x5; keep at least 4 reps between floor and ceiling.
PRG-38 | Ranges that are too wide fail | Very wide ranges (top more than 2× the bottom) take forever to earn a jump and invite laziness; 6-12 is about the widest he allows (2024).
PRG-39 | Wide ranges for lifts where reps fall fast | Use a wide range like 6-12 where reps drop fast across sets or the lift is hard to progress; incline and preacher curls plateau easily and need it.
PRG-40 | Higher ranges: easy reps, hard jumps | Wider or higher ranges make each added rep easier, but the higher the range, the harder it is to clear and add weight.
PRG-41 | Adjust the range to a plateau | When stalled in a range, adjust it to the exercise: widen if low reps stall, shrink if high reps stall; try widening first so tonnage climbs at the same weight.
PRG-42 | When testing a range, start wide | When beta-testing a new range, start wider and narrow it; shrinking is safer than expanding.
PRG-43 | The "543" elevator scheme | For heavy deadlifts and OHP, keep the weight and build sets and reps up a ladder, then add weight and restart at the bottom.
PRG-44 | Fixed-weight squat and bench ladder | For squat and bench, keep the weight and climb a rep ladder, then add weight and return to the start; a failed session is repeated.
PRG-45 | Strength work: 1-3 sets | Keep strength work to 1-3 sets (4 max); 5-6 sets means sandbagging. Descending reps (5-4-3) keep the tonnage high-quality.
PRG-46 | Autoregulate strength work by sets, not load | Keep the strength-lift weight and autoregulate through sets and reps; a weak day is made up with same-pattern back-offs.
PRG-47 | Top sets plus lighter volume sets | Pair a few top-intensity sets with lighter volume or back-off sets; how heavy the strength sets are decides the back-off reps.
PRG-48 | The 85% rule (step loading) | Build reps and sets at a load until it feels like ~70% effort, then add weight to get back to ~85%, cut sets or reps, and rebuild; never keep piling volume at 70%. Your 85% grows with you.
PRG-49 | Rep-goal system | Maxing every set at the same weight toward a total rep goal, then adding ~5 lb, is "excellent" and essentially evolving ranges by another name; he later prefers RIR for clearer brackets.
PRG-50 | Eastern-style big jumps | Keep a weight challenging for 4 reps until easy, then make a big jump; leaving reps in reserve doesn't slow progress.
PRG-51 | Static schemes force sandbagging | With static schemes the first set is too easy or the last is ground out; 5x5 progresses fast early, then plateaus hard, and is not a good hypertrophy reference.
PRG-52 | Static schemes can only add weight | Static schemes progress only by adding weight, force conservative jumps, make tonnage jump in steps, and after a miss you deload below where you started; they never teach plateau handling ("5x5 syndrome").
PRG-53 | Where static ranges are fine | Static ranges are fine for novices (weight keeps rising regardless) and as deliberate tools: back-off or accessory volume after strength work, a secondary lift you don't push, chin-ups, or the only horizontal press of a low-volume day.
PRG-54 | An evolving version always beats static | An evolving version of a scheme always beats its static form, and static targets should not be used past the beginner phase.
PRG-55 | Default jump sizes | The default jump is 5 lb per side on barbell lifts and 2.5 lb per side on OHP and curls, with "5 lb per hand" logic for dumbbells.
PRG-56 | No universal jump | Jump size depends on the lift, the range and the whole program; decide plate loading before choosing the range, and if they don't match change one.
PRG-57 | Failed transitions mean jumps are too big | If you stall or transitions to a new weight fail, halve the jumps; if many transitions fail, rework the plan.
PRG-58 | Small muscles get small jumps | Small muscles need small jumps and microplates; 5 lb plates hurt the ego but are necessary. Don't ego lift small muscles.
PRG-59 | Microloading also stalls eventually | Microloading extends linear progression on presses but will also stall; then combine it with rep and set manipulation.
PRG-60 | Coarse dumbbell jumps need a longer range | With coarse dumbbell jumps, extend the range so you can land at the bottom after the jump.
PRG-61 | Jumps shrink over a career | Jump size and how often you add weight shrink exponentially over a career, so slowing early progression extends it.
PRG-62 | Slow, steady, uninterrupted progress | Slow, steady, uninterrupted progress beats fast gains followed by stalls, injury or regression ("not a shooting star"; the tortoise beats the hare).
PRG-63 | Deliberately slow linear progression | Bodybuilders should deliberately slow linear progression to delay the big plateau, even to a crawl; there are no strength deadlines, only physique deadlines.
PRG-64 | How fast you got strong matters | Slower strength gain with more accumulated volume gives more hypertrophy; how fast you got strong matters more than how strong you got.
PRG-65 | Gains slow but never stop | For a sensible lifter gains slow but never stop (a car at 1 mph still gets far); intermediate pace is the baseline, and elites progress at your rate but don't quit.
PRG-66 | Each lift progresses at its own rate | The more muscle a lift involves, the faster it progresses; OHP is the slowest after curls.
PRG-67 | Realistic post-novice rates | Small yearly gains on isolation and modest gains on compounds are normal and still build muscle.
PRG-68 | Visible change takes months | Nothing massive happens in 2-3 months; judge changes over months, not weeks.
PRG-69 | Growth happens when progress gets hard | For intermediates hypertrophy happens exactly when progression gets hard; fast progress on a new lift is neural adaptation, not muscle.
PRG-70 | "Natural limit" is a programming limit | Plateaus after 8-9 years are methodology, not genetics; long-term lifters stall because they stop making training hard or think they know everything.
PRG-71 | What counts as a plateau | Repeating a weight or reps for 2-3 sessions is the "breaking point" where gains happen, not a plateau; months at the same weight is one. Plateaus are normal and PRs every session are unrealistic (2023).
PRG-72 | Plateaus are a recovery or programming problem | Plateaus come from poor recovery or diet, low effort, bad programming or a bad lift; in bodybuilding they usually mean too much volume to recover from or too little stimulus.
PRG-73 | First response: manipulate volume and intensity | Answer a plateau first with new rep ranges, more volume, widened ranges or frequency, not with new exercises; volume, intensity or supersets bypass the law of accommodation.
PRG-74 | Never deload and re-run the progression | Taking weight off and re-running the same progression is "a longer run-up at the same wall" and wastes quality tonnage; only drop weight if you hit failure mid-session. A missed 3x5 should become 5,4,3 instead.
PRG-75 | Don't swap a lift because it stalled | Swapping a stalled lift dodges the plateau; the "progress" on the new lift is neural relearning, and stalling is when the body must build muscle. Letting failure trigger rotation hurts mental strength.
PRG-76 | When truly stuck, add a same-pattern variation | One stimulus alone stalls; add (not swap) a carryover variation of the same pattern as back-offs or on another day. Two variations can give a year of gains before a third is needed.
PRG-77 | Plateau busters | For stuck lifts he endorses back-off sets, drop sets within the range, rest-pause on isolation, and occasional low-rep phases on bench.
PRG-78 | Plateaus are mostly programming-made | Most plateaus are built into programs (novice programs are designed to end in a missed set of five); smart programming goes around the wall.
PRG-79 | Plateaus can't be fully avoided | Aim for fewer and later plateaus and see them coming ("push them ahead of you").
PRG-80 | A deliberate stall is not a plateau | Holding strength work steady (a controlled plateau) while pushing volume or a secondary lift is a "tonnage sacrifice", not a plateau; don't try to progress a main lift and its variation at once.
PRG-81 | Fixed-increment programs rush the wall | Programs that add a fixed weight every session or week rush novices into the wall and then cycle deloads; "you don't force progressive overload, it happens".
PRG-82 | Tonnage rises steadily, no roller-coaster | Bodybuilding tonnage should rise steadily with only small planned dips, neither flat nor a roller-coaster; powerlifters wave and peak, bodybuilders don't.
PRG-83 | Strength work is the lighthouse | Strength work opens every session when you're freshest; its 1-3 heavy sets drive progression though they are a small share of session volume. Never superset it; if short on time do only the strength work.
PRG-84 | Back-offs balance the strength set | A light opener calls for more back-off volume, a heavy one for lighter back-offs; same-style back-offs let strength progress trickle down, while speed back-offs nearly stall progress. Dropping strength reps is fine only if tonnage is made up elsewhere.
PRG-85 | Most tonnage lives in variations and accessories | Most tonnage should trickle into variations and accessories; if most of a session's tonnage comes from the first heavy lift, a plateau there drains everything. Variations should progress more slowly than the main lift.
PRG-86 | Lifts can feed or block each other | Main and secondary lifts feed each other, and similar lower-body lifts allow more tonnage together, but four similar presses compete and block overload.
PRG-87 | Specialization costs the rest | Single-lift focus phases (e.g. Smolov) cost the rest of the program and leave a tonnage drop; alternating priorities make a staircase that never drops.
PRG-88 | No blocks or peaking for bodybuilders | Bodybuilders don't need strength/hypertrophy blocks, mesocycles, 4-week waves or peaking; the whole year is a hypertrophy block and variation lives inside the program (rotations, evolving ranges). RPE, deloads and mesocycles are powerlifting terms he excludes.
PRG-89 | Load never comes down in his programs | In his programs only tonnage rises week to week; intensity and volume are managed within sessions, with no deloads.
PRG-90 | Linear periodization lives in evolving references | Linear periodization is progressing less often via volume and intensity changes; his programs contain it through evolving references, while pure linear progression (same reps, more weight) eventually fails.
PRG-91 | Undulating intensity is for peaking | For bodybuilders, dropping to 80% intensity sacrifices for nothing; alternating intense and volume sessions outside blocks is good.
PRG-92 | Training stages last long | Each training stage lasts months to years; stay in one as long as you progress.
PRG-93 | Novice ends when linear progression stops | The novice stage ends when session-to-session linear progression on a few lifts with little volume stops and you need more exercises, days and volume, not when you reach strength standards.
PRG-94 | Moving up a stage | Move up when you want and can handle more volume or start plateauing: bodyweight → weighted, harder variations, +1 set per superset, more frequency rather than junk volume per session.
PRG-95 | Run a program as long as it progresses | Keep a program as long as it progresses; one that works for 3 months then stalls is bad.
PRG-96 | Program hopping is a plague | Program hopping chases novice neural gains, resets adaptation and costs years; switch only when progress truly stops. "Shock the muscle" and "resensitise" are nonsense; muscle stays responsive.
PRG-97 | Valid reasons to change a program | Change a program when there is no progress, no set/rep manipulation left to push volume, or the exercise selection no longer works; carry over what worked (lifts, references, supersets).
PRG-98 | Program changes are rare and small | Changes should be rare and small; many changes at once can't be assessed, and one bad day is no reason to panic-modify.
PRG-99 | Same template, rotating lifts | Advanced lifters keep the same template and rotate lifts within fixed patterns; complexity and flexibility rise as you advance, while novices stay simple.
PRG-100 | Autoregulated rotations within a line | Rotate lifts within the same line of specificity, not in arbitrary 6-week blocks and not because of a stall; start with scheduled biweekly rotations and later choose by recovery. Rotating exercises weekly to "shock" lowers specificity (2024).
PRG-101 | Isolation ranges | Isolation work progresses best in moderate-to-high ranges; never go below 6 on small muscles.
PRG-102 | Compound ranges | Compounds progress in 4-8, 6-10 or 8-12, never below 4; main lifts low, accessories medium, isolation high, accessories never low.
PRG-103 | Small muscles still need overload | Small muscles need progressive overload scaled to their size; baby weights on arm isolation are a rookie mistake, and 30-rep pump sets or the same 30 lb curls for a year don't grow arms.
PRG-104 | Prioritize, place first, overload | The #1 way to grow a muscle is to prioritize it with isolation, place it first and progressively overload it; lagging parts trained last get skipped and don't grow.
PRG-105 | Add load once bodyweight reps pass a threshold | Bodyweight reps stall and stop driving hypertrophy; once clean reps reach a threshold, add load (even 5 lb) and use a lower range.
PRG-106 | Weighted pull-up and dip prescriptions | Weighted sets need a fixed range for tracking; bodyweight sets can be AMRAP.
PRG-107 | Calisthenics progresses through variations | Calisthenics raises intensity mainly through harder variations (leverage, range, rings, diamond), with loading after mastery.
PRG-108 | Load bodyweight-capped movements | Any movement capped by bodyweight should be loaded once easy: hyperextensions, crunches, leg raises, abs work.
PRG-109 | Log every set | Log weights and reps for every set of every exercise, even curls and even if you don't track food; not counting reps is the easiest way to make no progress.
PRG-110 | Fixed rep standards | Fix ROM, tempo and technique first and keep them constant, or progress is fake; cutting ROM to add weight changes leverage, not muscle. Ugly reps mean retake the weight; tightening form is itself overload.
PRG-111 | Machines are hard to track | Too many machine sets make progression untrackable; machine users rarely add small plates, yet they should log too.
PRG-112 | Judge recovery by expected progress | Judge recovery by your expected rate of progression, not by "listen to your body"; a well-built program lets you predict lifts ~2 months out and hit the planned reps.
PRG-113 | Learn to write your own program | Learn to program and write your own; you are the subject and reviewer of your own experiment, advanced lifters especially.
PRG-114 | Alpha-test on paper, beta-test in the gym | Test a new program on paper first, then in the gym, starting slightly under-ambitious; keep an untouched copy and log what works.
PRG-115 | Build up from minimal | Add one lift or a few sets at a time until stable, and ease into new workloads.
PRG-116 | Flexibility needs regulation skill | Program flexibility goes with the ability to raise tonnage, but more flexibility demands more tracking and autoregulation skill; you can't autoregulate if you've never regulated, and instinct training is winging it.
PRG-117 | Consistency over cleverness | The best method or exercise is the one you'll stick to for a year or more; a simple method done long beats a smart one done twice.
PRG-118 | Missed sessions cost more than tonnage | Skipped sessions add up to lost progress; keep practicing lifts rather than stopping.
PRG-119 | Regaining strength is not progress | Regaining strength after time off is not progress; after a long break restart slowly with less volume. He claims "Peter Pan lifters" quit and restart to relive newbie gains.
FAT-01 | Scheduled deloads are unnecessary for bodybuilders | Scheduled deloads are unnecessary or harmful for bodybuilders; needing one means you are "digging a hole" with too much frequency, volume or strength work. A deload is an emergency response to a programming mistake and useless unless injured.
FAT-02 | Deloads exist for peaking | Deloads are for strength athletes who peak; bodybuilders never peak, so only the recovery half could matter. The term spread with the internet and powerlifting and is absent from earlier bodybuilding eras.
FAT-03 | Trim the program instead of deloading | Reduce workload yourself before a deload would be needed; doing slightly less means you never need a week off and lose less tonnage than a deload costs.
FAT-04 | A week off is detraining | A week off, or 3-4 days fully off, is detraining, not deloading; you can only deload from a load. Time off is for injury or when away from the gym.
FAT-05 | Time off costs performance, not muscle | Size regresses slowly but neural adaptations detrain fast, so time off costs performance and work capacity; "you shrink during a deload" is an exaggeration.
FAT-06 | Deload fatigue clears in days | Fatigue clears in about 3 days of a deload; for the rest of the week nothing grows, and repeated deload weeks add up to a lot of lost work over years.
FAT-07 | Coming back stronger proves nothing | PRs after rest are expected and only show full performance, or that you were under-recovered; bodybuilding is about maintaining performance, not PRs. Strength may even rise after a break while volume capacity falls.
FAT-08 | Deload weeks backfire behaviourally | Knowing a deload is coming makes people overzealous beforehand; classic mesocycles pile low-intensity volume and then overshoot into junk volume, and deloads stop lifters learning day-to-day fatigue management.
FAT-09 | Deloads produce confirmation bias | Lifters credit the deload for preventing CNS fatigue that never came, like sacrificing a chicken nightly to keep away a wolf that never existed.
FAT-10 | No resensitisation breaks | Mesocycles, low-volume phases, resensitisation breaks and "mTOR resets" are unnecessary; muscle is never desensitised, a plateaued muscle needs more work, and a good program runs indefinitely.
FAT-11 | If deloads help you, keep them | He is tolerant: some top naturals grow with deloads, and if they help you keep them; if they feel wasted, skip them but manage fatigue another way.
FAT-12 | If you must deload, keep the usual lifts | The better deload is your usual lifts at low intensity and low volume, keeping blood flow and movement patterns.
FAT-13 | The deload is built into every week | Evolving reps and sets plus rotations vary volume, intensity and frequency slightly while tonnage stays stable; small dips from rep ranges are the only variation he allows. He opposes blast/cruise, polarized volume/intensity days and clear hard/easy weeks.
FAT-14 | Micro-deloads by muscle, on the day | Picture each body part flashing red (needs work) or green (needs recovery); reduce a muscle that needs rest or rotate it out, and train a fresh muscle even if a rotation planned rest, so every muscle stays continuously stimulated.
FAT-15 | Volume, intensity and frequency manage fatigue | Volume, intensity and frequency are one thing: tools to manage fatigue. Any program is a block of fatigue, so get the most from the least.
FAT-16 | Autoregulation needs built-in mechanisms | Autoregulation needs a menu of rotation options built into the program and suits advanced lifters; small recovery shortfalls can accumulate, so novices should work toward it rather than start with it.
FAT-17 | Training dictates recovery | Set the optimal work first, then see what recovery it needs, not a fixed 5+1 week block; bodybuilders let training dictate recovery, strength athletes the opposite.
FAT-18 | Pre-plan easier swaps for bad days | Have easier options ready for bad days and hard weeks; keeping the secondary movement preserves tonnage better than keeping strength work.
FAT-19 | Longer cycles drop the heaviest lifts periodically | Running an 8-day program over 6-day weeks makes heavy back squats or conventional deadlifts drop out every few weeks, resting body and mind; the next exposure gives PRs.
FAT-20 | No stimulus without fatigue | Stimulus and fatigue are the same thing seen two ways; if you're not fatigued you didn't push enough. Target the maximum fatigue you can recover from, and don't demonize fatigue.
FAT-21 | Accumulate fatigue, don't flush it | Fatigue decays fast and fitness slowly, so a pure flush risks fitness; performance exists to let you accumulate fatigue.
FAT-22 | Stimulus-to-fatigue ratio is overrated | SFR is an excuse to avoid heavy free-weight compounds; compounds give more of both stimulus and fatigue and are only worse if excess fatigue outruns excess stimulus.
FAT-23 | The deadlift's "bad SFR" is programming | The deadlift easily overshoots recovery through too much volume or poor form, so undershoot slightly ("fill the glass without overflowing").
FAT-24 | SFR matters where fatigue is low-value | SFR does matter where fatigue lands on tendons and stabilizers rather than the target: machines rest them, squats/deadlifts for abs are terrible SFR, heavy deadlift singles aren't optimal, and chasing full ROM can add needless fatigue. It matters most once many small muscles are isolated.
FAT-25 | Fatigue scales with load, reps and muscle size | Fatigue is roughly reps × weight, modulated by the lift; adding or removing work for big muscle groups matters more than for small ones.
FAT-26 | Name the type of fatigue | Distinguish muscular, cardiovascular, mechanical, tendon, CNS and mental fatigue; many "fatigue-seeking" methods really target cardio. Muscular fatigue is lost fiber force, rebuilt bigger with food and rest.
FAT-27 | CNS fatigue barely matters for bodybuilders | Most people can't reach CNS burnout; supposed burnout is usually injury or mismanaged volume and intensity, and you don't need rest days for the CNS.
FAT-28 | Strength loss blamed on CNS is usually life | If your muscles can move a load, it can't fry your CNS; strength loss blamed on CNS is usually a calorie deficit, poor sleep, stress or a bad day.
FAT-29 | The CNS is trainable | Undertraining the CNS out of fear limits gains and future work capacity; bodybuilders should grow it to serve the muscles rather than plan around it, and a slightly under-recovered CNS costs little since you don't test 1RMs.
FAT-30 | Sessions still tax the whole system | Each session still fatigues the nervous system and squats tax it most; squat and bench share CNS and stamina, so the system rather than the legs can limit when you train again. Upper and lower "trees" interfere little.
FAT-31 | Daily low-volume training still accumulates | Even very low daily set counts don't fully recover in 24 h because tendon and nervous fatigue accumulate; Bulgarian-style daily training fails for bodybuilding.
FAT-32 | Leave the gym with energy left | Being wrecked is not a goal; being wiped every session means working dumb, and an arm day should leave you energized.
FAT-33 | Stop when sets turn into junk volume | End the session when fatigue pushes sets out of the useful window; if late work at under half your fresh capacity feels hard, it is junk, and a drained last part of a long session means shorten it and raise intensity.
FAT-34 | A good session is repeatable | A good session can be repeated within days and for years; giving everything one day and being out for seven loses to managed daily training, and holding back to survive tomorrow is a scheduling problem.
FAT-35 | Lost reps are the early warning | Losing reps at the same weight signals under-recovery: do less volume that day. For bodybuilders bar weight should never go backward; only volume dips temporarily.
FAT-36 | Fatigue carries across exercises | Fatigue carries across exercises, even to unrelated muscles, so put the least important lifts last; on full body vary the order, since always skipping the last quarter undertrains a quarter of the body.
FAT-37 | Order: explosive, heavy, then hypertrophy | Order a day explosive → heavy → hypertrophy, so earlier lifts prime later ones; giant sets run in descending intensity. Never open with a 1RM, which wrecks the rest of the day.
FAT-38 | Cardio fitness limits sessions | Cardiovascular fatigue limits sessions; supersets with a lower-body compound or whole-workout giant sets sabotage performance. To stop fading before failure, build work capacity and eat before training.
FAT-39 | Warm-up sets count | Warm-up sets count toward fatigue, so they belong in planning.
FAT-40 | Squats tax him more than deadlifts | He finds high-bar squats far more taxing than deadlifts, contrary to "experts"; heavy knee flexion wrecks his recovery while frequent heavy hinges don't. He can deadlift heavy and squat two days later, but not the reverse.
FAT-41 | Never heavy squat and heavy deadlift together | Never do heavy squats and heavy deadlifts in the same session; a heavy squat with a light hinge variation is fine. Lifts on the same "tree" share fatigue, so after a brutal squat day adapt the deadlift range or variation, and two heavy hinges are counterproductive.
FAT-42 | Lower-back fatigue is the limiter | Lower-back fatigue limits both legs and upper back; manage it to train legs hard and often, and load the upper back without the legs (DB rows, pullovers, chest-supported rows), using hinge styles that reduce axial load.
FAT-43 | No rows right after heavy pulls | Don't put heavy rows right after squats or deadlifts, in the session or the next day; put heavy rows on an upper day.
FAT-44 | Cap deadlift and squat volume | Heavy pulling volume adds up fast: most people should use a deadlift variation for back-offs, never stack deadlifts, back-offs and rack pulls in one day, and choose back-friendly lifts after the heavy pull.
FAT-45 | Lighter knee flexion after a heavy pull | If a heavy pull was 1-2 days earlier, make the next knee-flexion session lighter.
FAT-46 | Don't fight for extra deadlift reps | Grinding 2-3 extra heavy deadlift reps can cost the next hinge session in tonnage and adds injury risk; "stimulate, don't annihilate" joints and tendons.
FAT-47 | Machine squats allow more sets | Belt, hack and leg-press squats cause less systemic and spinal fatigue than barbell squats, so they allow more sets.
FAT-48 | Don't trash yourself on lower days | Keep reps conservative on lower days; the last lower day may leave you beat up but without back pain. Babying the lower back is justified only if you need it tomorrow.
FAT-49 | An arm day is a sneaky recovery day | An arm or isolation day barely taxes the CNS, its blood flow aids recovery from compounds, and it can replace a rest day; full rest days should become rare as you advance.
FAT-50 | Light pump sets aid recovery | Light pump work days after a heavy session aids recovery.
FAT-51 | Shoulders serve both pressing planes | One set of shoulders serves horizontal and vertical pressing, so manage the overlap: keep bench volume low on a heavy OHP day and skip vertical presses when shoulders are fried.
FAT-52 | Nucleus overload must stay cheap | Nucleus overload stays cheap because it is not made progressively harder; take a bigger share of recovery and hard sets suffer, and small costs add up over a year at high frequency.
FAT-53 | Volume above what you can recover from is wasted | Volume above maximum recoverable volume is wasted, like a surplus that only adds fat; needing 10 low-intensity days to recover shows an unbalanced program.
FAT-54 | More days raise fatigue | Adding days raises both volume and frequency; if 6 days feels wrong, fit the same volume into 4.
FAT-55 | Fatigue peaks then flushes over the week | Full body 3×/week builds fatigue to a Friday peak that is flushed over the weekend; his programs include days off for that reason.
FAT-56 | Injuries come from accumulated fatigue | Track past fatigue to prevent injury; injuries come from accumulated fatigue, not that day's work, and perfect form with poorly managed volume still leads to injury.
FAT-57 | After a break, restart at a third | After a break restart at about a third of usual tonnage; muscle returns fast, and old loads straight away cause injuries.
FAT-58 | Overtraining is real but program-relative | Overtraining is individual and relative to the program; train just below its edge, where you still recover but loads become irrelevant past it. The more advanced you are, the less overtraining is possible.
FAT-59 | Overtraining is really under-recovering | Within-session overtraining is nearly impossible, but you can come back under-rested; skinny, weak under-eaters overtrain easily and grow best on low volume, high intensity and long recovery.
FAT-60 | Many who think they overtrain are right | Many who think they overtrain really do, because they answer regression with more volume; burnout usually means too much in the gym and too little outside it, so lanky high-metabolism lifters should cut volume and raise calories.
FAT-61 | No progress = too little or too much stimulus | Lack of progression means too little stimulus or too much; Mentzer's fix of more rest days until training every 5 days makes under-stimulated lifters regress.
FAT-62 | Mental fatigue is part of recovery | Mental fatigue is an ignored part of recovery and mental stamina is usually lower than physical; feared or repeatedly failed lifts spike stress, confident lifts barely do. Stop when your mind checks out and train again tomorrow; for advanced lifters the brain can become the limiter.
FAT-63 | Strength work that burns you out: adjust it | If strength work burns you out mentally or in the tendons, lower its intensity or modify the sets.
FAT-64 | Manual workers train hard but rarely | Manual workers should train intensely but rarely and with low volume, never impairing the ability to work; construction work acts like low-intensity, high-volume, high-frequency training.
FAT-65 | Sport and lifting share one recovery pot | Sport practice and lifting draw on the same recovery; add a sport gradually, avoid heavy lifting the day before practice or competition, and cut lifting if sparring degrades. Doing three lifting and three martial-arts sessions is chasing two rabbits.
FAT-66 | Weighted abs not before heavy lower days | Weighted ab work impairs core rigidity, so don't do it the day before heavy lower work; do it at the end of leg days.
