SLP-01 | Sleep is the most underrated part of recovery | Sleep is as important as diet or more; perfect diet, training and low stress mean nothing without it, and your ability to work harder is limited by your ability to recover. He avoids caffeine to protect it.
SLP-02 | Most adults need 7-8 hours | Most adults need 7-8 h; the need is largely genetic and falls with age, and the 10-12 h of elite athletes is unrealistic for working people.
SLP-03 | Skipping sleep is quitting the workout halfway | Sleep what you need; cutting an hour to be productive backfires. Between sleep and entertainment, always sleep.
SLP-04 | Hypertrophy training raises sleep need | Hypertrophy training raises sleep need because the nervous system needs more rest.
SLP-05 | You can still grow on poor sleep | You can still gain on sub-par sleep, but sleep matters for mood, hormones and disease risk.
SLP-06 | Quality matters as much as quantity | A short optimized night can beat a long poor one; oversleeping can leave you sluggish with no added recovery.
SLP-07 | Naps are highly anabolic | "Nothing is more anabolic than a nap."
SLP-08 | Early wake-up culture is pointless | Total sleep, volume and tonnage matter, not the clock; wake when your job and biology need you, not to copy celebrities (a "dick-measuring contest").
SLP-09 | Bed before midnight, not for growth hormone | Go to bed before midnight because late-night internet steals sleep; he doubts that sleeping after midnight costs growth hormone and thinks any early-bedtime benefit is minimal.
SLP-10 | Train at your best time | Train when you're strongest, though people adapt to any time.
SLP-11 | Not right before bed; night shifts train before work | Training right before bed revs you up too much; night-shift workers should train before their shift, which also wakes them up.
SLP-12 | Cool the body before bed | Take a cold shower until cold to the bone or slightly shivering, then sleep in a cool room with the window open, especially in summer.
SLP-13 | No screens before bed | Ban doomscrolling before bed and read instead.
SLP-14 | The bedroom is for sleep | Keep the bedroom sacred: no phone, TV, laptop or pets if possible, and reserve lying down for sleep so it becomes a sleep trigger.
SLP-15 | Find your need without an alarm | Find your sleep need by sleeping without alarm, light or noise, and wake without an alarm when possible; don't obsess over cycle lengths, go to bed as early as you can and focus on quality.
SLP-16 | Insomnia is often psychological | Fear of not sleeping feeds insomnia like a panic attack; don't fight it with pills, let the mind drift, and with poor sleep accept it and organise training around reduced recovery. Tired mornings lead to caffeine abuse and worse sleep.
SLP-17 | Cut caffeine and toxins | Control caffeine, alcohol and drugs; the health basics are sleep, no toxins, resistance training and moderately low body fat, while thyroid hacks, infrared lights and enemas are bogus.
SLP-18 | If a deficit hurts sleep, eat late | If dieting hurts your sleep, fast in the day and eat a big meal before bed; sleep matters more than hunger.
SLP-19 | Avoid crushing an arm in your sleep | Avoid side-sleeping that crushes an arm or shoulder, since pinched nerves can eventually hinder growth; numb arms on waking are harmless if sensation returns. Change position or pillow and use band nerve-decompression drills.
SLP-20 | Short on sleep: sleep instead of training | If kept up very late, sleep instead of training; forced low-energy sessions gave him bad workouts or injuries. Say no to social pressure that wrecks sleep.
SLP-21 | Slightly unwell: train, but not heavy | When slightly unwell, hungry or underslept, still train but avoid heavy work; real flu may be the exception. He claims no cold or flu in ~10 years of training and that training makes the sick feeling go away.
SLP-22 | A muscle needs ~48-72 hours | A muscle needs about 48 h, up to 72 h, and even a fully trashed muscle is fresh in 4-5 days; no muscle needs a full week off. Avoid the same isolation lift on consecutive days.
SLP-23 | You don't need 100% recovery | You can retrain before full recovery; a muscle can only be restored to 100%, never beyond, so waiting for or emptying it fully gains nothing, and waiting for 100% pushes people to bro splits. Training early adds fatigue and lengthens the next recovery, a problem only if constant.
SLP-24 | Constantly training under-recovered steals gains | Training the same thing before recovery again and again means training sore, underperforming and growing slower; showing up under-recovered wastes training and risks injury.
SLP-25 | Bigger muscles cost more recovery | Bigger muscle groups and the lower body cost more recovery and volume sets recovery time; small muscles recover faster, so the 85% rule (PRG-48) must be applied more strictly to big ones.
SLP-26 | Hard lower-body work now needs 72 h+ | Lower-body recovery slows as you get stronger; he now needs ~72 h or more after hard lower work and finds lower body 3×/week too much, so lower reps may suit very taxing lifts. Needing ~10 days before deadlifting again means poor recovery and work capacity.
SLP-27 | Upper back recovers very fast | The upper back is small muscles with a big work capacity and recovers fast; pull-ups done with the back allow daily frequency.
SLP-28 | Recovery figures for other muscles | Muscles differ: chest needs a few days, biceps recover in about 3 days, neck is fast, triceps are slow especially if untrained, lats are sore but always ready, and shoulder sessions should be spread.
SLP-29 | Recovery speed is individual | Recovery speed varies per muscle and per person; recovery "pots" drain at different rates.
SLP-30 | The recovery pot model | Training damage drains a recovery pot whose size is your maximum recoverable volume; keep it relatively full and never empty outside a powerlifting peak. Each muscle and tendon has a pot, pots feed each other (legs also drain the lower-back pot), and time spent with the pot low wastes gains.
SLP-31 | Spread volume rather than empty the tank | Several partial drains a week give more quality work than one crash to zero; junk volume is lots of fatigue for little stimulus.
SLP-32 | The CNS bucket holds the muscle buckets | The big CNS bucket spends more than any muscle but refills no faster, so leave water in the tank; it keeps refilling while you train other muscles, and its capacity is large and trainable.
SLP-33 | Most lifters over-allocate rest | Most lifters overestimate their damage and over-allocate rest, so they over-recover and miss stimulus windows; "gains happen when you rest" is a platitude, since gains are triggered in training and materialize in recovery.
SLP-34 | Recovery is where growth happens | Growth is actualized in recovery (supercompensation), so training and recovery are equally important; if training is too dense, recovery can matter more than training.
SLP-35 | A rest day is programmed | A rest day is planned and as important as a training day; an impromptu skip is not a rest day. Let progression, recovery, tendons and psychology set how many you need.
SLP-36 | Taxing days go before rest days | Put the most taxing (lower-body) days right before rest days or the weekend, giving time to eat and sleep.
SLP-37 | Add a rest day if 7 days is too much | If training 7 days is too much, add a rest day after day 5; it won't kill gains. Mind what the last day does to the first day of the repeating week.
SLP-38 | Active recovery: early yes, later mostly no | He now holds that recovery is just rest: constant active recovery keeps you fatigued, passive couch rest is important, and light walking or mowing is fine.
SLP-39 | Optional Sunday session (2023) | His 2023 program had an optional Sunday of low-fatigue work plus outdoor activity.
SLP-40 | Off days leave the upper body alone | On off days leave chest, shoulders and upper back alone, for mental and physical recovery.
SLP-41 | His recovery recipe | Optimal recovery is less frequency, less junk volume, better nutrition and lots of passive rest: eating, sleeping and napping, not cold plunges or runs in the rain.
SLP-42 | Soreness is not a sign of growth | Soreness isn't a growth signal; strength and tonnage over time are. Constant heavy soreness signals poor adaptation, new programs make you sore from adapting, and trembling or flu-like symptoms aren't growth signs.
SLP-43 | Soreness should fade with training | Soreness should fade with training age and frequency; still being routinely sore after 5 years means program hopping, and once-weekly muscles stay unconditioned. Once you reach no-soreness, soreness becomes meaningful.
SLP-44 | Feel is an unreliable recovery gauge | Touch-soreness and feel are unreliable recovery gauges; soreness can last a week, and the 48-hour rule is an alternative.
SLP-45 | Exception: a lift that keeps you sore | A lift that keeps you sore for years, like his RDLs, is a good workout.
SLP-46 | Chronic stress impairs recovery | Chronic stress and mental illness impair recovery and raise injury rates; work stress ruins focus, weight selection and sleep, and lifters who often need deloads may be overtaxed by life.
SLP-47 | Office jobs recover well, physical jobs add volume | Office jobs give excellent recovery windows as long as you eat enough; blue-collar jobs add volume that must be managed.
SLP-48 | Plateau checklist includes recovery | When stuck, check recovery (eat and sleep more), then the rep range or jumps, then psychology.
SLP-49 | Age lowers recovery but doesn't force less volume | Age lowers recovery but doesn't force dropping volume; older lifters manage frequency and volume carefully, and you lose muscle by stopping training and diet, not by turning 40.
SLP-50 | Raising testosterone naturally | Testosterone rises naturally with a clean diet, lots of sleep, low stress and years of rigorous training; any physical activity helps hormones.
SLP-51 | Cold showers: harmless, not a revolution | Cold showers don't hurt gains and are probably healthy, but won't revolutionise recovery; they chill only the surface, so he doubts protein-synthesis blunting. He suspects (unverified) a testosterone or capillary benefit and hasn't tried ice baths.
SLP-52 | Post-training contrast showers | After training a short cold blast while warm, and hot/cold on the forearms, boosts blood flow; only once used to very cold showers, and people with weak hearts should be cautious.
SLP-53 | Cold exposure and immunity | He credits nutrition, water, sleep and not hiding from the cold for his immunity; adapt with cold showers and going out lightly dressed.
SLP-54 | Daily sun on the skin | Get sun on your skin daily; he walks shirtless on breaks.
SLP-55 | Sauna: probably healthy, growth effect unknown | Sauna is likely good for health, its effect on growth is unknown, and you should watch your heart.
SLP-56 | Light hand work for blood flow | Blood flow around joints speeds recovery, so light hand work helps: rice bucket B tier, daily foam rolling OK if not too hard. Band finger extensions gave him knuckle tendinitis.
EXS-01 | Compounds plus isolation, neither alone | A hypertrophy program is compounds for most of the tonnage plus isolation for whatever the compounds miss. Compounds are the "shotgun", isolation the "sniper", and neither works alone.
EXS-02 | Isolation is as serious as compounds | Isolation gets the same priority and effort as compounds, not a token "3 sets of curls" tacked on at the end where it gets skipped. It taxes the nervous system less, so be generous with it.
EXS-03 | Naturals skew toward compounds | A program that is mostly isolation is how enhanced pros train; for naturals he calls it "garbage". Naturals should lean on compounds.
EXS-04 | Compound-only training leaves known gaps | Big-three or compound-only programs (he names BBB, Starting Strength, GreySkull) leave the long-head triceps, biceps, forearms, rear/side delts, calves, neck, abs and upper back behind. Adding rows and weighted pull-ups still misses them.
EXS-05 | The dominant muscle takes the work | In a compound the strongest muscle does most of the work and leverages decide activation, so some "targeted" muscles barely grow. More total load is not more load on one muscle, and the bigger a prime mover gets the more it steals from small muscles, so skipping isolation early creates deficits that are hard to fix.
EXS-06 | Which muscles need isolation | Quads, glutes and chest are prime movers in heavy compounds and need little isolation; most other muscles need it, arms more than legs. Hamstrings often stay small in strong naturals despite hinging.
EXS-07 | True isolation is a myth, prioritisation is real | No movement isolates one muscle, but each prioritises some muscles, and that is what makes programming possible.
EXS-08 | A compound is any big multi-joint lift | Any multi-joint lift that recruits a lot of muscle (dips, push-ups) is a compound. The big three are a powerlifting construct and not mandatory for bodybuilders.
EXS-09 | Cover every movement pattern | The bare minimum is a "big ten" that covers every body part. The size patterns are vertical press, horizontal press, knee flexion, hip hinge, horizontal pull and vertical pull. Check each body part when choosing.
EXS-10 | Minimalism hurts the physique, except legs | Minimalist programs cut exercises "like cost-cutting consultants" and are the enemy of a complete physique. Leg minimalism (one knee flexion plus one hinge) is fine.
EXS-11 | Isolation is not for "definition" | "Isolation for definition, compounds for size" is bro science; striations come from being lean.
EXS-12 | Athletes train only purposeful muscle | Athletes and fighters should train only muscle their sport uses, cut most isolation, pick lifts that reproduce the sport's joint angles, and never load the sport movement itself (dumbbell uppercuts, banded punches).
EXS-13 | Keep the exercise list short | Variety comes from what you do with a few lifts, not from many lifts. Too many exercises means each is trained too rarely, technique and tonnage become unstable, and you become "a technician of new tricks rather than a master".
EXS-14 | Exercise and variation counts by level | The number of exercises per muscle and variations per lift grows with training age but stays small.
EXS-15 | Hard vs soft variations | A soft variation keeps the pattern and changes the implement, grip, pause or rep range (near-full carryover, unlimited). A hard variation is a different pattern (pull-up vs pulldown, squat vs leg press), is a new skill, and should be capped. Rotate soft variations to prevent overuse without changing the pattern.
EXS-16 | Change a lift only for a reason | Add or swap a variation only for a reason: a stall despite changing rep ranges, technique, overuse or injury. Never for boredom-driven novelty or to "shock the muscle" (he rates that D tier); constant switching keeps you relearning.
EXS-17 | Rotation options must hit the muscle the same way | Rotations alternate close variations in one slot (week A / week B). Good pairs: Kroc/Meadows rows, RDL/stiff-leg, V-bar/rope pushdowns, bench/close-grip, dips/pause dips. Bad pairs: bench vs OHP, floor press vs paused bench, deadlift vs RDL, close-grip vs skull crusher, sumo vs conventional deficit. An "or" in his programs means pick one or rotate weekly, never both in one session.
EXS-18 | Train a lift at least every 10-12 days | A lift practised less often than every 10-12 days doesn't belong in the program; heavy rotation that drops a lift to once a month is too rare. Big compounds should stay one variation; isolation can rotate more freely.
EXS-19 | When to rotate: scheduled or by need | He has said both that rotations must be scheduled and that they should only happen "when the muscle needs it". The 2023 version (scheduled for most, autoregulated for advanced) may reconcile them.
EXS-20 | Don't drop a lift because it plateaus | Rotating a lift out at every plateau teaches you to quit and resets you to beginner neural gains; grind through instead. Rotating back exercises every two months means never breaking through.
EXS-21 | Rotations fight overuse and accommodation | Variations and rotations are a tool against overuse injury and accommodation, and give mental freshness. They are his main day-to-day volume-control tool (rated B tier); changing volume and intensity can also get around accommodation.
EXS-22 | His own roster barely changes | He keeps the same lifts for years.
EXS-23 | No lift is mandatory | For a bodybuilder every lift, including squat, bench and deadlift, can be replaced by another in the same pattern. If a lift hurts after a fair try, plenty of others do the job.
EXS-24 | Fit the lifts to your build | Leverages, height and mobility decide which lifts suit you; the program adapts to the lifter. Drop lifts that don't suit you instead of clinging to them (sunk cost).
EXS-25 | Enjoyment beats theoretical optimality | A lift that is 75% optimal and that you actually do beats a 100% optimal lift you skip. Remove exercises you dislike; advanced lifters especially should refuse them.
EXS-26 | Judge a new lift after about three months | Start a new lift light and judge it only after training it seriously for about three months, by progress rather than feel; allow a tuning phase. Only program lifts you have done regularly for 3+ months. Keep a ranked list of lifts done, a "to try" list and an "exercise graveyard".
EXS-27 | What makes a good hypertrophy lift | His criteria: stretch under load, high stability, good stimulus-to-fatigue (judged per target muscle), low cardio cost, quick setup, and progressable. A lift without a stretch can't rank above "decent"; if form breaks as soon as you load it, it's useless for hypertrophy.
EXS-28 | Prefer lifts with a negative | Most lifts should have a controlled eccentric. Positive-only or explosive lifts (Olympic lifts, Pendlay rows, dumped deadlifts) are weaker for hypertrophy; safely loading a lengthened muscle gives the best gains.
EXS-29 | Feel and soreness are quick checks only | Mind-muscle connection and soreness are quick ways to see what a lift hits, but long-term progress decides.
EXS-30 | Staples over gimmicks | Prefer time-tested lifts over influencer variants and combo lifts (hip-thrust-to-floor-press, Cuban press, king squat, Bradford press). One load can't suit two patterns; boring stable lifts beat flashy ones. Intermediates are prime targets for gimmicks.
EXS-31 | Technicality-to-hypertrophy ratio | Avoid highly technical lifts (full Olympic lifts, advanced ring skills, power cleans for novices) or include them only sparingly. Use block/hang/partial versions, or split the lift into its parts, which he estimates is about 5x better for hypertrophy.
EXS-32 | Olympic lifts rank low for size | Olympic lifts are near the bottom for hypertrophy (technical, no eccentric); use hang, block or power versions, or split them into pull, front squat and press. Power cleans have no place in novice programs.
EXS-33 | Branch across patterns | Spread work across movement patterns instead of piling variations of one lift; pair antagonists or unrelated muscles. Bench-only pressing invites plateaus and shoulder trouble.
EXS-34 | Don't be dogmatic about tools | Don't play favourites (barbell-only, big-three-only, machine-only). Bodybuilders compete on a stage, not with implements, so use every tool.
EXS-35 | Copying feats won't copy a physique | Doing an athlete's signature feats won't give you his physique; borrow only what builds muscle. Gymnasts' arms come from 10+ years at 5-6 hours a day, and 15 pull-ups don't make that back.
EXS-36 | "Functional training" is mostly noise | Every resistance exercise is functional for something; curls are functional for hypertrophy. Most "functional training" talk is nonsense.
EXS-37 | How much exercise selection matters | He has called exercise selection both paramount and a by-product of programming.
EXS-38 | His roster drifted to stretch-heavy, stable lifts | His own favourites moved from the barbell big lifts to lifts with a better stretch and stimulus-to-fatigue: RDL over deadlift, weighted dips and ring push-ups over bench, heel-elevated/SSB/belt/split squats over back squats, a Smith incline press over the military press. Per-muscle picks are in ch. 6, the big lifts' role in ch. 7.
EXS-39 | The specificity tree, later downgraded | Early on he organised lifts as a tree: branches are movement patterns, each topped by a king lift that lower lifts must feed, and no lift without close neighbours. Later he rated specificity C tier and "not important for bodybuilding"; closeness is judged by the muscle trained, not carryover.
EXS-40 | Choose exercises before anything else | Build the exercise list before choosing the template, volume, intensity and frequency. "If the exercise selection is rotten, the whole program stinks."
EXS-41 | Open with the heaviest compound | Always open with the heaviest, most demanding compound or strength lift, never with isolation (laterals, calf raises, leg extensions, leg curls, pulldowns, shrugs). You are freshest at the start, and the last exercises get skipped, so order by importance.
EXS-42 | Prioritise a weak point with a compound | Opening with isolation for a weak point is pointless; open with a compound variation that targets it instead (e.g. incline press for upper chest).
EXS-43 | Upper days long, lower days short | Upper days are a "laundry list" and lower days a "Post-it", because lower-body lifts bring more volume and systemic fatigue per exercise.
EXS-44 | Upper days: horizontal and vertical press plus a row | An upper day needs a horizontal press and a vertical press, plus a horizontal row; he calls it the "winning combo".
EXS-45 | Don't stack similar heavy compounds | Don't put similar heavy compounds back to back or superset two energy-expensive compounds (e.g. squat + snatch-grip deadlift, deadlift + hack squat, RDL + chin-ups). Bi-sets at most; a deadlift goes first, never fourth.
EXS-46 | No two versions of the same lift in a day | Two near-identical lifts in one session are redundant (bench + dips, ring dips + ring push-ups, leg press + hack squat, RDL + good morning, two pushdowns); pick one and rotate.
EXS-47 | Know the purpose of each exercise | Plan the day by listing body parts, then the lifts; every exercise should have a known purpose, like a recipe.
EXS-48 | Supersets: different muscles, not the same one | Superset different or antagonist muscles (safe for beginners). Three or four exercises in a row for the same muscle is the "cardinal sin" unless done on purpose; side raises plus rear flies fatigue each other. In giant sets go from heaviest to higher reps.
EXS-49 | Don't pre-exhaust the target | Pre-exhaustion lowers the target's force output: pec deck before pressing is a mistake, and hamstrings before deadlifts shifts load to the lower back. Light band flies for a pump are fine; lats before barbell rows is more defensible. Pre-fatigue is for advanced lifters only, since earlier it masks poor technique.
EXS-50 | Free weights stay the base for naturals | Free weights should be most of a natural's training; machines and cables complement them. Machine-only lifters stay small, never learn fatigue management, and their tendons don't adapt.
EXS-51 | Machines, cables and Smith became legitimate | Machines, cables and the Smith machine are good for stable, hard, joint-friendly sets and extra volume, because they offload stability. Most people misuse them with too much weight and too little range; don't go low-rep on machines.
EXS-52 | Stability is a means, not the goal | Unstable lifts are fine if done consistently, but most top hypertrophy lifts happen to be stable.
EXS-53 | Calisthenics for the upper body, weights for the legs | Calisthenics are great for the upper body and poor for legs; skills (planche, levers, muscle-ups, one-arm moves) build skill, not muscle. Best bodyweight moves: pull-up, inverted row, push-up, leg raise, rings.
EXS-54 | Unilateral work went from disliked to A tier | Unilateral lifts are worthwhile; by 2023-24 he rates them A tier and split squats are his favourite leg lift.
EXS-55 | Bands are support tools | Bands are good for joints, pump, recovery and finishers but unnecessary as main hypertrophy work.
EXS-56 | Carries and strongman implements are a small part | Carries, sleds, yokes, Atlas stones and sandbags are a small part of his training and not very effective for hypertrophy, except farmer's carries for traps and forearms.
TEC-01 | Bodybuilding form: target, pain-free, reproducible | Good bodybuilding form targets the intended muscle, is pain-free and is reproducible every rep; how much you lift is not a criterion. It may mean lifting less at first, but it recovers better and correlates with long-term progress.
TEC-02 | Execution is half the result | Execution is about half of results and programming the other half; a great program executed badly won't deliver. Form is "mandatory" to lift for years without setbacks.
TEC-03 | Technique sits between load and range | Technique is S tier because it directs stress to the muscle, but it is individual. His 2024 order: load as the precondition, then technique, range of motion and tempo; time under tension is just a by-product of tempo.
TEC-04 | Pick the version that works the muscle hardest | Choose the variation or leverage that makes the target work hardest, not the one that makes you strongest; "challenge the muscle with as little weight as possible". For hypertrophy pick the technique that piles up the most tonnage safely over time, even if its 1RM is lower.
TEC-05 | Strength form vs bodybuilding form | Powerlifting technique minimises range and maximises explosiveness (arch, low bar, sumo), which puts optimal strength form and optimal bodybuilding form at opposite ends. Earlier he said there is "only form".
TEC-06 | No universal form, but universal faults | There is no single perfect form, but some faults are always wrong: lumbar rounding on deadlifts, flaring elbows on bench, knee valgus on squats. Deadlift hip height, upper-back rounding, knee travel, bench touch point and elbow tuck depend on the lifter. Leverages are no excuse for bad form.
TEC-07 | Neither form police nor "form doesn't matter" | Both extremes are wrong: the "form police" who demand one way, and those who say technique doesn't matter. Rules exist but are flexible guidelines; form policing comes from powerlifting judging and is valid only in competition.
TEC-08 | Beginners get rigid rules; exotic form is earned | Beginners need strict good-vs-bad rules first. Exotic form (rounding the upper back, Arnold-style body English) is earned, and a strong lifter's sloppy form never justifies it for novices.
TEC-09 | Beginners chase technique, not weight | Beginners focus on technique; if technique regresses as the weight rises, you are on a dangerous path. Until a lift is mastered, progressive overload trains learning rather than muscle.
TEC-10 | Warm-up reps and top-set reps look the same | Poor form is a choice: if your warm-ups are clean, your top sets can be too. Warm-up reps and top-set reps must look identical.
TEC-11 | Good form isn't pretty form | Proper bodybuilding form is not the same as pretty form, and visual cues can't judge danger. Lumbar rounding on deadlifts is a symptom (hips shooting up, lost thoracic extension), not the root cause.
TEC-12 | Check your own form | Film your lifts about once a month and be honest; his own deadlift drift only showed on video. A technique you feel rock-solid in is likely your strongest, and technique should be verifiable by eye and by how the body feels afterwards.
TEC-13 | Every technique must be justifiable | Every lift, rep range and frequency should be justifiable; bodybuilding is experimentation, but any technique has to be both effective and safe.
TEC-14 | The last rep looks like the first | Bodybuilders own technique from the first rep to the last: the 10th rep looks as crisp as the first, only slower. Near failure only speed changes, never form. Form breakdown is acceptable in strength sport, not for size.
TEC-15 | Avoid mechanical failure | Mechanical failure (your structure gives out before the muscle) is bad mainly because tonnage stops going to the target. You want to be limited by the muscle, never by structure. It comes from fixable weak links: thoracic extension, setup, technique (e.g. good-morning squats, hips shooting up on deadlifts).
TEC-16 | Artificial vs organic mechanical failure | "Artificial" mechanical failure (bad technique or bar placement) is correctable. "Organic" mechanical failure is a deliberately weak position (e.g. a crane-style stiff-leg with the bar near the toes) used to fail the target sooner.
TEC-17 | Weight selection makes or breaks form | Structural integrity comes mostly from weight selection: perfect form at 400 lb can fall apart at 450. Good technique won't save a bad loading decision, and "there is no bad form" ignores this.
TEC-18 | Stop when form starts to break | If you sense form breaking, stop and put the bar down. If a squat rep would turn into a good morning or the hips shoot up, rack it and keep that rep in reserve for a later exercise.
TEC-19 | Ego lifting defined | Ego lifting is using more weight than the exercise can turn into a justified stimulus; cutting range to lift more counts. "If 10 lb dumbbells challenge you, use them." Accusing others of ego lifting often reveals insecurity.
TEC-20 | A horsed rep is worth less weight | A heaved rep gives roughly the stimulus of a deliberate rep with about 30 lb less; you could cut load ~30% and lift slower for similar hypertrophy (not strength).
TEC-21 | Rep ranges follow how form holds up | Lifts whose form breaks under heavy loads get higher reps from the start; low reps only where you can grind safely. Isolation needs perfect form and mind-muscle connection, so keep it in higher reps.
TEC-22 | Respect the "red zone" | Every movement has a red zone, the disadvantaged range that stimulates the muscle most and carries the most risk. Enter it with caution; don't avoid it. Breakdown usually happens at the turn from negative to positive.
TEC-23 | Don't rush through the bottom | Bouncing the bench, dive-bombing squats, slamming deadlifts and touch-and-go reps skip the hardest range and give up tension, technique and joint health.
TEC-24 | Pristine form vs slightly heavier reps | He once said pristine form can leave you on "baby weight" and that slightly ego-lifting with more weight and a better stretch can be better; elsewhere he calls cutting range and body English ego lifting.
TEC-25 | No lumbar flexion under load | Any lumbar flexion under load is form breakdown and wrong for bodybuilders. It is a correctable flaw, not inevitable, and it also kills leg drive. Anyone without a severe spinal condition can find a variation with a fully neutral back.
TEC-26 | The lower back is a bridge, not a crane | The lower back is a rigid, neutral bridge between legs and upper body; only its angle changes during a rep, never its flexion. The legs produce the power. Lower-back pain the day after deadlifts means you aren't pulling with the legs.
TEC-27 | Upper-back rounding is tolerable, but drags the lower back | Thoracic rounding is less dangerous than lumbar rounding and acceptable in some contexts, but it tends to pull the lower back into rounding. The spine follows the neck: head down starts the chain.
TEC-28 | Rounding is usually not a choice | On squats and deadlifts the back usually rounds because the body can't stay upright and reshapes around the bar on the positive. It is a technique and intention issue more than weak muscles; causes include weak core, glutes or thoracic extension, setup and poor cues.
TEC-29 | Neutral spine is not a vertical torso | The torso can be angled, even horizontal as in Pendlay rows, as long as the spine stays straight. The more the torso leans forward, the more potential for rounding.
TEC-30 | Simple bracing | Before a pull or squat: shoulders back, breathe and brace, flex the upper back, and keep that spine position through the rep. Bracing is a normal nose breath and tightening the abs as if about to be punched, without a big belly breath, sucking in or a ritual. He rates bracing C tier, mattering on heavy squats and deadlifts.
TEC-31 | Closed jaw, nose breathing | Keep the jaw clenched and breathe through the nose; an open mouth is a "strength vacuum". On squats hold the breath down and exhale on the way up.
TEC-32 | Lifting posture is everyday posture | Head pulled up as if by a string, chin up, chest out, shoulders back and down, glutes slightly tense, core braced. Lifting with good form is the best long-term posture fix; hunched computer posture limits depth and feeds rounded deadlifts. He ties this to Musashi's posture advice.
TEC-33 | Hips shooting up overloads the lower back | When the hips shoot back or up on squats or deadlifts, glute and posterior-chain drive is lost and the lower back takes the load. Reversing the squat too fast causes it.
TEC-34 | Control the negative | A negative that gravity does for you doesn't count; resist it. "The weight should not lead you." Cables and machines invite dropping it; free weights force control.
TEC-35 | The negative sets up the positive | Negative technique dictates positive technique, and slow negatives preserve form. The muscle you feel stretching on the way down is the one you'll feel on the way up.
TEC-36 | At least about one second down | Control the eccentric for at least ~1 s; beyond that tempo is preference. Pick one sensible tempo and keep it regardless of load.
TEC-37 | Slow down most near the stretch | Slow the rep most in the range closest to the stretch; letting the bar drop there is cheating and where injuries happen.
TEC-38 | The positive is the fatigue gauge | Only control the negative; the positive is where you slow down and fail. Negatives should stay the same speed or get slower through a set, since speeding up means momentum. On the last rep he lengthens the negative.
TEC-39 | Don't count or exaggerate tempo | Prescribed 3-4 s (or 4-0-1-0) negatives on everything are counterproductive: they cut load and progression and confuse novices. Dropped negatives are just as bad: "too slow and too fast both belong in the garbage". Fixed tempos suit only very advanced lifters.
TEC-40 | Time under tension is a by-product | Time under tension is overrated (C tier), a by-product of tempo like the pump is of high reps; chasing it means baby weights.
TEC-41 | Tempo's weight in his hierarchy grew | Tempo is S tier in his 2023 list (control and stretch), yet it ranks last among load, range and tempo: set range and load first, then add tempo. Slow tempo's real job is keeping ego in check.
TEC-42 | Explosive but controlled positive | Drive the positive with intent, but not so hard that form breaks (e.g. the bar leaving the shoulders); intent matters more than speed. The first reps at 70-80% should move fast.
TEC-43 | Bar speed doesn't show effort | Lifting speed says nothing about effort: his slow reps may look like a max with 5 reps left, and explosive reps can still build muscle.
TEC-44 | Fast unloaded negatives and negatives-only are not useful | Fast negatives to "lengthen the muscle faster" are hogwash; the stretch reflex at the bottom stresses the muscle more than extra slow eccentric time. Negatives-only training is impractical and of little relevance.
TEC-45 | ROM is distance under load, relative to leverages | For hypertrophy, range of motion is how far the load travels under tension, and it depends on leverages; there is no objective "full ROM" (standards come from competition). A tall lifter's parallel squat can travel more than a short lifter's ass-to-grass.
TEC-46 | Full range by default, within comfort | Longer range generally means more hypertrophy (more stretch, more travel), so full range is the default, but use the range you can do comfortably and sustainably. Trimming a little barely matters; keep at least one full-range leg lift.
TEC-47 | Effective range is what counts | What matters is the part of the range where the target is under the most stress; extra range only helps if the load challenges the muscle through it.
TEC-48 | Range dictates load, never the reverse | "Let ROM dictate the load, never the load dictate the ROM." There are two valid reasons to limit range: more target recruitment, or better performance (tempo, load, reps). Cutting range to add weight usually removes the most stimulating part.
TEC-49 | Range cuts usually chop the bottom | Most range cutting removes the bottom of the negative (the weakest point), which is exactly the part that matters.
TEC-50 | Partials are earned and planned | Partials have a place (A tier in 2023) but are scheduled with intent, never improvised after a failed rep; novices start with full range. The 2021 "odd-lift partial craze" produced small lifters who never built a base.
TEC-51 | What a good partial is for | A good partial lets you do more reps with more weight for the target, and can be added deliberately to help full-range progress. Earlier he said partials are for high reps, not for moving very heavy weight.
TEC-52 | Lengthened partials | In 2024 he says lengthened partials likely beat full range, which beats shortened partials, probably because they allow more load and reps in the stretch.
TEC-53 | More range isn't always better | Extreme deficits and cambered bars mostly add stress on joints and tendons (pecs, shoulders) rather than muscle.
TEC-54 | No ROM dogma | "Full range or don't lift" is elitist; combine full range with partials. Finding your best range isn't preaching short range.
TEC-55 | Build depth gradually | Don't force ranges you lack the mobility or leverage for; build depth gradually like load.
TEC-56 | The lockout adds almost nothing | At lockout the joints are stacked and the skeleton holds the load, so it contributes almost nothing to hypertrophy (C tier). Lockout and depth standards exist for judging; "joints are meant to lock" is an appeal to nature.
TEC-57 | Lock out only when it helps | Lock out if it keeps you in the groove, is pain-free and earns more reps or load; skip it if it throws off technique or hurts. Never locking out "to keep tension" can reduce total tension if it costs reps. Lock out dips for stability.
TEC-58 | The top squeeze is low value | Squeezing at the top is C/D tier: the muscle is least active there, and removing it doesn't reduce growth. A squeeze only helps you feel the muscle; holding lockout for 2 s is just resting, and thrusting the hips at the top of a squat or deadlift does nothing.
TEC-59 | Stretch over squeeze | If you must choose between the top squeeze and the bottom stretch, prioritise the stretch.
TEC-60 | A bodybuilding pause is short | Pause until momentum is gone, keeping the stretch, with no rocking or heaving; no 20 s pauses. Pauses add a small stretched-tension bonus, not a major driver.
TEC-61 | Pause reps: useful, used sparingly | Pause reps (B tier) clean up form, protect the red zone and cure fear of the hole, but the stretch reflex adds hypertrophy, so use them sparingly. Regular back-offs may beat paused ones; don't open the day with paused work. Pause squat is the best paused lift, paused bench second (a lifesaver for shoulder pain); pause without pins to keep control.
TEC-62 | Isometric holds are poor for size | Isometric holds are D tier for hypertrophy (arm wrestlers use them for strength without size); constant tension through the range beats holds.
TEC-63 | Cheating defined | Cheating is using momentum or leverage to bypass part of the range; a partial is also a cheat. It is not a moral question, only one of effectiveness.
TEC-64 | Cheat reps: from tool to mostly waste | He now prefers strict reps: cheat reps are mostly wasteful and body English is ego lifting. Cheat only in full control, on safe isolation lifts, and never on the negative.
TEC-65 | Rules if you cheat | Decide before the set; apply the same cheat force every rep (escalating cheat is the problem); the cheat only brings the weight to where the muscle can finish it.
TEC-66 | Cheating sends tonnage elsewhere | The real problem with cheating is that it's harder to direct tonnage to the target; more weight moved by other muscles doesn't make the target bigger.
TEC-67 | Body English: rows yes, isolation no | Some body English is fine on heavy back rows (Yates rows, a slight torso shift to finish a hard dumbbell row), not on rear-delt or arm isolation. Stop when reps get dirty.
TEC-68 | What mind-muscle connection is | Mind-muscle connection is the ability to prioritise a target within a compound so stronger muscles don't take the work: feeling fibres stretch and contract, not the pump. Pump doesn't prove a muscle worked, nor does its absence prove it didn't.
TEC-69 | Feeling is not recruitment | He doesn't feel his quads when squatting, yet they grow "like weeds". Mind-muscle connection (B tier) matters mainly for muscles that struggle to grow, which is really a form issue; it is no excuse for baby weights.
TEC-70 | Progress without feeling is fine | If you progress without feeling the muscle, don't worry; build the connection when progress stalls. Its value is finding the technique that recruits the muscle.
TEC-71 | How to build the connection | Build it with isolation work before or after compounds, slower tempo with lighter weights, and stretching the target on the negative; then return to heavier loads. Pump helps too; the "spiritual" route is picturing the ideal muscle.
TEC-72 | Obsessing over feel keeps you small | Piles of useless reps, lower intensity and zoning out in search of feel keep muscles small. The goal is neither feel nor maximal speed but a challenging weight under control.
TEC-73 | Stay mentally in the set | Phone use during a set kills focus; checking out may leave a piece missing; pain from an unpadded bar distracts from the muscle.
TEC-74 | Every lift has a technical rating | Each lift has a technical rating: how much practice it takes to master and keep. Front squat, rings and Olympic lifts rate high.
TEC-75 | Technical lifts are "jealous" | High-rated lifts decay fast and must be practised constantly; multi-joint lifts done once a month are relearned every time. Bodybuilders shouldn't invest in technical lifts that don't pay back in growth; easier variations (SSB or cross-arm front squats) lower the rating, and close variations maintain the main lift.
TEC-76 | Enough skill, then stop | Once neural adaptation to a lift is reached, more skill is useless; there is no point breaking the technical ceiling. "Technique is the enemy of the bodybuilder" [*inference*: this reconciles with TEC-03 as "master enough, no more"].
TEC-77 | Skill comes fast and largely stays | You lift with your nervous system: technique comes and goes fast, muscle slowly. Skill largely stays once built and relearning is faster; a week off squats or deadlifts often costs nothing, a year off feels wobbly. Once-a-week squatting isn't enough practice for a novice.
TEC-78 | Learn and tweak lifts light | Introduce movements with a learning curve extremely light; change stance or torso angle only with light weight, never at high intensity.
TEC-79 | Variations change main-lift form | Variations can bleed into the main lift for better or worse: box squats and good mornings can bake in a good-morning squat, stiff-legs bleed into the deadlift. Keep training the main lift itself. Exaggerating a fault (SSB, good mornings) can also teach the right pattern.
TEC-80 | Bodyweight swings change technique | Bodyweight changes alter squat, deadlift and bench technique, so aggressive bulk/cut cycles force relearning.
TEC-81 | Leverages change setup, not the pattern | Leverages change stance, grip, depth and lift choice, not the basic movement. Don't copy anyone's form; apply the pointers, since some techniques only work with the right structure.
TEC-82 | Tall lifters need adapted technique | Tall lifters need wider stances and grips, low bar, sumo or block pulls, and a longer technical adaptation; shoulder-width dogma fits lifters of about 5'7"-5'9". High-bar and conventional deadlifts force tall lifters into more lean and rounding, and short lifters' ass-to-grass can injure them.
TEC-83 | Some bodies reject a pattern | He cannot squat or pull with a wide stance (narrow hips, large waist). Upper-back rounding on heavy squats or RDLs may simply mean you aren't built for that lift.
TEC-84 | Warm up with the lift itself | Warm up with lighter sets of the lift itself to rehearse the pattern and get blood moving. No treadmill, no static stretching, no elaborate activation; he dropped band and plate activation. Warm-up reps use the same tempo and technique as top sets and are the place to fix technique (e.g. spotting too much lower back at 135).
TEC-85 | As many warm-up sets as needed | Do as many warm-up sets as you need; one set before a heavy deadlift is unrealistic. Only the first exercise of a movement pattern needs a full ramp. Warm-ups add volume, and the time needed is individual (longer for squats, older or bigger lifters).
TEC-86 | Warm-ups prime, they don't grow you | Warm-ups aren't hard enough to build muscle but prime you for heavy weight; feel the muscles work and don't rush. High-rep warm-ups pre-fatigue a little, which doesn't matter for bodybuilders, but don't burn energy on high-rep warm-up systems.
TEC-87 | Mobility comes from lifting through range | Lifting through full range with manageable weight and clean form builds mobility; every loaded exercise is a weighted stretch. Static stretching and foam rolling are at most a supplement, and he doesn't stretch. Extreme fascia stretching is a gimmick.
TEC-88 | Mobility is a muscular skill | Mobility is a muscular skill on a spectrum ("robust rigidity") with no universal standard. You don't have to squat ass-to-grass, but every inch gained is tonnage. Program heavy lifts suited to your body plus light lifts that push mobility limits.
TEC-89 | Weighted stretching is underused | Loaded stretching is an underexplored, effective method (B tier); traps grow just from holding weight. The fully lengthened position is the weakest and riskiest.
TEC-90 | Tight hips and squat stance test | Tight hips limit squat depth and make the hips shoot up on deadlifts. Test with your natural squat stance or a seated figure-four, not toe touching.
TEC-91 | Straps, not mixed grip | Bodybuilders pull double overhand until grip fails, then strap up. Mixed grip creates imbalance and biceps-tear risk, and hook and mixed grips exist for competition (one deadlift video calls hook grip acceptable). Never mixed grip with straps or on shrugs/rows.
TEC-92 | Strap anything grip would limit | Use straps wherever grip would limit a non-grip exercise (rows, back work, snatch-grip shrugs, Bulgarian split squats), but not on locked pulldowns; train forearms directly. He straps rows only when needed, usually not on the first set.
TEC-93 | Stack the wrists on every press | Keep the wrist stacked over the forearm on any press; a bent-back wrist leads to tendinitis. Stacked wrists also make you stronger, and you shouldn't feel your wrists on OHP. Break the stack only for a clear benefit.
TEC-94 | Thumbless grip where the bar can't roll | A thumbless/false grip deloads the wrist where the grip doesn't need securing (curls, raises, vertical presses, reverse curls, pull-ups). Never on heavy horizontal pressing, where the bar can roll.
TEC-95 | Grip mechanics | A closed, thumb-wrapped grip is strongest. Set the fingers so the bar can't shift (play halves grip strength); the last three fingers do the gripping. "Squeeze the bar as hard as possible" is mostly myth.
TEC-96 | The legs move the deadlift | The deadlift is "a leg press against the floor" with the back as a rigid bridge: neutral spine, very deep hips, knee flexion, strong hip drive. "Lift with your legs" is a poor cue because you always do; the problem is overusing the lower back.
TEC-97 | Deadlift setup | Bar over the whole foot, close to but not touching the shins. Bend the knees slightly, push the butt back and reach, then pull yourself into position so the hamstrings are tense, take the slack out, shoulders over the bar. Torso between vertical and horizontal, never parallel. Dragging the bar up the shins means you sat back too much; shoulders behind the bar means you're squatting it.
TEC-98 | Patient off the floor | Break the bar from the floor with steadily increasing leg pressure and a neutral spine, not "grip and rip". The bar leaves slowly but never slows down, and everything locks out together. People bend over to fake explosiveness off the floor.
TEC-99 | The "fishing rod" deadlift | When the knees lock early with a rounded back, the legs leave the lift and the back finishes it, causing the struggle past the knees. Most people who fail at the knee have a setup problem, not a strength problem.
TEC-100 | No rounded-back deadlifting | No one benefits from a rounded lower back on deadlifts; it is defensible only for elite lifters or a competition PR. Olympic lifters who round do so deliberately with light, controllable weight. Abort the pull if the back rounds.
TEC-101 | Full deadlift lockout | Unlike other lifts, the deadlift locks out fully: hips, knees and shoulders together with a neutral spine. No leaning back, hip thrusting or hyperextension. A soft lockout usually means thoracic rounding.
TEC-102 | Deadlifts are positive-only | Don't fight the deadlift negative: put the bar down and reset. Slow negatives tire you and sandbag the positive; a controlled negative gives slightly more hypertrophy, but the concentric-only puller gets more reps and fewer injuries. Use RDLs for eccentric work; no touch-and-go.
TEC-103 | Low deadlift reps because structure fails first | Keep deadlift sets low-rep: at high reps thoracic extension gives out before the legs, so 15-rep deadlifts are junk volume. With shaky form, do a few sets of 4-5 and add weight only when confident. Place deadlifts early, not at the end of back day.
TEC-104 | Pick a variation that keeps the back neutral | If the floor is a problem, choose a stance or variation that allows a neutral back: sumo, trap bar, blocks/rack (max work off blocks, submax from the floor). No cat-back floor pulls; behind-the-back deadlifts not recommended. Deadlift range is arbitrary anyway (set by plate diameter).
TEC-105 | How deadlift variations differ | Variations differ in torso angle and knee and hip flexion, and torso angle plus knee flexion decide what's trained. Sumo's sticking point is off the floor, conventional's near lockout; the trap bar has more quad drive.
TEC-106 | Deadlift odds and ends | Learn leg drive with submaximal conventional deficit deadlifts for about 10 reps, and finish deadlift day with light hyperextensions. No heeled shoes for floor pulls (they add a deficit). If the hips shift, move one foot slightly out; a staggered stance only matters if it hurts.
TEC-107 | "Deadlift CNS fatigue" is lower-back strain | What people call deadlift CNS fatigue is usually lower-back strain from pulling with the back; squats are actually more taxing.
TEC-108 | Squat with the knees first | The squat is knee flexion first: break at the knees and let the hips hinge naturally at depth (limit it, don't resist it). Hinging first turns it into a good morning. Torso angle changes come from the hip, never spinal flexion, and should match on the way down and up.
TEC-109 | The good-morning squat is the cardinal fault | When the hips rise first and the squat becomes a good morning, the tonnage number stays the same but the lower back takes the load instead of the quads, and it leads to injury. Fix it with technique, thoracic extension, core and glute work and 1 s paused squats, not a box.
TEC-110 | Knee-dominant stance for size | For size keep the squat knee-dominant: medium-to-narrow stance, toes forward, knees allowed forward (knees past toes is fine); heel elevation or a slant board helps. Very wide stances raise the number but cut quad work, and a copied wide, toes-out stance repeatedly injured him.
TEC-111 | Squat depth | Squat as deep as you can without back or knee pain or butt wink (a "power leak"); stop before it. Parallel or a hair below is a fine heavy standard, the last few inches add little, and quarter squats stress the knees and grow nothing. Safety and repeatability beat depth.
TEC-112 | Quads are stretched below parallel | Quads keep stretching below parallel; the idea that quads only need a short range is a myth. Graduate to free squats once you can go below parallel, since half squats shear the knee.
TEC-113 | Controlled descent, no dive-bombing | Control the descent; never dive-bomb (patellar tendinitis, knees shooting forward). The bottom should feel rock solid, like sitting; reverse with a controlled bounce, practised light.
TEC-114 | Bar and upper back on the back squat | No pad: the bar sits tight on the skin. Use as narrow a grip as you can to pack the upper back (narrow it set by set in warm-ups), never relax thoracic extension, and drive the bar into the traps out of the hole. Grip width still depends on proportions; pad pain fades within 2 weeks to 2 months.
TEC-115 | Unrack and walk out | Unrack from directly under the bar with minimal knee bend and walk out in about four steps, widening into your stance.
TEC-116 | High bar vs low bar | Try both. Low bar suits tall, lanky lifters (bar over midfoot, back a rigid bridge); high bar suits heeled shoes and knee flexion. On low bar use a thumbless grip and press up from under the bar to spare the wrists.
TEC-117 | Box squats don't fix squats | On a box most people sit back, rock and good-morning up; the box catches you where the squat gets hard and reinforces forward lean. If used, go straight down and up. Touch-and-go SSB box squats are fine for glutes.
TEC-118 | No knee valgus | Push the knees out and press through the whole foot. Valgus may be acceptable for a one-rep max athlete but harms tendons over a bodybuilder's many reps; light paused or higher-rep squats help control it.
TEC-119 | Front squat is a rack-and-thoracic lift | Most front squatters (he says 80%) are limited by thoracic extension and the rack, not the legs; the lift fails when the bar dumps. Elbows high, wrists not cocked back, fingers only stop the roll (a three-finger grip is fine), torso upright. It took him about two years to learn, and he'd tell his past self not to bother.
TEC-120 | Safety squat bar folds you forward | The SSB pushes you forward (the opposite of a front squat), taxing the posterior chain. Never use your normal working weight the first time, and don't flip the bar.
TEC-121 | Machine squats need machine form | Smith machine squats are S tier with feet and hips forward, since the machine replaces the hip hinge; don't copy free-weight form on it, and novices should learn free squats. Belt squats: close stance, vertical torso, knees forward, heels elevated, not too low; wide toes-out stance is misuse.
TEC-122 | Split squats for quads vs glutes | For quads: front foot close, knee travelling past the toes and tracking the foot, foot straight, slow reps unless you're an athlete. For glutes: front foot further out and the torso leaning onto the thigh as you descend. Prefer split squats to lunges.
TEC-123 | Bench: shoulder safety first | On bench, protect the shoulders first: arch, shoulders back and tight, leg drive, elbows tucked not flared. The arch shortens the range and keeps the shoulders "in the pocket"; leg drive adds stability, not magic force. He avoids feet-up bench.
TEC-124 | Elbow flare on bench | He has called flared elbows always wrong and, later, the way to recruit more chest.
TEC-125 | Grip width on bench | Narrower grips deload the shoulder and allow more pressing volume; wide grips internally rotate it. Tall or broad lifters may need a wider grip.
TEC-126 | Touch point and the Spoto pause | Touching the chest is a powerlifting requirement; long-armed lifters can cut range. A pause just shy of the chest (Spoto) keeps the pecs loaded and saves the shoulders.
TEC-127 | No touch-and-go bench | Touch-and-go bench is mostly ego and momentum and stresses the shoulder; pause instead, which also fixes uneven pressing. The ideal is a controlled negative with a micro-pause.
TEC-128 | Bench negative speed | He preaches slow negatives in general, but on bench he lowers "fairly fast but controlled", using the lats, because slow negatives never helped his positive.
TEC-129 | Lower the incline | Most people set the incline too steep, which trains shoulders rather than upper chest; lower it.
TEC-130 | Dips technique | Lean forward for chest or stay vertical for triceps, and don't switch mid-rep (losing the groove costs ~30% performance). Control the eccentric and pause at the bottom; upper arm parallel to the floor is a legitimate full range, deeper optional. Grip slightly wider than the shoulders; parallel bars are most shoulder-friendly. Lock out at the top for stability.
TEC-131 | Ring push-ups and spotting | Ring push-ups give a deeper, safer chest stretch than cambered bars; deficit ring push-ups are unnecessary. When spotted, tell the spotter exactly what help and how many reps you want; lift-off should be minimal and even, and you de-rack by extending the elbows.
TEC-132 | OHP: the bottom is where delts work | On the overhead press the bottom range (shoulders flexed under load) is where the delts work, so start low; pin presses and nose-level starts skip it (worse than skipping lockout). Elbow lockout is optional, but lock the shoulders and push the head "through the window". He never locks his elbows (tendinitis).
TEC-133 | OHP range in his own pressing | In 2020 he said both that he prefers full range down to the collarbone and that he doesn't use full range on the overhead press.
TEC-134 | OHP details | The real overhead press danger is leaning back until it becomes an incline press; some momentum is fine for novices, but no OHP after squats or deadlifts for them. Stand, brace and hold thoracic extension. Press in the scapular plane with the elbows slightly forward; behind-the-neck only with mobility and a light ramp (he avoids it).
TEC-135 | OHP rep floor rose | Low reps degraded his overhead press form, so he raised the minimum.
TEC-136 | Order presses by shoulder demand | Order presses from most to least shoulder-taxing. Seated vs standing doesn't matter if the legs aren't involved.
TEC-137 | Pull with the back, arms are hooks | Rows and pulls are done with the back; the arms are hooks, and the back moves and stretches with the weight. Arm-only rowing is a hammer curl.
TEC-138 | Let the shoulder blades travel on rows | On rows let the scapulae protract so the back gets stretched; the bottom stretch matters most and pulling all the way back can be counterproductive. Constant "shoulders back" is the wrong cue for rows (pull-ups keep scapular engagement).
TEC-139 | A row is a held deadlift position | On bent-over rows the hamstrings, glutes and lower back hold rigid while the back pulls; floor or touch-and-go rows drift into hip hinges (his own cheat rows turned into RDLs). Novices who can't deadlift can't row safely.
TEC-140 | Barbell rows: moderate reps, pronated grip | Below 6 reps you jerk the bar with the legs. Barbell rows are somewhat full-body: not too strict, not too cheated. Pronated grip, no supinated or mixed grip.
TEC-141 | Dumbbell and Kroc rows | Dumbbell rows: both feet on the floor (staggered/tripod), no knee on the bench; pull in an arc so the lat stretches and the weight travels forward; no hip shifts to heave. Kroc rows: weight in front, pronated wrists, upright torso, not too few reps (5-8 invites cheating).
TEC-142 | Steer the row with elbows and torso | Flared elbows and a higher touch point bias upper back, traps and rear delts; tucked elbows and a lower touch bias lats (lower also rhomboids). A more horizontal torso means more lats. Never row high to the chest.
TEC-143 | Rounded-back rows, earned | Rounded-back T-bar rows and deep rounded rack hinges aren't really dangerous with moderate load, an engaged posterior chain and slow tempo, but they fatigue the erectors. The lumbar rule for squats and deadlifts is unchanged.
TEC-144 | Standardise row reps | The weight must touch the chest or the set is over; cable rows that swing from the hips are cheats.
TEC-145 | Strict bodybuilding pull-ups | Start from the top with the negative, lower more slowly than you pull, keep the scapulae engaged, stay under the bar in line with the body, and pull with the back. No dangling or kipping, and don't force the head over the bar. Strict reps cost load but give better hypertrophy; keep the same grip and tempo when loaded.
TEC-146 | No dead hang at the bottom of a pull-up | A dead hang at the bottom disengages the lats and scapulae. The lat's maximum stretch is just before you lose retraction, so train that "striking range". Dead hangs are a separate tendon drill; weighted hangs don't grow lats.
TEC-147 | Pull-up grip cues and learning drills | Pressure on the pinkies, rotate the arms outward, elbows close, take the slack out first; use a narrower grip if wide feels all arms. Learn back-pulling with scapular engage/disengage drills on the bar.
TEC-148 | Pulldowns: vertical, slow, full stretch | Pull in line with the cable, slowly, with the handles overhead rather than in front. Let the stack stretch the lats at the top until just before the shoulders internally rotate. Straps aren't needed on locked pulldowns.
TEC-149 | Pullovers: stretch the rib cage, not the shoulder | Lie across the bench with the hips dropped below the shoulders, slight elbow bend, elbows turned in overhead, inhale on the negative. Stretch the rib cage, not the shoulder: stop when the lat stops stretching, no full shoulder extension, and skip the dead top. If the triceps take over, bend the elbows more or use an EZ bar. Not on the floor or a bed.
TEC-150 | RDL: bent-knee, negative-first hinge | The RDL is a negative-first hinge with bent knees: start from a rack, push the hips back, keep the bar close (or slightly floating with the weight still under you), go until the plates nearly kiss the floor, no bounce. A stiff-leg lets the bar float away. Most people's RDLs are partials or stiff-legs.
TEC-151 | Good mornings | Knees slightly bent, not locked; no need to reach parallel (he stops around 45°, matching his squat and deadlift). The lower back is a bridge, not a crane; the SSB is the best bar; use safeties, never go to failure, and treat it as the day's main hinge. 2024 hamstring version: narrow stance, feet forward, shoulders past the hips, sets of 12.
TEC-152 | Hip thrusts | Close stance, full range down to the floor; think about the knees (down-in on the way down, up-out on the way up). The top squeeze does little. Pad the bar.
TEC-153 | A curl moves only the forearm | Keep the elbows at the sides and move only the forearm. Raising the elbows makes it a front raise, and hip drive turns it into a "supinated clean"; this explains poor biceps more than genetics does.
TEC-154 | Don't fully straighten on curls | Stop just short of full extension; the last inch stresses the tendon, not the muscle, and disengages the biceps. On preachers stop with the forearm about parallel.
TEC-155 | Curl details | Curl one arm at a time or alternating, start neutral and supinate on the way up, take sets to failure without ego. On incline curls the weak bottom dictates the load; with a controlled negative, momentum extends the stretch; use a thumbless grip.
TEC-156 | Wall curls and cheating | He first said wall and seated curls don't truly stop cheating, then that wall curls reduce cheating and allow slower negatives.
TEC-157 | Triceps extensions: only the elbow moves, no lockout | On skull crushers lock the shoulder in maximum flexion and move only the elbow (swinging turns it into a pullover); do them on a bench, not the floor; elbows may flare; pause at the bottom. Don't lock the elbows on triceps work: the lengthened part matters. Prefer dumbbells and slow negatives.
TEC-158 | Lateral and rear-delt raises | Laterals: slow and controlled, no swinging, stop at parallel (higher adds nothing), thumbless grip; seated or leaning versions reduce cheating. Rear delts: don't grip, hook the hand or wrist behind the handle, forearm in line with the upper arm, emphasise the bottom stretch.
TEC-159 | Calf raises: the bottom is the growth | Never let the heels touch, pause at the bottom, use a slow tempo and don't rebound off the Achilles; the bottom is where growth comes from.
TEC-160 | Abs: stretch and control | Slow negatives, a stretch at the bottom, no swinging or rebounding. Effective range beats maximal range (hanging knee raises beat toes-to-bar).
TEC-161 | Forearms, traps and neck | Forearms: train the muscle, not the tendon; keep the wrist stacked; on finger curls let the bar roll to the fingertips. Traps: let the shoulders protract so the traps catch the bar in a stretch, squeeze back rather than up, torso vertical. Neck: chin tucked, spine stacked, clean slow reps per direction, with a harness rather than plates on the head.
CHE-01 | No separate upper chest; build the whole chest | There is no separate "upper chest" to chase; bench and dips develop it, and people who obsess over it usually have no chest. Splitting the chest into regions is an "isolationist mindset".
CHE-02 | A stubborn chest needs a press you feel | Grip, arch and dominant delts or triceps can steal bench work ("225 on the bar isn't 225 on the chest"). If you don't feel a press in your chest, switch to push-ups, dumbbells or dips for a while, or superset flies or band work between bench sets.
CHE-03 | Bench press is not mandatory | Dips, push-ups and ring push-ups can replace the bench with no other change to the program. Choose by what agrees with you, and swap the bench if it doesn't hit your chest or keeps injuring you. His chest first grew from home push-ups, and by 2024 he had dropped the bench himself.
CHE-04 | Weighted dips are top tier | Weighted dips ("squat of the upper body") are top tier for chest, triceps and shoulders. They give more pec and shoulder stretch than bench and may stress the pecs more. The shoulder risk is slightly higher, with no safety net.
CHE-05 | Ring push-ups are among the best chest builders | Ring push-ups, especially weighted (backpack) and decline with the rings to the armpits, free the wrists and elbows, have no floor limit, let the scapulae retract, and bias the upper chest when declined. He ranks them first in his hypertrophy tier list.
CHE-06 | Push-ups aren't only for novices | Loaded, or placed at the end of a press day, push-ups stay effective for advanced lifters.
CHE-07 | Flies and ring work add chest volume while sparing the shoulders | The less the shoulder is taxed, the more chest you can stimulate. Use flies, band flies and ring push-ups to grow the chest past dominant shoulders.
CHE-08 | Flies are finishers with a bent-arm stretch | Use flies for high reps and supersets, not heavy single sets. Top picks: low-to-high cable fly, bent-arm pec deck, fly press, barrel press, and ring flies (build up from neutral ring push-ups first). Straight-arm DB flies and the straight-arm pec deck are mediocre and risky.
CHE-09 | One king press, maybe a second, maybe one isolation | The chest needs one big press, perhaps a second press or accessory and perhaps one isolation; more is pointless unless you specialize in chest.
CHE-10 | Rotate horizontal presses, including shoulder-friendly variations | Use more than one horizontal press over time, including variations that unload the shoulder: paused, close-grip, slingshot, dumbbell, football/cambered bar, pin or Spoto press. Replace one bench day per week with a paused variation.
CHE-11 | The dumbbell press is underrated | Start with dumbbells if you don't feel the bench in your chest; dumbbells beat a barbell for incline.
CHE-12 | The incline press is a chest/shoulder hybrid | The steeper the incline, the more shoulder. Treat the incline as a priority lift and don't stack it right after a heavy flat press. Dumbbell or Smith incline and weighted incline ring push-ups are his favourites.
CHE-13 | Low-value chest movements | Floor press (no stretch; 3 years of DB floor press gave him a poor chest), plate/Svend press, standing cable press, pullovers for chest, chain dips, Hindu and one-arm push-ups, finger push-ups.
CHE-14 | Pair the press with a complement | Horizontal press then vertical press (his favourite bench day), bench supersetted with pullovers, or a heavy horizontal press then ring push-ups.
CHE-15 | A big chest doesn't need a 3-plate bench | Long-armed lifters bench less but may build bigger chests.
CHE-16 | Uneven chest: fix the bar path, add one-arm DB work | An uneven chest comes from uneven shoulder height. Fix the bar path and add one-arm dumbbell bench sets.
CHE-17 | Strong chest, weak shoulders: bench once a week | A chest-dominant lifter with lagging shoulders should cut bench to one low-rep session a week (see SHO-06).
CHE-18 | Pullovers made his chest look thicker | He says pullovers made his chest look thicker from the front, though elsewhere he lists pullovers for chest among low-value moves (CHE-13).
BAK-01 | The upper back has the most growth potential in naturals | The upper back is the most underdeveloped area in naturals and the slowest to mature; the best natural backs belong to lifters in their 30s-40s. After 10 years of training, adding upper-back volume made his grow "like weeds".
BAK-02 | The upper back tolerates the most volume and frequency | The upper back is a "glutton for volume"; train it almost every session. Its limits are the shoulder, biceps and elbow tendons and lower-back fatigue, so favour rows and pulls that spare the lower back. 4 sets of pulldowns won't build big lats.
BAK-03 | Push/pull balance: from "pull more" to "many need more front" | He now sees many lifters as back-dominant from the "pull twice as much as you push" advice; they need less back and more front volume. Everyone should still do plenty of back work.
BAK-04 | A complete back needs a hinge, a vertical pull and a horizontal pull | The back is several areas, each needing its own pattern; vertical and horizontal pulls are not interchangeable. Deadlifts alone give a thick "fridge" back with no width; pull-ups were the only difference between his flat and his detailed back at the same 210 lb. 6-7 back exercises is "bro-split garbage".
BAK-05 | Open the back day with a heavy pull | Open with a floor pull/hinge, a heavy row or a weighted pull-up, never a light pulldown or machine. Don't burn out on the opener; it doubles as a warm-up.
BAK-06 | Back thickness comes from hinges as well as rows | The upper back works isometrically to stabilize hinge loads, so heavy hinges can replace one or two back exercises; the deadlift's weighted stretch at a 45° torso grows the mid back. Pull-ups alone leave you "flat as paper" from the side.
BAK-07 | The deadlift's role for the back shrank | The deadlift is now only mediocre for lats, builds a thick back with no V-taper, and ignores the small upper-back muscles.
BAK-08 | "Legless" pulls as the bread and butter of upper-back growth | Cable, seal, DB and chest-supported rows and hanging pulls should carry upper-back growth; he expects bigger backs from this approach.
BAK-09 | Pull-ups are the king of lat and upper-back builders | Weighted pull-ups when possible; nearly every natural with big lats built them with pull-ups. They decompress the spine, barely tax the CNS, and pair well with presses and small-muscle work. Weighted beat bodyweight, which beat machines.
BAK-10 | Deadlifts and rows don't build lats; vertical pulls and pullovers do | At a 500-520 lb deadlift he had "essentially no lats" and wrongly blamed his insertions; the tonnage went to the rhomboids and mid traps. Slow pull-ups and pullovers then grew them. Only a few people with particular leverages grow lats from deadlifts.
BAK-11 | Do pullovers every week, short of failure | Pullovers transformed his lats and ribcage and are his favourite lift; put them in every program, on push or upper days. Dumbbell or machine is best; the cable version is a nightmare to set up. They supplement pull-ups and pulldowns but can't replace them.
BAK-12 | Pullover rep ranges | Keep pullovers in moderate reps; very high reps shift the work to the triceps long head.
BAK-13 | Pull-up prescriptions for lats | Big lats come from heavy weighted pull-ups plus volume, not light pulldowns. Bodyweight pull-ups can be done daily with varied grips.
BAK-14 | Lat pulldowns: from "replace them" to a top pull-up substitute | Some pulldowns are top tier and the pulldown is the best pull-up substitute, worth rotating in even if you can do pull-ups. Best: close-grip neutral, wide supinated, and the diagonal/iliac pulldown heavy for 8-10 to failure. Wide pronated and behind-the-neck are only decent; the standing cable pulldown is bad.
BAK-15 | Pull-up grips have different flavours; vary them | Chin-up = lower/front lat plus biceps and core; pronated = more upper/mid back; neutral = best stretch, most shoulder-friendly; wide = lower lat/V-taper, more traps. The sternum (Gironda) pull-up is the "best of the best" but needs about 10 chest-to-bar reps first.
BAK-16 | Lat mind-muscle connection comes from stretch and slow tempo | Very slow, fully stretched pull-ups, one-arm cable rows and wide snatch-grip rows to the sternum build the lat connection. Wide overhand grip is best for him; Pendlay rows lack the bottom stretch.
BAK-17 | Don't stack lat work | One heavy lat movement to exhaustion beats several for a pump; never put two lat movements back to back.
BAK-18 | Lat menu (2023) | His 2023 lat options with rep ranges.
BAK-19 | Ribcage expansion from pullovers is real | Pullovers enlarge the ribcage look; his roughly doubled, which makes his 36 in waist look small. It shrinks if you stop.
BAK-20 | Developed lats show from the side and make the rear delts pop | Well-developed lats protrude from the side and make the rear delts stand out.
BAK-21 | The serratus is the most underrated aesthetic muscle | The V-taper is shoulders, lats and serratus. Pullovers, vertical pressing and pulling, twisting sit-ups and serratus punches train it; it's never limiting and training it helps the shoulders.
BAK-22 | Dumbbell rows are top tier and underrated | No lower-back tax, an arc path with full lat stretch, and they fit anywhere in a session. Kroc rows are "incredibly underrated" for traps and upper back and very safe.
BAK-23 | Rows: 8-12 reps, controlled negative and stretch | Low reps break row form. Pendlay rows bias the upper back; Yates rows bias the mid back and lats. Rows grow the upper back, not big biceps.
BAK-24 | The barbell row is a hinge/row hybrid | It loads the lower back, so program it as the day's hinge or opener in higher reps, or replace it with supported rows. Never 3x5 or 5x5, never grind; don't put it after squats and deadlifts. It is only "decent" for lats.
BAK-25 | Seal and chest-supported rows for heavy rowing and tired lower backs | Taking the legs and lower back out suits heavier, lower-rep rowing. The EZ-bar incline chest-supported row is top tier; plate-loaded chest-supported machines and the T-bar are mediocre.
BAK-26 | Pendlay rows: poor stretch, mediocre for lats | The toughest, most technical row, with no controlled negative. Good for pulling strength and traps, mediocre for lats, not for novices.
BAK-27 | Inverted and ring rows are underrated | Good bodyweight back builders; supinated versions are safe and good for lats, though hard to load. The body row between dip bars is top tier.
BAK-28 | Grip and torso angle steer the row | Neutral or close grip favours the lats; pronated, wide or high-pull favours traps and upper back. You can superset a row with itself if another exercise sits between; do the weak muscle's grip first.
BAK-29 | Mid back: Kroc rows or flared-elbow rows | The mid/low traps and rhomboids are best hit by Kroc rows or rows with flared elbows, torso fairly vertical, elbow high. Sternum pull-ups with fat grips also bias them.
BAK-30 | Low-value rows | Cheat barbell row, renegade row, TRX inverted row, standing cable "shotgun" row, T-bar row ("noob trap"), narrow-handle seated cable row. Cable rows are fine as finishers; one-arm seated cable rows beat two-arm.
BAK-31 | Low-value lat movements | Front lever, one-arm pull-up, muscle-up, dead hangs (for lats), straight-arm pulldown (pump finisher only), kettlebell swings, Jefferson deadlift.
BAK-32 | Lower back: stabilization work is mostly enough | Squats, floor pulls and rows cover the lower back through isometric stretch; it rarely needs dedicated work unless injured. Hyperextensions are the best isolation if you want one.
BAK-33 | One erector bigger than the other: unilateral hinges | Use single-leg hyperextensions or single-leg RDLs.
BAK-34 | Mirror-muscle training causes shoulder and back pain | Prioritize the posterior chain in compounds and on dedicated days.
BAK-35 | Traps grow from weighted stretch: heavy load in the hands | More weight beats more range of motion; perfect-form 50 lb DB shrugs never build big traps. The trap's job is to hold weight in the hands, and he predicts zero-ROM heavy holds may prove optimal.
BAK-36 | Traps need less direct work than he used to give them | RDLs and floor pulls cover the traps; trap isolation is optional. Shrugs are redundant but nearly free volume for giant sets; naturally big-trapped lifters can skip them. His only trap lift is belt-squat shrugs.
BAK-37 | Past the novice stage, deadlifts aren't enough for traps (2020-2022) | Add heavy snatch-grip shrugs, above-knee rack pulls (upper traps work near lockout), high pulls and shrug finishers.
BAK-38 | Shrug-finisher rule (2020-2021) | End every floor-pull set with shrugs, then hold for a stretch until you must let go; or shrug at the top of the last RDL set, strip 20 lb and repeat. Trap NO was his best NO result.
BAK-39 | Shrug reps: mostly 10-15 | Traps take lots of volume; 3x8 or 2 sets is too little.
BAK-40 | Trap-biased exercise choices | Good: snatch-grip shrugs (mid-trap stretch, no lower-back fatigue, widen the grip gradually), power/cheat and Kelso shrugs, chest-supported incline shrugs, Kroc rows, above-knee rack pulls, upright rows, strapped carries, heavy belt rows. Poor: slow-tempo shrugs, cable shrugs, overhead shrugs, laterals, band pull-aparts, overhead carries.
BAK-41 | Shrugs superset with almost anything; change torso angle by region | After OHP, with hammer curls, or with the same bar after stiff-legs, but not heavy shrugs with RDLs. Shrugging in a hip-hinge position targets the mid and lower traps. Use straps.
BAK-42 | Hang cleans can replace shrugs | Hang cleans or hang clean pulls give trap stretch plus explosiveness.
BAK-43 | Farmer's walks build traps | Carries done after pre-fatiguing traps, neck and shoulders are "a killer" (programming of carries: FOR-18).
BAK-44 | Mid and low traps grow from any pulling | Train them through pulling before considering NO for them.
SHO-01 | Vertical pressing is the bread and butter of delt size | Most shoulder gains come from presses; the incline press is a hybrid and a delt movement first. All his recent shoulder gains came from "spamming" overhead presses. Judge by delt size, not pressing load.
