SHO-02 | Lateral raises support pressing; they don't replace it | Big round natural shoulders come from pressing plus isolation; spamming raises at the cost of presses is a trendy mistake. Press before laterals, and never put raises right before or after a press.
SHO-03 | Pressing frequency | Vertical presses at least twice a week, building toward three; by 2024, 1-2 vertical presses plus 1-2 isolations.
SHO-04 | Shoulder selection: 1-2 presses + 2-3 isolations | Pick any comfortable press; the only requirement is that the arm moves overhead.
SHO-05 | Shoulder-dominant lifters can cut vertical pressing | If your shoulders dominate the chest and arms, cut vertical pressing. No rule says horizontal pressing must outnumber vertical.
SHO-06 | Chest-dominant with lagging shoulders: skew toward vertical pressing | Make the vertical press the first upper day's main lift and isolate the side and rear delts. Being much stronger vertically than horizontally signals that the shoulders steal chest work (see CHE-17).
SHO-07 | His overhead press choice evolved | Seated DB and machine presses are top tier. The push press is poor for delt size (legs do the work), the Z press mediocre, the behind-the-neck press fine in a pain-free range.
SHO-08 | Bodyweight shoulders: deficit handstand push-ups | The more vertical the torso, the more shoulder.
SHO-09 | Side and rear delts are what most naturals lack | The front delt is overdeveloped from pressing; hitting side and rear delts regularly gives capped, 3D delts.
SHO-10 | Lateral raise reps 8-15, never low | Lateral raises belong in moderate-to-high reps; very low reps (e.g. 4) are a mistake.
SHO-11 | Lateral raise frequency: down to once a week | By 2024 once a week is enough, and 2-3x often hinders progress.
SHO-12 | Cable laterals with a cuff are the best lateral raise | Constant tension, no grip limit, and no trap takeover, AC stress or cheating when the pin is at hip level. Lean-away ("Egyptian") raises are excellent. Rotate variations (cable, DB, upright rows, Y-raises) rather than repeating one raise twice a week.
SHO-13 | Upright rows are valid | A wrongly demonized side-delt/trap isolation when done with a manageable weight; a wrist-roller handle helped his shoulder pain.
SHO-14 | Power raises are for advanced lifters only | Power raises and lying lateral power raises give a great stretch and near-pure side-delt work; build up gradually.
SHO-15 | Lu raises are overhyped for size | Too light for hypertrophy, but fine for mobility or rehab.
SHO-16 | Rear delts: frequency is the main pillar, with higher reps | Once a week won't grow rear delts. Use higher reps, often short of failure on bands.
SHO-17 | Rear-delt exercise choices | Arnold raises are his favourite rear-delt isolation (2020-2021); ring face pulls are top tier, reverse flies A tier. Face pulls are healthy and good for NO, done seated at the end of push days, but aren't the best builders; rear-delt rows are B tier. Laterals and face pulls aren't interchangeable; bent-over raises can overuse the traps.
SHO-18 | Don't stack front, side and rear raises with little rest | The rear delts get nothing at the end; two rear-delt isolations in one superset is too much.
SHO-19 | Front delts need little or no isolation | Presses cover the front delts for most people; prioritize laterals. Front raises are optional extra volume.
SHO-20 | Shoulder NO: isolation only, never presses | For nucleus overload use laterals, rear flies and face pulls only.
SHO-21 | Weak points stay weak | His quads grow on little volume while his shoulders lag on much more; in 2022 he barely pressed because his shoulders "grew too easily".
ARM-01 | Arms need isolation; compounds alone leave them small | He has never seen a natural with big biceps from chin-ups alone; at the bottom of a chin-up the back takes the stretch. If rowing 3 plates didn't grow your biceps, 4 plates won't. "70% of arm gains come from compounds" is made up.
ARM-02 | Treat arm training as seriously as squat and bench | Progressive overload, intensity and strict form. "Train your arms as an afterthought and they will look like an afterthought." Torso-dominant powerlifter physiques come from programming, not genetics.
ARM-03 | Pump-only, high-volume arm work is a myth | Light, high-rep, pump-chasing arm work done by feel doesn't build arms.
ARM-04 | Chin-ups are a back exercise | Chin-ups won't build big biceps alone, but they suit back-and-biceps days and programs without curls.
ARM-05 | Biceps reps: heavy but not below about 4-6 | Biceps respond to heavy, strict curls, but since 2023 he never goes below 4-6 reps and uses stretch curls in higher reps for tendon health.
ARM-06 | Biceps volume: 3x10 once a week is too little | With one curl in a session do 4x8-12. Once a week can work with a top set plus back-offs.
ARM-07 | Keep curl selection small | 2-3 curls trained hard, not five curls dissecting the heads; many variations reduce neural adaptation.
ARM-08 | A good biceps exercise maximizes biceps use | Stable elbow and shoulder, controllable stretch, no swinging. Top tier (2022): close-grip EZ preacher, close-grip EZ curl, spider curl, behind-the-back cable curl. Excellent: cable, braced concentration, pinwheel, machine curls, arm blaster. Bad: cheat barbell curls ("a supinated clean"), waiter curls, one-arm chin-ups.
ARM-09 | Preacher curls are a staple | Especially close-grip EZ; the pad lets you go to failure safely. He keeps partial preacher curls because they suit his tendons.
ARM-10 | Incline and stretch curls: from poor to top tier | Biceps work best around 90° elbow flexion; the incline curl trains the lengthened position. Fixes: slight incline only, no bouncing, or cable/Bayesian versions. He now does incline cable and spider curls for elbow pain.
ARM-11 | Cheat curls built his biceps, but aren't needed | Cheat curls were his only curl for about 6 years (from 15 to ~22) with no tendinitis, but he calls that survivorship bias. Barbell cheat curls become whole-body moves; dumbbell versions stay more honest. Not for beginners.
ARM-12 | Find a curl you love; swap any curl that hurts | Nobody hates every curl. Regular curls hurt his shoulders, so he uses cross-body pinwheel curls; hammer curls in front of the body over-recruit the front delt.
ARM-13 | Head emphasis | The long head grows from almost any curl; the short head and the upper biceps need targeting. Curls bias the distal biceps; short-range rows with fixed shoulders bias the proximal biceps, so doing only curls can mimic poor peak genetics.
ARM-14 | Brachialis and brachioradialis make the arm look wider | Hammer curls "cost nothing"; he prefers full range for the brachialis (brachioradialis work: FOR-13).
ARM-15 | If forearms or triceps dominate the biceps, favour supinated curls | Cut pronated work and twist the pinky outward, especially with a short biceps insertion.
ARM-16 | Band curls for tendon health | Band curls don't hurt recovery and suit NO, while high-frequency DB or barbell curls tend to cause tendonitis.
ARM-17 | The EZ bar changes wrist comfort, not biceps activation | It mainly changes wrist comfort and forearm involvement; dumbbells make cheating harder.
ARM-18 | Small isolation needs humility | Strict light curls hit the biceps; most heavy curls are cheated.
ARM-19 | Maintaining big biceps: high intensity, low volume | Already-big biceps can be kept with little, heavy work.
ARM-20 | Presses don't build the triceps for most naturals | He benched 335 with almost no triceps; you can add 200 lb to a bench with little going to the triceps if you're chest- or shoulder-dominant. Those who grow triceps from bench alone are outliers. Beginners should isolate from day one.
ARM-21 | The long head needs extensions with shoulder flexion | The long head is the biggest, most neglected part of the triceps. Pressing and pushdowns build the lateral head; overhead extensions, French presses and skull crushers with shoulder flexion hit the long head, which pushes the biceps up. Pullovers give it only a passive stretch.
ARM-22 | Long-head reps: mostly 8-12 | Small muscles shouldn't always be trained at very high reps; 5x5 is acceptable once a week.
ARM-23 | Long-head to lateral-head ratio of 2:1 | Three long-head movements overdo it. A novice needs only one long-head movement (lateral isolation optional).
ARM-24 | Choose tendon-friendly extensions that cover all three heads | Top tier: lying extension with the bar behind the head (shoulder fully flexed), overhead cable extension (progress it for 2 years), kneeling cable extension, French press against an incline. Cables spare tendons but bore you, so mix in free weights; grip or balance limits cost points.
ARM-25 | Skull crushers: let the shoulders flex past vertical | Dumbbells beat the EZ bar for depth. Skull crushers and close-grip bench aren't interchangeable (long head vs lateral/medial); superset them with zero rest instead.
ARM-26 | Skull crushers inside press giant sets made his presses stronger | He credits long-head activation; supersetting two triceps moves is fine if they hit different heads.
ARM-27 | JM press: from elbow-unfriendly to elbow-saving | The JM press combines skull crusher and close-grip press, hits the lateral head, and is best on a Smith machine or SSB. Start very light.
ARM-28 | Close-grip bench: a low-impact press, mostly chest | Adds pressing volume and spares the shoulders; open arm days with it, not after the isolation.
ARM-29 | Pushdowns: V-bar for heavy, rope for the long head and elbows | Rotate V-bar and rope/reverse-grip/band pushdowns; don't do two pushdowns back to back.
ARM-30 | Triceps menu (2023) | His 2023 triceps options with rep ranges.
ARM-31 | Poor triceps movements | DB kickbacks, bench dips, Tate press, floor extensions, standing EZ overhead extension, dip machines.
ARM-32 | Dips for triceps need a vertical torso and top-range work | Normal forward-lean dips are mostly chest and shoulders; use a vertical torso and slingshot or top-range work for triceps.
ARM-33 | Never isolate triceps before dips or presses | Superset presses with long-head extensions afterward instead.
ARM-34 | Triceps NO and high-rep work | For lagging triceps, light high-rep extensions on non-pressing days, never on bench days. Progress by adding reps, then slots, then days; add weight only once every day is capped.
ARM-35 | Band and supinated cable extensions for cranky elbows | They give a pump and ease triceps tendinitis.
ARM-36 | Diamond or tiger push-ups for lagging triceps | Finish with push-up variants to failure.
ARM-37 | Arm days: unnecessary for novices, eventually required | To maximize arm size you eventually need an arm day. Open with a heavy curl (or a triceps press if triceps are the priority), not chin-ups; include other body parts to avoid junk volume (no 6-7 triceps exercises); keep it low stress and enjoyable.
ARM-38 | Superset biceps and triceps; never two same-muscle moves back to back | On arm days supersetting biceps with triceps is a must; never put two biceps or two triceps movements back to back. Templates are in the table below.
ARM-39 | Long-armed lifters just have to get very big | A "spider physique" is a matter of method, not fate.
FOR-01 | Pulling doesn't build big forearms; isolate them | Elite 600-900 lb pullers often have thin forearms. His raw-pulling years stalled his forearms; direct work made them "blow up". His big forearms come from years of isolation, not genetics.
FOR-02 | Use straps; strapless pulling doesn't build forearms | Don't let grip limit big-muscle lifts. Going strapless or mixed-grip on back work doesn't build forearms; deadlifting for forearms is "the worst advice". Straps don't hurt forearm growth.
FOR-03 | Grip strength and forearm size are separate | Grip types are support, pinch and crush. Support grip (load stretching the fibers) builds size; crush grip gives no stretch and is least useful for size. Grip training is only one isometric function.
FOR-04 | Train every forearm function, plus weighted stretch | Train wrist flexion and extension, pronation, supination and elbow flexion, plus some holds. Judge an exercise by the function it trains; good ones stimulate only the forearm. What grows forearms in pulls is weighted stretch, not squeezing.
FOR-05 | Forearm progression path (2023) | Pick one movement you enjoy, then add 1-2, then cover every wrist/elbow function and rotate.
FOR-06 | Finger curls are his staple | Replace wrist curls and grippers with finger curls (loaded negative, stretch): switch for 1-2 weeks "and never go back". They are forgiving on the wrist, an arm-wrestler favourite, and fixed his wrist pain. The dumbbell version is top tier.
FOR-07 | Wrist curls and extensions: 8-20 reps; drop anything felt in the wrist | Do more extensions than curls if curls and chin-ups already load the inner forearm. Use standing wrist curls if seated ones hurt.
FOR-08 | Do curls and extensions even if you already do hammer curls and carries | Direct forearm work costs almost no fatigue; fat grips don't replace isolation.
FOR-09 | Forearm frequency: from cautious to "spam them" | Once work capacity is built, forearms recover fast; train them hard several times a week. "Forearms are the only thing to spam."
FOR-10 | Don't let forearm work sabotage pulling | Do grip work on lower or rest days, not before upper or deadlift days. Chin-ups pre-fatigue the brachioradialis, so isolate forearms fresh. Grip stamina is a currency; don't spend it all on holds.
FOR-11 | Isometric holds: about 15 s, heavy | Short heavy holds carry over better than long ones. High rack pulls held 5 s beat floor pulls for forearms.
FOR-12 | Dead hangs at the end of lower days | Dead hangs give the most aggressive stretch and decompress the spine; not always to failure.
FOR-13 | Brachioradialis needs neutral or pronated elbow flexion | The brachioradialis is underdeveloped in most people. Use reverse curls, hammer and pinwheel curls, towel hammer curls, slow pronated preacher curls, riser curls and pronation twists.
FOR-14 | Top-tier forearm picks (2023) | Preacher hammer curls (towel/hangman/rope, stop just below parallel), standing reverse curls, supinated forearm curls with sane range, pronation twist with a belt loop, dead hangs.
FOR-15 | Copy arm wrestlers and welders, not strongmen | They do huge volumes of loaded wrist flexion and get forearms that match their upper arms; copy their towel curls and pronation work but not their partial table-only ranges. He says strongman forearms come from bodyweight and systemic stress.
FOR-16 | Grippers are a low-fatigue accessory, not a size builder | Do them between squat sets or anywhere. They rank C tier: no negative, only one lump.
FOR-17 | Fat grips and towels skew pulls toward the forearms | Dropping them for 6 months shrank his forearms. They work on pull-ups, supinated/preacher curls, V-bar and skull crushers, but ruin hammer curls and don't replace isolation.
FOR-18 | Program farmer's walks like a squat or deadlift | They build forearms and traps but are taxing; put them at the start of a session or on their own day, not in a superset.
FOR-19 | Forearms take years | The best natural forearms belong to older lifters; like calves, forearms are used all day. Small forearms are not a lifelong curse.
FOR-20 | Poor forearm movements | Plate flips, newspaper twist, DB wrist twists, plate pinches, Zottman curls (pronation curls beat them), knuckle/fingertip push-ups.
FOR-21 | Rice-bucket training is for conditioning and wrist health | Not a size builder.
LEG-01 | Big legs need a heavy knee flexion plus a heavy hip hinge | Any squat pattern, hack, belt squat or leg press plus any heavy hinge. You can grow huge legs without squats or deadlifts specifically, but never without these patterns. The lower body needs few exercises.
LEG-02 | Quads grow from compounds | Naturals have big legs because they squat seriously. Quads are the easiest part to grow and a poor NO target.
LEG-03 | Don't be a bird-legged upper-body bro | Even upper-focused lifters can fit in a little leg work. Train legs for long-term robustness, not a hormone boost.
LEG-04 | His squat choice moved to heel-elevated and machine squats | Heel-elevated, SSB, belt, hack and split squats replaced the back squat; he back squats about once a month. Elevated heels give more quad stimulus at less fatigue.
LEG-05 | Quad emphasis on squats | A vertical torso, forward knee travel and a narrower, toes-forward stance bias the quads. A posterior-dominant 4-5 plate squatter may grow glutes and hamstrings instead; SSB squats are more posterior.
LEG-06 | Pick the squat style by build | High bar for most bodybuilders; low bar only if you can't high-bar (tall, long femurs). Low bar moves more weight through leverage, not more quad. Long-legged lifters whose squat turns into a good morning should hack squat.
LEG-07 | Split squats: his favourite leg movement | Best stimulus-to-fatigue of any leg movement. Treat them as heavy main compounds, third in the session, never closing a superset. Load them on an SSB, Smith or belt squat, not dumbbells. Forward lean biases glutes; belt-squat split squats are near-pure quad.
LEG-08 | Split squats for the VMO and teardrop | Most bodybuilders, him included, have overdeveloped outer quads; split squats, box front squats or leg extensions bring up the VMO. Toes-out heavy squats build the outer sweep.
LEG-09 | Leg rep ranges (2024) | Machine and unilateral squats go higher; the barbell squat stays moderate because high reps are too taxing on cardio.
LEG-10 | Quad tier list (2023) | Top: heel-elevated, Platz, feet-forward Smith squats, machine belt squat, pendulum hack/leg press, Bulgarian split squat, leg extensions, deficit sumo squat, SSB pause squat. A: high-bar, front, hack, barbell hack, sissy, split squats, seated leg press, low-box squat. B: low-bar, 45° leg press, improvised belt squat, reverse hack, Jefferson, Zercher. C or lower: shrimp, single-leg press, king squat, walking lunges, overhead/Bosu squats, vertical leg press, step-ups, DB/banded leg extensions, sumo deadlift.
LEG-11 | Front squats: loved, but not worth it for most bodybuilders | Too technical; hack or belt squats do the job better. Fine as the main squat if poor hip internal rotation forces a duck-footed back squat. The box front squat is the version he likes.
LEG-12 | Box squats are mostly poor for hypertrophy and novices | They turn into a wide-stance hinge. Exceptions: box front squats, a close-stance parallel box squat for carryover, and a strict low box (A tier in 2023).
LEG-13 | Pause squats are the best squat accessory | The SSB is ideal for them, but they shouldn't be your only squat.
LEG-14 | Leg extensions: from knee-risky to top-tier isolation | Leg extensions are true quad isolation. Never open leg day with them; leg press and extensions duplicate each other.
LEG-15 | Leg press and hack squat are legitimate builders | Only a minor gap to the squat. Hack squat for those avoiding squat technique, leg press for bad backs; never both in one day.
LEG-16 | Lunges: reverse lunges good, walking lunges worst | The reverse lunge is top tier and can replace RDLs; walking lunges are the worst single-leg option. Load lunges; don't use them as pump finishers.
LEG-17 | Calisthenics can't build maximal legs | Pistols and shrimp squats fall short; split squats beat them.
LEG-18 | Trap-bar deadlift: fine for novices, weak for bodybuilding | It reduces the hinge and is "useless for quads"; he dislikes it.
LEG-19 | Adductors are worth isolating | They add size around the thigh.
LEG-20 | Squats don't build hamstrings | Heavy hip hinges and knee flexion do. Deadlifting gave him a hamstring sweep, but conventional deadlifts lack a stretched negative, so add RDLs and leg curls.
LEG-21 | A hip hinge is necessary in every program; leg curls can't replace it | Someone doing only leg curls ends up smaller and weaker. Any hinge counts, back extensions included.
LEG-22 | Do as much or more hamstring work than quad work | He recommends this for knee health. Leg curls alone create an imbalance, so include heavy posterior-chain work.
LEG-23 | The RDL became his king posterior-chain lift | The deadlift became an accessory to the RDL; the RDL is top tier in every style.
LEG-24 | Deadlift verdicts by year | For hypertrophy he sees the deadlift as fatiguing for what it gives; drop it if your proportions don't suit it (strength view: see ch. 7).
LEG-25 | Deadlift programming: low reps, low volume, once a week | Open the day with it, keep reps low and let RDLs take the higher reps. Never high-rep deadlifts (thoracic extension gives out first); twice a week is redundant; don't pair heavy deadlifts with RDLs on one day.
LEG-26 | RDL and stiff-leg prescriptions | RDLs strain easily at very high reps. They can go to failure as the only heavy hinge. Stiff-legs are more strength-oriented, RDLs more hypertrophy (stretch). His RDL is a full-range "reverse deadlift" from the rack.
LEG-27 | Squat then RDL; never RDL and good morning on one day | Squat followed by RDL is the "match made in heaven"; rotate RDL and good morning weekly. Don't pair good mornings with back squats (front squats are fine).
LEG-28 | Good mornings are great in moderate reps | More stretch than the RDL but worse risk/reward; never heavy 5s or 1RMs; the SSB is the best bar. Load caps like "two plates" are nonsense.
LEG-29 | Hyperextensions are top tier | They teach the hinge, are easy on the back, and progress for years (bodyweight → DB → barbell); loaded back extensions can be the main posterior-chain builder. Snatch-grip hyperextensions are on his every lower day.
LEG-30 | Leg curls: from second-best to top-tier free volume | The lying leg curl has a better per-hamstring stimulus-to-fatigue ratio, though he trains legs without curls himself. Curl after the hinge, never as the opener; pick seated or lying and progress it. GHR top tier, Nordics A, razor curls B, dumbbell leg curls bad.
LEG-31 | Hamstrings respond to high reps (2020) | In 2020 he used high reps for hamstring work, supersetting curls with extensions.
LEG-32 | If glutes and lower back steal the hinge, bias the hamstrings | Use hyperextensions, leg curls, and RDLs with the bar pushed into the quads or further forward; or pre-exhaust glutes and lower back with heavy rows before SLDLs.
LEG-33 | Underdeveloped posterior chain: open leg day with a hinge you enjoy | Good mornings, any RDL, or hyperextensions.
LEG-34 | The single-leg RDL is underrated | His favourite hamstring lift in 2023: more stretch, little lower-back load, rehab-friendly. Use a band or belt squat for balance.
LEG-35 | Kettlebell swings: heavy, not CrossFit style | Heavy swings are top tier for hamstrings (2023); they don't build lats.
LEG-36 | Pull from the floor in one style that suits you | Conventional, sumo, trap bar, Jefferson or block pulls; pick one and progress it rather than training sumo and conventional both. Tall lifters: sumo or blocks. He tried sumo for 8-12 months and dropped it. Above-knee rack pulls aren't a hinge.
LEG-37 | The snatch-grip deadlift is his favourite deadlift variation | It is harder, with more upper-back stretch and more quads. Deficit pulls work posterior chain and quads deeper, block pulls more upper back; don't do both in one day.
LEG-38 | Heavy squats and hinges are the best glute builders | Glutes can't really be isolated and any lower-body movement grew his; most men don't need glute isolation. Choose movements that force glute use rather than tweaking squats. Women squatting and hinging in a surplus will build big glutes.
LEG-39 | Hip thrusts: from fine add-on to redundant | By 2024 there is almost always a better option; split squats beat hip thrusts when time is short. Hip thrusts beat glute bridges (range); single-leg hip thrusts should be loaded.
LEG-40 | Best glute builders in his view | Reverse lunges (SSB reverse lunges "destroy the glutes"), forward-leaning split squats, reverse hypers, butt blaster. Poor: donkey kicks, frog pumps, banded crab walks, lateral lunges/step-ups, abductor machine (adductor machine is better).
LEG-41 | Big glutes are injury insurance | He calls well-developed glutes protection against injury.
CLF-01 | Calves stay small because they aren't trained | Once trained, calves grow; small calves are bad training, not genetics. Squats don't grow calves.
CLF-02 | Calves need lots of load because they carry you all day | Bodyweight raises add too little; overweight people grow calves from the load they carry.
CLF-03 | Train calves several times a week | Once a week won't work; he prefers high frequency over high volume at low frequency.
CLF-04 | Calf reps: mostly 10-20, heavy work 6-12 | Volume matters more than intensity; don't go very low because the Achilles is fragile. Sets of 10-15 must be weighted.
CLF-05 | Calves heavy vs light | By 2024: progressive overload on two loaded raise variations. A good calf exercise is easy to load, allows a deep stretch and slow tempo, and needs little balance or cardio.
CLF-06 | Superset calves between other sets; never leave them for the end | Calf work done "if I feel like it" never happens. He does most calf volume in upper-day giant sets; calves don't hurt squat or deadlift performance. Never open a session with calves.
CLF-07 | Novices can skip calves; intermediates must start | He waited nine years and regrets it: "The right moment is now."
CLF-08 | Calf tier list (2024) | S: standing two-leg raise with the foot elevated, slow tempo, high reps, machine or Smith; calf press on the leg press; weighted StairMaster on tiptoes. A: single-leg raise, 4-5 min tiptoe farmer's walk with straps. B: seated raise, biking with heels off, calf launcher. C/D: donkey raises, running/jump rope, plyometrics, bodyweight raises, DB hops. Padded standing machines are often too vertical.
CLF-09 | Calf variations | Toshiki (explosive) and Piana (squeeze, slow negative) raises; seated EZ-bar or DB-on-knees finishers; seated leg-press raises; SSB and belt-squat raises. When two raises compete, the first one progresses most.
CLF-10 | Calf NO | Most NO sets should go to calves.
CLF-11 | Calves should roughly match the upper arm | He cites Reeves' proportion ideal; his own calves would need to reach about 20-22 in to look big.
CLF-12 | Tibialis raises 2-3x a week | Use a machine, slow negatives and a big stretch. The tibialis can't get monstrous but becomes visible from the side.
ABS-01 | Abs need direct isolation | Compounds and bracing don't build a six-pack; bracing gives an isometric contraction with no stretch. Ab isolation is "non-negotiable", and every program of his includes at least one.
ABS-02 | "Abs are made in the kitchen" is wrong | Dieting only reveals abs; abs that show only when skinny are a "skinny pack". Trained abs stay visible year-round and at higher body fat.
ABS-03 | Train abs year-round, best during a bulk | Abs grow under fat in a surplus, so a bulk is no excuse.
ABS-04 | Ab frequency: 2-4 times a week | Frequent ab work, but no longer daily; never once a week.
ABS-05 | Load abs and progress them at about 6-12 reps | Weighted, progressively overloaded ab work replaced high-rep daily sets.
ABS-06 | The stretch is the key ab-hypertrophy factor | You already get the contraction from bracing. Crunches (thoracic-only flexion) and floor crunches with knees up miss the stretch; the sit-up pattern (fixed pelvis and feet, whole spine flexing) is "the exercise". Criteria: good stretch, high effective range, easy to load and progress.
ABS-07 | Best ab exercises: weighted decline sit-ups and hanging raises | Weighted decline sit-ups and crunches, hanging leg and knee raises, kneeling cable crunches and the ab crunch machine. Weighted knee raises (dumbbell between the feet) replaced leg raises once those got too heavy. Sit-ups built his abs the most.
ABS-08 | Leg and knee raises do train the abs | Hip flexors initiate, but the abs catch the pelvis and legs on the negative; hip-flexor involvement is no reason to reject an exercise. Raises made the biggest difference to his lower abs and decompress the spine.
ABS-09 | Few ab exercises, progressed | Pick 1-2 bracing and 1-2 stretching ab exercises and progress them rather than many variations. Repeating only one movement can limit growth.
ABS-10 | Placement: after heavy lower compounds or supersetted, never left for last | Abs left for the end or for home get skipped; if you must cut a block, move abs earlier. Superset them with presses and pulls (hanging leg raises with pull-ups or rows decompress the spine). Hack squats pair with abs better than barbell squats; do less ab work on heavy deadlift days.
ABS-11 | Pullovers before sit-ups pre-stimulate the abs | Pullovers stretch the abs without draining their strength; he calls them an underrated core movement and does them weekly.
ABS-12 | Planks: low value for size | Planks teach novices to brace but become redundant with heavy compounds; prefer dynamic stretched movements. If kept, use long-lever planks, put them on upper days or in a circuit.
ABS-13 | Train the obliques; they won't widen the waist | Waist width is hip bone structure and love handles are fat; bigger obliques tighten the side and make love handles look smaller. Wide-waisted lifters should isolate them all the more.
ABS-14 | Oblique exercises: twists, suitcase deadlifts, full-range side bends | Russian twists, twisting or slow-tempo sit-ups, suitcase deadlifts (unilateral load hits them harder with less weight), and full-range side bends on a hyperextension bench (top tier). Standing DB side bends, twister machines and windshield wipers are poor.
ABS-15 | Squats, deadlifts and pull-ups grow the obliques through stabilization | His obliques got big early on without isolation.
ABS-16 | A long torso or narrow frame makes the core the focal point | A long torso, long abdomen or narrow frame makes the core the focal point, so prioritize it.
ABS-17 | Lack of core development is the most common weakness he sees | It is the most frequent weak point in his physique reviews.
ABS-18 | Gymnasts get abs from constant core control | L-sits, the hollow body and little rest between reps. Calisthenics abs look good partly because those athletes are lean; gaining weight exposes undeveloped abs.
ABS-19 | Ab and lower-back days are idiotic | Spread ab work across the week; an "abs and cardio" day is filler.
NCK-01 | Everyone should train the neck directly | The neck ties the physique together, helps facial aesthetics, and protects you in crashes and contact sports. It grows fast on little volume, shrinks fast if you stop, and compounds stall it. A pencil neck "costs nothing" to fix; the sleep-apnea worry is a myth.
NCK-02 | Use neck curls and extensions, not bridges | Bridges carry too much fatigue and injury risk (wrestlers already bridge). A harness was far better than plates or bands. Front neck: lie across a bench with a slow tempo.
NCK-03 | Neck variations | Band neck extensions driven by a hip hinge feel safer; a bent-over harness extension while holding a bar adds mid and lower traps.
NCK-04 | Neck reps: 10-20, for safety | Keep neck sets in a moderate-high range; never below 10 or above 20.
NCK-05 | Neck frequency: 1-2x a week on lower days | One or two sessions a week already gives great results; more than 3-4 is pointless and risks overuse.
NCK-06 | Superset neck with leg exercises so it's never skipped | The neck is almost never limiting; adherence is the real problem. Pair one neck exercise with the first lower-body lift.
NCK-07 | Neck directions | He now trains flexion and extension only; rotation matters only for drivers and combat athletes. Tuck the chin before flexion if your posture is poor.
NCK-08 | Neck and trap work help each other | Training one supports the other.
NCK-09 | Don't overdo the neck on a square jaw | A neck should roughly match the jawline.
STR-01 | Strength and size are different qualities | Strength and size are different qualities with some crossover, and training for one is not training for the other. Two lifters who add the same amount to a lift end up different sizes, and the one focused on volume and tonnage is bigger.
STR-02 | You can't grow without getting stronger | Strength is not the bodybuilder's goal, but you will have to get stronger to grow. Size and strength both rise, one faster than the other.
STR-03 | Within a person, strength reflects muscle | For one person on long-practised lifts, strength gains reflect muscle gains (bigger legs mean a bigger squat), and small size increases bring surprisingly large strength increases. Between people, leverages dominate, so you can't read strength from size.
STR-04 | Strength athletes train functions, bodybuilders train muscles | A powerlifter squats to train his squat, and a bodybuilder squats to train quads, glutes and hamstrings. In his bucket picture, powerlifters drain the muscle buckets to fill the big nervous-system bucket, and bodybuilders do the reverse.
STR-05 | Specialising in one costs the other | No program maximises both size and strength. At equal bodyweight bodybuilders are weaker and powerlifters stronger, which he puts down to training, not genetics, and no natural bodybuilder holds powerlifting records.
STR-06 | Bodybuilders have "accidental strength" | Bodybuilders are weaker than powerlifters but much stronger than average. Powerlifters are small for their strength and imbalanced: big legs, chest, front delts and traps, with small arms and parts of the back.
STR-07 | Strong lifters need a higher threshold before size shows | Lifters gifted for strength have to get much stronger before size shows. They should use that strength to add sets and reps, not to max out. For a naturally weak lifter like him, strength gains turn into size.
STR-08 | Prefer rep strength over 1RM strength | A 10RM tracks muscle mass better than a 1RM, which mostly reflects the nervous system, so bodybuilders should focus on rep strength. 1RM calculators built from strength athletes don't apply to them.
STR-09 | Size over numbers | He would take a big chest over a 500 lb bench. He isn't proud of any lift and says he "doesn't care about strength".
STR-10 | Neural gains are front-loaded and capped | Neural and technical gains on a new lift mostly arrive early. After that you are at 95-100% technical mastery, and further strength at the same technique is muscle. Saying all strength gains could be neural is "wrong and demoralizing".
STR-11 | Learning or rotating lifts gives artificial progress | Progress on a new or rotated lift is mostly relearning, not muscle. A box lets you think you are stronger at the squat. A second +100 lb on the bench brings far more muscle than the first.
STR-12 | Strength is largely skill | Strength is largely the skill of recruiting motor units in a pattern, and it needs a fresh nervous system. High-frequency programs (daily squatting, Bulgarian or Wisconsin-style) raise one lift without much muscle, and he never prescribes them for size.
STR-13 | Big strength jumps without visible change are a red flag | Past the novice stage, a big percentage strength gain with no muscle gain is biologically impossible. Strength that jumps while nothing visibly changes is a red flag.
STR-14 | Strength detrains faster than size | Time off costs strength and work capacity well before it costs size. Presses, especially the OHP, regress quickly when not practised.
STR-15 | Fat-driven strength isn't real progress | Mass moves mass, but strength gained by adding mostly fat raises absolute strength, not pound-for-pound strength. It is leverage, and it disappears on a cut.
STR-16 | Bodyweight helps presses far more than hinges | Extra bodyweight helps pressing through leverage (a shorter range of motion) and helps hinges much less. In a deficit, hinges barely suffer while bench and OHP drop a lot.
STR-17 | Pure strength needs mass, preferably muscle | For pure strength, carry as much mass as possible, preferably muscle. Getting really strong means letting bodyweight rise, so staying very lean ("otter mode") means never getting really strong or reaching full size.
STR-18 | You can get stronger without gaining weight | Strength can keep rising at a stable bodyweight. He uses his own lifts as proof.
STR-19 | Pound-for-pound matters | Relative strength matters. Wilks-type scoring favours light lifters, and without it big frames that bulk would dominate. Being lighter also makes calisthenics easier.
STR-20 | Leverages dominate lower-body numbers | Long femurs or a long torso make squats hard and deadlifts easier, and arms past the knees would make a deadlift world-record holder. Tall lifters have more absolute strength and shouldn't compare themselves with short ones, but short lifters keep a squat advantage through range of motion. A bigger structure (wide hips, big joints) means more strength potential.
STR-21 | Strength work is a year-round pillar | A small dose of heavy strength work is the backbone of hypertrophy training and stays in all year, not only in a block. It is little tonnage, but its high-intensity peaks drive progression.
STR-22 | Strength work opens the session | Strength work comes first, and a day with zero strength work is bad, with occasional exceptions. Pre-activation must be light: an explosive opener taken short of failure (a kettlebell swing) primes you, while a leg-curl opener only fatigues. A small dose fires up the nervous system for the accessories.
STR-23 | Strength work is a minority of the tonnage | Strength work is a small share of total tonnage, and the rest is hypertrophy work. Strength exists to drive tonnage. Switching back and forth between strength and hypertrophy phases is "like living with divorced parents".
STR-24 | Strength sets are solo sets | Never superset or giant-set heavy squats, deadlifts or top sets. At most pair them with neck or calves. His bench depends on focus.
STR-25 | Plan strength-work progression carefully | Plan how reps and sets evolve more carefully for strength work than for volume work, because it opens the session and affects everything after it.
STR-26 | No strength blocks for bodybuilders | Bodybuilders don't need strength blocks. "Build a strength base first" is a lie, because strength grows on a bodybuilding program anyway. Bodybuilders don't peak, so peaking practices don't transfer, and speed work at 40% 1RM is junk volume.
STR-27 | "Get strong" must name the lifts | Advice to "get strong" must say which lifts. Unless the answer is every lift for every muscle, ignore it. Trying to get as strong as possible, with good form, on every movement for every muscle *is* bodybuilding.
STR-28 | Bodybuilding progresses strength more slowly | A bodybuilding program raises strength more slowly, because effort is spread over more muscles and the goal is different, but it develops every body part. Powerlifting maximises strength, while bodybuilding keeps it stable to support steady tonnage.
STR-29 | The 85% rule doesn't maximise strength on purpose | His 85% rule deliberately doesn't maximise strength gains, because doing so would minimise volume accumulation.
STR-30 | Train weak points, not flattering variations | Train your weak points, not variations that flatter your strengths.
STR-31 | Approach the bar certain it goes up | Walk up to the bar certain that the programmed weight will go up. Since adopting this, he has never failed a squat or deadlift recorded in his notebook.
STR-32 | Swap the main lift occasionally | Swapping a main lift for a while (squat → good morning, bench → weighted push-up) is fine, and a week off won't cost strength.
STR-33 | Chasing numbers was his worst period | His worst years were the ones spent chasing numbers: constant injuries, poor gains, and a rounded-back pull when he was obsessed with the number.
STR-34 | Deadlift: few sets, never more than 5 reps | Deadlift for 1-3 sets of 2-5 reps, never more than 5, and a single heavy set of 5 is often enough. 3x3 works very well if you leave reps in the tank.
STR-35 | Novice deadlift: sets of 6, no high-rep maxes | Novices can deadlift sets of 6. Don't do 12-rep maxes on the conventional deadlift, where cardio and structure give out before the muscles.
STR-36 | Deadlift rarely; let other hinges do the work | His later practice is to deadlift from the floor rarely and get most posterior-chain volume from RDLs, good mornings and hyperextensions.
STR-37 | Squat: 4-8 reps, never singles to triples | Squat in the 4-8 rep range, never singles, doubles or triples, to avoid knee valgus and the back tipping forward. Ramping single sets of 5 to a top set beats 3 sets at the same intensity.
STR-38 | Bench: never below 4 reps | Never bench below 4 reps, and keep to 4-8 if your shoulders are temperamental. Close-grip bench is his favourite variation and progressed very fast.
STR-39 | Overhead press: low reps, never supersetted | As strength work, the overhead press is done for low reps and never supersetted. 12-15 reps is too many when it opens the day, and 3x3 gives less volume than he wants.
STR-40 | 3x3 for strength lifts only | 3x3 is a favourite strength scheme: it leaves little room to sandbag and is very intense. Use it for bench, squat, pull-ups and deadlift, not for tonnage lifts like rows or RDLs.
STR-41 | Weighted pull-ups: low volume, heavy | Weighted pull-ups are done for very low volume and high intensity. After leg day they also decompress the spine. His own loads are in ch. 1 and Appendix C.
STR-42 | Strength isn't only low reps | Mix rep ranges, because strength isn't built only with low reps. Successful powerlifters train varied ranges. Switching from high to low reps spikes strength short term, but long-term progression is what counts.
STR-43 | Reps at a given %1RM are individual | How many reps you can do at a given percentage of your 1RM varies between people, so online percentage and RPE calculators mislead.
STR-44 | Never test 1RMs as a bodybuilder | Maxing out is a powerlifting skill for peaking and brings no hypertrophy benefit. 1RMs don't even build strength (2-3RMs or 1 rep in reserve might), and every max-out uses up muscle-building capacity.
STR-45 | Estimate your max from reps | Estimate your max from rep performance and program by intensity. A double just under your max beats a single: double the tonnage, with less risk and fatigue.
STR-46 | Constant max testing stalls progress | Testing your max often "smashes the piggy bank": it spends what you have built instead of building more.
STR-47 | Specificity wins | The best way to bench more is to bench, and the best deadlift accessory is more deadlifting.
STR-48 | What carries over to the bench | Close-grip bench carries over and skull crushers barely do. Push-ups and ring push-ups don't build bench strength, but dips carry over a lot. A slingshot carries over little to raw benching, and using only a slingshot for 2 months could cost raw strength.
STR-49 | Dips as the pressing strength lift | He believes not everyone can reach a 4-plate bench, but everyone can reach a 4-plate dip. Dips don't hurt him, so far heavier dips feel achievable.
STR-50 | What carries over to squat and deadlift | Front squats carry over to the back squat less than people think, and pause squats carry over more. Front squats barely help the conventional pull beyond upper-back rigidity. A stronger squat helps the deadlift only up to a point. The SSB box squat in deadlift stance may be the best conventional-deadlift accessory, and sumo carries to conventional better than the reverse.
STR-51 | Pause reps are a variation, not a replacement | Pause reps build strength but force lighter weights and can't replace touch-and-go reps, so use them as variations. Even top lifters use variations, so pure minimalism fails.
STR-52 | Partials and odd lifts mask true strength | Partial range of motion can help the full lift, but partials and unusual lifts hide your true strength.
STR-53 | Machine numbers don't transfer | Machine numbers don't transfer to free-weight lifts. Leg presses of 500-600 lb are simply the kind of load legs should handle.
STR-54 | Squats are more taxing than deadlifts | With proper technique, squats are more taxing than deadlifts. Big deadlift-to-squat ratios come from sloppy pulls and timid squats.
STR-55 | Where heavy lifts fail | In hinges and squats you fail on the way up or at the turnaround, never on the way down. Many "mid-squat" failures are really a failure to build speed out of the hole. A rounded ("fishing-rod") position makes the lockout harder.
STR-56 | Grip: mixed, hook, straps and thick bars | A mixed grip can beat straps for some lifters. Powerlifters should give the hook grip a real try first, and thick-grip training makes a normal bar feel easier.
STR-57 | Strength standards are C-tier | He ignores strength standards unless you compete. They rest on unverified self-reports or powerlifter data and round plate numbers, and they ignore leverages, experience, the nervous system and timeline. They compare you to weak people and turn hypertrophy training into number-chasing. If you compete, use your sport's competition standards.
STR-58 | Size-from-strength standards are illogical | Standards that promise size from strength ("a 200 lb OHP gives big shoulders") are illogical; flip them and you get nonsense like "20 in arms means a strong curl". A 3-plate bench goal can keep a bodybuilder's chest small.
STR-59 | The 1/2/3/4 standard only fits novices | The 1/2/3/4-plate standard roughly holds for novices because the loads are low, but beyond novice, leverages make fixed numbers meaningless. The OHP step is far harder than the others.
STR-60 | Minimum baselines he endorses | A trained adult man should never drop below 20 push-ups. The average man can't do a pull-up or deadlift his bodyweight, and every able-bodied man should. A year of training should get you past a few push-ups and a light OHP, and any lifter can reach a ~400 lb pull.
STR-61 | Strength targets he links to growth | The strength gains he cites as signs of growth are rep targets on hypertrophy lifts, not 1RMs.
STR-62 | Round numbers create plateaus in your head | Beliefs like "a natural can't bench 400" and idolising round numbers create mental plateaus.
STR-63 | Rejects powerbuilding | Heavy big-three work plus high-rep accessories usually delivers neither size nor strength. It trains one narrow kind of strength (1RMs on three lifts) that has to dominate and drains energy, and most powerbuilders never compete. The fix is to drop the 1RM training, and then you are just bodybuilding the silver/bronze-era way. He debated Dr. Milo Wolf on this.
STR-64 | Train like a powerlifter, look like one | Training like a powerlifter makes you look like one: bottom-heavy and not aesthetic. Lifters chasing the big three often stay skinny-fat (the "Starting Strength problem"), and most powerlifters wrongly expect aesthetics. You don't need a 700 lb deadlift for a big back.
STR-65 | The big three are a small part of strength | The big three are a small part of the body's strength potential. Big naturals may be weak on them but strong on isolations and back work. The big three should express strength, not pigeonhole you.
STR-66 | "Strength copers" | He claims that lifters who chose strength because they couldn't build a physique, or who say "the best way to get big is to get strong", are lying to themselves.
STR-67 | What bodybuilders can learn from powerlifters | Powerlifters who stall add volume and run hypertrophy blocks, because strength eventually needs bigger fibres, and many have huge quads. Their peak-drop-peak cycle works for strength, not for tonnage.
STR-68 | Strength sports are minimalist, bodybuilding maximalist | Strength sports are minimalist by default and bodybuilding is maximalist. The more specific the need, the more minimalist the method. Bodybuilding and powerlifting are "cousins who shouldn't mix".
STR-69 | Strongman is a better strength test | Strongman tests strength better than powerlifting, and a strength athlete who never competes isn't one. Former strongmen and powerlifters in their 30s-40s fit well into bodybuilding.
STR-70 | Modern strength athletes carry lean muscle | Modern strength athletes recomp into lighter classes and carry lean muscle, and a fluffy lifter loses to leaner lifters in his class.
STR-71 | Unverified lifts don't count | A lift with no video, witness or record doesn't count, and neither does one with bands, chains, boxes, cambered bars, above-parallel squats or unweighed plates. Weigh and load plates on camera, ideally at a sanctioned meet, and claim records only in competition.
STR-72 | Strength claims need bodyweight | A strength claim means nothing without the lifter's bodyweight. In his view, IFBB pros at 250-270 lb pulling ~600 lb are weak.
STR-73 | Fighters: hypertrophy helps, max testing doesn't | Lifting doesn't make you a fighter, but hypertrophy carries over to fighting, while fight training does nothing for size. Strength matters more for grappling than striking. Fighters shouldn't test 1RMs (higher reps, less injury), and their staple is any floor pull for low reps.
STR-74 | Muscle multiplies power, if it's the right muscle | Muscle multiplies power, but only the muscle the sport uses. Olympic lifts are counterproductive for athletes (bad racks, 1RM testing), and a rugby player should keep lower-body compound volume low.
STR-75 | Manual workers still need strength training | Blue-collar workers still need strength training, because the job trains only certain qualities. Even with a grip-heavy job, use straps or a brief heavy hold.
STR-76 | Women should strength train every pattern | Women should strength train every movement pattern. They get very strong without ballooning.
STR-77 | Male maximal strength peaks around 35-40 | Male maximal strength peaks around 35-40, going by most past Olympic lifters and powerlifters.
AES-01 | Bodybuilding is beauty through illusion, not mass | Bodybuilding pursues beauty through illusion, not a pile of mass or big measurements; flow, insertions and posing beat raw size.
AES-02 | Train with aesthetic intent or get an accidental physique | Pure strength, big-three-only or compound-only training gives an "accidental" physique: big trunk and quads, small arms (spider, fridge, frog). A minimalist strength routine won't build an aesthetic body. His own 2020 big-three-only experiment confirmed it.
AES-03 | Training for looks is legitimate, not vain or feminine | Anyone who trains for looks is a bodybuilder. Wanting to look good is legitimate, and calling it vain or feminine is shaming that pushes men toward neglecting themselves.
AES-04 | No purely aesthetic stimulus exists | You train function and orient it toward looks; a developed muscle always functions, and a stronger curl means a bigger biceps.
AES-05 | Maximizing form costs function, and vice versa | Pick form or function as the priority and accept getting only some of the other. Size and function correlate only when the sport dictates size; he accepts being slower and less athletic.
AES-06 | Athleticism isn't a look | A six-pack shows leanness, not athletic ability; strongmen and fat athletes have strong cores under the fat. His "sumo test": a sumo wrestler's legs are athletic.
AES-07 | Have a dream physique, ideally a fictional one | Pick a dream physique and build your program around it, preferably from statues, paintings, manga or comics rather than real people. Dreamers' results skew toward their dream; an anime physique can be approximated by matching its muscle distribution.
AES-08 | Physique preference is subjective | Any goal is valid (small legs, a slim beach body); ideals exist to be broken and you need no permission. Push the muscles that serve the goal and maintain the rest.
AES-09 | Imbalances must be deliberate | Most imbalances are accidents, and "bodybuilding should not have accidents". Legs so small they detract from the physique have no merit, and long legs are no excuse.
AES-10 | Natural powerlifters and bodybuilders look alike for a reason | They look alike only because natural bodybuilders train like powerlifters. Aesthetic programs must include isolation and give presses more volume; squatting as much as pressing builds a big lower body.
AES-11 | Natural ideal is about 12-18% body fat | A natural should carry about 12-18% body fat with lots of muscle; stage condition is pointless for naturals. Old-school naturals and physique competitors 100 years ago were in this range and looked tremendous.
AES-12 | His own body fat sits around 16-18% | He keeps a visible six-pack and oblique and serratus separation at 16-18% and credits direct core training, not genetics. People guess 12% because of his muscle-to-fat ratio.
AES-13 | He never cared about being very lean | Being very lean was never his priority. Fat on the arms helps them look big when relaxed; by stage standards he is fat, but normal people see a six-pack.
AES-14 | To look big at rest, carry some fat | Muscle plus some fat is what makes a physique look big at rest; stripping it just makes you look small. Most admired naturals carry a fair amount of fat, and on a skinny man muscle plus some fat transforms the look.
AES-15 | Too much fat ruins the look; a gut kills big limbs | There is no point being big at too high a body fat. If your stomach protrudes past your chest you aren't in shape; excess fat goes to the waist and love handles.
AES-16 | More muscle makes the same body fat look leaner | Two people at the same body fat look different; on a large lean mass, trading fat for muscle at the same weight looks far better. Fat loss during recomposition is hard to judge by eye.
AES-17 | People underestimate body fat by about 5 points | Subtract about 5 points from body fat claims. Knowing the exact figure is useless; norms are built on untrained people and no measuring method is best, so look and feel good.
AES-18 | Visible detail needs muscle size, not just leanness | What shows depends on muscle size and fat pattern as well as body fat. Lower abs and serratus need size; to get striking abs, "grow them" rather than shred.
AES-19 | Veins come from muscle growth | Existing veins enlarge around muscles as they grow (his biceps, lats, shoulders); you need to be fairly lean but not extremely lean.
AES-20 | Apollonian V-taper vs Herculean X-taper | The Greek tradition has two branches: Apollonian (wide shoulders and lats, small waist, smaller legs, low fat) and Herculean/Farnese (thicker waist, big limbs, legs and core). He prefers the Herculean, ancient "bear mode"; the Farnese Hercules is closer to his ideal.
AES-21 | His "Berserker" ideal: big limbs, strong core, upper back | Massive arms, forearms and legs slightly out of proportion with the trunk, a strong core, developed upper back and smaller shoulders. Big limbs pull attention from the torso, an illusion.
AES-22 | Arms should look bigger than the shoulders | He dislikes big round delts on smaller limbs; the arm should dominate the shoulder.
AES-23 | Classical/silver-era alternative | A classical look has smaller legs and posterior chain, a tight waist and a V-taper from shoulders and lats; the silver era didn't chase glutes or hamstrings. Use upper/lower plus arm/upper hybrids instead of two lower days.
AES-24 | Steve Reeves is his top natural reference | Reeves followed Greek proportions and stopped training calves until his arms caught up. His target is slightly bigger than Michelangelo's David.
AES-25 | As one area grows, its neighbours must follow | Growing arms need a growing chest or it looks flat; big pecs with spaghetti arms look bad; fix lagging arms without swinging to huge arms on a small torso. Flow dies with big quads and small calves, or big biceps and small forearms.
AES-26 | Flow matters more than muscle size | How the whole frame connects matters more than individual size. The core makes or breaks it, while neck and calves can lag without hurting it much.
AES-27 | Cheerleader effect | One outstanding muscle makes the weaker ones around it look better.
AES-28 | Novices grow everything; advanced lifters must choose | Beginners can grow every muscle at once. Past a point, assess objectively where you can slack off to put volume elsewhere.
AES-29 | Limbs make the bodybuilder | No real bodybuilder has small arms, and big arms are what normal people lack. A big back and chest without arms looks big but not like a bodybuilder; only a huge V-taper gets away with small arms. Arms and forearms are always visible in a T-shirt.
AES-30 | Isolate the arms or become a "spider" | Powerlifter-written hypertrophy programs neglect the triceps long head, biceps, forearms, rear delts and traps, producing a big torso on spaghetti arms. Add isolation for them.
AES-31 | Triceps long head is top tier | The long head is the biggest part of the arm and visible from every angle relaxed. It props up the biceps and its peak, adds width and back-pose thickness, and links with lats and rear delts (the "pitbull" look).
AES-32 | Forearms are top tier | Forearms are always exposed, don't depend on flexing, carry little fat and look vascular even when fluffy. A big wrist makes them look blocky; deadlifts build forearms less than people think.
AES-33 | Biceps are only second tier; brachialis adds thickness | Everyone trains biceps, sleeves hide them, they depend on flexing and give less thickness than triceps. The brachialis is the hidden MVP of arm thickness.
AES-34 | His arm and forearm targets | He aims for 19 in arms, since 20 in is "mythical" for him, and 15 in forearms. Long arms need about 2 extra inches to give the same effect.
AES-35 | His own biceps-triceps balance | His biceps outgrew his triceps; once the long head caught up he re-emphasised biceps, and he now accepts biceps dominance may never change.
AES-36 | V-taper toolkit | A V-taper comes from lats and shoulders: pullovers for the ribcage, pull-ups for outer lats, lateral raises and face pulls for side and rear delts, later upright rows and weighted pull-ups. Without lats you get a T-taper; pullovers and core work also fix an hourglass look.
AES-37 | Shoulders are top tier for daily looks | All three delt heads give the most width and look good at any body fat; the rear delt gives the shoulder its pop and 3D look. "Shelf muscles" (delts, traps, upper chest) give the bodybuilder look.
AES-38 | Aesthetic lifters over-hammer shoulders | Heavy shoulder focus is a habit borrowed from enhanced bodybuilders; naturals can have capped delts but not the bulky popping delts of steroid users.
AES-39 | You can never have too much upper back | A thick upper back marks a seasoned lifter; traps are top tier and growing them does not make you look narrower (he disagrees with Gironda here). "Bear mode" needs shrugs and more rows.
AES-40 | Width vs thickness | Width comes from outer lats and medial delts; thickness from mid/lower traps, lower back, chest and arms. Fix a flat-looking physique with chest, arms and traps. Erectors are second tier and show in clothes; ignore the lower-back "smile", which needs extreme leanness.
AES-41 | Chest is second tier, upper chest third | The chest fills the shirt; the upper chest is hidden by it, and he doesn't care about it. A flatter chest with dominant shoulders is a valid preference.
AES-42 | A better front usually means more front training | A front that outshines the back usually reflects training priority; golden-era aesthetics prioritised the front (chest and arms).
AES-43 | The core ties the physique together | The eye goes to the midsection first, and weak abs make everything look worse; without his abs, he says, his physique would look terrible. Never skip core, especially with a wide waist.
AES-44 | Waist width is mostly bone structure | Ab and oblique training won't ruin a V-taper; it only thickens the wall a little. Bodybuilders with thick obliques often had fat there first, and waist fat goes when you cut.
AES-45 | With a wide waist, build big abs and everything around it | Big abs read as a strong waist. Add a beefy back, delts for width, big arms and legs; with a poor structure, pack on mass rather than chase being shredded. Wide hips → obliques, lats, chest, shoulders.
AES-46 | Abs only count on a muscular body | The abs obsession produces over-dieting skinny guys (the Zyzz-era ab chasers) and guys who justify a gut. Abs matter only on a muscular body.
AES-47 | Do heavy squats and hinges thicken the waist? | He said heavy knee flexion and hip hinges develop the waist somewhat, so don't overdo them if you want it tight, though a few sets won't bloat you.
AES-48 | A big stomach at low body fat is usually bloating | Poorly digested food, water, two huge meals or breathing cause it. Building the core makes the stomach look smaller; vacuums only help with enough mass on top.
AES-49 | Neck standard: in line with the jaw from the front | From the front the neck should line up with the jaw; much bigger looks like a ball. Of three stages (pencil, intermediate/aesthetic, monster), intermediate is enough. Long necks need more work, and big traps on a pencil neck look wrong.
AES-50 | A thick neck is the #1 facial upgrade | A thicker neck gives a squarer jaw, a leaner-looking and more masculine face; a pencil neck makes a face look worse. Even non-lifters should train it; it is top tier. For a baby face: lift, train neck, grow a beard.
AES-51 | Jaw training isn't worth it | The jawline is mostly genetic and depends on leanness; jaw training stresses the teeth and causes tension.
AES-52 | Looksmaxxing: lifting is the base stat | Your body is the base stat and clothes and grooming are "buffs" on it; nothing requires paying anyone. Tailored jeans are worth it, and elastic athletic jeans suit big legs.
AES-53 | His thigh-size scale | He grades male thighs by circumference but warns not to obsess, since leg size depends on leg length. In 2020 his calves lagged too far behind his thighs.
AES-54 | Big legs make the waist look smaller | He likes big legs: they make the waist look smaller, are rarely developed and get noticed. Big male legs aren't feminine, and muscular legs can't be too big.
AES-55 | Legs shouldn't dwarf the upper body | The "frog physique" (big legs, small top) comes from PR-driven, squat-heavy or powerlifting training. Squat volume can be reduced, and training legs minimally once a week is fine for an upper-body goal.
AES-56 | Glutes are top tier for men | Glutes are the most 3D muscle, visible through pants and not lean-dependent; male glutes are square, store little fat and never hurt a male physique. Women look at them a lot.
AES-57 | Calves and quads second tier, hamstrings third | Calves and quads show in shorts and pants; hamstrings show only from behind. Obsessing over the VMO is "small guy syndrome".
AES-58 | Use genetic gifts to save time | If a muscle grows well from compounds (his quads from squats), skip extra work for it and isolate weak parts instead.
AES-59 | Rank muscles by how they look relaxed in clothes | Muscles that show in clothes without flexing and regardless of leanness rank highest (see table). Abs are bottom tier because clothes hide them and they depend heavily on leanness.
AES-60 | Judge a physique relaxed, in everyday clothes | Relaxed poses in everyday clothes are the real test, because you can't hide weaknesses there; stage flexing is not the reference.
AES-61 | Being short is an advantage for looking big | Shorter segments look more 3D and bigger with less muscle. On stage, though, tall freaks with good insertions usually win.
AES-62 | Frame is clavicles, waist, hips and joints | Frame means clavicle width, waist, hips and joint size. Small joints make muscle bellies look bigger: thick wrists shrink forearms, thick elbows blunt peaks, big knees stop the quad sweep. Greek proportions were scaled from the wrist.
AES-63 | Narrow frames: build the outline | With a narrow or small frame, build the outline (triceps long head, medial and rear delts, traps, neck) and big limbs that extend sideways, and use open poses. Narrow clavicles → prioritise arms and as much delt as possible.
AES-64 | Short stocky vs tall lifters | Short stocky lifters shouldn't chase shoulders and mass for their own sake; tall lifters need everything big, especially thickness from squats and deadlifts.
AES-65 | Mass tolerance depends on frame | A great frame wants minimal excess mass; a poor frame needs size even with some fat.
AES-66 | Real-world size perspective | Average gym-goers are lighter than people assume, so a relatively lean 200 lb already looks like you lift. A lifter training 10+ years for aesthetics shouldn't look early-intermediate, and a lean 165 lb lifter at 5'8" can look huge.
AES-67 | Pump, light, angle and posing change the look hugely | Window-light mirror shots flatter; at 220 lb he doesn't look it unpumped, and 20-30 lb lighter but pumped, people would say he grew.
AES-68 | Measurements: objective but misleading | Tape measurements track progress objectively, but pump and fat inflate them and big numbers can look worse than smaller ones. Use a locking tape and measure on dates fixed in advance. He claims Blaha's "18 in" arms are really 14-15 in and that Blaha wears a belt shirtless to fake a V-taper (his unverified opinion).
AES-69 | His physique review method | Identify strong and weak points; estimate body fat to choose cut, recomp or bulk; spot undeveloped high-potential parts; fix the program toward the goal. People misjudge their own strengths, and even stage physiques have at least two weak areas.
AES-70 | His grading scale | He grades each muscle S/A/B/C. He rates himself 6.5/10 and has never met a 10; he graded a top French natural 6/10 and was accused of unrealistic standards.
AES-71 | Posing matters | A nice physique without posing skill is wasted. Pressing a limb against something makes arms look bigger and leaner; lats push out the delts and triceps in poses.
AES-72 | Archetypes describe where the mass sits | He uses animal archetypes (spider, frog, otter, bear, whale, barbarian, hippo, lobster) to describe physiques by mass distribution and body fat (see table).
AES-73 | Otter mode is what most women pick | The otter look (Fight Club Brad Pitt) is what most women pick and what most people can reach; getting too big or strong takes you out of it. Stopping there stalls progression, but you see size coming and can stop.
AES-74 | Calisthenics builds statues but stays light | Calisthenics gives the most statue-like bodies outside bodybuilding, but its athletes stay light and need heavy pulls, shrugs, rows, legs, calves and arms to look like bodybuilders. Olympic lifters look aesthetic through core, shoulders and upper back.
AES-75 | Muscle is the best way for a man to look better | Muscle changes how people treat a man (halo effect); looking strong is effectively being strong socially, signalling work ethic and protection. "Looks don't matter" is a cope; he felt the shift at 15 and around 19, when at a party men greeted him and ignored the woman beside him.
AES-76 | Women like muscle, not mass monsters | Women like muscle (chest, shoulders, arms, abs, glutes) but not mass monsters or shredded steroid looks; many prefer bulky to shredded. "Too big" usually means enhanced or posturing; no woman ever told him he was too big, and strength numbers don't impress them.
AES-77 | Lifting makes women more feminine, not bulky | Most women look better heavier with more muscle. Women's usual goals (lower body, toned arms, abs) need different programs.
AES-78 | Muscle alone doesn't fix dating | Personality still counts: average-looking funny, confident men do fine, and bragging about muscle puts women off.
AES-79 | The look takes years | Changes take months to show and years before people notice. A natural with 5-10 years of training looks far better than anyone on a beach, and a man's physical prime can run to about 40-45 if he keeps lifting.
AES-80 | Cosmetic surgery is a cop-out | Surgery is a cop-out except for loose skin and gynecomastia; work for a few years first. He reads ab implants from abs on a protruding gut that never change year to year, and butt implants from glutes out of line with squat strength.
INJ-01 | Injuries are preventable, not bad luck | Injuries come from variables you failed to control, so they are preventable; calling them bad luck is lazy and irresponsible.
INJ-02 | Volume/intensity mismanagement is the top cause | Ego, too much load and forcing the extra rep cause most injuries; together with poor positioning they account for the "vast majority". Perfect form cannot save poor volume/intensity management.
INJ-03 | Injury = load and fatigue exceeding tissue capacity | An injury is pressure exceeding what the tissue can handle, a failure to adapt to a cycle of stress. Risk depends on load and accumulated fatigue together.
INJ-04 | The "red zone" is where injuries happen | Acute injuries happen where the negative reverses into the positive; almost nobody gets hurt at lockout. Negative-first lifts (squat, bench) are the most dangerous at high intensity, and a controlled negative is injury prevention.
INJ-05 | Grinding the last rep causes most injuries | Most gym injuries come in the last grindy reps of heavy sets. With no grinding, staying in the rep range and no last-rep chasing, risk is "almost zero". All his own past injuries came from grinding reps he wasn't capable of.
INJ-06 | Overuse comes from repetition, not frequency | Overuse injuries come from repeating the same movement, angle, stance and load. Frequency itself is not the cause. Back-off sets in the same style as the main lift, minimalist HIT and Big-3-only training all feed tendinitis.
INJ-07 | Muscle should give out before tendon | Mechanical failure with weak technique shifts tonnage onto skeleton and tendons and causes tendinitis. Maximize stress on the muscle and minimize it on tendons; tendons are never fully spared, but there is a sweet spot.
INJ-08 | Never high volume, frequency and intensity together | You can combine two of high volume, high frequency and high intensity, never all three; the trifecta "summons injury".
INJ-09 | Life stress and sleep affect tendons | Life stress, poor sleep and alcohol promote tendon inflammation; his old tendinitis flares on stressful days with no training change.
INJ-10 | Form is "where", programming is "when" | Form is awareness in the moment, programming is awareness over time; injuries become predictable when a weak "where" meets a bad "when". His early videos covered only form.
INJ-11 | Injuries come from autopilot and surprise | Most injuries happen on autopilot or from unexpected motion or timing, so stay present and do the most dangerous lift first while fresh (see INJ-50).
INJ-12 | Partial-only training leaves deep ranges unprepared | If you only train partial range, a failed rep that drives you deep finds the muscle unprepared, and that is when pecs, backs and biceps snap. Train full ROM at least sometimes, deep, controlled and light.
INJ-13 | Freak injuries come from freakish choices | Freak injuries are rare and come from doing something "freakishly stupid", such as attempting a weight you've never approached. Structural failure mid-lift is "entirely avoidable".
INJ-14 | Most real injuries happen outside the gym | Most real-world injuries are domestic (boxes, lawnmowers, ice), not heavy gym lifts, because in the gym you're warm and primed.
INJ-15 | Tall, lanky lifters get hurt more | Tall lifters get hurt more because their strength outpaces recovery and longer levers put more torque on the lower back and knees.
INJ-16 | No lift is inherently dangerous | Danger depends on how, by whom and at what load a lift is done; rounding risk exists only for a given intensity, lift and person. Inactivity is more dangerous than heavy squats or deadlifts.
INJ-17 | Injury-prone people should adapt the stimulus | Some people are more injury-prone; if hard training keeps hurting you, change the stimulus rather than hoping your body changes.
INJ-18 | Injury prevention is the best way to grow | An injury is a gap in tonnage, which means no growth, plus a mental-health hit; preventing injury is the best growth strategy.
INJ-19 | Bodybuilding should be injury-free | You should be able to train 10 years with zero injuries and stay mobile to ~80; bodies wrecked in their 40s come from lifting badly. He claims Coleman's body came from not letting injuries heal.
INJ-20 | Tendons, not muscles, limit older lifters | Pain-free bodybuilding is how you keep growing with age; with training age, tendons become the limit (triceps limited by elbows, quads by knees).
INJ-21 | Tendons and spine need loading | Tendons and the spine exist to be loaded; underloading atrophies them and they fail in old age. There is no "tendon capital" to save, though overload gives tendinitis.
INJ-22 | Push through discomfort, never through pain | The cautious lifter goes further than the one who pushes through pain: "pushing through pain = ER, through discomfort = PR". Step back from a set that feels sketchy.
INJ-23 | Never train through joint or tendon pain | Joint/tendon pain is a warning; pushing through turns minor issues into lifelong ones, and joint pain never goes away by itself. If a movement hurts, stop and re-check form; if form changes don't fix it, drop the lift, since there is "always a way around".
INJ-24 | Muscle pain is fine, tendon/joint pain is an alarm | There are two kinds of pain: pump and muscle soreness (fine) versus tendon, ligament or joint pain (alarm). Tendons have more nerve endings because they repair poorly. Tension in the muscle is "more painful than dangerous"; pain at the tendon insertion is not.
INJ-25 | Hurt is not injured | Pain is not injury and doesn't tell you the degree of damage; judge by function, not sensation. Most times lifters think they're injured it is nothing, and an unnecessary break hurts them.
INJ-26 | Graded pain scales: occurrence and aftermath | He grades pain during the lift (occurrence) and after the session (aftermath), each with a matching response; see the table below.
INJ-27 | Pain is partly learned (nocebo) | Expecting pain on a lift creates it and obsessing makes it grow. Assess small pains and push them aside so you can spot real injury (music helps), but don't swing to "all pain is imaginary". After his ribs healed he still felt squat pain until he "unlearned" it.
INJ-28 | Joint noises rank low as warnings | Pain or a feeling that something shifted matters more than noise. Painless elbow clicking after tendinitis or sternum cracking on dips is fine.
INJ-29 | Judge a lift by next-day pain | Tendon and back irritation shows most the next morning (blood leaves the tissue overnight), so judge a lift by its aftermath, not by how it felt.
INJ-30 | Recurring pain fades or worsens | Recurring pain rarely stays the same: it either fades or worsens as a warning. Long-standing pain that never snapped is likely structural and under control.
INJ-31 | A low-back "pump" is SI overuse | A burning low-back or SI "pump" after deadlifts or squats is not a pump but SI-joint/tendon overuse from bad programming; stop before it sets in. He felt it every session on BBBS and no longer does with proper volume.
INJ-32 | Tendinitis is a spectrum ending in rupture | Tendinitis is the most common lifting injury, ranging from occasional pain to deep all-day pain. Ruptures (biceps, patellar, hip labrum) usually follow months or years of ignored tendinitis.
INJ-33 | Rest does not cure tendinitis | Rest only seems to cure tendinitis; it returns because the movement cause is unchanged. Immobility "fossilizes" the tendon and atrophies the muscle that shares its load. Take at most 1-2 days off when pain is bad, mainly to get over the emotional hump; longer builds a fear association.
INJ-34 | Avoid painkillers and anti-inflammatories | They mask the signal you need, and in his view anti-inflammatories weaken tendons and block adaptation ("not medical advice"). If you are on them anyway, avoid max singles and stay high-rep. He claims a powerlifter on painkillers squatted through months of hip pain until the labrum tore.
INJ-35 | Train in the pain-free "valley" | Train between inactive moderate pain and acute pain; this widens the pain-free window. End the session before pain turns acute. Warm-up blood flow eases pain, but if it returns mid-session you are re-aggravating it. Rehab is never painless; the pain becomes dull.
INJ-36 | Tendinitis is "a game of angles" | Find which movements load the painful tendon and switch to variations that work the muscle without pain. Reintroduce the painful movement unloaded; if it always hurts, ban it, because there are always equal alternatives.
INJ-37 | Blood flow and surrounding muscle fix tendons | Send blood to the tendon with light, high-rep pump work, and build the surrounding muscles and the antagonist, since an aggravated tendon is doing too much work. Only corrective work that stresses the tendon enough to adapt ends tendinitis for good. A pump matters more for tendon health than for growth.
INJ-38 | Fix the environment first | For tendon recovery, fix stress, sleep and badly managed rest in the program first. He also mentions MSM powder and more water.
INJ-39 | Pain gone after a deload = too tendon-heavy | Tendon pain that disappears after a deload means training is too tendon-heavy; the deload is only a band-aid.
INJ-40 | Cold and pressure changes worsen tendinitis | Tendinitis worsens in the cold, with pressure changes (flying) and when not warmed up. Healed injuries linger as tendon damage felt after poor sleep, on waking or in the cold.
INJ-41 | Prevent tendinitis rather than heal it | Preventing tendinitis matters more than healing it; this is his lesson from chronic elbow tendinitis.
INJ-42 | Ice helps tendinitis but won't cure it | Ice helps tendinitis, especially the exposed patellar tendon, but won't cure it alone, and it rarely reaches the rotator cuff/AC joint. In 2024, "despite the current consensus against icing", he says ice saved him every time.
INJ-43 | Icing rules | Use a reusable gel pack with a thin cover, never directly on skin. Build duration gradually, then stay still as long as you iced; never ice before or during activity.
INJ-44 | Ice timing after training | Let the tendon cool after training before icing, then move slowly afterwards.
INJ-45 | Cold and contrast showers help | Cold showers and contrast showers help sore tendons and the low back.
INJ-46 | Rotate variations, grips and stances | Rotating variations, grips and stances prevents overuse and is how he gains year-round; small joint-angle changes shift strain while training the same muscle. On machines, rotate grips and machines too.
INJ-47 | Risky lifts at higher reps | Put risky lifts in higher rep ranges so you can sense the bad rep coming. A 1RM concentrates risk into one all-or-nothing rep; an injury on rep 3 at 70% is milder, and a rounded 8RM deadlift is far less risky than at 1RM. 3RM and 10RM give the same hypertrophy, so pick the one you can progress safely.
INJ-48 | Small muscles and fragile structures get higher reps | Small muscles and tendon-heavy movements skew to the volume end; don't carry low isolation-style rep choices onto the wrong lifts.
INJ-49 | Strength work at ~1 RIR | Stopping strength sets at ~1 RIR cut his strength-work injuries to almost zero; strength sets taken to failure carry the highest injury risk.
INJ-50 | Dangerous lifts first, stabilisers fresh | High-danger lifts open the workout when you're fresh. Don't pre-fatigue stabilisers (abs, hamstrings, lower back) right before a compound that needs them.
