INJ-51 | No heavy squat then heavy deadlift | Heavy back squat then heavy deadlift in one day is a "death combo": squat fatigue rounds the thoracic spine on the pull. Don't progress squat and deadlift simultaneously; a heavy low-back lift followed by a floor pull, or barbell rows after deadlifts, is how people get hurt.
INJ-52 | Pull more than you press | Balance the body: pull more than you press, more posterior chain than quads, and hinges at least equal to knee flexion. There is no perfect ratio (not necessarily 2:1), but heavy pressers must pull roughly as much, and the type of pull matters, since weighted chins and plate rows don't train the scapular girdle.
INJ-53 | Repetitive Big-3 programs injure novices | Grease-the-groove and highly repetitive Big-3 programs (back squat 3×/wk, no variations) cause injuries in novices; the big lifts need rest between sessions.
INJ-54 | Train antagonists and opposite movements | Train opposite movements and antagonists, such as hand opening, wrist extension, and curls on triceps days.
INJ-55 | Imbalance = undertrained partner | Strengthening a muscle prevents injury; an imbalance is an undertrained counterpart, not a "too strong" muscle. Keep feeding the big muscle and train its partner more.
INJ-56 | Train joints in all directions | Train joints in every direction they move and fix weaknesses (e.g. unstable knees in a hinge) in the gym rather than avoiding them.
INJ-57 | Start light after changing stance, grip or equipment | When changing stance, grip or equipment, drop the load a lot at first; big changes are mainly dangerous when you jump to unfamiliar heavy weights.
INJ-58 | Warm up before anything heavy | Cold heavy sets cause strains; he strained a pec benching 135 cold after already handling 225-plus. Injuries at heavy weight are far worse than warm-up injuries.
INJ-59 | No static stretching before lifting | Static stretching before lifting is useless and risky on cold tissue; stretch only when warm or after training. Test 2 weeks without it, and keep a favourite stretch if you love it.
INJ-60 | Ramp back after a break | After a break, ramp back stepwise; jumping back to old weights is how he strained his patellar tendon and got sore knees.
INJ-61 | State your injuries when asking for critique | Injuries are critical programming information; include them when asking for program critique.
INJ-62 | Equipment and setup hazards | Flat soles only (banana-soled sneakers roll the ankles in and cause knee cave); bulky squat pads are more dangerous than bar discomfort; inflated Smith loads carried to free weights cause injuries; never re-rack by stepping forward before lockout; put heavy split squats in a rack with safeties, a Smith or a belt squat.
INJ-63 | "Strength restoration" between sets | Tiny, non-invasive band or light exercises in specific reps and angles target problem tendons, supersetted between sets of main lifts at almost no recovery cost. Never schedule them outside training or you'll skip them. Next-day soreness means you did resistance training instead. Start with one routine for your worst area.
INJ-64 | Dead hangs to decompress the spine | Decompress the spine with dead hangs or pull-ups at the end of squat/deadlift/lower days. Be warm (not cold before deadlifts), never jump into a hang, and avoid fully relaxed hanging, which can trigger SI spasms.
INJ-65 | Hypers + leg raises for back pain | Back hyperextensions plus leg raises is the "golden combo" for back pain (decompression, hip health).
INJ-66 | Hip-flexor stretch after leg days | Post-workout hip-flexor stretching after leg days is the only stretching he kept. Long sitting tightens them, which strains the low back and impairs squats and deadlifts; it fixed his bad hip-flexor pain. Stretch cautiously, since cold, short hip flexors can tear.
INJ-67 | The lower back is the top injury site | The lower back is the most common injury site in squats and deadlifts, from bad technique and too much frequency; low-back pain is the #1 reason people can't squat or deadlift for years.
INJ-68 | Squats hurt backs more than deadlifts | Squats cause more lower-back injuries than deadlifts (often via butt wink). Done properly, the deadlift is one of the safest lifts: it starts with the positive, you see form breakdown coming and you can drop the bar. "Deadlifts aren't bad for your back; deadlifting with bad form is". He has never been hurt deadlifting.
INJ-69 | Next-day back pain = wrong deadlift | Next-day low-back pain after deadlifts means a style you're not built for or improper deadlifting, regardless of load.
INJ-70 | Lower back as a stationary bridge | Use the lower back as a stationary bridge and drive with the legs; dynamic flexion under load injures, not a fixed angle. Pulling rounded from start to finish is less risky than a neutral spine that rounds mid-lift. Leg damage grows back; spinal/structural damage only deteriorates.
INJ-71 | Keep the thoracic spine neutral | Thoracic rounding leads to lumbar rounding (the spine gives up in stages), so keep the upper back neutral; strong thoracic extension is in his view the most important thing for staying healthy. He opposes rounded-back pulls for bodybuilders; elite outliers like Konstantinovs prove nothing for average lifters.
INJ-72 | Most "back injuries" are SI, nerve or muscle | Discs are hard to destroy; most "back injuries" are SI-joint, nerve or muscle issues. The SI joint needs recovery, so don't hit it every session; only lower-day programming and recovery fix it, and a belt does nothing for it.
INJ-73 | After a back tweak, keep moving | After a low-back tweak, keep moving: unweighted good mornings/squats, then find a pain-free lower movement (leg press, hypers, DB RDLs), then return to squats and deadlifts light. Complete rest atrophies the protective muscles; rehab with hyperextensions rather than good mornings.
INJ-74 | A strong back and core protect | "A stronger muscle is a safer muscle": lower-back isolation is recommended, and deadlifting/squatting is the best prevention for back and knee injuries. Someone who deadlifts 400 lb won't hurt his back picking something up; people in their 30s with back pain mostly have weak backs/cores.
INJ-75 | Good mornings: high risk, high reward | Good mornings top his danger list, since failure pulls you into loaded lumbar flexion with no safe bail, but they remain high-risk/high-reward, not banned. Never do them with a fatigued lower back. Deadlift negatives are also risky because you lower while fatigued.
INJ-76 | Never AMRAP a deadlift | Pulls from the floor make ugly reps too easy to grind, so never AMRAP a deadlift.
INJ-77 | Daily posture carries into lifts | Sit tall, use a lumbar pillow, keep the head neutral and don't slouch; slouching carries into lifts and causes back, shoulder and disc problems. Decline starts at the head, then thoracic spine, shoulders, lower back, so fix it in that order. Don't hyperextend into a rigid spine; change positions often.
INJ-78 | Scoliosis is no reason to stop lifting | Back and core muscles act as a brace, and he deadlifts heavy with scoliosis; posture can't fix bone. He believes his came from a one-shoulder backpack, and the asymmetry overloaded his lower shoulder, which was repeatedly injured on bench.
INJ-79 | Machines don't prevent back injuries | Smith squats can't self-correct, so you end in a loaded good morning, and leg press and lever machines can still hurt the back.
INJ-80 | Patellar tendinitis: pattern, frequency, muscle | Patellar tendinitis is fixed in three parts: correct the movement pattern, manage frequency and tonnage, and build the muscles that unload the tendon. Never by rest. Even perfect form wears tendons if frequency is too high.
INJ-81 | Box-squat and widowmaker rehab | His knee rehab: box squats progressed by reps then load, then "widowmakers"; stay in the widowmaker phase as long as possible and keep the rebuild maximally conservative. See the table below.
INJ-82 | Knee rehab habits and wraps | Morning knee extensions from small to full range, then knee bends; avoid impact sports, running on concrete and cat-style stretches that overextend the knee. Use short elastic wraps angled onto the tendon, not sleeves or hole-braces, and never too tight: X-shape for pain below the kneecap, around the kneecap for kneecap pain, wrapped inside → out. He still tapes his previously torn left knee when it flares.
INJ-83 | Signs of patellar tendinitis vs tear | Signs: pain at the knee break (not the rest of the rep), worse going up stairs, a warm/tender knee, sudden "giving way" when walking. Pain that fades once warm suggests tendinitis; pain that keeps rising suggests a tear.
INJ-84 | Squats aren't bad for knees | "Squats are bad for knees" is a boomer myth; deep squats are fine with controlled knee travel. Knee pain comes from bad form, too much frequency, fat and inactivity; the danger lies in knees shooting forward or caving out of the hole.
INJ-85 | Posterior chain protects the knee | Weak hamstrings and too-vertical, quad-only squatting prime knee injury; posterior chain work and hip hinges protect the knee.
INJ-86 | Knee-unfriendly options | Sissy squats, leg extensions (for some), pad-on-knee calf machines, Platz-style narrow toes-out squats and locking the knees every rep are knee-unfriendly. His own patellar injury came from jumping 225 → 275 in a narrower Platz-style stance after a break.
INJ-87 | Frequent heavy flat bench injures shoulders | Frequent heavy horizontal pressing is the main cause of shoulder injury regardless of form, unless you're an outlier. Injuries are almost always to the rotator cuff/AC joint, not the delts; he claims they are the #1 injury in retired powerlifters. The tell is an achy soreness between pec and shoulder the next day.
INJ-88 | Vertical pressing is shoulder-friendly | Vertical pressing done properly is easy on, even restorative for, the shoulder; end push days with it and press on top of you, not in front. Heavy OHP after bench made his shoulders "feel amazing".
INJ-89 | Rotate horizontal presses, don't cut them | Don't cut horizontal volume for sore shoulders; rotate variations: push-ups, paused/Spoto/close-grip/slingshot/floor press, DB bench, rings, and alternate push-ups and dips.
INJ-90 | Touch-and-go bench wrecked his shoulder | Touch-and-go bench protracted his right shoulder until at 24 he could barely raise his arm; paused bench, Spoto press and the Slingshot let him bench pain-free again.
INJ-91 | Fix bench mechanics for shoulder pain | For shoulder/AC pain fix the arch, slow negative, bar path and elbow tuck, keep both scapulae on the bench (narrow benches cause issues), and use a hand-off or lift-off rather than a traumatic solo unrack. He uses an in-between elbow angle, not a 90° flare; pain near the armpit means tuck the elbows.
INJ-92 | External rotation and upper back for pressers | External rotation, rear delt and upper-back work keep shoulders healthy, and heavy pressing needs it directly: an upper-back movement every push day. Keep rehab movements in even when pain is gone.
INJ-93 | Train shoulder end ranges light | The shoulder is weak at its end ranges: train high-mobility ranges light, never heavy. Sloppy heavy or internally rotated raises cause impingement; upright rows aren't inherently dangerous, the danger is a big-mover mindset.
INJ-94 | Avoid behind-the-neck and neck presses | Avoid behind-the-neck and guillotine/neck presses; going too deep disengages the lats and causes impingement.
INJ-95 | Don't stack shoulder-heavy work | Don't stack shoulder-heavy work: incline + weighted dips, heavy dips + heavy bench, or bench + OHP supersets.
INJ-96 | Kipping and overweight pull-ups hurt shoulders | Kipping pull-ups are a top source of shoulder/rotator cuff injury; overweight lifters also hurt their shoulders on pull-ups.
INJ-97 | Pullovers high-rep with shoulders supported | Keep pullovers high-rep with the shoulders supported; pain usually reveals pressing-tight shoulders.
INJ-98 | Lifters who can't press can still grow shoulders | Lifters who can no longer press can still grow shoulders with raises, especially cables, including people over 50.
INJ-99 | Warm the shoulder before squat and bench | Warm the shoulder before squatting or benching (band dislocates once warm, rotations, raises); pain present at the start tends to stay all session. Shoulder pain in the squat setup may come from benching, not mobility.
INJ-100 | Watch shoulder fatigue at high weekly volume | At high weekly volume, watch shoulder-joint fatigue; if pain starts the whole program goes down.
INJ-101 | Never overextend the elbow under heavy curls | Never fully extend or overextend the elbow under heavy load in curls; stop the negative short and keep a slight bend. Bouncing off the tendon at full extension does nothing for growth and causes distal tears. If the shoulder moves with the arm, overextension is safe; with the shoulder frozen (preacher curls) it is not.
INJ-102 | No heavy supinated pulling, no mixed grip | No supinated/Yates rows, supinated shrugs, or mixed-grip deadlifts or rows: the biceps gives before the back. Bodybuilders should use double overhand, then straps; straps prevent tears and asymmetry. He claims he's never seen a tear with double overhand; mixed grip also causes bar rotation, uneven traps and lower-back torsion. Vertical supinated pulls are generally fine.
INJ-103 | Distal biceps tears are permanent | Biceps tears at the distal attachment need surgery, and the biceps never looks the same.
INJ-104 | Pull-ups are limited by tendons | Pull-ups are limited by the elbow, biceps tendon and shoulder, not the back. Rotate grips and implements, and don't drop into a disengaged dead hang and rebound. Weighted chin-ups are the riskiest pull-up (supinated overextension under load). Going unweighted for elbow pain is only temporary.
INJ-105 | Light band curls for biceps tendon health | Light, low-anchored band curls (as many reps as possible, noticeable but not hard) keep the biceps tendon and elbow healthy and keep the deep range. Use them as a finisher on arm days or after heavy push days; never cold or explosive.
INJ-106 | Biceps tendinitis: stay pronated | With biceps tendinitis, stay pronated and reintroduce supination gradually; the cause is often presses, dips or pull-ups. Use an EZ bar if a straight bar hurts.
INJ-107 | Causes of elbow tendinitis | Elbow tendinitis comes from repetitive triceps-heavy pressing (close-grip bench), locking out every rep, deep French presses and skull crushers. He never locks out on bench. Avoid close-grip and football-bar pressing on back-to-back days.
INJ-108 | Elbow-friendly triceps choices | Prefer cables over EZ bar/dumbbells, rope or plate overhead extensions, DB or shoulder-extension skull crushers, the JM press, and high-rep, high-frequency band pushdowns; vary grip width. The JM press cured his elbow tendinitis (Todd Hutchings' advice).
INJ-109 | Prime cranky elbows first | Prime cranky elbows with light triceps isolation (band extensions, hammer curl + pushdown) before dips, pressing or chin-ups.
INJ-110 | Train lighter and short of lockout with elbow tendinitis | With elbow tendinitis keep training lighter, stop short of lockout/full extension, work up to the painful point and extend gradually. Pops are fine, but don't force through pain.
INJ-111 | Avoid arm wrestling | Arm wrestling carries a high elbow-injury risk; pros commonly have 2+ surgeries, and he avoids it.
INJ-112 | Band hand-opening for grip overuse pain | Elastic-band hand opening (finger extensors) fixes wrist, finger, elbow and forearm pain caused by constant gripping; do it as often as possible, especially after gripper work.
INJ-113 | Wrist curls carry overuse risk | Wrist curls (especially pronated, high-volume, or with a fixed dumbbell) carry overuse risk; they "destroyed" his wrists. Pain-free beats heavier: for curl-related forearm pain, go lighter and do more reps.
INJ-114 | Press with a stacked wrist | A cocked-back wrist under heavy load leads to overuse injury and the bar landing on the neck or collarbone; strengthen with rack holds and arm-wrestling-style work.
INJ-115 | Fixes for wrist pain | The band wrist-traction stretch (Uncle Nick) cured his wrist pain; he does it between front-squat sets. For wrist pain from pressing overuse, change variations, use rings or an EZ bar, add high-rep wrist curls and rice-bucket work.
INJ-116 | Callus care | Shave or clip calluses right after training (never after a shower), the day before a rest day, and moisturise hands nightly with a balm; this stopped tears that used to happen every ~3 weeks. Lift normally 2 days later.
INJ-117 | Pec tears come from unprepared stretch | Pec tears come from stretching through a range the muscle isn't prepared for; train the chest in more than one plane and stretch it deeply under low load. He claims PED users tear tendons while naturals tear near the armpit.
INJ-118 | Flyes light and comfortable | Flyes should be light, high-rep and comfortable, with the arm bent if needed.
INJ-119 | Pec strains usually heal alone | Pec strains usually heal on their own; pain when breathing or bracing points to the ribs/intercostals instead.
INJ-120 | Dips: progress them, stop on sternum pain | Stop dips if they cause sternum pain (especially young lifters); painless sternum cracking is fine. Don't do dips twice a week with one unprogressed; replace one.
INJ-121 | Spotting rules | Take the bar the moment the lifter asks and never jerk it out; bench without safeties needs a spot.
INJ-122 | Hamstring pulls are nearly 100% avoidable | Train the lengthened position under control (RDLs, hypers, GHR, leg curls, paused lifts) at higher reps; deadlift-only lifters train only the positive. RDLs nearly eliminated pulls in athletes he advised; no foam rolling needed.
INJ-123 | Uncontrolled hamstring stretches can tear | Uncontrolled Nordic/GHR stretches and ballistic toe-touch stretches can tear hamstrings, especially in heavy lifters new to them.
INJ-124 | Hip/glute pain rebuild order | For hip or glute pain, pause the painful lift, train the least painful lower movement, rehab glutes with thrusts/bridges/hypers at low intensity, then reintroduce squats, then deadlifts.
INJ-125 | Wide stances injure many | Wide stances (sumo, wide squats) injure many, including him; for hamstring pain at the hip, drop wide-stance and lunge-type moves.
INJ-126 | Neck: high volume, never heavy | Train the neck with high volume, never a 3RM; prefer controlled neck curls or bands over bridges/plates. Warm up the neck and keep reps in reserve if pressing triggers neck pain; OHP to failure gave him days of shooting neck pain. A thick neck protects in car crashes and against concussion.
INJ-127 | Hernias come from twisting under load | Hernias come from fast twisting under load; keep the trunk still in lower-body lifts and one-arm rows.
INJ-128 | Spinal flexion is safe done properly | Sit-ups and spinal flexion/twisting are safe done properly (hinge at the hips, use the abs); if flexion hurts, use hanging leg raises.
INJ-129 | Rib and intercostal rehab | For ribs/intercostals: serratus punches, pullovers and his R3 routine every lower day, years after the break. He broke ribs on a heavy squat and re-aggravated them with allergy sneezing (accounts of the event vary).
INJ-130 | Heavy bodyweight slows ankle healing | Heavy people's ankle/Achilles injuries heal slowly; don't start calves heavy and avoid high-impact sport. His rolled ankle wouldn't heal at 240 lb but healed fast at 220 lb.
INJ-131 | Keep training what you can | Keep moving and train what you can the next day; only a broken bone or torn tendon justifies stopping. An unrehabbed injury spreads, and compensation injures the other side. After shoulder surgery keep training everything else and do cardio; moping for a month brings nothing.
INJ-132 | Return slowly; twice makes it chronic | Return slowly and methodically; rushing to regain lost time, or letting form slip again, causes reinjury. Once is okay, twice the same way creates a chronic pattern. Every one of his injuries came from impatience about a target weight and cost months.
INJ-133 | The psychological milestone | The psychological side matters as much as the physical: the weight you got hurt on is the key milestone, and it should be retaken only when it feels non-challenging. Small rehab wins create a virtuous circle.
INJ-134 | Take responsibility, don't blame the lift | After an injury, find what you did wrong instead of declaring the lift evil.
INJ-135 | Return order after an upper-back injury | After upper-back pain take 2 days off, then avoid painful lifts, and see a doctor if everything hurts. Return order: squats and good mornings first, then vertical then horizontal presses, then deadlifts, rows last.
INJ-136 | Serious injuries need doctors | Serious injuries need proper rehab and doctors; he is not a doctor and doesn't advise on surgical cases. He also says GPs wrongly prescribe rest and painkillers (see INJ-33, INJ-34).
INJ-137 | The body re-adapts | People who think they'll never squat or deadlift again are usually wrong, though robust mobility after a serious injury can take years.
NUT-01 | Training matters more than diet for naturals | For a natural lifter, training drives muscle growth and diet only fuels it; the "diet is 70-80%" line is nonsense that comes from drug users. Nutrition only matters once training is on point.
NUT-02 | Diet is the top factor for longevity | Diet matters more than cardio or lifting for longevity and disease; it is overrated for muscle and underrated for a long, healthy life. He puts longevity ahead of pure hypertrophy and says the two go together when the diet suits you.
NUT-03 | Diet determines most long-term health | Diet can be the difference between dying in your 60s and your 80s; fixing diet and training would remove most health problems, yet people take pills for symptoms instead.
NUT-04 | Food builds mass; training directs it to muscle | Food is anabolic only if you train. Extra food or protein without training just makes you fat, and without calories and sleep no amount of hard training builds mass.
NUT-05 | Make diet an automatism; push training instead | Once a diet works, keep it stable and put the effort into training. Constant diet tweaking feeds doubt and anxiety; the diet should not be the struggle, the training should.
NUT-06 | Check diet before deloading after a strength drop | If strength or tonnage drops unexpectedly, look at your diet before deciding you need a deload.
NUT-07 | Nutrition is his self-declared weakest area | He calls nutrition his least-researched area and avoids diet videos because the topic is emotional and industry-funded.
NUT-08 | Calories in vs out sets weight; food sets composition | Energy balance governs weight change for everyone, but macros and food choice decide what kind of weight you gain or lose. You can lose fat eating junk, but that misses the point; claiming otherwise hands overweight people an excuse.
NUT-09 | A calorie is not just a calorie | The source of calories and how they are combined (e.g. protein with carbs) affect insulin and where fat goes; an all-junk intake is not equivalent to whole food at the same calories.
NUT-10 | You need a surplus to build mass | No muscle is gained in a constant deficit; eat until the scale moves. Elsewhere he says maintenance is all most people need for gains.
NUT-11 | Stalled scale: add a small snack | If the scale isn't moving, add one small snack such as a bowl of rice with chicken.
NUT-12 | Maintenance rises slowly as you grow | Maintenance calories rise as you recomp or gain muscle, and appetite usually signals it; needs climb very slowly unless activity or climate changes.
NUT-13 | His own intake: about 2,000-2,400 kcal | At 6'2" and ~210 lb he eats roughly 2,000-2,400 kcal and calls his metabolism slow; 4,000 kcal would make him gain fast.
NUT-14 | Huge calorie claims are miscounted or drug-fuelled | Claims of 4,000-10,000+ kcal a day are miscounted, drug-fuelled or specific to very active athletes; Phelps' intake reflects hours of swimming, not lifting. Eating huge and burning it off (G-flux) is no better than not eating the excess.
NUT-15 | Depressed? Eat at maintenance | If you are depressed, don't diet; eat at maintenance rather than bulking.
NUT-16 | Protein needs are vastly overrated | He eats far less protein than commonly recommended and still grows; eating much more gave him health problems and no extra gains.
NUT-17 | 2 g/kg or 2 g/lb is a waste | Intakes like 2 g/kg, 2 g/lb or 200 g/day at 180 lb are wasted; past a point protein is just burned as fuel. Mainly steroid users can use that much, and supplement sellers push the overshoot.
NUT-18 | ~0.8 g/lb is near optimal, but more than needed | About 0.8 g/lb is "close to optimal", yet even the low end of the optimal range exceeds what he needs. Studies don't test lower intakes, so "suboptimal" may still give most of the gains; missing 10 g a day won't stop progress.
NUT-19 | Novices especially need little protein | Beginners lack the anabolic capacity to use high protein, so the extra is expensive calories better replaced by carbs. He ate less than prescribed early on and still grew.
NUT-20 | High protein isn't needed even when bulking | Protein matters only until you have enough, bulking included. He aims for the smallest protein and calorie intake that still lets him grow; old-school lifters ate less, from steak and eggs.
NUT-21 | Raise protein slowly when raising calories | When adding calories, add protein slowly. Animal protein is so satiating that skinny lifters on high-protein diets fail to eat enough.
NUT-22 | Bioavailability makes or breaks a protein source | How much of a source's protein your body can use matters as much as the label. Cooking meat raises it; red meat's value lies in its other nutrients, not its protein.
NUT-23 | Diversify protein sources | Fish, fatty fish and plant proteins should complement animal protein. By 2022 his sources were chicken, turkey (now mostly), sardines, salmon, eggs, tuna and lentils.
NUT-24 | Insects are valid protein, not a revolution | Insects are a usable protein source but "not a nutrition revolution": they carry parasite risk and were never a staple anywhere. Buy only tested products.
NUT-25 | Macro balance is individual | The right carb/fat/protein balance differs per person; find it by trial and error and by looking at your ancestry. A past high-fat, high-protein, lower-carb diet made him fat, sluggish and weak; others get fat on his diet.
NUT-26 | He thrives on a high-carb, low-fat diet | Most of his calories come from carbs, with low fat and moderate protein. Carbs improve his recovery, work capacity, endurance and look; he is leaner and more vascular on them, and fatter when he swaps carbs for fat.
NUT-27 | Beginners manage calories, not macros | Beginners only need to manage calories. He ignored macros for his first five years; balancing macros is easy, the hard part is choosing foods that agree with you.
NUT-28 | Carbs don't make you fat; processed sugar does | Low-fiber, high-sugar processed products make people fat, not carbs as such. Carb tolerance depends on digestion, body fat and ethnic background, and carbs can inflame some people. "Earn your carbs": most people eat pasta, rice and bread they don't need.
NUT-29 | Keep fat low, but never near zero | Naturals need some dietary fat for hormones; enhanced-style diets that cut fat almost completely would crush a natural's testosterone. His fats come from olive oil, fish, oat milk and occasional butter; avoid palm oil.
NUT-30 | White rice is his staple carb | White (sticky) rice digests easily, and its low satiety lets him eat more and fill glycogen. Not everyone digests it; if it constipates you or insulin sensitivity worries you, swap to brown rice, sweet potato, quinoa or lentils, and avoid wild rice if it bothers you.
NUT-31 | Exercise changes how sugar acts | Insulin sensitivity depends on exercise: sugar after training acts differently than sugar on the couch. He could keep his physique on processed carbs, but his health would suffer.
NUT-32 | Cycle carbs by training day | Put more carbs on some training days and fewer on others. Which days get the most has changed: in 2021 it was lower-body days (hungry heavy squats kill a workout); in 2024 it is upper-body days, with fewer carbs and more animal protein on leg days and carbs the day after legs.
NUT-33 | Rest days: less food, carbs near zero | On rest days eat less, mostly protein and non-starchy vegetables, with carbs near zero unless you are a hardgainer. In 2021 he ate carbs only on training days, first to cut and then as a discipline so he never skips a session.
NUT-34 | Carbs the day before training fill glycogen | Eating carbs the day before a session fills glycogen (Reeves' practice and his). Carbs on press day don't directly improve pressing, only through weight gained over time.
NUT-35 | Meal timing is "D tier" | When you eat matters little next to what and how much; distribution only affects how you get through the day. OMAD isn't inherently bad for hypertrophy.
NUT-36 | There is no anabolic window | Protein timing doesn't matter: synthesis runs for about a day and earlier meals already supply nutrients. The window was a protein-powder marketing tactic; protein or fat after training doesn't slow absorption, and a sugary post-workout drink is optional and mostly placebo.
NUT-37 | Pre-workout: carb meal 2-3 h before, never fasted | Eat a carb-rich meal 2-3 hours before training, plus a banana if hungry; be fed but not full. He never trains fasted and eats on waking.
NUT-38 | Six meals a day is a myth | 1-3 meals a day are fine if calories and nutrients are met; six big rice-and-chicken meals came from steroid users. Novices eating 2-3 times will see calories rise naturally.
NUT-39 | His pattern: two big meals plus snacks | He eats two big sit-down meals with snacks or a breakfast between, sometimes one meal; he can't do OMAD because it would mean ~2,500 kcal in one sitting.
NUT-40 | Distribute calories around hunger tolerance | If you can't sleep hungry, start eating later and save calories for an evening meal; if you can't function hungry, eat most early and keep the post-training meal light and high-protein. He trims the post-workout dinner, not the pre-workout meal.
NUT-41 | Some train best full, others hungry | Some people train best on a full stomach, others hungry and aggressive. He likes being full on lower-body days because hunger hurts his focus.
NUT-42 | The body burns fat before muscle | Fear of muscle loss from fasting is a myth created by supplement companies: muscle is expensive and preserved, and true muscle breakdown comes only deep into starvation. Muscle lost during fasts comes from training performance dropping.
NUT-43 | Fasting has no hormonal magic | Fasting doesn't boost testosterone or growth hormone; intermittent fasting just extends the overnight fast. Its value is mental toughness and easier calorie control; "starvation mode" is mostly an excuse.
NUT-44 | He doesn't fast; it darkens his mood | Fasting makes him unproductive and gloomy, and puts many people, especially the overweight, in a dark place. A binge-then-fast cycle signals imbalance; if you struggle to eat enough, fasting isn't for you. In 2021 he said fasting helped him rein in his appetite after over-bulking.
NUT-45 | Very short eating windows are impractical | 1-2 hour eating windows don't work: satiety makes large intakes near impossible, going to bed hungry hurts sleep and next-day work, and they invite junk binges.
NUT-46 | Water fasts need minerals; dry fasting is dangerous | If you water fast, add vitamins and minerals; dry fasting is dangerous.
NUT-47 | Ramadan: aim for maintenance and train fed | During religious fasting aim for maintenance, not a surplus. Prep the pre-dawn meal the night before and train 2-4 h after it (or at lunch), never late afternoon or evening while dehydrated. Pre-dawn: soft pasta or sticky rice and water with a pinch of sea salt; break the fast with protein and fats, then carbs, plus lots of vegetables. Gains continue, just not optimally.
NUT-48 | Eat mostly whole foods you cook yourself | Most of what you eat should be home-cooked whole food; restaurants and delivery only occasionally. You control salt and condiments, get more satiety and micronutrients, and pay far less. Every man should learn to cook; cooking badly beats outsourcing.
NUT-49 | Minimize processed food; no processed meats | Processed means anything prepared for you, and it's worse with more steps; makers optimise for taste and addiction. Poor food quality damages your hormonal profile even with perfect macros and may feed anxiety and depression. Nobody should eat processed meats.
NUT-50 | Micronutrients are the hard, underrated part | Judge foods by micros, not macros. Calories, protein and fat are easy to hit; vitamins and minerals are what junk misses. Covering micros from whole foods usually covers calories too; potatoes and cake have similar macros but very different micros.
NUT-51 | Plants should be most of your food volume | Eat lots of vegetables; he is "plant-based in the old sense", not vegan, using animal products for bioavailable protein. Leafy greens, onions, garlic, peppers, tomatoes, mushrooms, carrots; oats almost daily plus fruit.
NUT-52 | Improve diet by subtraction, step by step | You improve more by stopping bad foods than by adding good ones; there's no point taking an antidote while still drinking poison. Introduce changes gradually, since removing all junk at once can backfire (acne, feeling bad).
NUT-53 | Junk food is empty calories | Junk food helps nobody's health; only very-high-TDEE hardgainers benefit. Cake won't kill you in a big surplus, but the more junk you eat, the more the rest must cover micros. He sees the "junk food doesn't exist" camp as possibly worse than clean-eating cultists.
NUT-54 | No food makes you big; no superfoods | Once macros are met with healthy food it's just calories; organ meats for size are an old gimmick. Some foods (kiwi, garlic, onion, ginger, olive oil) are simply rich in micronutrients.
NUT-55 | Supermarket produce is low in micronutrients | He believes supermarket produce is depleted (poor soil, early harvest), while admitting he has no source. Buy organic and local from farms that rest their soil, favour hardy roots like potatoes, or grow your own.
NUT-56 | Fruit instead of processed sweets | Use whole fruit (bananas, apples) as snacks rather than processed sweets. Fiber supplements are pointless; get fiber's value from vegetables and fruit.
NUT-57 | There is no universal diet | Nutrition is even more case-by-case than training: amounts, ratios, timing and micros differ. Reject absolutes and cookie-cutter plans and experiment by removing and adding foods; by your 30s-40s you should have most of it figured out.
NUT-58 | Use family history and ancestry to choose foods | Ask what relatives on both sides died of, protect your family's weak organ and copy your healthiest relatives. He built his diet with a longevity nutritionist from family history (no DNA test); without such help, use credible sources on which foods aggravate or protect against a condition.
NUT-59 | Drop foods that disagree with you | Remove foods that disagree with you, even "healthy" ones (broccoli wrecks his stomach); don't trick the body with lactose pills. For bloating, find the trigger (dairy, red meat, gluten, oats, pasta); clean foods with similar macros are interchangeable.
NUT-60 | No food is universally good or bad | Dose matters per person: an amount that helps one person may harm another. Beef and milk aren't bad in themselves but may be bad for you.
NUT-61 | Remove foods gradually, before a recomp | Eliminate foods one at a time and do the experimenting before a recomp, not during it. He dropped milk, then cow cheese, then goat cheese over two years, and pork before beef.
NUT-62 | Stubbornness costs more in diet than in training | Being stubborn with diet costs more than being stubborn with training, because hard work compensates less for a bad diet.
NUT-63 | Dropping dairy transformed his physique | Quitting dairy, especially milk, removed his love handles, facial bloat and water retention at the same bodyweight, revealing separation and veins; he links this to estrogen in milk. If you are lean but have stubborn love handles or unexplained bloat, try cutting dairy.
NUT-64 | Dairy isn't good for most people | Being able to digest lactose doesn't make dairy healthy; most people do poorly on it, though some groups (Scandinavians) thrive on it. The "other species' milk" argument is a nature fallacy.
NUT-65 | Red meat shouldn't be anyone's main protein | Red meat rarely helps any condition and isn't needed for gains; he has cut it close to zero after relatives who ate it died early.
NUT-66 | Cutting animal products cost him nothing | Each removal of animal products improved his health and physique with no loss of strength or muscle. He now eats mostly plants and carbs with modest animal products; eggs and fish can replace meat.
NUT-67 | Eggs: a few a week, whole, pasture-raised | Eat whole pasture-raised eggs with deep orange yolks, where the vitamins are; eggs are a micronutrient bomb, but their protein is overrated. Twelve a day is ridiculous and can't be used.
NUT-68 | Fish: limit canned tuna, favour sardines | Limit canned tuna and salmon for mercury; sardines are safer; eat fatty fish like trout twice a week. Source locally.
NUT-69 | Soy and tofu are fine | Soy has had no negative effect on him. Studies test normal intake, not overconsumption, and estrogenic effects depend on genetics and receptor sensitivity.
NUT-70 | Raw garlic and onions daily, as food | Eating raw garlic and onions daily changed how he looks and feels: pumps hours later and vascularity without fat loss. By 2024 garlic is the only food he credits for vascularity (mostly it is training and leanness); tolerance builds, and it won't cure illness, only support general immunity. For his dosing protocol see SUP-23.
NUT-71 | Salt is not the enemy | Lifters who cook their own food should salt it: sodium is needed for contraction and intramuscular water, and water without minerals "just passes through". Use sea or pink salt and get blood pressure checked.
NUT-72 | Hydrate by urine colour; drink filtered water | Judge hydration by urine: clear, without constantly peeing pure water. Vegetables and salads supply much of your water. He believes tap water carries hormones and toxins, so he drinks only filtered water (filter, reverse osmosis) and no coffee, tea or soda.
NUT-73 | Cut sodas, juices and energy drinks | Drink water instead of sweet drinks; this change alone can drop several pounds. He drinks water (sometimes with lemon), oat milk and occasional orange juice.
NUT-74 | Alcohol impairs muscle building | Alcohol hurts muscle building and performance, though occasional drinks won't kill gains. Daily "moderate" drinking is addiction, a binge wrecks next-day training, and liquor is worst. He rarely drinks and calls alcohol poison.
NUT-75 | A diet must be sustainable for life | Build your diet from foods you enjoy and can repeat for life. Bland bodybuilder food is "theatrics" that breeds misery and eating disorders; spices make dense food easier to eat.
NUT-76 | No cheat meals | Needing a weekly binge means the diet is wrong; he "doesn't eat clean", so there's nothing to cheat from. Planned refeeds for performance are different from cheat meals for feelings, and weekly rich binges stress stomach and skin.
NUT-77 | Extreme diets suit only a minority | Keto, carnivore, vegan, zero-fat and raw-meat diets suit a small minority; humans are omnivores and no diet should ban whole food groups. Carnivore and vegan mostly help by removing processed junk.
NUT-78 | He doesn't count calories; count if it helps | He doesn't count calories or macros because he is already obsessive about training; counting is a legitimate tool if it helps you. In 2020, eating purely by appetite during a recomp backfired on him: dropping dairy led to unnoticed weight loss and lifts going backwards.
NUT-79 | Keep a professional relationship with food | Treat food as sustenance and diet as a tool, not a personality; eating only for pleasure is close to addiction, though pleasure is fine as a by-product. Fix a bad relationship with food early.
NUT-80 | Clean-eating obsession can become a disorder | Obsessive clean eating can become an eating disorder, though calling healthy eating "orthorexia" pathologises it. Judge foods by whether they agree with you, not by labels; eating with others reduces disorders.
NUT-81 | Remove temptation rather than test willpower | Keep tempting food out of the house instead of proving willpower; for a craving, wait it out. If you grew up food-insecure, an empty fridge can cause heavy stress, so he instead makes a month's snacks last a month.
NUT-82 | Eat until satiated, not full | Pause briefly mid-meal and pack away the rest if hunger is gone; don't eat the instant you feel hunger, and don't overeat to compensate for extra activity.
NUT-83 | Limit sugar, but don't ban it | He keeps sugar limited with fruit first, but calls banning all sugar too extreme; he eats a sweet breakfast he enjoys. Avoid palm-oil spreads like Nutella.
NUT-84 | Artificial sweeteners aren't harmless | The problem with sweeteners is addiction to sweetness. Banana test: if a banana doesn't taste very sweet, your taste buds are ruined.
NUT-85 | Hardgainers must prioritise eating | Skinny and tall lifters usually just don't eat enough (no proven link to low testosterone). They need a surplus and scheduled meals, never just 1-2 a day; because appetite shuts off quickly, more meals help them, and tall lifters need patience.
NUT-86 | Hardgainers: drink calories, skip the bro diet | Skinny lifters on chicken-and-broccoli diets don't grow; drink calories through homemade blender shakes (peanut butter, milks, avocado, blueberries) sipped through the day or before bed, or eat cake or pizza if needed while keeping the diet balanced. Quick dense snack: oats with bananas and honey.
NUT-87 | GOMAD is idiotic | A gallon of milk a day brings excess fat and calcium, puts all your eggs in one basket and has hidden long-term costs; any growth comes only from protein and calories.
NUT-88 | The vertical diet is for Efferding's athletes | Red meat plus white rice, low fiber and no onions or garlic suits Efferding's strength athletes, not non-athletes or teens; the orange juice is meant to boost appetite, not replace vegetables. He notes Brian Shaw quit it.
NUT-89 | Women: moderate surplus by appetite | Women should run a moderate surplus guided by appetite; their gains tend to go to legs and glutes.
NUT-90 | Fat loss: swap processed food for whole food | Replace processed foods with vegetables and whole foods instead of crash dieting; fiber-rich food satiates and calories fall on their own, while rapid loss risks loose skin. Stop snacking and eat only planned sit-down meals (even 4-5 instead of 3); salad-starving dieters out-snack it.
NUT-91 | Snacking within macros is fine unless overweight | Snacks within your intake are fine unless you are overweight; people with a low metabolism do better on snacks plus occasional big meals than on six big meals.
NUT-92 | Shop smart and plan the week | Shop with a list, after a meal, local and seasonal; buy perishables only as needed and plan the week.
NUT-93 | Healthy food is not expensive | Eating healthy is affordable; he lived on a small monthly budget in the US and got jacked. Dorms without kitchens make gaining hard.
NUT-94 | Cook chicken in oven or pan, well seasoned | Bake or pan-cook chicken with heavy seasoning; never boil or steam it and avoid rotisserie. Garlic and onion powder go on anything. Use oven and pans rather than the microwave or ready-meals; he credits that switch with better body composition and hormones.
NUT-95 | Cook fresh rather than weekly meal prep | He stopped weekly meal prep: budget prep made him hate his food, and dry chicken and rice followed by a Sunday binge is "weak-minded". He cooks fresh with a few precooked dishes.
NUT-96 | Skin and acne follow diet | For acne, stop harsh face washes, drink water, cut soda, refined sugar, dairy and fast food, and sleep more. Saturated fat, junk or spices cause body odour.
NUT-97 | Post-meal brain fog may signal too many carbs | Brain fog lasting over 30 minutes after a meal may signal too many carbs or gluten. He gets sleepy for an hour after meals, so he puts his last meal near bed.
NUT-98 | After a big meal: salad or lemon water | He ends a big meal with salad and balsamic, or drinks water with a squeezed lemon before bed.
SUP-01 | Supplements are near-worthless for muscle | Food, training and sleep build muscle; supplements are "D tier", worth maybe 1%, and the least important part of a bulk. Programming is "100 times more important", and most products are nonsense, some dangerous.
SUP-02 | Supplements only concentrate what food gives | A supplement gives what food gives in concentrated form, so eat real food first; healing needs habits, not pills.
SUP-03 | A supplement that builds muscle is a steroid | Any supplement that truly builds muscle is a steroid; everything else is a dud. The "miracle" products that worked were banned as hormonal, and a flashy tub that works may hide illegal substances. There are "no natural steroids".
SUP-04 | Supplements stand in for lifestyle changes | Pills replace harder habits: vitamin D replaces sun, ashwagandha replaces dealing with stressors, organ-meat pills replace eating organs. The funnel moves you from a hard habit to an easy, pricier pill.
SUP-05 | Vitamins: crutch or worth taking? | In 2022 he said a balanced diet removes the need for vitamin supplements ("crutches"); in 2024 he listed multivitamins and vitamin D among the few supplements worth anything.
SUP-06 | Joint and recovery supplements beat muscle ones | Supplements for joints and recovery matter more than those sold for muscle.
SUP-07 | Try cheap ones; drop what shows nothing | If you try supplements, pick cheap ones not marketed at beginners and drop anything that shows no improvement.
SUP-08 | Take half the label dose | Take half the recommended dose of any supplement: results are the same and you buy half as much. Natural stacks are copied from PED users; cut yours down (he cut ~80% of his). He exempts creatine, which he takes above label (SUP-12).
SUP-09 | His stack is about three items | He keeps only about three cheap, sustainable supplements; cutting 80% of his stack changed nothing. The only ones worth anything, he said in 2024, are whey, creatine, multivitamins and vitamin D; the rest is a bonus at most.
SUP-10 | Prefer tonic herbs to supplements | He prefers raw garlic and ginger, as "tonic herbs", to bought supplements.
SUP-11 | Creatine works, but the effect is small | Creatine is natural, cheap and one of the most proven supplements; for him it brings longer, higher-volume workouts and intramuscular water. In 2020 it also stood in for the red meat he minimises.
SUP-12 | Creatine daily, no loading, no cycling | Take creatine every day without loading or cycling; loading phases exist only to make you finish the tub, and megadoses don't scale gains. He takes more than the label dose for work capacity.
SUP-13 | Creatine side effects vary by person | Some people get hair loss (DHT), bloating or water retention, or kidney stones; he gets none and his hair is thicker than ever. Stop if it causes hair loss.
SUP-14 | Whey is a convenience, not a necessity | Whey only makes protein easier to consume and was the first hyped "magic potion"; you don't need it to be jacked. He used whey or pea protein for convenience (low appetite, 1-2 meals a day, avoiding animal products). In 2024 he called powder not worth the money at current prices while still using and recommending it.
SUP-15 | At most one scoop a day | Use at most about one scoop of protein powder a day; if shakes make up most of your protein, your diet is wrong. Test it: stop or cut powder for a while, and 9 times out of 10 nothing changes. Stockpiling and bulk discounts breed dependence and overconsumption.
SUP-16 | Pick plain powder; gainers are garbage | Choose a powder that is mostly protein, without added carbs or sugar, and don't dry scoop. Weight gainers and carb powders are sugar and industrial by-products sold far above the cost of the food they come from; make your own shake or eat rice.
SUP-17 | Small or slow shakes; night shake if short | He claims only a limited amount of whey is absorbed per hour, so sip shakes slowly or take smaller ones. A night-time shake is fine if protein falls short, as during Ramadan.
SUP-18 | MSM is his key joint supplement | MSM powder is his favourite joint supplement and the only thing that fixed his knees; it is anti-inflammatory, cheap and horribly bitter, takes weeks to work, and his joints suffer quickly when he stops. He saw little difference from pricier glucosamine.
SUP-19 | Collagen in his whey for tendons | He adds collagen to his whey shake for tendon health.
SUP-20 | He takes bee pollen daily | He started bee pollen to treat his spring pollen allergy without antihistamines and now takes it daily because he finds the benefits too large to stop. It gives him euphoric, almost manic energy and may worsen anxiety; he believes it helps skin, hair and digestion. Pollen without hard training builds nothing.
SUP-21 | Bee pollen protocol: microdose up | Allergic people risk anaphylaxis, so microdose from early April and raise slowly; stop if you react. Take it on waking on an empty stomach, after lunch and after dinner, letting it dissolve in saliva, with a spoon of farm honey.
SUP-22 | Buy local organic pollen pellets | Buy local organic pellets, not capsules, creatine blends or Amazon products; local pollen helps local allergies, and paying more than a modest monthly cost is a scam.
SUP-23 | Raw garlic: low dose, every other day | Eat 1-2 raw cloves every other day, starting with half a clove twice a week, always with a meal; never a whole bulb (vomiting, effect lost). He says studies show one clove per 24 h maximises allicin.
SUP-24 | Garlic prep: raw, thin, rest, chew, at lunch | Always raw (cooking destroys allicin), sliced very thin, rested briefly, chewed thoroughly; eat it at lunch, not before bed, since evening trainees feel it in their session. He calls the 10-minute rule a myth. Buy organic garlic that is hard, strong-smelling and unsprouted; never garlic pills.
SUP-25 | Garlic benefits and downsides | Raw garlic gives pumps through blood flow, steady all-day energy, immunity and vascularity; a cheaper, healthier "stimulant" than spike-and-crash pre-workouts. It lowers LDL and blood pressure; the pump fades with habituation but better circulation stays. Downsides: breath and body odour, gas, stomach pain, heartburn; some can't digest it.
SUP-26 | Ginger every 48 hours | Start with a small piece of ginger every 48 h and build up. He credits it with better focus than nootropics, better insulin sensitivity (full muscles instead of fog after carbs) and libido; downsides are sour armpit odour and diarrhoea. He cycles garlic on upper days and ginger on lower, higher-carb days. Buy dry, hard, mould-free ginger with yellow flesh from Asian markets, never store-bought pickled ginger.
SUP-27 | Fish oil did nothing for him | He stopped fish oil because it did nothing, and his doctor said too much omega-3 was bad for his condition; he also limits fatty fish.
SUP-28 | Minor compounds he has tried | Turmeric in every dish while healing (he rates it despite debated absorption); taurine before bed gave him vivid dreams and deeper REM sleep; B12 made no noticeable difference.
SUP-29 | Filtered water needs minerals from elsewhere | Filtered water lacks minerals, so get them from diet or a mineral supplement.
SUP-30 | No sleeping pills; fix the cause | Don't take sleeping pills; fix whatever is disrupting sleep (see ch. 4).
SUP-31 | Anti-stimulant: no coffee, no pre-workout | He takes no caffeine or pre-workout; needing them to train is a weak, compromised relationship with lifting, and motivation from a spike stops when tolerance or supply runs out. Using caffeine to start out is fine, but not as a crutch; pre/intra-workout supplements are "C tier".
SUP-32 | Caffeine borrows energy and wrecks sleep | Caffeine borrows energy, builds tolerance and replaces natural wake-up hormones; even a noon cup affects sleep, and an evening pre-workout ruins recovery. Viewers who quit for two weeks lost headaches, fatigue and shaking. If you use it, take regular breaks.
SUP-33 | Want a stimulant? Drink coffee, not pre-workout | If you want a stimulant, drink coffee; a $40 pre-workout is a scam because only caffeine and beta-alanine matter. One morning coffee is a ritual; coffee all day is addiction. He still abstains himself.
SUP-34 | Intra-workout: water or a carb drink | During training a carb drink is enough; plain water is fine.
SUP-35 | Smelling salts are a meme | Needing smelling salts to lift isn't sustainable.
SUP-36 | Test boosters are scams | Test boosters are disguised steroids or junk, placebo at best; nothing natural raises testosterone or simply grows muscle. He notes tongkat ali has been eaten for centuries in Southeast Asia without producing muscular populations, and deer-antler testosterone claims were debunked.
SUP-37 | Turkesterone and ecdysterone do nothing | Any gains people report on turkesterone or ecdysterone come from placebo, training harder, newbie gains or muscle memory; he never took them, and the names deliberately evoke steroids. Vegans eat quinoa without getting huge: "show me the monsters".
SUP-38 | Pump products, fat burners and nootropics don't deliver | Pump supplements, fat burners and fat-loss creams don't work; nootropics pushed to uneducated audiences are risky "smart pills".
SUP-39 | Never buy from fitness YouTubers | Never buy supplements or programs pushed by fitness YouTubers; the industry is a hydra with replaceable heads, and the best creator stance is never to mention supplements. Creators who sell or take supplement money lose integrity, even if the company is good (see ch. 13).
SUP-40 | Marketing sells image transfer to teens | Supplement marketing runs on social proof, newbie-gain testimonials, image transfer and teen audiences; buyers expect to look like the seller, and "might as well try it" funds the industry. Prices bear no relation to ingredient cost and are padded with fluff ingredients.
SUP-41 | Bunk-supplement buyers partly scam themselves | Buyers of bunk turkesterone partly "scammed themselves" by trying to dodge hard work; whatever they felt came from training harder, so the placebo defence fails. He predicted a new magic muscle supplement (perhaps a black-pepper or capsaicin extract) within 6-12 months.
BLK-01 | Default for naturals: small permanent surplus | Men who are not very over- or underweight should stay in a small surplus for their whole career ("perma-bulk"), unless they start out fat; overshooting body fat once ruins it.
BLK-02 | Who should bulk: from extremes only to most | In 2020 only extremes should bulk or cut (very skinny bulk, very fat cut, everyone else near maintenance). By 2024 lean, small and formerly chubby lifters must lean bulk, and maintaining or cutting is a mistake for them; moving past the lean "auto mode" physique requires an awkward bulking phase.
BLK-03 | Skinny naturals must gain weight, slowly | Skinny naturals should slow lean bulk on a small surplus, never dirty or aggressively (they just turn skinny-fat). You must gain weight to gain muscle; building muscle at maintenance at 130 lb is a meme. Skinny women need to gain too.
BLK-04 | Fat people: lift while you cut | If you start fat, cut while lifting: it keeps muscle, makes the cut easier and keeps skin firmer. A high-fat, low-muscle novice can still build muscle on a cut. Cut until skinny-fat or skinny, then build.
BLK-05 | Skinny-fat: recomp at maintenance, never cut | Skinny-fat is not a body type but metabolic distress. Stay at maintenance (or a very slight surplus or deficit) and put the effort into training; cutting skinny-fat is a mistake.
BLK-06 | No cutting in the first years unless fat | Unless fat or obese, don't cut during your first ~5 years: you haven't built anything to reveal. Teens shouldn't cut, though overweight teens can.
BLK-07 | After big weight loss: maintain before surplus | Someone who lost a lot of weight but is still soft should recomp at maintenance before adding a surplus, because waist fat returns first. Someone overweight who stress-eats should fix the emotional eating and learn to hold maintenance, then cut slowly.
BLK-08 | Formerly very fat: stop cutting, then bulk | Very fat people often keep cutting past need. Once fat is under control, lean bulk; if dysmorphia makes you want to cut, cut once, then lean bulk.
BLK-09 | No upside to bulking into the 20s | Sitting around 22% body fat has no upside except for strongmen chasing every pound; bulking up to ~20% is a bad idea for most.
BLK-10 | Tie bulks and cuts to a goal | Bulks and cuts make most sense tied to a performance goal (a lift, a show, one-arm pull-ups). Athletes shouldn't bulk for a bench or cut for abs; diet serves the sport.
BLK-11 | Tall lifters and bulking speed | Tall lifters need more mass to look impressive. He has said both that slow, patient bulking works for them and that more aggressive gaining can be justified.
BLK-12 | Keep the surplus small | Bulk on a small surplus; a few hundred calories is enough and more is pointless for a natural past beginner.
BLK-13 | Big surplus = fat; big deficit = muscle loss | A big surplus doesn't build more natural muscle, only fat; a big deficit burns muscle as well as fat. Protein synthesis is limited, your capacity to get fat is not.
BLK-14 | Novices start at maintenance | Novices should eat at maintenance and raise calories slowly as appetite grows; a big surplus is a waste.
BLK-15 | Hardgainer exception: eat more | Very skinny, fast-metabolism lifters who stall on a small surplus should eat more aggressively.
BLK-16 | Raise calories slowly, then hold | Raise calories slowly and hold them steady until the bulking intake becomes your new maintenance (proof you gained muscle), then raise again. Constant swings in intake disrupt training.
BLK-17 | Small daily surpluses beat huge days | A steady small surplus beats occasional huge eating days, which are mostly stored as fat. Force-feeding makes you lethargic and diet-fixated.
BLK-18 | His own method: bare-minimum surplus | He bulks on the smallest surplus that still moves the scale; for him an extra bowl of rice is enough because his metabolism is slow. He admits this instinctive method may not suit hardgainers.
BLK-19 | Gain about 0.5-1.5 lb per month | Bulk slowly; past the beginner stage, more than a pound or so a month is mostly fat.
BLK-20 | The more advanced, the slower | The more advanced you are, the slower the bulk must be. An advanced lifter gaining 2 lb a month is just gaining fat.
BLK-21 | Judge gain relative to bodyweight | Judge monthly gain as a percentage of bodyweight; fast gain is justified only for true beginners filling out.
BLK-22 | Spread gains over years | Gain your mass over years, not months.
BLK-23 | Fast fat-free gains are a lie | Never try to gain 20 lb fast "without fat", even if skinny; anyone claiming 20 lb of muscle with no fat is lying, and no one bulks 100 lb and gets 90% muscle.
BLK-24 | Slower gain is more likely muscle | The same weight gained faster may give the same strength increase, but the slower gain is far more likely muscle.
BLK-25 | Each bulk more conservative | Each bulk after the first should be more conservative ("first bulk is like a first love"); lifters tend to do the opposite.
BLK-26 | Teens have time | Teens can slow bulk for up to 10 years. Forcing large gains (e.g. for a sport season) backfires: it is mostly fat and fosters eating disorders.
BLK-27 | Training dictates diet | Calories can't become muscle by themselves. Training creates growth and the surplus only fuels recovery, so extra calories must coincide with rising volume, workload and intensity, or they are stored as fat.
BLK-28 | A bulk must raise work capacity | A bulk should raise work capacity and volume; if volume regresses on a bulk, there's no reason to bulk. Eat to fuel progression, not to gain weight.
BLK-29 | Progressing? Don't add calories | If you are progressing without a surplus, don't add calories. Raising his surplus while still progressing was his "first mistake" in 2023.
BLK-30 | Bulking helps but isn't required | Novices and intermediates can gain without bulking; a surplus only makes progression easier, and its main advantage is faster visible gains, which help adherence.
BLK-31 | Food explains many program results | Running a demanding strength program at maintenance vs eating everything feels "night and day" for leverage and recovery; many program results reflect noob gains and food, not the program. Energy-demanding programs (e.g. his Doom Slayer) should be run on a bulk.
BLK-32 | The calisthenics check | If bench rises while push-ups regress, or rows rise while pull-ups drop, you're gaining fat: slow down or stop the bulk. Keep calisthenics in while bulking.
BLK-33 | Strength in proportion to bodyweight is a bad trade | Strength rising only in proportion to added bodyweight is a bad trade and mostly not muscle; heavier scale and heavier bar don't equal muscle ("mass moves mass", better leverage).
BLK-34 | Watch the waist | Rising lifts and bodyweight with a growing waist means fat, a trap for bodybuilders using powerlifting principles. A rapidly growing waist means visceral fat, and every inch there looks bad.
BLK-35 | Mirror as often as the scale | Check the mirror as often as the scale: "bulking hypnosis" or size goggles hide fat gain, and on a cut "lean goggles" hide size loss.
BLK-36 | Signs to stop or slow a bulk | Stop or slow the bulk when bloat appears with no visible muscle (go to maintenance or a small deficit), when extra calories no longer improve gym performance, when your face gets chubby, or when you no longer like your body.
BLK-37 | No target bodyweight | Don't set a target bodyweight; it becomes an obsession and forces overeating. Set a time frame instead. He once aimed 180→225 lb to hit 100 kg.
BLK-38 | Weigh in every few weeks | Weigh only every 2-4 weeks; earlier he weighed every month or two, or not at all, judging by strength and mirror.
BLK-39 | Calorie tracking: optional, personal | He has never tracked calories on a bulk because it feeds his obsessive tendencies, but tracking is the easiest way to know you're gaining, suits people who need regimentation, may be needed to get shredded, and he would count if he cut.
BLK-40 | Body-fat numbers are unreliable | Body-fat scales are inaccurate, nobody can eyeball body-fat % accurately, and knowing the number is nearly useless.
BLK-41 | Dirty bulking is mostly fat | Dirty bulking adds mostly fat and leverage, not muscle. Junk food is low in protein and micronutrients, so you feel sluggish and bloated; whatever muscle you keep would have come from any surplus. Big naturals got big in spite of it.
BLK-42 | There is always more fat than you think | After a dirty bulk you must cut and lose the "size and strength" you thought you had. Everyone who cuts finds more fat than expected, and you can't predict your shredded weight by subtracting fat. Some people never lose the love handles.
BLK-43 | GOMAD is stupid | A gallon of milk a day overshoots into a large surplus with no room to manage calories, and brings too much milk fat and protein. At most it suits very skinny or very active athletes, and it can promote dysmorphia.
BLK-44 | Excess fat is anti-anabolic | Excess fat lowers testosterone, so the fatter you get the less anabolic you are; obese naturals might even lose muscle. Visceral fat and the tendon load of extra bodyweight aren't fully reversed by leaning out.
BLK-45 | Force-feeding harms your relationship with food | Force-feeding can trigger eating disorders the way crash diets trigger anorexia. If you hate your food, the bulk fails.
BLK-46 | Hard bulking works only if you track | Performance lifters who compare old numbers at the same bodyweight, and aesthetic lifters who track measurements, can bulk hard; most people don't track, so it fails for them. In his view their strength-driven mass doesn't prove you must dirty bulk.
BLK-47 | Slow is a default, not a law | Some people need faster bulks or cuts; if slow cycles stop working, go a bit more aggressive. Slow is a conservative default.
BLK-48 | Whole foods first, then dense extras | Base the bulk on whole foods, then top up with enjoyable calorie-dense foods. Pure clean eating is hard for hardgainers; junk is fine only if very skinny. Eat more vegetables on a bulk than on a cut, and avoid foods that bloat you.
BLK-49 | For mass, calories over macros | For mass, total calories matter more than macros; eat carbs, which are easy to eat and not filling.
BLK-50 | Appetite tricks | To eat more: small, frequent meals (a huge meal kills appetite); drink calories (morning shake of ice cream, milk and nut butter); a few pieces of raw ginger before bed; sandwiches with butter, peanut butter, honey or mayo; keep a go-to high-calorie meal ready.
BLK-51 | The bulk must be enjoyable | A bulk must be enjoyable and sustainable (rituals, food you love); there's no need for 15 meals a day. Consistency of the surplus is what counts.
BLK-52 | "Can't gain" means not eating enough | Skinny people who "can't gain" aren't eating enough; there is always a cheap way to eat more, and appetite is only a signal you can disobey.
BLK-53 | His rest day is his only non-surplus day | On his weekly rest day he eats low or no carbs and fewer calories; it is the only day not in a surplus.
BLK-54 | Recomp is real but slower than a bulk | A recomp is its own thing, not a bulk/cut hybrid: heavy training at maintenance makes the body draw on fat stores, like sculpting a statue. It is slow but real; for growth, a slow bulk beats it.
BLK-55 | Recomp needs fat to trade | A recomp needs both muscle and fat. Skinny people have nothing to recomp; the higher the body fat, the easier the trade. Ideal candidate: someone after bulk/cut cycles, happy with size, wanting to redistribute (best started when fluffier).
BLK-56 | Recomp body-fat floor | Don't recomp below a body-fat floor; very low body fat tanks hormones and costs muscle.
BLK-57 | Commit at least a year | Commit to a recomp for at least a year; two months then switching wastes time. What a week of bulking or cutting gives can take a month or two on a recomp.
BLK-58 | Keep everything stable | No big diet or training changes before or during a recomp. The only changes: a gradual calorie increase as muscle is gained (or you slide into a slow cut) and a small tonnage increase.
BLK-59 | Judge a recomp by gym performance | A working recomp shows tonnage and strength rising at the same bodyweight. Getting weaker means untracked weight loss or wrong diet, training or hormones; feeling depleted means it's failing. Measurements may drop the first year, so use the mirror (cuts, veins).
BLK-60 | Monitor by weight, not body-fat tests | Monitor a recomp by bodyweight every few months, not by body-fat tests every two weeks.
BLK-61 | No recomp without training | You can't recomp without training; sedentary maintenance gives a "reverse recomp" (lose muscle, gain fat).
BLK-62 | Recomp is for sculpting, not size | A recomp is slow when advanced and suits sculpting specific body parts rather than chasing overall size; long-neglected muscles grow even without a surplus.
BLK-63 | Stalled recomp: widen the window | When a recomp stalls, don't cut deeper: run a very slow bulk ("widen the window"), then recomp again, even if the cycle takes 5 years. When strength stalls for a long time, the recomp is over.
BLK-64 | Maintain to recomp, not recomp to maintain | A recomp that only holds mass is wasted time. A heavy lifter for whom more gaining adds only fat can recomp for a year or two.
BLK-65 | Miserable stalled cut → recomp | If a cut has stalled and you're miserable on very low calories, switch to a recomp at maintenance.
BLK-66 | Body-fat set-point window | Each person has a body-fat window where they're most anabolic; push it slowly. The window shifts with age, training and hormones and may shrink to a true set point.
BLK-67 | Cut slowly | Cut slowly. If you got fat, accept being fat for a long time and lose it slowly.
BLK-68 | No crash cuts | Crash ("suicide") cuts are dangerous to health, cost strength and muscle and feel awful. Use one only if body image is hurting your mental health, knowing it's not optimal.
BLK-69 | Don't cut training volume on a cut | Don't preemptively cut volume, reps, sets or strength work when dieting: start the cut on your current program and let failure show where to adjust. Switching to more volume at lower intensity is a "fallacy".
BLK-70 | Strength loss means the deficit is too big | Strength dropping on a diet means the deficit is too large. Losing some strength from weight lost is normal, but not from the deficit itself; early in a cut glycogen isn't depleted, so big drops aren't diet-caused.
BLK-71 | Muscle loss in a deficit | In a deficit the body prioritises organs, so some muscle loss may be unavoidable. Advanced lifters lose muscle mainly because they get weaker and can't produce their usual stimulus; naturals avoid this through patience.
BLK-72 | Building muscle in a deficit | Building muscle in a deficit is possible but slower than at maintenance, which is slower than in a surplus.
BLK-73 | Don't wait for a strength goal to cut | An overweight lifter shouldn't wait to hit a strength goal (e.g. a 3-plate bench) before cutting; lose fat while gaining strength and hit it lighter. While dieting, the diet is the priority, not strength chasing.
BLK-74 | Managing hunger on a cut | Fight cut hunger with low-calorie filling foods and protein, and save calories for the evening; he says a spoon of bee pollen kills mild hunger.
BLK-75 | Mini-cuts | A mini-cut makes sense if injured for weeks during a bulk. Mini-cuts "never really hurt", but a young lifter shouldn't do many.
BLK-76 | Fasting works only via the weekly deficit | Fasting works only through the weekly deficit it creates ("volume fast" vs "frequency fast"); a weekly 24 h fast plus a surplus isn't an "ultimate recomp" strategy.
BLK-77 | He defers on cutting advice | He defers on cutting advice because he has spent almost his whole career bulking, with only one (bad) cut.
BLK-78 | Avoid bulk/cut cycles | For non-extreme naturals, bulk/cut cycles become yo-yo dieting: the same 10-20 lb up and down forever, no progress, metabolic stress and bad habits ("bulk-cut purgatory"). In his view, cycles come from pro (enhanced) bodybuilding.
BLK-79 | If you cycle, make cycles long and gentle | If you do cycle, make phases as long as possible with the smallest calorie difference (even a six-month bulk is short), and avoid micro-bulk/micro-cut zigzags around a target weight.
BLK-80 | Naturals live at roughly 12-18% | Naturals should live in a moderate body-fat range, presentable year-round; he prefers the upper half for himself.
BLK-81 | Year-round shredded is a trap | Year-round shredded leanness keeps you catabolic, creates your own "natural limit" and small lifters, and harms hormones. Naturals should be very lean only for a few weeks before a show; in his view natural shows should judge at 13-14%.
BLK-82 | Limits of natural leanness | Natural competitors need about 10%; much lower costs so much mass you may not look like you lift. 10% is reachable while staying big only if done slowly.
BLK-83 | Shredded means hungry, weak and small | Being shredded means hungry, unfocused, weaker and smaller. He never wants it and would cut only to see his body without fluff, or when he has little gym time.
BLK-84 | Big naturals spent years in a surplus | Every natural who reached 180-200+ lb spent years in a sizable (not necessarily dirty) surplus, and any big, strong natural carries some fat at some point. An aesthetic physique still starts lean.
BLK-85 | More muscle carries fat better | More muscle lets you carry more fat and still look lean; muscle spreads fat and veins show. Some subcutaneous/intramuscular fat and water make naturals look fuller and bigger.
BLK-86 | Abs: built in the gym, revealed by diet | Train abs on a bulk so they show sooner; natural abs can stay visible year-round at moderate body fat. Don't use the six-pack as a cutting gauge, since fat distribution varies.
BLK-87 | "Ab anxiety" keeps lifters small | "Ab anxiety" or small guy syndrome means clinging to a skinny six-pack and refusing to gain weight. Adding weight raises total fat but not necessarily body-fat %.
BLK-88 | Maingaining is a rebranded lean bulk | "Maingaining" is lean bulking rebranded, and recomp isn't "gaining" because bodyweight stays the same. In his view marketing it to skinny beginners (often by enhanced people) leaves them recomping and never growing; say "lean bulk" or "recomp" instead.
BLK-89 | Bulk in winter, maintain in summer | Bulk slowly through winter and spring, and maintain or recomp in summer when appetite is low; cut only if competing. Alternating bulk and maintenance phases is "smart".
BLK-90 | His status in early 2025 | At 220 lb and higher body fat he was recomping while still progressing, planning to resume the bulk.
BLK-91 | No spot reduction | Fat comes off in an order you can't control (core and face first, legs and lower back later; already-lean areas don't lose more). Creams and pills are marketing, and local high-rep work is trivial next to diet. Love handles come off only by slowly using fat stores.
BLK-92 | Yo-yo dieting scrambles fat distribution | His repeated bulk/cut swings made his fat distribution go haywire with new deposits, and dirty-bulk fat is stubborn.
CAR-01 | Lungs must never end a set first | Never end a squat or deadlift set out of breath with reps left in the muscles. Getting winded after one rep, on curls or after a bench set means poor conditioning; fix it with conditioning work or a slower tempo.
CAR-02 | Poor conditioning costs size | Poor conditioning means unfinished sets, lost tonnage and ultimately lost size. You can't build high lower-body tonnage without stamina; many people fail intermediate programs this way.
CAR-03 | Balance heart and muscle endurance | Balance cardiovascular and muscular endurance. Extra conditioning won't hinder growth, but being out of breath while lifting means cardio is limiting the muscle work, so fix cardio or methods.
CAR-04 | Puking is low work capacity | Vomiting in training signals low work capacity, not a good session; improve conditioning.
CAR-05 | More than 5 reps is not "cardio" | People who call anything over 5 reps "cardio" lack work capacity, and avoiding reps reinforces it. High reps for legs are a requirement.
CAR-06 | Lifting alone rarely taxes the heart | Lifting isn't metabolically demanding (you rest as long as you like), so you must actively seek breathlessness. Mostly-upper-body machine training barely taxes the heart; older machine-only lifters should add lower-body work.
CAR-07 | Fitness is separate from looking big | Being big, lean or vascular doesn't mean fit, and stamina and cardiovascular health are separate qualities. You should be able to talk for 40 minutes without breathlessness and climb stairs without getting winded.
CAR-08 | Conditioning is easy to keep | Once built, cardiovascular endurance is easy to maintain; it recovers fast between sessions and matters mainly within the workout.
CAR-09 | Cardio done right helps gains | Cardio doesn't kill gains or testosterone. Good cardio boosts gains through more volume, better pumps and faster recovery.
CAR-10 | Cardio's real cost is calories | Cardio doesn't cannibalise muscle but uses calories meant for recovery, so how much you can do depends on intake. Skinny men prioritising muscle should avoid cardio-focused activity; long slow distance limits growth only through calorie shortfall.
CAR-11 | Cardio isn't needed for leanness | Cardio isn't needed to be lean or to recomp; it burns calories but can raise appetite, so weigh the trade. With his low appetite he never used cardio to burn calories.
CAR-12 | Count cardio in your recovery budget | Cardio also loads joints and muscles, so factor it into frequency and recovery. Weighted cardio has a recovery cost but is time-efficient and synergistic with lifting.
CAR-13 | Heavy legs can hurt endurance | Low-intensity cardio barely touches a lifter's recovery, but heavy leg training can hurt endurance, so fighters must program legs carefully.
CAR-14 | 5-15 hard minutes after lifting | Do a short block of high-intensity cardio after each session (it replaces pump finishers), plus low-intensity walking, hiking or swimming.
CAR-15 | Never cardio before lifting | Never do cardio before lifting; it lowers lifting intensity. Do it after, on another day or at another time, and warm up by ramping the lift (wear layers), not with cardio.
CAR-16 | On a cut, cardio in a separate session | On a cut, do cardio separately (e.g. in the morning) because energy is too low to pair it with lifting; on a bulk, right around the lifting session is fine.
CAR-17 | Program sprints like deadlifts | Treat sprints like deadlifts: intense, well warmed up, infrequent and far from heavy squat/deadlift days. Hamstring strain risk means warming up and doing weighted stretches.
CAR-18 | Cardio on leg days | Hard cardio on top of leg training now counts as a plus for recovery and conditioning.
CAR-19 | His 2024 cardio routine | Hard cardio twice a week (a threshold mile on a self-powered treadmill, or a stair stepper on tiptoes with a heavy backpack) plus outdoor weighted carries on his days off.
CAR-20 | Joggers need several runs a week | Joggers typically need several runs a week to benefit.
CAR-21 | Giant sets are a bodybuilder's GPP | Supersets and giant sets of non-interfering movements, done non-stop, tax the heart without separate cardio or hurting strength, and conditioning rises with working weights. Giant sets are "passive cardio with almost no cost" as long as they don't become a circuit.
CAR-22 | Giant-set examples | Examples: a lower-body move → weighted carry → ab move; start with a squat then easy exercises; end upper days with push-ups + lateral raises + face pulls until quality drops; pull-ups + dips for novices. If a pull-up/face-pull superset kills performance, your conditioning is poor.
CAR-23 | Lower-body lifts build work capacity best | Heavy high-rep squats and deadlifts are "compressed HIIT". High-rep lower-body work plus giant sets give enough moderate conditioning; if you skip legs, add supersets to compensate.
CAR-24 | Conditioning is specific | High-rep squats carry over to breathing under heavy squats better than sprints. Find your most cardio-taxing lift and make it easier over time (variations, shorter rests, giant sets). Strongmen use farmer's walks or hikes; powerlifters use squats or rack/isometric pulls.
CAR-25 | Don't rely on weights alone | He doesn't recommend doing cardio only through weight training; add an activity you like, such as swimming or hiking.
