CAR-26 | AMRAP compound sets: use sparingly | Very high-rep AMRAP compound sets are limited by heart and lungs and hard on joints; use them sparingly. A few sets of paused squats beat a long treadmill session for leg size and strength.
CAR-27 | GPP menu for lifters | Good GPP for lifters: pull-ups, push-ups, ab work, plate carries, traps, bodyweight hyperextensions, sandbag carries/tosses, sled drags. Nothing that fries you for the next session.
CAR-28 | Walk daily; lifting isn't enough activity | Lifting alone isn't enough activity: walk daily, walk instead of riding, carry groceries. Sitting still breeds muscle and tendon pain, and desk workers need more cardio than blue-collar workers.
CAR-29 | 10k steps: fine, but slow | 10k steps gets people walking, but in his view its promoters forget that desk workers lack the time. Walking is low in impact and fatigue but time-costly.
CAR-30 | Minimal-effort cardio does nothing | Minimal-effort "cardio" (slow pedalling while gaming, a very slow treadmill walk, under-desk steppers) does essentially nothing.
CAR-31 | Any hard method works; he prefers HIIT | Any method works if your heart rate is up and breath short. It's boring but necessary, so link it to something you practise. His preference is HIIT (sprints, beep tests, giant sets): time-saving and fun.
CAR-32 | Best picks: fartlek, StairMaster, rucking | His top conditioning tools for bodybuilders are fartlek, the StairMaster, heavy rucking, the rowing machine, crawling, sandbags and rebounding.
CAR-33 | Running ranks low for bodybuilders | Running is hard on the lower back, shins and knees and interferes with leg training; long-distance running requires losing muscle and costs too much time and energy. Sprints and fartlek stay highly rated.
CAR-34 | Treadmills weak; manual ones the exception | The motorised treadmill is weak because it does part of the work; self-powered treadmills are much harder because you create the momentum. Outdoors, run on dirt, grass or sand, never pavement or stone (knees).
CAR-35 | Steppers and jump rope | The StairMaster is now his best cardio machine; jump rope is poor for bodybuilders (high coordination cost, low tax once learned).
CAR-36 | Swimming: noble, not a physique shortcut | Swimming is relaxing and builds shoulders, but it is not a physique shortcut: it takes far more time than lifting for the same stimulus and builds a disproportionate physique. It beats running for slimming while building muscle.
CAR-37 | Cardio machines don't build muscle | Rowing machines and air bikes don't build legs or back ("chicken/Peewee tier" as muscle builders), even though the rower is top-tier cardio. Running does nothing for his muscles and can't replace lifting during a break; uphill running is tough on hamstrings and glutes.
CAR-38 | Athletes: the sport covers conditioning | An athlete's sport already covers conditioning, so lifting should target the fibre damage the sport leaves. You can do plenty of health cardio, but you can't be both a competitive endurance athlete and a high-level bodybuilder.
CAR-39 | Combat conditioning in short intense pockets | Fighters should condition with 30-40 min sessions of intense pockets with rest, not long runs. An optional conditioning day is safer than a third lifting day. Each day combines cardio, thoracic extension, abs, posterior chain and carries or pulls.
CAR-40 | Rugby and cycling conditioning | For rugby, fartlek "Indian runs"; for a cycling-focused day, short dashes paired with leg and ab work.
CAR-41 | Keeping leg size as an athlete | To maintain leg size as an athlete, train legs once a week with a little strength work, or squat/deadlift every 15 days and do cardio otherwise.
BEG-01 | Anything works, so choose for the long term | Almost any program works for novices. That should free them to pick what sets up the rest of their career, not justify random programs, and novice results prove nothing about a program, coach or channel.
BEG-02 | Build a base and habits, not performance | Novice status is short but you are an intermediate or advanced lifter for life, so use the novice period to build a base, prevent injuries and form habits rather than to chase numbers.
BEG-03 | Don't rush out of the novice phase | Treat the novice phase like childhood and prolong it. The noob-gain "poison" and the "noob" stigma make people rush.
BEG-04 | Noob gains are a quantity, not a window | Noob gains are an amount of development, not a time window that closes. A bad program wastes time but not potential.
BEG-05 | Novices need only the bare minimum | Novices are built to grow from very little, so do the bare minimum and don't add volume you don't need. "Optimal" means adding complexity only once the simpler thing stops working.
BEG-06 | Novice is about experience, not strength numbers | Novice status depends on experience, not strength. Strength standards are worthless for classifying lifters.
BEG-07 | Novice ends when you can write your program | You stop being a novice when you want to, and can, write your own program.
BEG-08 | What makes an intermediate | An intermediate can overload by means other than adding weight and knows what they want and where their weak points are. Intermediates struggle because volume becomes a new overload variable.
BEG-09 | Training stage is per body part | Training stages apply per body part: a 600 lb deadlifter can be a novice at arm training.
BEG-10 | How long the novice phase lasts | His estimate of the novice phase's length has moved around. His latest view is that many are done within a year, but bad programming and program hopping stretch it to years.
BEG-11 | Three full-body days a week by default | The novice default is 3 full-body days a week, 48 h apart (Mon/Wed/Fri); 2-3 days works.
BEG-12 | Build frequency before sets, sets before weight | Of volume, intensity and frequency, complete beginners should first build frequency (2x/week → 3x), then add sets rather than weight.
BEG-13 | Novice frequency mainly prevents injury | For novices, frequency matters more for injury prevention than for hypertrophy.
BEG-14 | Sessions of about 45-60 minutes | Novice sessions should last about 45-60 min and lengthen as you advance; 1.5-2 h sessions are junk volume for a novice.
BEG-15 | Novices lack work capacity, not recovery | Novices don't recover faster than advanced lifters. They lack work capacity and mental stamina, can't exert themselves as hard, and burn out mentally before physically.
BEG-16 | Small additions can wreck a novice | A few extra sets can wreck a novice for a week; some even get sick.
BEG-17 | As much as possible from few hard sets | Get as much as possible from as few hard sets as possible and stay shy of maximum recoverable volume. Overshooting sets is for experienced lifters.
BEG-18 | Start around 3 sets per muscle per week | A novice muscle starts at about 3 sets a week, rising to 4-6 only when needed.
BEG-19 | About 12 sets a day in bi-sets | A novice day is 1-2 bi-sets of 3 sets. Start with bi-sets before tri-sets and avoid heavy giant sets, which are too hard on a beginner's cardiovascular system.
BEG-20 | Accessories at 3 x 8-12 | Three sets of 8-12 are enough for novice accessories.
BEG-21 | No deloads for novices | A novice program that needs a deload is too much; novices must learn to manage fatigue instead.
BEG-22 | But don't train too easy | Novices must experience high intensity; the "number one beginner mistake is training too easy".
BEG-23 | Novices need written, rigid structure | Novices need a written, fixed structure; flexibility is earned. They can't be trusted with lift rotations or optional exercises, and should start on an established program because they lack the data to program for themselves.
BEG-24 | Leave some room for choice and mistakes | Within that structure, leave some room for choice (rep ranges rather than fixed numbers, a small choice of lifts) and for first mistakes, which can't be catastrophic. His novice program is "deliberately open-handed".
BEG-25 | Full body is the best novice split | Full body is the best novice split; a novice wanting 3 days is better served by full body than by half a PPL. No gentleman splits.
BEG-26 | His template: supersets, few compounds, rest isolation | His novice template is 3 full-body days with supersets throughout (they build work capacity), 2-3 compounds per day and the rest isolation.
BEG-27 | Don't stack heavy compounds in one day | Don't pile several heavy compounds into one day (squat + row + deadlift + bench); novices "overdo compounds".
BEG-28 | Isolation from day one | "Compounds cover small muscles" is false. Compounds build the centre line, while arms and delts need isolation; skipping it leaves big quads and chest with "toothpick arms" after 2 years.
BEG-29 | No dedicated arm day for novices | A novice arm day is a waste; add one curl and one triceps movement to existing days and add forearms later.
BEG-30 | Catch weak points and small muscles early | His current view is that "just grow everything" is bad advice: dormant weak points cost years of catch-up, so train small parts, including forearm isolation, from day one.
BEG-31 | Introduce every movement pattern | A good novice program introduces every movement pattern and builds a wide base: slower progress but a higher ceiling. The squat, bench and deadlift alone don't cover every pattern.
BEG-32 | Don't force a style on novices | Novice programs shouldn't force a style (powerlifting, rings). Start with a broad strength-and-size goal and specialise later, since most people only discover what they want after lifting a while.
BEG-33 | Powerlifting novice programs work only because anything works | Starting Strength, StrongLifts, GreySkull and 5x5/3x5 are powerlifting programs that lack isolation and catapult novices into weights they shouldn't handle. They work only because anything works for novices.
BEG-34 | Run the program until it stops working | Run a novice program until it stops working, not for a fixed 4, 8 or 12 weeks, and don't program-hop. A fixed timeline is a red flag.
BEG-35 | Template users: 6-12 months per template | If you train from templates, run one for 6-12 months before alternating with a second.
BEG-36 | Monotony is fine; fancy methods come later | Repeat the basics with close variations. Fancy lifts, drop sets and lift rotations come later; master variations before rotating them.
BEG-37 | Close variations are allowed | Variations close in specificity are fine, so novices needn't repeat an identical lift 3x/week; they help reach deeper ranges.
BEG-38 | Keep it fun and keep them hungry | Keep novice training fun and give "just enough to keep them hungry". Endless squat, bench and deadlift makes novices quit.
BEG-39 | His own beginner program ladder | His ladder runs from a novice/"beginner" version to Greek Statue / Adam / Baki / Tokita Ohma, then intermediate programs. After graduating, he points to the full-body hypertrophy program in his "natural apogee" playlist.
BEG-40 | Start light and accumulate quality reps | Start light, even with the empty bar, and accumulate quality reps without struggling or compromising the next session. Repeat a hard weight before adding more; no PR is worth an injury.
BEG-41 | Fixed schemes first, evolving ranges on accessories | Fixed schemes (5x5, 3x5) are fine at first; use evolving ranges on accessories, then spread them. If every lift uses evolving ranges, tonnage collapses when all hit their low point at once.
BEG-42 | Use rep ranges where form stays clean | Novices should use higher reps in a range where form stays clean; back work that loads the lower back and hamstrings needs at least 6-10 reps.
BEG-43 | Early bar progress is mostly neural | Early strength gains are mostly neural and technical, not muscle.
BEG-44 | Skill first; strength never exceeds skill | Strength should rise but never outrun skill; real training starts once you can perform the big lifts.
BEG-45 | Teach other overload methods early | Teach novices to overload by manipulating reps, sets and volume, not just load.
BEG-46 | New lifts: start easy, then go to failure | For unpracticed lifts or rep ranges, start easy without failure, then push to failure.
BEG-47 | Free weights and bodyweight before machines | Novices should learn on free weights and bodyweight; machines, cables and the Smith are for advanced lifters. Novices on machines "stay small for years", and a machine-and-cable novice program is a bad sign.
BEG-48 | Calisthenics is an ideal entry point | Calisthenics is an ideal entry and perhaps the best base for bodybuilding: learn to move your body through space without needing a gym, rep ranges or plate math.
BEG-49 | Pull-ups over pulldowns; vertical over horizontal | For novices, vertical pulls matter more than horizontal pulls and pull-ups beat pulldowns, which are accessories. Vertical pulls decompress the shoulders and spine when benching often.
BEG-50 | Pull-up progression for novices | Use negatives if you can't do a pull-up, then many low-rep sets or AMRAP sets, and stay at bodyweight until a clean 15. No weighted chins for novices.
BEG-51 | No barbell or Pendlay rows for novices | Novices can't pull from the floor well yet, so use cable or dumbbell rows; cable rows teach the lower back before it must bridge a row. Most intermediates should skip barbell rows too.
BEG-52 | A full-ROM floor pull, at low volume | A full-ROM pull from the floor is mandatory for novices, but deadlift volume stays low. Any full hip hinge counts (conventional, sumo, trap bar, Atlas stones).
BEG-53 | Deadlift entry points and grip | Ways in: trap bar (high → low handles → conventional), rack pulls to learn the hinge, deficits to teach leg drive, dumbbell deadlifts. No Jefferson or snatch-grip deadlifts for novices; grip double overhand with straps (see EQP-07).
BEG-54 | Squat free; no box; parallel is enough | Novices should squat free and unsupported, to parallel. A box fixes depth instead of mobility and box squatters can't reach parallel later, so squat higher and deepen over months; mobility is innate, just unpracticed.
BEG-55 | Paused back squat is the one variation | The paused back squat is the one novice variation. No front squats, which need thoracic mobility and front-rack time, unless the lifter loves them.
BEG-56 | Goblet squats first, heel-elevated later | Learn the normal barbell squat before heel-elevated squats. Goblet squats are a good start (B tier); bodyweight squats are C tier.
BEG-57 | Several horizontal presses a week | Novices need several horizontal presses a week; prefer paused and close-grip bench to floor press. Too much overhead plus incline pressing overloads the shoulders, and range you can't control should be trained with an easier pressing mode.
BEG-58 | Incline or knee push-ups, not wall push-ups | Beginners who can't do push-ups should use incline or knee push-ups, not wall push-ups.
BEG-59 | Strict standing dumbbell curls first | Start with strict standing dumbbell curls; cheat curls only later and very light. No heavy partials, and never curl daily.
BEG-60 | Novice shoulders: face pulls; lateral raises added later | Face pulls are great and cheap for novices. In 2020 he said side raises were unnecessary (OHP plus face pulls was enough); by 2022 his novice example includes lateral raises.
BEG-61 | Back hyperextensions for novices | Back hyperextensions teach novices to use their legs and build the lower back; they waste tonnage for advanced lifters.
BEG-62 | Abs: bracing first, then stretch work | Beginners should start abs with bracing only, then add stretch exercises; pullovers once a week, building range gradually.
BEG-63 | Learn by modifying the program you run | Learn to program by modifying the novice program you run, then use it as a skeleton: copy its rep ranges, match its tonnage, stay minimalist. The first draft will look terrible; if the base program is very bad, start from scratch.
BEG-64 | First self-written programs are too greedy | First self-written programs almost always have too many exercises, sets, reps and too much frequency, and the jump from novice to intermediate volume crashes people. A bridge program, or a novice program that already ramps volume, helps ("novices are not made of sugar").
BEG-65 | Only following programs keeps you a novice | Novices who only follow programs never learn to program, so they plateau, quit and restart as novices. His novice program exists to launch people into self-programming.
BEG-66 | Everyone should eventually write their own | Everyone should eventually write their own programs; from 2024 he adds having an experienced coach review them.
BEG-67 | Experiment early, but don't wing it | Beginners should experiment, not optimise, to build instinct, but without winging it too much.
BEG-68 | Novices don't need optimisation or coaching | Optimising is "the surest way for a novice to stay a novice". Knowledge is a small part of the value and application the rest, so eat more and run a free program instead of paying a coach, and spend less time on fitness YouTube.
BEG-69 | "Optimal" is relative: start with what you can | For a very weak beginner, 5 minutes of knee push-ups is optimal. Doing something beats waiting for the perfect workout; avoid paralysis by analysis, since anything B-S tier works.
BEG-70 | About 90% of lifters quit, mostly as novices | Most people who start lifting quit, mostly in the novice phase.
BEG-71 | "Novice syndrome": overdoing it is the main crash cause | Overdoing it, not lack of potential, is the top reason novices crash, and it is mostly mental. Burnout is over-stimulation (e.g. 2 h sessions), not overtraining; a program that stops you training for a full week is bad for you.
BEG-72 | Newbie gains spoil people | Fast early gratification and easy linear progression spoil lifters, who crash when progress slows; many quit on reaching "intermediate" expecting magic.
BEG-73 | Adherence is the number one principle | Adherence is the first program principle: remove barriers (meal prep, any structured program, planned transport) and use "floaters". Lower volume and intensity at first so the habit lasts.
BEG-74 | Avoid the "Arnold effect"; set modest goals | Avoid the "Arnold effect" (credited to Zack Telander) and draconian pro lifestyles; set modest, real goals.
BEG-75 | Many novice programs are filters | Many novice programs are filters that only outliers survive; injured novices indict popular programs. Most novices fail to reach intermediate because of injuries, plateaus and boredom.
BEG-76 | Imperfect form is OK at tiny loads | Imperfect form is acceptable for novices because loads are tiny, as long as they stay in a safe rep range. Still master the objective form rules, warm up with as many sets as needed, and never jump straight to heavy weight.
BEG-77 | Novices miss early injury signals | Novices follow programs to the letter and miss pain that signals an injury in the making.
BEG-78 | Beginner wrist pain often comes from pull-ups | Wrist pain in beginners mostly comes from pull-ups done by gripping hard and pulling with the wrists.
BEG-79 | Deadlifts tax beginners as much as advanced lifters | Relative to their weaker muscles and nervous system, deadlifts are as taxing for beginners as for advanced lifters.
BEG-80 | Novices should just eat more | Novices should eat more and gain weight; diet barely matters unless they are very skinny or very fat. No perfect macros or supplements needed, though he still recommends whole foods.
BEG-81 | Skinny novices bulk; average novices maingain | Skinny novices should bulk rather than recomp; average novices can maingain from day one; for skinny-fat novices the approach hardly matters. Avoid extreme bulk/cut cycles and dirty bulks.
BEG-82 | Strength programs push teens to bulk for leverage | Strength-centric novice programs push teenagers to bulk for leverage instead of for muscle.
BEG-83 | Kids can start conservatively around 10 | Children can start conservative training around 10 (goblet squats, pull-ups, push-ups), heavy squats and deadlifts at 15-16. He rejects "don't lift before puberty ends"; teens should prioritise form, work capacity and enjoyment over muscle.
BEG-84 | Teen training plan | Teens: no ego lifting, moderate-to-high rep ranges with some low-rep work, and every body part trained semi-frequently. A 14-year-old boxer should do 2-3 full-body sessions, not 4 days.
BEG-85 | It's never too late to start | It's never too late to start; using past inaction as an excuse is a "reverse sunk-cost fallacy". Elderly, disabled and amputee people benefit (e.g. with unilateral work).
BEG-86 | Realistic timelines take years | Going from skinny to big takes years, not months; being small at 17-20 is normal.
BEG-87 | Combat athletes and laborers: low lifting frequency | Combat athletes and manual workers should lift 2-3 compound-focused sessions a week plus grip, core, neck and bridging work. No Bulgarian-style training.
BEG-88 | Obese beginners: balanced resistance training | A heavily overweight beginner should avoid calisthenics (joints) and powerlifting (a slippery slope to staying fat) and use a balanced resistance program. Bodyweight templates suit light people and frustrate heavy ones.
BEG-89 | Returning after a long break | After a long break, return with short, low-volume sessions of favourite exercises and let muscle memory work. No hard programs (e.g. his Guts program) for returners.
BEG-90 | Women: same physiology, different goals | Women respond like men physiologically but have different goals; "just train like men" is lazy. They need muscle for health and should avoid doing too much and burning out. A reluctant partner can start with bodyweight work at home or light bar and dumbbell work framed as time together.
BEG-91 | Fear of being mocked is unfounded | Fear of being mocked is a fragile ego: nobody notices you, and everyone good once looked foolish. Fear of getting "too big" is laughable for a natural.
BEG-92 | Trust and test, don't copy | Build your own judgment and understand everything you do; copying someone's methods won't give you their body. Your first program or guru seems best only for lack of comparison.
EQP-01 | Strap up when grip limits a pulling set | Use straps as soon as grip limits a back or pulling set, and spend the saved grip on direct forearm work. "Letting grip limit the back is criminal"; straps let you isolate the back better and don't hurt forearm growth.
EQP-02 | Bodybuilders should strap their pulls | Every non-powerlifting bodybuilder should use straps on pulls (deadlifts, rows, shrugs); there's no reason not to, and they are "essential for deadlifts".
EQP-03 | Straps are worth 20-30% on back work | Without straps he would lose 20-30% performance on back work; straps can multiply upper-back gains.
EQP-04 | Don't strap when grip isn't the limit | Do warm-ups bare-handed and skip straps where grip doesn't limit (for him, pull-ups and dumbbell rows). Novices don't need them at first, and overuse steals forearm work.
EQP-05 | Straps on dumbbells can disrupt the path | On dumbbells, straps restrict wrist rotation and can disrupt the path and recruitment.
EQP-06 | If you dislike straps, use them for volume work | If you dislike straps, use a regular grip for most working sets and straps for volume work.
EQP-07 | Hook and mixed grip are powerlifting dogma | Double-overhand grip limits floor pulls "100% of the time". The hook grip is a "biological strap" that differs from straps only by ego and discomfort, mixed grip is terrible, and the double-overhand → mixed/hook progression is powerlifting dogma irrelevant to hypertrophy.
EQP-08 | Anti-strap arguments are silly | Claims that straps cause injury, ruin form, feed ego or are "cheating" are silly. He points to strongmen who pull and carry huge weights in straps without wrist injuries; a year of strap use gave him only bruises, and a bruise isn't an injury.
EQP-09 | Remove stability or grip so the target limits the set | Removing the stability demand (Smith) or the grip demand (straps, wrist cuffs for laterals or triceps extensions) lets the target muscle alone limit the set.
EQP-10 | How to choose straps | Pick grainy straps (IronMind) over silky ones, long enough to loop 2-3 times; pay more than $5 and stick with one pair. They stiffen and improve with sweat (he never washes them).
EQP-11 | No lifting gloves | Gloves let the bar slip, smell and need replacing; bare-hand discomfort goes away within about a week.
EQP-12 | Callus care | Rub a lotion bar into your hands every night (chalk dries them) and shave calluses with a nail clipper about every 2 months.
EQP-13 | Gear only on heavy sets, never warm-ups | Gear is fine if it doesn't change the lift's strength curve and helps recovery or strength. Do warm-ups raw and add gear from about 75-80% intensity ("don't belt up for one plate").
EQP-14 | Elastic gear isn't for novices | High-poundage elastic gear (wraps, slingshot, bench shirt) has a learning curve, isn't for novices, and is useless worn as fashion.
EQP-15 | Belts add rigidity, matter little for hypertrophy | A belt adds rigidity, not stored energy; it takes some lower-back stress, helps leg drive and adds weight for most people, so powerlifters should use one. Its effect on abs is negligible for hypertrophy. No shame in using one unless it hides bad form; he tried one once and never again.
EQP-16 | Sleeves and wraps are mostly placebo | Sleeves and wraps are mainly psychological and placebo unless very rigid, but still worth buying, since "a placebo effect … is still an effect". He wears cheap elbow and wrist wraps for warmth.
EQP-17 | Slingshot: useful once, now dropped | The slingshot takes weight off the bottom of the bench, deloads shoulders and chest and keeps elbows tucked; buy the size for your bodyweight. By 2025 he no longer uses his and is selling it.
EQP-18 | Squat shoes: from heels to flat | His current view: he squats in flat shoes with no difference and keeps lifting shoes only for high-bar. He calls wrestling shoes the best general shoe (2023).
EQP-19 | Shoe rule: wide toes, one shoe for anywhere | The sole should be at least as wide at the toes as at the heel. Barefoot is great, but if you are later forced back into shoes you lose strength, so pick one shoe you can wear anywhere.
EQP-20 | Commercial gyms are optimal; train hybrid | His current view is that commercial gyms are optimal for building muscle. He trains about 50/50: legs at home; pull-ups, overhead and isolation at the gym. He joined a gym because home limits explained his lagging triceps and delts.
EQP-21 | Owning your space can increase gains | Owning your training space lets you train as aggressively as you want (drop weights, grunt, music, shirtless); he progressed far more after building his own gym. In 2020-2021 he said "eventually you need your own personal gym".
EQP-22 | Commercial gyms have hidden time costs | Travel and overhead add up, and walking between machines, hunting for clips and waiting for equipment kill focus and pump.
EQP-23 | Pick a gym that lets you train hard | Other gym-goers slow progress most (shared racks, chatty members, staff policing deadlift lowering, pop music). Leave a gym that bans grunting or dropping weights; "intimidating" hardcore gyms are better.
EQP-24 | Home gym economics | You can start a home gym cheaply, and owning equipment creates commitment. The usual "it saves money" argument is weak, though quality gear resells well.
EQP-25 | Don't build during a price crisis | Don't build a home gym during a crisis when prices spike; do calisthenics meanwhile.
EQP-26 | Pool money for a shared gym | If you need company, pool money with friends for a shared neighbourhood gym.
EQP-27 | Core minimal kit: pull-up bar, rings, bands | A pull-up bar, wooden gymnastic rings and bands are enough for excellent gains; rings are invaluable without a gym. Add a dip station.
EQP-28 | Calisthenics alone limits legs and size | Calisthenics is a valid program you can run for years, but legs respond far better to barbell compounds and getting big is very hard without free weights, so mix them.
EQP-29 | Build a home setup in stages | Build gradually: pull-up bar → small rack or squat stands → Olympic bar → plates. With no room for a rack, get a dip/pull-up station plus compact squat stands. A bench and squat rack are non-negotiable for full chest and legs; a barbell-only setup struggles with small muscles and risks a "spider physique".
EQP-30 | Rings: wrist-friendly, upper body only | Rings are easy on the wrists and key for underrated horizontal pulls, but upper-body only; ring-only arm training is foolish.
EQP-31 | With only bars: weighted pull-ups and dips | With only pull-up and dip bars, weighted pull-ups and dips are the staples; kettlebells beat plates for loading them (compact, less dangling).
EQP-32 | Light loads: reach failure, not 50 easy reps | With little weight, keep reps higher, combine weights and bands to reach failure at lower reps rather than doing 50 easy pump reps, and superset movements for the same muscle. Not optimal, but it works.
EQP-33 | Improvised loading ideas | Load push-ups with a sturdy backpack, do heel-elevated dumbbell squats for high reps, use ring rows with feet far back as an upright-row substitute, or run a light kettlebell circuit.
EQP-34 | Kettlebells are inferior for hypertrophy | Kettlebells are inferior for hypertrophy and acceptable only as minimalist training; sandbags aren't practical for hypertrophy.
EQP-35 | Mindset beats equipment | Lack of equipment is no excuse: he cites African lifters in scrap-metal gyms, medieval peasants, Apollon's homemade railway-wheel bar and a bamboo gym.
EQP-36 | Only buy what you'll use weekly | Only buy equipment you will use at least once a week; later he sells anything not used weekly.
EQP-37 | Buy nice or buy twice | Buy high-end but not luxury gear; boutique bars cost double for no gain. Quality bars, racks and brand-name stations resell easily; knockoffs don't.
EQP-38 | Ways to save money | Buy cosmetic seconds (Rogue Boneyard), wait for sales, call manufacturer sales reps for non-commercial models, and bundle orders to hit shipping thresholds without buying unwanted pieces.
EQP-39 | Delivery logistics | Check bundle stock (ask for missing items to ship later), be present for delivery and have heavy items brought inside, and rent a truck plus a strong helper for plywood and mats.
EQP-40 | Cheap DIY substitutes | Use hardware-store wooden blocks instead of fitness-brand ones, spring clamps instead of collars, a cheap mirror instead of expensive quotes, and pay a welder for cuts.
EQP-41 | Gradual build vs full bundle | Building gradually spreads the cost; buying a full bundle at once saves shipping and gives peace of mind.
EQP-42 | Barbell and rack are the centrepiece | Start a home gym with one quality Olympic bar and a rack, not a collection of specialty bars. A bar without a rack mostly limits you to floor pulls; never clean a bar onto your back to squat.
EQP-43 | Buy the best bar you can | A good bar lasts decades and flexes back under load, while cheap bars bend permanently.
EQP-44 | Try a bar model before buying | Train with a bar model for weeks before buying, and test stiffness if you dislike stiff bars. His favourite is the Rogue Ohio Power Bar, plus a cheap Boneyard beater bar for rack pulls.
EQP-45 | Bar care | Stainless bars need no upkeep; non-chrome steel rusts, so oil and brush it often. Brush chalk out after every session and store the bar horizontally on a wall or vertically, off cold humid floors.
EQP-46 | Always set safeties when alone | Always set safety arms when training alone; you need total trust in your safeties.
EQP-47 | J-cups: roller cups solve both problems | Plastic-lined J-cups resist sliding but the liner wears at heavy loads, after which steel damages the bar; steel cups wear the knurling. Roller cups fix both; his hack was another brand's roller cups with the rear guard cut off, secured with a strap.
EQP-48 | Rack-mounted monolift arm | A rack-mounted monolift arm costs far less than a dedicated monolift; he uses it weekly for bench lift-offs without a spotter. Chalk-mark the holes. He walks out squats and OHP by choice and loves 3x3 racks.
EQP-49 | Chalk-mark pin heights | Chalk marks on the rack let you repeat pin heights for partials, pin presses and squats.
EQP-50 | Weight is weight: buy cheap plates | Buy the cheapest plates in good condition; closing mom-and-pop gyms are the best source.
EQP-51 | Calibrated plates for very heavy loads | At very heavy loads, buy calibrated plates, because cheap "45s" vary enough to create a dangerous side-to-side imbalance. He recommends Vulcan (cheapest marked calibrated).
EQP-52 | Build your own platform | Don't lift on bare concrete (drops damage it, and it's hard on knees and stability); build your own platform for a fraction of retail.
EQP-53 | How to build the platform | Plan on paper and do a mock assembly; buy plywood in person (online stock is warped). Use mid-quality wood for the base and the best wood for the top; glue the base layers but screw the mats and top wood so damaged pieces can be swapped. Shim sloped garage floors, add a vapour barrier in arctic climates, and use wood screws if bolting a rack.
EQP-54 | Horse stall mats, with drawbacks | Horse stall mats are the best and cheapest mats; avoid online puzzle mats. Use two layers or screw them to wood on sensitive floors. Drawbacks: slippery for squat, OHP and bench leg drive (wood is "night and day" better), they crease, shift and collect moisture.
EQP-55 | Platform first, gym on top | Build the platform before installing the gym on it; a large platform becomes immovable.
EQP-56 | Plan the space months ahead | Chalk the footprint, number every item on a paper map, measure to the inch and leave 3D clearance for loading plates. Don't overpack, because a cramped gym kills enthusiasm. Put the rack in first, near the back rather than in the middle.
EQP-57 | Live through a winter before a garage gym | Cold is a major drawback: heaters only take the edge off, bands freeze and steel numbs hands. Live through a winter before building a garage gym; he tolerates it but wouldn't recommend it.
EQP-58 | Shelter an outdoor gym | For a garden gym in a rainy climate, build at least a crude shed; a wet bar is a squat hazard.
EQP-59 | Good machines rethink the movement | Machines are all-or-nothing: the best ones rethink the movement and adjust to anthropometry, but machines often can't replace free weights. Converging presses are "Gorilla tier" and may beat the bench; diverging presses are really shoulder presses; the Nautilus pullover is top tier; cable rows and triceps pulleys are good.
EQP-60 | Home machines: plate-loaded and multi-purpose | If you buy a machine for home, it must be multi-purpose and plate-loaded (easy to move and resell); internal weight-stack stations are a nightmare at home.
EQP-61 | Respect the Smith machine | The Smith machine was invented by Jack LaLanne for safe heavy loading, every serious bodybuilding gym has one, and mocking it is ignorance. The fixed path lets you apply full strength.
EQP-62 | A belt squat is worth buying | A belt squat gives quad tonnage with near-zero lower-back load, plus rows, single-leg RDLs and the best shrugs. Plate-loaded beats cable-loaded.
EQP-63 | Safety squat bar is a game changer | A safety squat bar belongs in every gym. He recommends the Elitefts Yoke SSB (thick pad); its thinner sleeves need clamps.
EQP-64 | Trap bar for shrugs and carries | The trap bar is "Leonidas tier" for shrugs, power shrugs and carries (upper traps).
EQP-65 | No cable stack: use bands on the rack | Without a cable stack, loop a band on the rack with a rope through it, or put a stick in a band loop (better than any V-bar), for pushdowns, rows and face pulls.
EQP-66 | Accommodating resistance is unnecessary | Chains and bands as accommodating resistance are unnecessary for bodybuilding, and useless if set up wrong (chains that never touch the floor, chains on machines).
EQP-67 | Dumbbell-only training is less efficient | Dumbbell-only training is limited: you outgrow them on deadlifts, goblet squats aren't back squats, and isolating legs and triceps is hard. Cables are a big upgrade, and barbells offer more than dumbbells (load aside).
EQP-68 | Adjustable dumbbells are a trap | Adjustable dumbbells are a "trap" (pricey base and plates), worth it only with frequent use. Buy used dumbbells from closing gyms or private sellers instead, but avoid cheap junk dumbbells, which can cause wrist tendonitis.
EQP-69 | Loadable dumbbell handles: niche | Loadable dumbbell handles are worth it only for frequent isolation work, arm wrestling or outgrowing rack dumbbells on rows. They are bulkier with a different strength curve (start light); leave room for the collar.
EQP-70 | Rope-plate loading replaces dumbbells | A plate on a rope is a cheap dumbbell substitute that needs no collars and feels heavier than its weight; bring a rope to the commercial gym for giant sets.
EQP-71 | Benches: try before buying | A "fat pad" bench stops sliding and keeps the scapulae on the pad but limits depth and elbow tuck, so try before buying. He uses a flat bench (adjustables are flimsier and he never inclines). Ab benches aren't worth it; pads are indispensable for hip thrusts.
EQP-72 | EZ bar; calves don't need a machine | A quality EZ bar is worth it for heavy curlers; for occasional curls a cheap one is fine. An EZ bar, a bench and a block make a home seated calf raise, so calves don't need a machine.
EQP-73 | Use a slant board, not plates under heels | Buy a sturdy slant board instead of putting plates under the heels.
EQP-74 | Neck training: get a harness | Plates and bands work for the neck up to a point, then a harness is needed; he now says a harness is far better and regrets not buying one sooner.
EQP-75 | Bands for tendon and joint work | Use bands for tendon and joint "nucleus overload" work at high volume and low intensity.
EQP-76 | Bands feel better than plates | Bands feel more comfortable and, he believes, more hypertrophic than plates; mix both for variety, joint health and adherence.
EQP-77 | SPRI tubes vs looped bands | SPRI tubes have rotating handles (better for curls and OHP), don't freeze and last a lifetime; buy the lightest first and never anchor them under a bench for barbell bench. Looped bands are better for barbell accommodating resistance (known tension).
EQP-78 | A few key grip tools, progressed | Pick a few key tools per grip type and progress them rather than buying every gadget; loading pins allow easy overload. Start with the easiest gripper and thinnest extensor bands and move up only once the level is mastered.
EQP-79 | Fat grips: from skipped to A tier | Fat grips are "A tier": pick a size where the fingers barely touch. Axle bars are B tier (fixed thickness); a thumbless grip gives a similar benefit. Thick bars do cut the load on target muscles.
EQP-80 | Towels upgrade any implement | Use a short, coarse, non-absorbent towel (a dish towel); wrapping any implement upgrades it for forearms. Beyond dumbbells, rope and towel are the best forearm tools.
EQP-81 | Forearm tool tier notes | Wrist rollers beat spring forearm twisters; finger-curl machines are C tier (do isolation light with free weights); grip-sport tools are A tier but costly; rings are C tier for forearms; a light mace is enough for forearm raises.
IND-01 | Lifting knowledge should be free | Charging for knowledge you got for free is unjust; all his own programs are free, and he likens paid plans to selling festival water when free water is hidden nearby.
IND-02 | Learn to program for yourself | A program you build and beta-test yourself is "ten times better" than a bought one, and knowing how to program protects you from exploitation; gurus who discourage self-programming keep you dependent so they can sell.
IND-03 | Most YouTube program sellers are scammers | He claims most people selling programs on YouTube are scammers; a "custom" program that is really a copy-paste template is a scam every time, and most influencer programs are cash grabs written in minutes.
IND-04 | Buying a program is a waste, with a few exceptions | Paying for a program is a waste, especially for a novice; if you do buy, he named a few he thought worth the money and others he thought not.
IND-05 | No pre-written program fits you exactly | Every pre-written program, even a paid custom one, must be modified by the lifter; if changing one thing "breaks" a program it is a bad program, and forbidding changes is ego.
IND-06 | Most lifters don't need a coach | Beginners and advanced lifters alike mostly don't need a coach; he calls coaching "a scam about 99% of the time" and says coaches profit from keeping clients helpless, for example by changing the program weekly to justify fees.
IND-07 | What a good coach or PT looks like | A good coach has the abilities he claims to build, or a track record of struggling clients who got there; he prepares sessions, stays off his phone, corrects form, is excited by a client's talent, and teaches the client to stop needing him.
IND-08 | Working out is not training | "Working out" means getting tired; "training" means getting better toward a goal. Many coaches just exhaust clients so they feel worked.
IND-09 | The supplement industry exists to empty your wallet | He describes the supplement industry as a conglomerate with one shared goal, your money, which has infiltrated every fitness sphere through sponsorships; ignore anyone who earns money or a cut from a supplement.
IND-10 | The supplement marketing playbook | Marketing creates a need, then plants suggestion ("you might not get enough protein"), then FOMO and fake scarcity; viral campaigns piggyback or sponsor memes, and the less an ad looks like an ad, the stronger it is. Understanding this makes you immune.
IND-11 | High-protein dogma was manufactured by sellers | In his view the high-protein dogma and the urgency of the post-workout meal were created by supplement sellers and magazines; protein pushers are often sponsored, and 1970s Weider magazines sold whey next to drugged pros.
IND-12 | If it must be bought, it probably doesn't work | Hard work can't be sold, so a routine that requires buying something, especially something expensive, probably isn't effective. Lifting is a cheap hobby, you can't pay your way to a good body, and people built great physiques 50-60 years ago without supplements, special programs or diet plans.
IND-13 | The guru "switcheroo" | Gurus give a sensible free tip (sleep, sunlight, whole food) to earn trust, then sell a lazy paid replacement for it.
IND-14 | They sell the cure to the illness they created | "When someone sells you the cure, they likely created the illness": influencers create body dysmorphia, low-T anxiety and fear, then sell the fix. Since your progress means you stop needing them, they profit when you never progress.
IND-15 | Fast-result promises are scams | Promises of results in under a month or "get big in two months" are scams that feed fear, insecurity and impatience and push PED use and dysmorphia; real progress takes years.
IND-16 | The industry is a pyramid | Manufacturers sit on top, influencers in the middle and buyers at the base; influencers blame you when a product fails and take the credit when you succeed.
IND-17 | Change comes from buyers, not sellers | Buyers of pre-made programs are more to blame than sellers, so change comes from educating buyers; novices should explore programming themselves.
IND-18 | He doesn't monetise the channel | No ads, sponsors, memberships, supplements or merch, because earning from YouTube makes you tailor content for clicks; he also avoids ads so they don't wake viewers who fall asleep to his videos.
IND-19 | Ko-fi donations as his only income | Ko-fi was his only income channel: voluntary, any amount, no platform cut, no preferential treatment for donors.
IND-20 | Guide, not guru | He keeps his name off the channel, presents as an avatar, and tells viewers to unsubscribe if he ever sells out or holds them back; nothing any YouTuber says, his own included, can be fully trusted, and his channel isn't the only answer.
IND-21 | His paid "strategist" tier | From late 2024/2025 he runs a paid Ko-fi tier offering physique reviews and personalised hypertrophy programs built from the client's favourite lifts, injury history, frequency, split, equipment and goal physiques (including from art or fiction). Help is reactive, the aim is independence, and he refunds and drops people not interested in that. He dislikes the label "coach".
IND-22 | No Discord, vlogs or merch; no bans | He refuses Discord, vlogs and merch because they feed parasocial worship, and he never deletes comments or bans anyone.
IND-23 | Teach only what you have lived | He only shares what he has personally experienced; teaching a topic without that experience is plagiarism.
IND-24 | Trust rules: demonstrate now, or prove it, and no lying | Rule 1: can the person demonstrate now what they teach (big or strong now)? Rule 2: if not, do they have dated, verifiable proof they once did? A record of lying, hurting others or cowardice disqualifies. Asking a teacher for proof isn't egoistic; legendary coaches (Gironda, Simmons, Coan) proved it with their own bodies first. He prefers advice from people who look like they lift, while keeping critical thinking.
IND-25 | No hypertrophy advice from the enhanced | Don't take advice from people who never reached inspiring muscularity without PEDs, who quit natural training after stalling, or who have implants; their size can't teach a natural method, and naturals look bad because they follow PED users' advice.
IND-26 | Beware the halo effect | People equate a good physique or size with knowledge; you can't switch this off, but judge information by logic. Social media rewards the biggest freaks, so average lifters take advice from outliers and wrongly conclude they lack potential.
IND-27 | Stay in your lane | Take strength advice from powerlifters and hypertrophy advice from bodybuilders; go to the source for each quality (a gymnast for rings, a bodybuilder for biceps). Generalists give worse information.
IND-28 | Judge a program by its numbers | Judge a program by its sets, reps, exercise selection and frequency, never by its creator's fame or physique; size and strength programs can be told apart by the numbers alone, and good programming advice is good whoever says it.
IND-29 | Popularity says nothing about quality | Channel size and follower count say nothing about information quality; millions of people using a program reflects its seniority, not its quality.
IND-30 | Question anyone who profits from you | Anyone who profits from a product should have their integrity questioned, even with good intentions; when a creator starts selling to you, start questioning him. Character matters almost as much as content, and integrity means acting on your own stated values.
IND-31 | The red-flag rule | One red flag is a flaw, two means be wary, three means unsubscribe. Flags include 20-inch-arm claims, abs out of proportion with real strength, flip-flopping between bodybuilder and strength-athlete identities, banning critics, and a contrarian "everyone else is wrong" attitude.
IND-32 | Test a guru by contradicting him | Politely contradict a guru in his comments and see if he bans you; blocking or refusing to answer is a red flag.
IND-33 | Pics or it didn't happen | Unverifiable claims are worthless. Liars go straight for world-record numbers, and progress updates from small, blurry or old photos signal deception.
IND-34 | Social-media physiques are illusions | Online physiques come from pump, flexing, lighting, best angles, edited thumbnails and the one best shot of hundreds. In his "fitness influencer syndrome", successful influencers stop growing and stay lean to look bigger on camera.
IND-35 | YouTubers misreport body fat, height and intake | YouTubers understate body fat and misreport height and calories, so YouTube fitness thinks 17-18% looks obese; influencers use claimed drops to sell.
IND-36 | A good educator gives keys, not doors | A good educator gives keys but never says which doors to open and reaches people unlike himself. Don't let a teacher own you; you are your own best teacher.
IND-37 | "Do your own research" is risky for beginners | For beginners, "do your own research" is Russian roulette, because the algorithm mirrors what you click and feeds them steroid users and teen influencers; many small natural channels give excellent advice.
IND-38 | Fake naturals are "killing YouTube fitness" | In his view, fake naturals selling transformations, programs and supplements off drug-built physiques are the core rot: they need you to believe training made their size, and you learn nothing from them. Pros on drugs mostly hurt themselves; influencers who juice to sell hurt others, so "their body, their choice" fails once they sell.
IND-39 | Natural viewers, enhanced role models | He claims over 99% of fitness viewers are natural while the biggest channels are enhanced, which normalises drug use and leaves young naturals with unrelatable role models, so they get smaller and more discouraged.
IND-40 | Drop anyone who lies about drugs | If someone lies to you about drugs, stop watching; unfollow anyone pushing you toward PEDs. Even so, a natural preacher who secretly lies still keeps you natural.
IND-41 | "Just TRT" and "downsizing" are lies | He treats "just TRT", "small dose now", "downsizing" and "reached my natural limit" as lies and calls TRT the new YouTube fraud; he claims he can't name one enhanced YouTuber who isn't lying at least a little. Pros who "downsize" were likely warned by doctors and rarely lose size.
IND-42 | TRT clinics are profit-driven | In his view TRT and youth clinics hook men on high levels, downplay side effects, prescribe to almost anyone who pays and push the age lower; the "low-T modern man" narrative is pushed by those profiting from testing and supplements.
IND-43 | PED "honesty" became promotion | PED openness, jokes, memes and "harm reduction" content made PED use mainstream and work as promotion; neutrality effectively sides with PEDs. By 2025 he sees honest enhanced influencers as the main problem, more than fake naturals. He compares it to tobacco and says prevention by preaching works.
IND-44 | PEDs to make money don't pay | Taking PEDs to earn from your physique is wrong and doesn't work: most young users never go pro or earn, and many pro-card holders still need side jobs.
IND-45 | Naturals shouldn't do business with PED users | A natural who promotes natural lifting shouldn't collaborate or do business with PED users, and he never will; naturals who tempt people toward drugs "are not his brothers".
IND-46 | Pro bodybuilding is a dying fringe sport | Natural bodybuilding is barely represented, enhanced bodybuilding is choking the sport, and pro bodybuilding is a fringe, dying sport of unrelatable freaks watched mostly by non-lifters. He no longer watches the Olympia.
IND-47 | Rich Piana as the honest exception | In his view Rich Piana was the one honest PED user, loved because he was open rather than selling lies, and created the "dad" influencer genre; NH believes he had implants.
IND-48 | Natty-or-not and call-out videos don't work | They give the target attention and victimhood, feed insecurity, black-pill a generation and benefit only the creator and the subject ("lifters' own tabloids"); starve fake naturals of attention and they fade.
IND-49 | How natty-or-not grew | He says the format, started around 2014 by Blaha, Vegan Gains, Philion, Doucette and MPMD, grew the biggest channels out of jealousy and greed; PEDs, name-dropping and drama are "the holy trinity of virality".
IND-50 | Criticise concepts, not people | Criticise practices and concepts, not people; he asks permission before naming someone in a response video, though published programs and methods are fair game.
IND-51 | Most fitness YouTube is drama | Most of YouTube fitness is drama and gossip, which is endless, easy and valueless.
IND-52 | Simple programs, nothing revolutionary | There is no "proven" program and nothing revolutionary: famous novice programs are famous because they're old, programs recombine the same concepts, and good advice is boring. Long science intros and pages of EMG text are fluff.
IND-53 | Strip the gimmicks | Most YouTuber programs become decent once the gimmicks are cut; cool names, showy suffering such as 50-rep finishers, and deliberately polarising, contrarian angles are how they're sold.
IND-54 | Constant rotation is a bad sign | Programs that rotate exercises constantly over 16 weeks are rarely well thought out.
IND-55 | YouTube fitness is powerlifting-centric | Much online hypertrophy advice is "strength training wearing a disguise", producing big trunks and legs with afterthought arms; novice programs groom aesthetics-seekers into powerlifters.
IND-56 | Powerlifting also brought the good programming | Around 2012 powerlifting brought advice that worked (compound focus, no endless reps, frequency planned for progression), and strength sports gave the platform its good programming principles; even today a medium powerlifting channel gives naturals better size advice than a big bodybuilding channel. Following powerlifters gave him big early progress.
IND-57 | No sport owns a movement | Bodybuilders used the big three as long as powerlifters; telling young lifters to do only isolation, or only the big three, is equally wrong. Today's "basics" are just the powerlifting meta (the pullover was once a staple).
IND-58 | "Powerbuilding" is a marketing term | "Powerbuilding" means the big three plus lazy isolation sets; compounds plus isolation is just how natural bodybuilders always trained, and most powerbuilders end up poor at both sports. Reclaim the word "bodybuilding".
IND-59 | Starting Strength is wrong for bodybuilding | He calls Starting Strength "garbage" for bodybuilding: trainees mostly got fatter, with big legs and a lagging upper body. A program that needs a book to understand is bad, and Rippetoe's "isolation only makes you sore" is nonsense in his view.
IND-60 | Programs and sources he rates | He ranks bodybuilding.com (community-vetted) above Reddit above T-Nation, and valued old /fit/ because nobody sold anything. He says Alsruhe's Dark Horse template beats 90% of bodybuilding programs and that his own programming derives from it; 4 Horsemen (by a strongman) is one of the best hypertrophy programs he has reviewed.
IND-61 | The pendulum of trends | YouTube fitness swings between extremes: bro bodybuilding then powerlifting, minimalism vs maximalism, science vs anti-science, maingaining vs face-stuffing. The truth sits in the middle, and he wants the pendulum to stop "halfway".
IND-62 | Minimalism speaks only to novices | Minimalists ("just do X instead") take away valuable work and only speak to novices; low-maintenance fitness for the lowest common denominator is mediocrity, and "no time" people have time for Netflix.
IND-63 | "Functional training" is mostly LARPing | Real athletes and soldiers just call it training or sport-specific training; bodybuilders needn't justify getting bigger, and "useless muscle" talk is propaganda. "Train like an athlete to look like one" is slower than isolating the hypertrophic movement patterns.
IND-64 | Suffering is theatre | Exhaustion, screaming, puking, collapsing and burn are theatre or cardio fatigue, not markers of a productive set.
IND-65 | Meme myths: fried CNS and fixed set counts | The "fried CNS" meme is nonsense at normal training loads, and fixed weekly-set numbers for naturals are just the new version of the old 8-12 rep myth.
IND-66 | Low-rep failure methods repackage HIT | Low-rep-to-failure methods are a softer repackaging of Mentzer's HIT; they work mainly by forcing people who never trained hard to train hard, and promoters use machines because free-weight low reps to failure are hard on tendons. He says Mentzer spread an ineffective method through charisma and looks.
IND-67 | Tier lists show their maker's taste | Creators like a lift first and then find reasons, so exercise tier lists download their maker's subjectivity, never match each other and go obsolete.
IND-68 | Too far toward ultra-stable lifts | Modern hypertrophy culture leans too far toward low-fatigue, ultra-stable lifts; stripping coordination out makes bodybuilding niche and joyless.
IND-69 | Evolve, but not for its own sake | Fitness should evolve and tradition or nostalgia aren't arguments, but stay conservative when the new method is worse; staples became popular for a reason.
IND-70 | "Science-based" is a marketing label | "Science-based" has become a brand and identity: calling your method optimal claims the high ground and shuts down criticism, "Doctor" titles are arguments from authority, and few of these creators are scientists or read studies well. In his view leaders don't correct their dogmatic fans (he names Israetel's).
IND-71 | Science fear-mongering makes people train less | Fear about fatigue and "optimal" training makes people over-control variables and train less, and promising big extra growth from each new method causes program-hopping; many would have trained more healthily without science content.
IND-72 | Meatheads vs science: each has a strong side | Science has nearly all the logical arguments, meatheads the appealing mindset; each camp has a strong and a weak side, and drama keeps either from wanting the pendulum to stop.
IND-73 | Parasocial bonds are harmful | Parasocial relationships with fitness YouTubers rob followers of identity, turn the creator into a father figure and make fans easy to steer into purchases. Loyalty is for family and your dog; watch for your own benefit. Constant positivity hooks dopamine, and he deliberately offends to break the bond.
IND-74 | Fanboyism blinds | He likens fanboyism to a mental illness: it blinds followers to lies, makes them swing from total love to total hate, and fanboys never build great physiques because they stop developing themselves.
IND-75 | Don't copy idols or hop gurus | Jumping from an exposed guru to his "saviour" is an abusive cycle and only you can save yourself; social media creates "clone wars", and if you clone someone, don't clone him.
IND-76 | Masculinity gurus sell an image | Masculinity and self-improvement gurus sell an image (money, girls, cigars) and test boosters, blame low T or outside factors and keep men frustrated; anyone selling how to be a man is a scam artist. Real masculinity is internal, free and individual, and the spiritual side is left out because it can't be sold.
IND-77 | Dogmatism comes from brands | Dogmatism usually comes from a brand that must sell something. Growth takes some dogmatism, because audiences dislike creators who change their minds and novices prefer black-and-white channels.
IND-78 | Growth requires clickbait | To grow on fitness YouTube you must clickbait, and most million-subscriber channels lose integrity; he chose to make what he wants and stay small. Titles matter more than thumbnails, and fear thumbnails ("big mistake") force clicks.
IND-79 | Entertainment replaced education | YouTube fitness began as informative longer videos and has been taken over by entertainment, Shorts and meme channels; edutainment drifts until entertainment replaces education, and Shorts are almost never informative, though a tutorial short beats a meme short.
IND-80 | Cut the fitness-content diet | Watching lots of fitness content is information gluttony; the more experienced you are, the less you watch.
IND-81 | Novices are the target market | Most fitness content consumers are complete beginners who imprint on the first big channel and form a "renewable pool"; most channels are good for novices, worse for intermediates and useless for advanced lifters, so big channels are gatekeepers to graduate from.
IND-82 | Fitness media is bodybuilding in disguise | Looking better is the most popular online fitness pursuit; CrossFit, Olympic lifting, MMA and calisthenics gained popularity through their athletes' looks.
IND-83 | Comparison content harms self-image | Constant exposure to bigger physiques online worsens self-image, and online genetics talk is used to make the gifted feel superior and others feel bad, never constructively. Many creators stopped progressing to maintain and monetise (some cut and bulk the same ~20 lb yearly), and those who put YouTube over training run out of real knowledge.
IND-84 | Strength standards aren't objective | Online strength standards are invented from YouTubers' egos ("3 plates in 2 years or you're a loser") or averaged from unfiltered data, so a universal standard fits no one; the honest answer to "when will I have a big chest" is that no one knows.
IND-85 | Comments are free engagement | Deleting comments or banning critics hurts a channel's reach, and buying subscribers kills its algorithmic standing.
IND-86 | Women's fitness is neglected and predatory | YouTube fitness is made for men; women get light "toning" weights, kickbacks and band work, or are told to train like men. Women need their own programming, ideally developed by women, because of different body types, injury tolerance and muscle distribution.
IND-87 | All lifters should support each other | Bodybuilders, powerlifters, strongmen and calisthenics athletes share a love of iron; swole-shaming and "power copers" who failed at aesthetics poison the community.
IND-88 | Fitness should breed above-average people | Fitness is elitist and shouldn't cater to the lowest denominator; body positivity removes agency, except when it gets fat people into the gym.
IND-89 | Experienced lifters should teach for free | Experienced lifters should teach newcomers to stay long-term and pass knowledge on for nothing in return.
IND-90 | Don't be the form police | Unsolicited form correction rarely helps; only help gym strangers who approach you first.
IND-91 | His 200-point channel grading | He grades fitness channels on 20 criteria scored 0-10: 17 core (programming, experience, integrity, usefulness, character, dogmatism, originality, black pill, humor, parasocial, empathy, god complex, production, science, clickbait, recycling, conciseness) plus 3 bonuses (seniority, not selling supplements, physique). Weights are equal although he thinks programming matters more.
IND-92 | Creators he recommends | For programming and size he recommends Nippard (best programming channel in his view, Essentials program), Alsruhe, AlphaDestiny, Schofield, Basement Bodybuilding, Nalewanyj, Israetel/RP for programming and nutrition (not PED advice), Michael Gundill, and Devon Larratt for planning.
IND-93 | Creators he criticises | He criticises AthleanX (in his view fake weights and can't program for size), Doucette (integrity 0 in his scoring, extreme standards that black-pill), Humiston, Liver King, MPMD and Huberman (supplement sales), and those he calls the Gymshark "fake natties" of his generation.
IND-94 | The "case profile" series on Blaha and Nether Beast | In a long series he alleges that Jason Blaha and Nether Beast faked lifts and plates, invented backgrounds, ran sock accounts and sent copy-paste client programs; these are his allegations, which he says he cross-checks while protecting informants.
SCI-01 | Your own body is the best evidence | Your own body's data is the most valid evidence; studies are group generalisations that are worthless until tested on yourself, and your experience matters more than his or all the science. "Optimal" is whatever you've proven works on your body.
SCI-02 | He doesn't cite studies | He never cites or links studies or uses them as arguments of authority; his channel shares experience, and a natural with a good physique is "a mine of knowledge" as valuable as peer review.
SCI-03 | You can grow without science | There is a threshold of needed knowledge, mainly technique and form adapted to your body; beyond that, literature is a bonus ("the cherry on top"). Biomechanics are important but learned through training rather than books.
SCI-04 | Read science only if it improves training | Learning only from your body costs mistakes and possibly gains, so combine both; read science only insofar as it improves your training, and if it takes away from training it's a hindrance.
SCI-05 | The middle ground: experience ×10 | The best approach sits between experience and science; real evidence-based lifting is "experience-based lifting times ten", pooling many people's experience. Good science coaching mixes bro-scientist and real scientist, which is nearly impossible. The science boom triggered an anti-science swing, and both camps strawman each other.
SCI-06 | The note-taking lifter is a scientist | Someone who trains 10 years, takes notes, learns to program and eats well is more of a scientist than paper-waving YouTubers; for him science means empiricism, and programming is a reproducible science with hard numbers.
SCI-07 | One observation is not evidence | One person's observation is experience, not evidence: share it, but don't call it scientific. A method working for you doesn't prove it's superior, because novices grow on anything new in a surplus.
SCI-08 | Bro science is often science not yet proven | Lifters reached many now-accepted guidelines by instinct decades before science backed them; nucleus overload, weighted stretching and lengthened partials were practised before the studies.
SCI-09 | ~90% has been known for a century | New "optimal" research covers the remaining 10% and mostly confirms the basics; supposed breakthroughs have sat in the literature for decades. Newer studies on volume, tempo, partials and lengthened training are inconclusive and contradictory, which "keeps the content flowing". Studies are best at saying what not to do.
SCI-10 | Simple but not easy | Hypertrophy is simple but not easy and has no hidden secret; if a principle seems complex, it's probably misunderstood. Some aspects, like nucleus overload, are underused.
SCI-11 | Anti-scientism, not anti-science | He opposes what science has become on YouTube ("meme science", scientism), not the scientific method. Scientism means trusting methods so much you stop checking them; "believe in science" is a contradiction because real science is skepticism, and calling critics "anti-science" is a dishonest shield.
SCI-12 | A study exists for any belief | For any belief you can google a supporting study; most people read only the abstract and conclusion, never the methods. Starting from a conclusion and then finding a study is scientism, and nearly every fitness YouTuber cites science only when it agrees with them.
SCI-13 | A paper is an opinion backed by facts | A paper must be interpreted, the same statistics can say many things, and findings are true only on a timeline. He gives information as opinion so he can update without staking his identity on a study; science channels silently swap papers without admitting error.
SCI-14 | Industry-funded studies favour their funders | Studies funded by food, pharma, supplement, soy, dairy or egg interests reliably favour their funders, and science used to push products becomes marketing; single-compound studies can't validate an untested blend, and sellers twist turkesterone studies knowing buyers won't check.
SCI-15 | Citing science can be an authority move | Citing a paper is itself an argument from authority; linking a paper to end a discussion is anti-speech, and "source?" is usually a dismissal tactic.
SCI-16 | Too much study-reading paralyses | Endless study consumption causes paralysis by analysis and raises "pampered lifters" waiting for the perfect workout; hyping science to novices is detrimental.
SCI-17 | Science used to win arguments is worthless | Using science to win arguments or feel superior makes it worthless; abstract-readers who think they're experts are worse than laymen. When he dismantles a study someone throws at him, the reply is always silence.
SCI-18 | Strength science isn't hypertrophy science | Most exercise science is designed around strength and function; applying it straight to hypertrophy is like a surgeon using veterinary studies. The fitness-fatigue model lumps muscle and CNS together and ignores that bodybuilders needn't peak.
SCI-19 | Pop science loses substance | Popularised science loses substance, a trade worth it only if it serves practitioners; pop-science fitness is "Kellogg's to oatmeal" and removes the work of reaching your own conclusions.
SCI-20 | Rule 3: credentials | His third trust rule is credentials or diplomas in the field; meeting any one of his three rules (demonstrates now, verifiable past proof, credentials; see IND-24) earns his ear, and all three is best. He respects PhDs but is cooler toward academics who never built muscle.
SCI-21 | Knowing means being able to replicate | To know something practical like lifting means being able to replicate it; once drugs are excluded, a natural's physique says something about his methods. Of the two outward signs of authority, social proof proves nothing and evidence the method works is what counts, as you judge an oncologist by patients cured.
SCI-22 | Science-heavy people are often small | Science-based influencers aren't bigger than experience-based lifters, which weakens their claim to a superior method.
SCI-23 | Judge methods by the physiques they produce | Judge methods by the physiques their programs produce across trainees, not by individual anecdotes.
SCI-24 | Fringe theories are usually wrong | A fringe theory that denies what most agree on (for example, that volume matters) is most likely wrong; anything that works eventually becomes conventional.
SCI-25 | Volume, intensity and frequency drive growth | Volume, intensity and frequency sit at the top of his pyramid as the main drivers of hypertrophy; beware anyone who says one of them can be ignored. Tempo is near the bottom for naturals.
SCI-26 | Growth comes from damage under heavy load | Resistance training aims at muscle-fibre damage that repairs bigger, and most growth comes from damage under heavy weight.
SCI-27 | Stretch vs concentric | Earlier he held that muscle is gained mostly via weighted stretch and that the negative is the key; he now believes the concentric builds more than the eccentric and calls the stretch-focused trend contrarian.
SCI-28 | Pump, burn and soreness aren't growth signals | The pump comes from fast light reps (fast empty-bar curls pump more than heavy ones), a burn means fast-fibre activation rather than a hypertrophy variable, and soreness isn't correlated with growth. He isn't anti-pump: blood flow may help a little.
SCI-29 | Metabolic stress and TUT are minor | Metabolic stress is an accessory at best; time under tension is relevant but too chaotic to be a main principle.
SCI-30 | Rep range and tempo matter little | Tempos across a wide range gave similar hypertrophy with load adjusted, and the special hypertrophy rep range is "mostly dead", since most ranges work close to failure.
SCI-31 | EMG predicts growth poorly | EMG and activation data are poor predictors of growth and often misread: EMG shows more chest than lats on chin-ups and more abs on squats than crunches, compound-favouring EMG studies use novices, and over the long term isolation is "king" for body parts. Small per-set differences (sumo vs conventional) still compound over years into visibly different development.
SCI-32 | More stretch or activation ≠ more growth | Better stretch or activation in a study doesn't mean more growth, and researchers chase spicy findings like leg press beating squats.
SCI-33 | Volume-equated frequency studies are misleading | The study showing equal growth at 1x vs 3x/week with volume equated is flawed in his view, because higher frequency lets you accumulate more volume, so volume is never equal in practice.
SCI-34 | Distrust of "failure is unnecessary" studies | He distrusts studies claiming failure is unnecessary, finding their parameters skewed toward the conclusion.
SCI-35 | Nucleus overload works in his experience | High-volume, high-frequency, low-intensity work adds satellite cells and myonuclei in his experience; he doesn't care whether the mechanism is hypertrophy or hyperplasia, only whether the muscle grows. Youth activity may raise myonuclei for life.
SCI-36 | Neural adaptation is a learning phase | Neural adaptation happens in a learning phase, not over years; once proficient, strength gains come from muscle unless form changes, and joints strengthen far too slowly to explain yearly gains. Past the novice phase, track strength and tonnage as the sign of muscle gain.
SCI-37 | Muscles don't need shocking | Muscles don't need confusing or "shocking"; bones, tendons and fibres just respond to the stress placed on them.
SCI-38 | Range-of-motion data are thin | Hypertrophy value can't be assigned universally by phase of a lift; it depends on the lift and range of motion, and nobody agrees which lift loads where. ROM/mechanical tension ranks 3rd in his tier list.
SCI-39 | Tier system for training parameters | He ranks training parameters S (vital), A (important), B (useful), C (negligible), D (irrelevant or sabotaging); build training from S and A, treat C and D as optional fun. Fast/slow-twitch profiling is D tier: find out in practice which muscles respond to high or low reps.
SCI-40 | Belief changes results | Through placebo, a suboptimal method you believe in can work, and through nocebo a distrusted optimal one can underperform.
SCI-41 | No body-fat method is accurate | No body-fat method is accurate, DEXA included; DEXA can be skewed by water intake, and a scan proves nothing without a baseline. Track strength and tonnage instead.
SCI-42 | PEDs work even without training | He cites men on testosterone outgrowing properly training naturals without training themselves, and notes real users take far more for much longer.
SCI-43 | Testosterone-decline claims are doubtful | Evolution can't halve testosterone in a century, so modern men are biologically like drug-free bodybuilders of 100 years ago; decline studies may reflect poor lifestyles.
SCI-44 | Supplement T-boost data are weak | Supplement testosterone data are weak and rat studies are irrelevant; he notes the human figures cited by MPMD for tongkat ali and ashwagandha. A study claiming cricket powder is more digestible than whey mostly showed faster absorption.
SCI-45 | NoFap and semen retention are myths | Testosterone dips briefly after ejaculation then returns to baseline, abstinence gives a brief rise before levels fall back, and feeling horny or aggressive isn't elevated T; boxers' sex before fights doesn't hurt performance.
SCI-46 | Long sessions don't make you catabolic | The idea that training past an hour or so turns you catabolic via cortisol is nonsense.
SCI-47 | Resistance training protects health | Longevity correlates with resistance training, it makes bones denser, and exercise doesn't age you.
SCI-48 | Muscle memory is real but not magic | Muscle memory speeds regaining lost muscle but isn't magic, and there's no evidence that training while starving "stores" gains for later.
PHI-01 | Build body and mind together | Body and spirit must be developed together; the ideal is the "complete man" or "educated barbarian". Training without reading and thinking is as unbalanced as theory without a body.
PHI-02 | Modern life inverted the mind-body balance | Modern life is almost all mind and little body, while antiquity was balanced. He blames mind-body dualism (traced to Christianity's view of the body as impure) and schools built to train desk workers, and wants physical education restored.
PHI-03 | The body is not a disposable vessel | Body and soul are two sides of one coin; a mind in an inactive body withers. He cites ancient models: Socrates on citizens having no right to be amateurs in physical training, Plato's name possibly meaning "broad shoulders", Nietzsche's long walks.
PHI-04 | Lifting is a Way and a spiritual practice | Lifting is "iron meditation", a ritual linking you to ancestors who did physical labour, and worship of the body rather than narcissism. Its true value is spiritual; if you are not physically training you are not walking the Way. Bodybuilding has been desacralized.
PHI-05 | Lifting teaches agency and independence | Lifting teaches personal responsibility and that hard work pays. It is anti-fragile, anti-consumerist and anti-establishment because strength makes you need institutions less. If everyone lifted, most social problems would shrink; he calls fitness "the most important thing a man can do".
PHI-06 | Results without effort can't fulfil you | Natural lifting builds lasting pride; results bought with a crutch can't fulfil you ("equivalent exchange": results without effort are paid for with lifespan). A big natural body built properly "brings a philosophy with it".
PHI-07 | The gym won't fix your life | The milestone you chase (three-plate bench, six-pack) won't make you happy. Muscle is "only a boost" that needs a personality to match, PEDs don't cure insecurity, and social skills matter more day to day.
PHI-08 | Fitness should enhance life, not consume it | Any physical culture is valid if your heart is in it; it doesn't have to be bodybuilding, but every man should be active in some form (lifting, running, swimming). Training ranks below family and work.
PHI-09 | Discipline spreads to the rest of life | Discipline and virtue carry over from one area of life to every other, the way a strong squat carries over to every knee-flexion exercise. Lifting has to be experienced; reading about it can't replace doing it.
PHI-10 | Lifting is Sisyphus, not war | Meaning, even happiness, comes from repeating the effort ("we freely choose our boulder", after Camus). Framing lifting as war is good motivation but takes the hobby too seriously, since you are not risking your life.
PHI-11 | Health comes before muscle | Being muscular is pointless if you die in your 50s; sickness takes the muscle anyway. Natural bodybuilding is also about a long life. Great genetics don't excuse neglecting health (he cites Apollon, a lifelong smoker who died young of throat cancer).
PHI-12 | Muscle is your lifeline in old age | People lose the ability to walk without glute, hamstring and quad muscle. He wants to be mobile and strong at 70-80 and keep the ability to squat and hinge heavy, even with less volume and intensity. "We all age but we don't have to get old."
PHI-13 | Decline comes from inactivity, not age | The age at which people say their body "stopped working" keeps falling; someone who takes care of himself should still thrive at 40, 50, 60 and beyond. Old men who keep their fire share a lifelong passion, physical activity and a family.
PHI-14 | Spend your energy fully while young | Work hard until 40 because that energy won't reappear at 60; two decades of hard work buy four easier decades with a good body. In old age, accept the contraction: expand like a supernova in your prime, then let wisdom replace strength. Clinging to youth is the neurosis of the old.
PHI-15 | Get regular medical check-ups | Most early male deaths come from ignoring check-ups. Do bloodwork, dentist and general check-ups regularly and start prostate screening on time.
PHI-16 | Prevention first, medicine as last resort | He refuses drugs while healthy but would take a pill needed to survive; psychiatric medication is fine as a crutch to start change, not for life.
